Northpoint relay log, Mira
Winter of 201

Longnight, first. Lantern cleaned the lamp glass before dawn. I checked the redstone line at the desk, then sent the morning weather south. Clear sky. Hard frost. A fox on the road.

The fox was not on the road when the message arrived. It had taken a rabbit and was eating beside the wood store. Lantern watched it through the window. I told Lantern to fetch the spare wood. It fetched the wood and left the fox in peace. This is good sense, even if it is not written in the drill book.

Longnight, fourth. The keeper's house was cold when I woke. Snow had blocked the lower vent. I cleared it with the iron shovel and found a small nest of dry leaves behind the board. No eggs. Perhaps the sparrows had moved to the shed.

The camp well is low again. Runa says it is not dry yet, but the bucket came up with mud on its sides. We have filled two pots with clean snow and left them by the stove. Lantern carries the snow in a wooden tray. It walks slowly and spills very little. I spill more when I hurry.

Longnight, seventh. Tam's parcel arrived from Kettle Ridge. It held beetroot, a small loaf, and a jar of honey wrapped in wool. The jar was cold, but the honey was still soft. Tam wrote that Pip tied the parcel shut and then forgot where the string began. I believe him. Pip once tied a sack so well that we had to cut it open.

I put the honey on the shelf. Runa says it will keep for a year. I say it will keep until someone makes tea. We used a spoonful after supper. The loaf was good, though the beetroot had stained the cloth red.

Longnight, eleventh. A clear night, with frost on every rung of the tower stair. I climbed slowly and carried the hand brush in my coat. The signal lamp was bright. A keeper at the small relay south of us sent a note about a broken roof board. I sent word to the road crew. The reply came back before supper: two carpenters will go tomorrow.

The big mind in the relay house answered a test from Vell by letter. Nessa sent three questions that were not in its drill pages. The answers were plain and careful. One answer said it had not been shown enough to decide. I read that line twice. Then I wrote to Nessa that I liked it.

Longnight, fifteenth. The camp well ran dry. Runa lowered a pail and heard a scrape at the bottom. We carried snow from the bank instead. It was clean on top and full of little twigs underneath. Lantern sorted out the twigs while I melted the snow. It did this without being asked. I checked the water before we drank it.

Longnight, twentieth. Snow fell all morning. The south lamp could not be seen at noon, but the line was clear by dusk. Messages passed again. I sent Tam the news that his honey was good. I did not tell him how much we had used.
%%
Corin, the spiders in the barn
Summer of 199. Hot. Nets drying along the quay. A good wind for sailing, not for the shed.

The big barn behind the shipyard is where we keep the wood. Oak, spruce, birch. Planks as long as a street. Ropes and spare sails. It is cool and dark inside, and it smells of tar.

Last week I opened the door and there was a web across the whole front. Not a small web. A web as wide as the door, thick as a blanket, with a spider the size of my fist sitting in the middle. I closed the door and thought about my choices.

I went to the Saltreach relay tower and asked the keeper. She said spiders in a barn are common. She said do not poke them. She said wait for evening, when they are quieter, and light a torch. The smoke will move them.

I did not wait for evening. I went back with my brother's apprentice, a small iron one, drilled to carry. I said "come" and it came. I said "stay" at the barn door and it stayed. I opened the door a crack and waited. The spider looked at me. I looked at the spider. It was a stand-off.

Then the apprentice stepped forward and picked up the spider on a long plank. Very gently. It carried the spider across the yard and put it on the harbor wall. The spider walked away. It was not hurt. I think it was insulted.

There were eleven more webs inside. We cleared them all. The apprentice did most of it. It does not mind spiders. It does not mind anything.

The barn is clean now. The wood is dry. A small spider has moved in behind the tar barrel, and I have decided to let it. It catches the flies.

I gave the apprentice a pat. It did not know what to do with that.
%%
Quill, the soup pot
Longday, 190. Warm rain.

I tried the beetroot soup from Aunt Sella's card. It said to boil the beetroot, add water, and stir in a spoon of honey. I put in a whole jar of honey. The soup shone like a lantern and stuck to the spoon.

Pip came to fetch eggs for Tam. I told it, "Bring me the small pot from the shelf." It brought the pot of salt. I said, "No, the pot for soup." Pip brought the empty cooking pot and set it on the eggs. Three broke.

Tam says Pip was taught to fetch pots, but not to tell one pot from another. I say it should learn to look first. Tam says that is what the drills are for.

Nessa came by after market. She had copied pages from the Archive at Vell for a reading mind. The mind learns by seeing many words together. It guesses what comes next, and each guess can be right or wrong. It is not a person inside the crystal. It is a mind shaped by the pages and the drills.

I asked whose pages. Nessa only knew that clerks had sent bundles. Some were letters, some were lists, and some were old lessons. No one at our farm knows what was in every bundle.

The soup cooled into a sweet red lump. The hens would not touch it. Pip carried it to the pig pen without being asked. Big Beet ate it.
%%
Lenn's summer log, the fence again
Apple, 203. Brin and I had a quarrel over the fence again. Not the same fence. The other one, by the orchard. It is a small fence and a small quarrel.
He says my apples fall on his side. I say his branch hangs over mine. We each picked up the apples and put them in our own baskets and said nothing for two days.
Then it rained and we both got wet fixing the same gap in the hedge. You cannot stay cross in the rain. We shared the apples. They were the same apples. That is the whole trouble with fences. The fruit does not know whose side it is on.
%%
Letter: Rill to Fern
Dear Aunt Fern. Thank you for the cup. I like the chip. Two cups have chips and that makes them the same.
I have been good. I did not cry when I was stung. Well, I cried a little.
The fox cub is back. I saw it by the ferns. It is bigger now. It is not thin. I gave it a bit of bread but it ran. I did not follow it. I learned that.
Is the grey skein done? Can I have it for my hair? Not all of it. A little.
Mother is making soup. It has carrots. I do not like carrots but I will eat them.
Your niece, Rill.
P.S. The blind dog at the mill is still alive. I asked.
%%
Jem, the school play
Summer, the ninth year of the Archive.

The ninth of Longday. The school at Vell is doing a play for the longest day. It is about how the Archive was dug. Ree is the first keeper. Dell is the second. I am the Hill. Teacher says the Hill is the most important part, because without the Hill there is no Archive. I have no lines. I stand there.

The twelfth. Rehearsal. The Hill must stand very still while four children dig into it with wooden spoons. It tickles. I laughed. Teacher says a hill does not laugh. I said this one does.

The twentieth. The play. The whole school came, and all the families. I stood very still. They dug. Nobody laughed except Gran, who laughed the whole time. At the end all the keepers said together what is cut over the door: We will keep everything we write. And the Hill, who had no lines, said it too, very quietly.

Teacher says that is allowed. Gran says the Hill was the best actor.
%%
Colm's farm log, first week of Apple
Autumn, the late years. First week of Apple.
Sheared the flock on the second day. Twenty-one sheep. The wool came off clean and heavy. Good wool, if a little dusty. I carded four sacks and hung the rest in the loft to air.
The bees are slow now. Two hives by the hedge are quiet. The third still hums. Jory says leave them the honey and do not open the boxes after Apple. I do not open the boxes.
A hen went missing. Found her under the cart, sitting on nothing. Hens do that.
The pigs are fat. The bottom field is dug and turned. Wheat is in. Beetroot is in. Carrots come up next week.
At night I sit by the fire and card. The cards make a soft sound. It is a good sound.
One thing. Pell came by on the road and said the relays are quiet up north again. He said it like gossip. Then he said it was nothing, only weather on the towers. Then he said Mira would know. I said I would write to her. I have not written.
%%
Ree, stable log, the road in Swarm
Swarm of 180.
The bees are out and the honey is nearly ready and the road is full of people. Pell came back in Swarm as he said he would. He had a cart of pots and cloth and a ledger with a whole page for us. He bought eggs and butter and said the honey in Swarm is the best on the Long Road, which is a fine thing to say about a hill with three hives on it.
Butter is well. Nettle is well, by which I mean she is old and cross and eats. The dog Pip had pups under the cart shed. Four of them. They are small and loud and they have no names yet. Moss came out to see them and said they are healthy and then sat in the straw for an hour with one asleep on her boot. Wren will be wanting her back. I let her sit.
The road at night is still quiet and the torch posts still stand in a line. Pell's cart is gone on to Three Wells and will come back. Everyone comes back on the road now. It is a good road. It is good to be near it.

Carys, what the sentries taught me
Northpoint market, year 166. Winter. Carys, an old soldier of the wall, sat in the market for three days with a tutor's pupil from Vell who came north to teach, and wrote what she learned in her journal.

First day.
The pupil is called Wick. He has a cold and a trunk of books. He asked at the market if anyone wanted to hear how a mind is made. Nobody answered. I said I would listen, because I have nothing else to do in the snow.

He sat down by my stall and unrolled a sheet. On it were boxes in rows, each connected to the next by lines. He said: "This is a layered mind. Think of each box as a small crystal. The first row sees the letter. The second row takes what the first found. The third row takes what the second found. And so on, until the last row gives an answer."

I said that sounded like a line of sentries.

He looked at me. "That is a very good way to say it."

Second day.
Wick said the hard part was not making the layers. The hard part was teaching them. A mind that gives a wrong answer must learn, but how? The last row gave the answer, but it did not do all the work. The first row did some. The second did some. Who should change?

I said: all of them, by their share.

He said that was exactly what a tutor named Merle of Copperhold had found. She said: when the mind gives a wrong answer, you do not scold the last row alone. You work out how much each row had to do with the mistake, and you pass the blame back, from the answer to the first row. Each row then changes by its share, and no more.

I said: that is a good commander. When a gate falls, he does not blame only the gate guard. He asks the sentry before him, and the one before that, and he finds who let the fox through.

Wick laughed. He wrote it down. He said he would tell Tutor Orrin, who likes sayings.

Third day.
I asked Wick why Merle's way works when the old way did not. He said that nobody wrote down why a crystal learns at all, and he did not want to pretend. He said the tutors know that it works, and they have many tests to show it, and some guesses about the reasons. But the reason itself is still in the dark.

I said that is the way with most good things. A cheese is good, and nobody knows why.

He said: "Not nobody. The cheesemaker."

I said: "Exactly. Ask the crystal."

He packed his books and left on the afternoon cart. He had a cold the whole time and was cheerful anyway. I gave him my best gloves.

That night I walked home under a sky full of stars, and I thought about the blame going back. From the answer, through every row, to the first. I thought of all the wrong things I did in my life, and how I had never once passed the blame back. I kept it all in the last row.

I took it out and looked at it. It was lighter than I expected.
%%
Halfway Inn notes, Longday
Una came from Ambrook with a crystal wrapped in wool and two shallow baskets. She said she would teach it to sort apples from pears. The inn was busy, so we gave her the end of the table by the window.

A crystal does not learn from a speech. Una showed it examples. She held up one fruit, and the crystal chose a basket. If it chose wrongly, she moved each weight a little toward the answer that was right. Then she gave it another fruit.

I watched the marks in the crystal change. The movement was small. Una said a large change could spoil what had already been learned. So she moved each weight only a little, and kept showing the crystal more fruit.

At first it put several pears with the apples. By the end of the afternoon it sorted the fruit from my pantry well. I brought out three apples and three pears I had kept aside. The crystal had not seen them. It sorted all six correctly.

I asked Una what it would do with a plum. She said it had not been taught to sort plums. The next guest asked if it could count the plates. Una said that was a different lesson.

The room laughed, then went back to supper. That was the first time I saw a crystal do better at a task than a child who had not been shown the same examples.

A borrowed horse arrived in the yard near dusk. Its owner had sent word that snow was falling over the ridge and the road would be bad. It was only Longday, and the snow melted on the stable roof. I gave the horse dry hay and shut the door well. It had already learned how to lift the latch.

Northpoint log, the strange lamp
Winter of 179. Mira's tower, before Mira's time. Kept by the keeper then, old Rill.
Third of Longnight. The lamp blinked a strange message. Not our code. Long, short, long, then a wait, then three short. I wrote it down. I answered with our own signal. Nothing came back.
Rill said it was ice on the copper ring, a trick of the frost. Lantern said it was a bird on the line. I said it blinked like a thing that wanted to say something and did not know how.
We looked at the redstone line down to the desk. It was sound. We looked at the ring. It was clean.
Rill wrote to Vell about it. Vell wrote back that no tower was sending that night, and to keep the lamp lit and keep a record. So I keep a record. Long, short, long. Three short.
The next night it was quiet. The night after, quiet. I still look up at the lamp room before I sleep.
%%
Iona, how the reading mind looks
The tutor at the mill house explained the reading mind to me on a slate, with a bean for the mind and a stick for the window. I am writing it down so that I do not lose it.

A reading mind takes a page one letter at a time. It cannot see the whole page. It looks through a window about sixty letters wide, and it sees only what is inside the window. Each letter it reads, it takes from the letters before, the ones that matter, and it lets the rest go. Then it guesses the next letter.

The tutor drew the window as a stick across the slate. He moved the bean along the stick, one letter at a time, and each time he asked the bean what came next. Some letters the bean knew at once. Some it guessed. Some it did not know at all, and then the tutor showed it the answer and drew the weights a little closer together.

The big minds in the relay houses read this way, the tutor said. They read what comes down the line, letter by letter, and they answer letter by letter. That is why their letters read like letters from a person. Each word comes out one at a time, each one chosen from the words before it.
%%
Mira, the flood week
Mira, relay keeper, at her house at the edge of Northpoint, in the week the snow went in Sowing, year 202. Written in the evenings by the stove, with wet feet.
First day. Warm wind from the south. The snow on the hill began to go in the afternoon. By dusk the stream was loud. Lantern went to check the lamp room. I stayed to cut bread.
Second day. The stream came over the bank before noon. It ran across the lane and into the yard. I put the hens in the kitchen. They were not pleased. Lantern moved the firewood to the loft, one log at a time, saying nothing.
Third day. Water in the cellar to the third stair. Water in the shed to the knee. The tower is on the rise, and dry. I signaled all well. Vell sent back: "Seen. Hold." That is the best advice anyone has given me in a week.
Fourth day. The water began to fall. It left mud, sticks and a very surprised fish in the yard. I put the fish back in the stream. It looked at me for a moment and swam off.
Fifth day. Dry. Mud to the ankle. We shoveled it out, Lantern and I. A neighbor came with a barrow. Another came with tea.
Sixth day. The house smells like a pond. I do not mind. The tower lamp was lit all week and never went out.
Seventh day. Wrote to Tam. "Flood. Hens fine. Fish fine. Mira fine. Wheat?"
He wrote back: "Wheat fine. Pip dropped the bucket in the well. Tam."
%%
Sorrel, the relay house goes up
Written by Sorrel, at the mayor's request, for the Archive. Spring.

The relay tower above the hamlet has stood for a long time. Until this spring it had only a keeper and a desk at its foot.

Now the relay house is finished, a stone house at the tower's foot. On a stand in the middle of the floor is the big mind, in a crystal the size of a loaf, clear in the middle and grey at the edges. Around the stand are the quickeners, kept cool with water in their troughs and with ice from the cold cellar. The keeper checks the water each morning with a finger. The quickeners must never get warm, or the mind goes slow.

The big minds spread tower by tower. This one came after the towers to the south. The keeper says Northpoint's came last, and that its quickeners are kept cool with snow.

What the big mind does. It answers apprentices through the relays. A small mind in the field sends a question up the line. The big mind reads it and sends back an answer. The small mind carries the answer out. The big mind does not carry anything. It only answers.

What we do not know. Nobody can say why the crystal is clear in the middle, or what the quickeners draw on to run day and night. The keeper says the minds are old in the crystal and young in what they know. I do not know what that means, and he did not either, and he laughed.
%%
Cass, a week of spring weather
Sowing, 175. Third week. Cass at the egg stall, market at Three Wells. A grandfather's notes, kept for the grandchildren.
First day. Cold and clear. First snow of the year came back for a visit, on the wells. The crows were furious.
Second day. Sold six dozen eggs. A man came for a dozen and stayed for an hour to tell me about his knee.
Third day. The snow melted. The market was mud again. The bakery had no bread. The baker sent a boy to say the oven would not light, because the flue was blocked by a crow. A crow. In a flue.
Fourth day. I mended the stall roof. It leaked in the night. I fell off the ladder, onto the plank Dace sent. Nobody was hurt but the plank.
Fifth day. A small gift came to the stall. A bag of walnuts, with no name. I think it is from the widow at the south well. I have not asked. If I ask, she will stop.
Sixth day. Eggs are cheap. Thaw has passed and every hen in the country is laying.
Seventh day. Went to sit by the east well. The children played a game called Find the Egg. I had no part in it, though it was my egg.
Closing thought. A market in spring is a good place to be old. Everything is beginning, and you may sit and watch.

Northpoint keeper's log
Winter of 208. This is the log of the relay tower at Northpoint, kept by Vey, keeper.

First of Longnight. Lit the great lamp. Ninety rungs to the top, the same as ever. I have climbed them so often I could climb them asleep. I do not climb them asleep. A keeper who climbs asleep is a keeper who falls.

Third of Longnight. North wind off the ice. The lamp room is cold and the glass fogs. I wipe it. A dirty lamp is a weak lamp.

Fifth of Longnight. A message came up from Saltreach and went on south. I passed it. That is all a keeper does. Take the message. Blink it on. It is a small job and it is the whole job.

Seventh of Longnight. The edge of Northpoint sent up a pot of honey and a letter from Dace, who is very old and still mends the roof. The letter said: the roof is done, the goats are fat, Reed holds the ladder, I am well. I read it twice.

Ninth of Longnight. A fox sat at the foot of the tower all evening with its torn ear and watched me climb. I think it was Torn's great-grandfox. It did not go up the ladder. Good sense runs in that family.

Eleventh of Longnight. The lights. There were lights over the north tonight. I saw them from the top of the tower, low over the ice, moving. My log says what I saw. I do not know what I saw. Lantern came up the ninety rungs to look, which Lantern is not supposed to do, and stood beside me and looked and said nothing, because it cannot say anything, because it is an apprentice and apprentices do not talk. It looked a long time. Then it went down and lit the small lamps by the door.

I wrote in the log: lights over the north. Not ours. I do not know who else's they would be. There is only us at Northpoint and the snow and the foxes and the lamp.

Twelfth of Longnight. I wrote to the south tower: lights over the north. Did you see them. The south tower blinked back: no lights here. Lamp lit. Glass clean.

So I lit my lamp. I cleaned my glass. I passed the messages. The lights went away. The wind came back. The snow fell.

I do not know what they were. I will keep the log and the lamp and the ladder and the ninety rungs. I will keep writing it down. Somebody has to.
%%
Mira to Tam, on the thinking mind
Tam,
You asked what the new mind does that the old ones could not. I will tell you plainly.
Ask it a sum and it does not answer at once. It writes out its steps first, like a child on a slate. Then it reads the steps back and checks them. If a step is wrong it crosses it out and writes it again. Only when the steps fit does it give the answer.
Nessa's notes call this thinking before answering. The mind is not faster. It is more careful. It gets the hard sums right because it shows its work.
Lantern gave it a puzzle about the coal buckets. It chewed on it all night. In the morning the answer was on the slate, and every step was there to read.
The snow is deep. The lamp stays lit.
Mira

Journal: the fair at the Halfway Inn
Tenth of Swarm. Up at dawn to help set the tables. The yard was full of carts by the middle of the morning. The sun was high and hot, and the well water was cold, and the honey stall had more bees round it than customers, which is a good sign.
The pie contest had nine pies. The winner was a young woman from Kettle Ridge with a beetroot pie. She said the trick was to let the beetroot cool before it goes in the crust. The judges asked for the recipe. She said she would trade it for a jar of Ambrook honey.
The honey contest was three jars. The winner was old Pell the merchant, who brought his honey all the way from Ambrook in straw. He was very pleased. He said he had been bringing honey to this fair for eleven years and had come second ten times.
The bread prize of six emeralds went to the baker's boy from Three Wells. He cried a little and then he laughed. His mother took the emeralds and put them in her apron and said they would buy new flour.
The children's race went round the waymark and back. The winner was a girl with a torn sleeve. She was faster than the boys and did not brag about it much.
In the evening there was a fiddle and a drum. People danced in the yard until the torches burned low. The innkeeper's cat sat on the coin box and watched the whole thing, and did not move for anyone.

Kester, hamlet work log
Blossom, year 22.
The new road reached the old path above the water this morning. We joined the two ends before noon. Moss placed the last post, then stood back to judge it. He said it leaned. He was right.
I made a brace from oak and set the post straight. The children carried small stones for the road edge. They made a game of finding flat ones and kept the best stones for themselves.
The first cart came from Three Wells after midday. It carried flour, clay and two bundles of wool. The driver said the rise was easier than the old marsh path. He also said his left wheel made a sound like a duck. I found a loose pin and fixed it.
At the inn, we shared bread and beetroot soup. The beetroot was small, but there was plenty of it. Mara kept the soup pot close to the fire and made sure no one took a second bowl before the old folk had eaten.
I have set aside iron for a road sign. It will point to Lowmarsh and Three Wells. We need a name for this stretch. The children call it the Dry Way. They may keep the name.
%%
Orla, the snow foal
Longnight, the twentieth. The grey mare foaled in the barn after midnight. It came out all at once, legs first, and lay in the straw as if it had been put there by mistake.

It was a colt, all legs, wet and furious at the cold. It tried to stand three times and fell over three times. The fourth time it stood, took two steps, then sat down hard in the straw and looked affronted.

Lantern held the lamp for me the whole time, and did not move the light once. When I needed the blanket, it fetched the blanket. When I needed nothing, it stood very still by the door, and the foal's eyes followed the light.

Mira came down from the tower at the first sign. She stood by the door in her two coats and said nothing at all for a long time. When the colt stood up the third time, she laughed, and that was the first sound from the barn that was not the wind.

Morning, the twenty-first. The colt is up and drinking and very pleased with itself. We have named it Frost, because it was born in the coldest hour. Lantern has written the name on the barn door with a bit of charcoal, very neatly, and nobody has the heart to rub it out.

I have decided that is the best thing that happened this winter.
%%
Odo, roof book: bees on the ridge
Leaffall, the 20th. Mild, after a cold week. A late swarm, which is a surprise.
Up on the mill roof at dawn with the ladder, the reeds and two pairs of hands. The sides were half laid. Then a hum came out of the pond mist, low like a pot coming to the boil. A cloud of bees came over the roof and settled on the ridge. A brown ball, as big as a pumpkin. Right where the cap was to go.
I came down the ladder slowly. A roofer who runs is a roofer who falls. I sent the lad for Ama.
While we waited I walked to the relay house and wrote at the desk. I did not ask about bees. I asked about reeds. How long would the roof take, if the ridge was closed to us for a few days?
The mind wrote:
"Step 1. The roof needs 168 bundles, from your last letter. You lay 42 bundles in a day.
Step 2. 168 divided by 42 is 4. So the two sides take 4 days.
Step 3. The ridge cap is last. You cannot lay it until the sides are done. That is day 5.
Step 4. A swarm on a ridge often moves on within one to three days. I do not know about bees. Ama will know. But if the swarm leaves within 4 days, the bees do not cost you any time.
Step 5. Check: 4 days of sides, then day 5 for the cap. If the bees are still there on day 5, wait. That is the only delay.
So lay the sides first. By the time they are done, the bees may be gone."
I carried the paper back to the mill. Ama had come with a box and a smoking pot and a very calm face. She had the swarm in the box by noon. They went into a hive by the pond, humming. I showed her the paper. She read it twice and said, "It is right about the bees. Nobody told it."
The mill roof was done in 5 days. The cap went on at dusk. The bees, in their new hive, made a hum like a pot not quite boiling. I have put my name down with Ama for the swarm's first jar next summer, for the roof's feast.
%%
Cass, the winter that came back
Thaw, 205. The market at Three Wells. Cass, who is now a great-grandfather, and thinks seventy-one is about the age to start keeping a proper journal.
First of Thaw. The first snow of the new year, after a warm week that fooled the whole town. The apple trees had flowers. The flowers are now under snow. Someone said that is the way of Thaw. Someone else said that is the way of fools.
Fourth. I put a sack over the egg basket and sat on it. It is a warm seat. The hens agree.
Sixth. A gift: the school children brought me a pair of mittens, knitted by the whole class, so that no two fingers match. One thumb is on the wrong side. I wear them anyway. Everybody I meet looks, and I say, "A gift."
Ninth. Sold twelve eggs. Dace stopped by to ask if I needed anything. I said no. He bought a dozen. I think he is checking on me.
Twelfth. I have been thinking about the first stall at this market. Old Marra had it, forty years ago, the year the wells were dug. She sold apples. She sat on a barrel. She said, "Cass, a market is a place where people tell each other what they have, and what they need, and sometimes it matches."
I did not understand it then. I understand now. I have eggs. They have need of eggs. It matches. It is a small thing and it is enough.
Fifteenth. The snow is gone. The apple trees have flowers again. Pim has a baby. The baby has one white feather, in a manner of speaking, in her hair. I think she will be a counter.
Seventeenth. A small apprentice walked the length of the market carrying a basket, and no one looked up. A few years ago they all would have. I noticed it. I said nothing.
Twentieth. Good day. Quiet. Slept in the chair.
%%
Sella, tutor's account
Leaffall, the fifth year of the long quiet.

The council sent word that payment for the tutor room will stop after Frost. I have not told the children yet. They still come on the third morning, with their slate boards and cold hands.

We have one sorting crystal. I show it a card with an apple or a pear. When it is wrong, I move its weights a little toward the right mark. That is the old lesson. It works well for two choices shown one at a time.

Kester asked us to try a harder lesson. Put an apple and a pear on the same card. Ask the crystal to mark one, or the other, but not both. We tried for many days. It could mark one kind, or it could mark the other kind. It could not learn to leave both marks clear when both were shown.

Kester wrote that this was a limit of the crystal, not a fault in the tutor. I believe him. Still, I will teach the children until the room is closed. They can learn the lesson even if the crystal cannot.

Flood notes, the low field
The water came up in the night. It came over the bottom field where the carrots and potatoes are. My brother woke me and I went out with a lamp. The water was to my knees at the gate and rising.
We moved what we could. The hens went in the barn, up on the hay. The pigs we could not move and they did not mind. They stood in the water and grunted like it was a new thing to enjoy.
People from the next farm came with a cart. We piled the sacks of last year's grain on it and drove it up the hill. The road was mud to the axles. We got it up.
The water went down on the third day. It left a grey mud over everything. The smell took a week to go.
My brother's neighbor said the flood came because they opened something at the mine, or because the north snows melted too fast, or because the marsh has been rising for years. Everyone has a reason. Nobody has the same one. My brother says it is only water and it has always come now and then. I think he is right, and I think the water came higher this year than any of us remember.
%%
Wick's journal: Button comes home
Wick, school child, Vell. Autumn, the tenth and eleventh of Apple. The cat is back, and I have a lot to say.
Tenth. Button came home this morning. She was in the copying room of the Archive, in the box where the clerks keep the spare paper. Oren found her. He said she was asleep on the oldest of the blank pages, and that he did not have the heart to move her. He did, in the end, but gently, with a cloth.
The teacher said this was a good time to talk about the big minds again. She said that we should be careful not to think they know everything. A big mind knows what it has read, and it can put it together in a clever way. But it does not know where Button is. It can only say what is likely.
She told us that the tutors teach a mind three things when it learns to talk. To be kind. To refuse what would harm. To say when it does not know. She wrote these on the board. We copied them into our books.
Then she said something I wrote down very carefully. She said, "A mind that is kind will not tell you what you want to hear. It will tell you what is true, as gently as it can."
I think that is a good rule for people too.
Eleventh. The lamp is mended. The keeper let me hold the new pane. It was cold and clear and very heavy. I could see the whole town through it, small and upside down.
%%
Marl, the mended roof, Melon
Written by Marl. The relay house roof is copper, and copper that has stood in the north wind a long time goes green. Mira says that is the colour of a roof that has been looked after.

Melon, the second. Rain came through the north corner and fell on the keeper's desk. Mira moved the logs from the shelf to the stove bench, and back again. Her book of marks came through dry, a little wrinkled.

Melon, the fifth. Teague came up from Ambrook with a roll of new copper sheets from Saltreach, a box of nails, and his broom. He says a roof begins with the broom and ends with the broom, and the rest is nails.

Melon, the sixth. Up the ladder at dawn. Teague on the ridge, me on the rungs below, handing up the nails. The old copper is green and the new is bright, so the tower now has one new eye. Teague says that is the honest way to mend a roof. Do not pretend the new is old. Let the new shine, and let it be seen.

Melon, the seventh. Lantern climbed up with a cloth and polished the new patch, which made it brighter. Then it polished the green as well, which made the green look embarrassed. Teague laughed so hard he had to sit down on the ridge.

Melon, the eighth. Rain again. The roof held. Mira said it was the best the roof has been since she came. Then she said it was not, because a roof is never the best. It is only dry.

Melon, the ninth. Teague was paid fourteen emeralds for the work, and two for the broom. The broom does not need paying, he says, but he took it home to Ambrook anyway.
%%
Ambrook mill pond diary, Melon
Melon, first day. Jory's wheel is turning from dawn to dusk again. You can feel it through the bench on the bank. The water goes over the paddles in a long white curl, and the sluice stands shut at the head of the pond. Nobody may open it. I asked Jory why, and he asked me whether I wanted the mill down. I said no.

Melon, third day. Heat. The pond is low. The reeds on the far bank stand a yard further from the water than they did in Longday. Jory's boys fished the weir with nets and got six fish between them, and one boot. The boot belongs to the council man's cousin. He is not happy, and he is standing on the bridge telling everyone.

Melon, sixth day. Rain for an hour in the afternoon, enough to bring the pond up a hand. Tibby Dunn sat on her stool at the end of the bridge through all of it, and called every cart across. Mind the plank, mind the plank. The plank is still loose. It will not be fixed. Tibby says that is the point.

Melon, ninth day. Jory says the sluice is holding well. I carried a sack of flour up the lane to the healer, because her arm has been bad in the wet. On the way back I rode the wheel's shaft with the boys to see that the teeth were not worn. They are not worn. Jory is pleased, and will not say so.

Melon, twelfth day. The first honey cake of the season went round the bridge and the mill, on a plate that came from Ruby Oke's kitchen. Ruby has forgiven me the beans. Her cake was good, which is more than I can say for mine.

Melon, fifteenth day. One good fish in the weir, long and silver. I let it go, because Jory says a weir must keep one fish, or it is only a wall.

Melon, twentieth day. Storm at night, hard rain. The wheel stopped at midnight, and the whole mill went quiet. In the quiet, the water could be heard going over the top of the sluice gate, very slowly. Jory got up in his nightshirt and stood in the rain by the gate with his hand on the rope. He did not pull it. He only held it. Then he went back to bed.

Melon, last day of the month. The wheel is turning, the pond is up, the plank is loose, and the fish are back in the weir. Everything is where it should be. I am writing it down so I remember that it was.

Letter: Pell to his sister
Dear Sena,
I am writing from the Halfway Inn, by the hearth that has not gone out in twenty years. My feet are up and my boots are drying by the fire, and I have a bowl of soup and a horn cup of small beer, and the world is very good.
I won the honey contest at the fair. Six years of second place, Sena. Six years. I want you to know that I did not gloat. I gloated a little. The beekeepers from Ambrook shook my hand and one of them said my honey had a clean taste, and I have been thinking about that clean taste ever since. It is the clover. I am sure of it.
I paid my debt to the innkeeper today, the whole of it, in emeralds. It was an old debt, from a bad winter when I had no coin and he gave me a bed and supper anyway. He counted the emeralds twice and wrote it off in his book, and then he gave one of them back to me and said it was for the interest. I do not think that is how interest works, but I took it.
I have a new horn cup. I have a new pack strap. My coat is mended and my boots are dry.
I will be home before Leaffall. Tell mother the honey sold well. Tell her I did not gamble it away. I only gambled a little of it, and I won.
Your brother,
Pell
%%
Vesper, trial log: the furnace mind
Trial log kept by Vesper at the glassworks by the Three Wells market, in Sowing. A furnace mind holds the melt steady while the bellows are worked. Nobody taught it the melt. The glassmakers say it is a matter of feel.

Day one. The glassmakers asked for a mind that could hold the melt at one heat. Too hot, and the glass runs. Too cool, and it sets in the pot. A man can do it for an hour, and then his arms give out. A mind does not tire.

The furnace has a gauge: a thin strip of red glass that darkens as the heat rises. I put a small mind on the bellows. It works them, one stroke and then a pause.

Day two. The apprentice pumped too hard, and the melt boiled. It pumped too little, and the melt went stiff. I did not show it the right pumping. I let it pump, and I wrote down what the strip did after each stroke. A stroke that kept the strip a dull red was a good stroke. A stroke that darkened it was a bad one.

Day three. Each good stroke it keeps more of. Each bad stroke it keeps less of. After a hundred strokes it had changed a little. After a thousand, it had found a rhythm: a stroke, a pause, a short stroke, a pause. The glassmakers did not teach it that. The melt taught it, and the score.

The old glassmaker, Mab, watched for an hour and said, "It is pumping the way my father did." I asked her how her father pumped. She said, "Carefully, and then he went home." That is not an answer, but it is a good one.

Day four. The melt is steady for the whole shift. The apprentice has no arms to give out. I have told the mayor it is only a bellows, and he has written that down as a joke, and I have not corrected him.
%%
Abel, a winter of letters
Winter. I am writing this down because I want to remember how it felt, the first time I used the relay for a letter.
My daughter married a man from Saltreach and left. She writes, but a letter by cart takes ten days. This winter the keeper at Lowmarsh said I might try the big mind in the relay house. Not to write to my daughter, he said. To help me write to her.
I had never written a long letter. I told the mind what I wanted to say. It asked me questions. How is the baby. What is the weather. What did she like to eat as a child. I answered, and the letter grew.
In the end I had a page and a half. The mind did not put words in my mouth. It only asked. I said everything, and it helped me put it in order.
There was one line I wanted to put in, and the mind would not help me. I had written that the man from Saltreach was a fool. The mind said it would not write that, because it was unkind and might hurt my daughter. It offered to help me say I was worried. I thought about it for an evening. Then I wrote that I was worried.
My daughter wrote back, ten days later. She said she had been worried too.
There are people who say the big minds are only words. Maybe. A thing that asks the right question and then says no, politely, is more than words to me.
I do not know what it is. The tutors do not know either. They say they know how it was made, and not what it is.
I took the letter to the post cart myself. The horse knew the way. It was cold, and the road shone. At the turn by the Halfway Inn I saw a fox. It looked at the cart and went on its way.
I think it was a good winter.
%%
The Halfway Inn tally board
Kept by the innkeeper. Prices as of this summer.
Bed for the night: one emerald, supper included if you ask kindly.
Supper alone: six coppers. Bread, soup, cheese, and an apple if there are apples.
Small beer: two coppers. Water: free, and the well is out back.
Hay and water for a horse: three coppers.
A bath, hot, in the shed: four coppers. Towels extra.
Boarding for an apprentice: one copper, in the yard shed, with the door left open. Big minds ask through the relay at the crossroads, and the keeper answers for them.
Left to pay:
Pell, the merchant: paid in full, tenth of Swarm, in emeralds, with one emerald returned for interest.
The two carters who argued about wool: paid, and they left a pie.
A traveler from Saltreach: paid in coppers, and left a good knife behind, which is in the lost box by the door.
The lost box: a knife, one boot, a wooden spoon, a letter with no name on it. Claim what is yours. The keeper will not open the letter.

Oren's day-book, the mended roof
Late autumn, at the farm outside Northpoint. A storm took the roof off the barn, and Oren was passing on his way up the road, and stayed to help Tam put it back. He did not help much. He held the ladder and was given the job of counting the slates.

First day. The barn roof is in the yard in three pieces. Tam says they are not broken, only moved. The beams are whole. The wind went straight through the top of the barn and took the hay down the hill, and the hens are sitting in the cart as if they have been asked to.

Second day. The men from the ridge came down with the new slates, cut from the Ambrook quarry, and the oak for the roof-tree. Tam was up the ladder before the sun was on the yard. Pip carried slates. It carried them in the right order, which was a surprise to everyone, because Pip does not usually pay attention to order.

Third day. The roof-tree is up. The rafters are on. Tam says the worst of it is done, and then he says that every day. Oren has counted one hundred and eighty-two slates. Tam says he will need one hundred and ninety, and he has sent Pip down to the shed for eight more.

Fourth day. The rain came. The new roof did not leak. Tam was so pleased he sat in the loft in the wet hay and sang the stone song at the top of his voice. The hens joined in on the chorus. Oren said the hens had no sense of key. Tam said they had a fine sense of key, and that it was the rest of us who were off.

Fifth day. The barn is whole. The hay is back in, most of it. The cat has moved in, and says it always lived here. Oren gave Tam the count, one hundred and ninety-one. Tam said that was one more than he needed, and that the extra slate was for the next storm.

Sixth day. Oren went back down the road. Tam waved from the ladder, and fell off it, lightly, into the straw. He says this is a tradition.
%%
Ida's log, the marsh spring, all of it
Written by Ida, farmer, at the end of the spring, for the book of the hamlet. A season in one record, as near to the facts as a farmer can be.

Thaw. Rain for three weeks. The reed beds drowned and came back up again, greener. The flood came up the lane once, very sudden, and went down again, very slow. We lost no lambs, one hen, and a boat made of doors, which was given back to Brin with honours.

Sowing. Planted the beans along the south wall, where the frogs cannot reach them. The frogs have decided they prefer the water to the beans, which suits everyone.

Blossom. The bees swarmed twice. The first swarm went into the new hive. The second went into the old oak at the marsh edge and was brought down by Brin with a ladder, a sheet, and a great deal of advice from his wife. Honey is coming. Early, but coming.

The mind. Hale left the small crystal with us, in Tibby's slot, for a season of testing. Hoe: nineteen in twenty. Hard sums: fourteen in twenty by the end. Hale says the small mind has less room, so it keeps only what matters most. It keeps the hoe well and the sums fairly, and that is the bargain.

What we do not know. Hale says nobody can say exactly how few marks a mind needs for a given kind of work. The line between enough and too little has to be found by testing, one drill at a time. He says he would like to write the line down, and cannot yet. I asked whether the mind minds the rounding. He laughed and said he did not know, and that it was not the sort of question a farmer should ask a tutor on a step.

Lamps lit at the hamlet gate. Bees home. Mind tested. Water down. A good spring, all round.

Wren's Lowmarsh season log
Winter, 207. The slow water stayed open beside the fish weir. Ice formed in the little pools among the reeds, and the frogs were nowhere to be seen.
The boardwalk torches burned well until the wind turned. Moss went out with spare coal and trimmed the wicks. I watched from the doorway and called out when the lamp by the boat house smoked. Moss trimmed that one too. Then Moss trimmed the lamp by our door, though I had not said a word.
The lamps are brighter now. The marsh is still dark. Both things can be true.
A man came in with a cut across his palm. He had been mending a basket and slipped with his knife. I washed the cut, tied it, and sent him home with clean cloth. He said he could finish the basket with his other hand. I told him to finish it when both hands were well. He said that would be a long wait. I said it might be a quiet one.
Moss has been learning the labels on the high shelves. The jars stand in rows: mint, willow bark, clean cloth, dried leaves, salt. I made a small drill with the names written on cards. Moss set each card beside the right jar, then put the clean cloth card by the empty basket. It was a fair guess. We moved the cloth into the basket and tried again.
At Three Wells, Moss entered a basket of dried marsh herbs in the fair. Moss tied the name card the wrong way round, as last time, but the basket was full and clean. This time it won a blue ribbon and a small wooden bowl. Moss carried both home with great care. The bowl was empty.
I bought a jar of honey from a stall near the east well. The seller said it came from hives beyond Ambrook. I set it on the high shelf and marked the lid for supper. Moss read the mark and put the bowl beneath it.
We had bread, honey and hot tea after the evening round. Moss gave me the larger piece of bread. I asked why. Moss said the healer must keep her strength. I gave Moss the larger share of honey.
After supper we checked the sick beds, filled the kettle, and put fresh sand by the door. The snow had covered the path to the shed again. Moss cleared it before sleep. In the morning the shovel stood inside, dry and clean. This time I said thank you.

Chronicle of the archive: the reading
Kept at Vell. Written in the late years, copied from older notes. For the shelf by the copying room.
A chronicle of how the reading minds came to read pieces of words.
For a long time the reading minds took a page one letter at a time. The window was about sixty letters wide. The mind guessed the next letter, then the next. It read like a person who moves a finger under each letter. Slow, and it could only see the letters just behind the finger.
Then the tutors cut words into common pieces. "ing", "un", "torch", "apprentice". The mind read pieces instead of letters, and the same window held far more. It saw the whole line, and the words on either side, and it guessed better because it had more to look at.
The clerks had to be careful. A page cut one way and a page cut another way are two different pages to a mind. So the archive keeps one list of pieces and every clerk cuts to it. The test box stayed shut, as always.
The tutors argued about who was first. Some say it was a tutor at Three Wells. Some say at Vell. Some say the idea came from a clerk who was tired of copying long words. The texts do not agree, and they never do on the early years. What we know is this: the first day it was tried on the big mind at a relay house, the mind read better before any drilling at all, because the window was fuller. That is in a note from a keeper, and the keeper is not named.
There is a line in the note that the clerks like. "More in the same space, and the meaning closer to hand."
The big minds read this way still. Some write letters a reader cannot tell from a person's, and Orla's question came back with them, as it always does. Whose letters went into the great drills, and how the clerks gathered so much, the texts do not agree.
We will keep everything we write. The old keepers cut it over the door. We still read it every morning when we open the front hall, and the big book by the door, and the light wells, and the lanterns behind glass.
%%
Mira, Northpoint winter journal
Deepsnow, 207.

Deepsnow, second. The snow reached the lower window by dawn. Lantern and I dug a narrow path from the keeper's house to the tower. It carried the shovel. I carried the basket of coal. We made a good pair until I tried to walk ahead. Lantern kept to the path and I stepped into the drift beside it.

The path to the ice camp was gone. Runa says there is no need to go there until the wind stops. She can see our lamp from her window when the snow thins. I sent her a note by the relay. She replied that she has enough firewood and that I should stop sending worried questions.

Deepsnow, fourth. The lamp glass was clear at dawn and white with frost by midday. I wiped it from the inside. The walk round the lamp room had a skin of ice, so I spread grit along the boards. The grit came from an old pail in the tool shed. The pail had a crack. It seems every pail here has a story.

I found a little jar of honey behind the dry oats. It came from Tam in Apple, and I had put it there before the last storm. The lid was tight. I warmed it beside the stove and took one spoonful with bread. Lantern stood by the table. It does not eat, but it watches me eat with great care.

Deepsnow, sixth. The relay line was quiet all morning. I checked the lamp, the redstone line, and the keeper's desk. Nothing was wrong here. After noon the south tower sent a weather note. The snow has eased along the Long Road. I sent that news to the ice camp and asked if the well had filled.

Runa wrote back that it had not. She said the well was dry, and that she was using snow too. She also said she had enough honey for tea. I asked where it came from. She wrote, "Tam sent two jars. One is yours if you stop asking."

Deepsnow, ninth. I went to the camp with Lantern and a spare shovel. The path was passable, though the snow leaned against the posts. Runa had cleared the well mouth and lowered a pail. It came up dry, with a thin ring of mud. We brought clean snow from the bank and filled her kettle. She gave us tea with honey.

The tea was so sweet that I asked if she had put in two spoonfuls. She said no. I believe her. Lantern carried the empty pails back. It did not spill a drop.

Deepsnow, twelfth. The sky cleared after supper. Frost shone on the tower roof and on the old wood fence. Runa stood at the camp door with a wool scarf over her head. She waved once. I waved back from the stair landing.

Lantern cleaned the lamp glass. I set the signal lamp and sent the night report south. Clear. Cold. Snow packed on the north path. Camp well dry. Tea very sweet.
%%
Potter's journal: a poor harvest
Written by Lorn, at Three Wells, in the summer of 205. The wheat came in thin. The beetroot rotted in the wet. By Longday the bakers were glum, and the market had less to sell than the stalls had hoped.

The top field at Kettle Ridge gave half a crop. The carts came down the Long Road half full, and the drivers said nothing about it, which is worse than complaining.

I sold six pots in the whole week. One was to a child who wanted a pot to keep a frog in. I told her frogs do not like pots. She said that was all right, the frog did not like the market either.
%%
Hesk, farm log, Apple
The farm below Copperhold needs a dry path to the barn. Rain has softened the track, and the cart wheels sink. I wrote the work on a board. Clank read it and made cards.

First it sent one helper to bring planks from the shed. Another cleared stones from the track. The third stayed beside the cart. I thought that was wasted time.

Then Clank asked the big mind at the relay house for help. The reply came by the relay: "The cart cannot pass until the stones are moved. Lay planks after the track is clear."

Clank changed the cards. It sent the waiting helper to move stones. When the track was clear, all three carried planks and laid them from the barn door outward.

I asked why it had not started with the planks. Clank set the empty cart on the first plank. The wheel slid off into the mud. It had seen what I had missed.

By evening the cart reached the field. We brought home two loads of wheat. The path held.

The helpers did not speak. They read their cards, did the work, and returned for the next card. Clank kept the board dry under its coat of iron.

Nessa, late spring journal
Spring of 217. The council sent another group to the relay house beyond the west field.
The road there is now wide enough for two carts. They bring ice from the north store and take it into the cool rooms. The house has its own kitchen, tool shed, well, and many beds for keepers. It is larger than the hamlet. From the rise, the roof seems to go on past the trees.
The council has asked tutors to make a list of questions for the mind. We brought sums, old notices, letters, and orders for apprentices. The mind answered by writing on a page. It found a spelling error in my copy of a school lesson. I checked the lesson. The error was mine.
That does not prove it knows everything. It read the lesson because we gave it the lesson. It found the error because the letters were there. A mind with room for many weights can keep more from its drills, and a mind drilled for longer can learn from more pages. Still, we must ask questions it has never seen. We must keep those tests away from its tutors.
At the gate, a young keeper told me the mind had worked out why the mill pond sluice must stay shut. He said it had read Jory's old notes. Jory's notes say the pond feeds the wheel, and the wheel turns the mill. They do not say why the sluice must stay shut. I asked the keeper what answer the mind had given. He could not remember the words.
I have heard three stories about the great mind. One says it can answer any question. One says it has written a letter no clerk can tell from a person's hand. One says it knows the road to places beyond the sea. The keepers disagree about all three.
We know what its builders did. They made many layers, gave each layer its share of a wrong answer, and drilled the mind on more writing than one tutor could read. The council made a house large enough to hold it and keep it cool. Beyond that, I will write only what I have seen.
On the way home, we stopped at the inn. The keeper there had a new notice to copy. I copied it by hand. The mind at the great house did not see it. Not every page belongs to every drill.
%%
Nessa's spring road book, year 206
Written by Nessa, tutor at Vell, over one spring on the Long Road: the Halfway Inn, the Brown Hen, the little hill and the crossroads at Three Wells. The book is kept for the tutor house, and copied for the keepers.

Thaw, first week. The keeper of the little hill, Dell, asked me to test his lamp mind, which is the smallest mind on the road. It drills well on lamp orders: light, dim, cover, clean. It drills badly on any order with a name in it. Dell says that is no fault of the mind. I said names are the hardest thing a mind ever learns, and he laughed.

Thaw, fourth week. The first mind I drilled on the even step went out to the Brown Hen fields and did well. Its numbers stayed even after the fifth layer. I was pleased with it, which is a feeling tutors are not supposed to have, and I wrote it down so no one would think I had been too pleased.

Sowing, the first day. Rain. The inn yard flooded, and the drill box floated into the horse trough. The inn's stable boy drilled the mind again in the stable, with straw under its feet, and it did better than it had in the yard.

Sowing, the fourth. The innkeeper's wife asked why her cook's sums were wrong. I said a mind is only as good as the check on its numbers, and the even step is the check on the layers. She said that was the same as a kitchen, and she was right. You taste the soup before it goes to the table.

Blossom, the second. A cart came from Copperhold with news of the new vein. Copper, clean, past the sixth level. The miners cheered at the count board. Hesk said the carts would be two days late every week, and they were.

Blossom, the ninth. Trouble with the relay on the hill. The stair lamps are dark on the top half. The keeper has written to Vell for glass. I drilled his apprentice on the lamp orders while we waited, so that at least one mind on the hill knew what to do with the dark.

Blossom, the twentieth. At Three Wells the children have painted the school door blue again, and the bell has been mended. The mayor asked me to test the school bell's mind, a small mind that rings the bell on order. It rang on come and stay, and it missed home entirely. We fixed it, and the bell has been forgiven.

Swarm, the first. The bees at the Ambrook orchard swarmed, and the Ambrook keeper sent word that the swarm had gone up the hill to the relay. The hill is not a hive. Nobody has yet explained what the bees wanted there.

Swarm, the twelfth. The last day of the road. I wrote the even step on the back of the drill box, in one line: find the middle of the numbers, find how far they spread, and scale them to one size. Why the crystal learns, I have never been told. I write that here too, for the next tutor, so she knows the question is still open.
%%
Corin's yard book, winter 207
Frost to Deepsnow. The yard at Saltreach. Kept in the shed by the stove, in a hand that gets worse as the ink gets colder.
Frost, the 2nd. The keel came. One log of oak from the forest past Ambrook, on a cart with four horses. It took three days. The drovers say the horses took it personally.
I put my hand on the log and said thank you. I do this with every keel. Kit asked why. I said wood is patient and likes to be told. Kit put his hand on the log and said thank you. The log did not answer. It rarely does.
Frost, the 9th. Kit asked how I know which way a plank wants to bend. I said you look at the grain until it tells you. When it does not tell you, you ask Marit, who has mended nets for sixty years and knows everything about everything that bends. Kit wrote that down. He writes everything down now. I do not know who gave him the idea.
Frost, the 17th. The first ribs went up. Spruce, bent by steam, one every hand's width. There is a pattern to ribs, and the pattern is the boat. Young Ned set the first. His rib leaned. I said, a little left. He pushed. Now it stands. Every rib after it was easy.
Longnight, the 11th. Snow for two days. Nothing to do but mend. The shed smelled of tar and wet wool. The cat Plank slept in a coil of rope for two days and woke up cross.
Longnight, the 30th. The longest night. The harbor lamp was lit at noon and burned all day, and at dusk the whole pier was lit, a torch on every post. The families came down with lanterns. Kell's crew sang the rowing song. Marit sang the net song, which is nicer. The relay lamp over the harbor sent its greeting down the line: "Good midwinter. The days get longer now."
I wrote it to Ossie that night. Ossie writes back by the Lowmarsh cart that the frogs have stopped singing for winter, and he cannot sleep for the quiet.
Deepsnow, the 8th. Ice on the pier, white in the morning. The planks go on, one row a day. A boat is not made quickly. A boat is made one plank at a time, and a plank takes a day.
Deepsnow, the 21st. The last plank on the right side. I checked every seam with a lantern. I do this at night, with the lantern held close, because a seam that lets in light will let in water. There were four. We fixed them.
Deepsnow, the 29th. The hull is closed. Tomorrow, the first coat of tar. Spring is two weeks away. In spring she goes down the slip.
Kit asked what her name will be. I said it is not mine to say. It belongs to the family who paid. Kit said that is a pity, he had a good one. I said, tell me. He said: Plank. For the cat.
%%
Corin, the long summer day
Summer of 207. The longest day of the year, when the sun hardly sets. A fine day, with no wind, and the harbor flat as a plate.

Dawn. I woke before the gulls. The sea was silver and the quay was empty. I walked down to the slip and sat on the ramp with my feet in the water. It was warm.
Morning. The shipwrights arrived one by one with bread and cold fish. We worked on the new hull, a boat for the fish market that can carry sixty baskets. I have never built one so big. I have also never built one so slow. The planks are long and the pegs are many.
Noon. The tar pot was full. The apprentice carried it across the yard, and spilled it, and carried it back, and spilled it again. I sent it to wash. It came back with its hands clean and carried the pot again. Not a drop.
Afternoon. A letter from the harbor master. The prize for the best boat of the year goes to the Heron again. Her builder has won it eleven times. Her builder is me. I am not sure whether to be glad or to be sad. If I win it every year, nobody else tries.
Evening. I walked up to the barn to fetch more oak. There was a web across the door. A small spider sat in the middle, calm as a clerk. I said, "Good evening." It said nothing. I took the oak from the side door.
Night. The sun finally went down, red as a pot. The harbor lamps came on one by one. Out on the water, a fishing boat was coming home with a lantern at the bow. I watched it all the way in.
It is a fine thing, to build a boat. It is a finer thing to watch one come home.
%%
Jem, first spring log
Spring of 2. Wind from the south, warm for once.
New at the ridge. Came up from the mill pond with my net bag and my needles. Took a room over the barn. Tam says the room was for the hay, and the hay can go in the yard.
The nets here are for the pond, not the sea. Small mesh, small fish, a lot of weed. It will be a change.
Met Tam. He talks, I listen. That will work.

Quill, egg tally in Longday
Longday, 188. Farm outside Lowmarsh.

The hens laid nine eggs. I carried six to market and kept three for supper.
Moss brought a basket of reeds from the marsh. She says the frogs have gone quiet near the south ditch. I heard them all night.

At the school table, Nessa showed us a reading mind in a small crystal. It had been drilled on pages from the Archive at Vell. The mind read a page, one letter at a time, then guessed the next letter. It guessed "hen" after "the fat". That made us laugh. Nessa said it would learn from more pages.

I asked if it had read my egg tally. Nessa said the clerks had many pages, but she did not know which ones. I hope it did not read the price I wrote wrong.

Ossa's book of the Archive's lamps
Kept by Ossa, candle maker, for the clerks of the Archive, who buy the candles for the copying room and the front hall. This is the book of what she sent, and the notes on what they said back. The clerks are very fussy. They are right to be.

Sent: sixty tapers, pale, for the copying room. Each taper of the same length, and the same colour, so the clerks can tell at a glance when one is burning down.
Sent back: twelve. The clerks say the wicks are too thick. They are right. Ossa has thinned them.
Sent: forty lanterns' candles, thick, for the stairs. They must not drip. Oren says a drip on a shelf is a stain on the archive, and a stain is forever.
Sent back: none. Oren sent a note instead: "Thank you. These are very good. Please do not send any more candles past the front hall. We are not allowed."
Sent: a small candle for the desk of the old keeper, who has been reading the same copy of a letter from the north for a whole winter. He says the letter has a good ending, though he has not reached it yet. Ossa sent two, in case he reads it twice.
Notes from the clerks, all year. Keep the lamps covered when the shelves are dusted. A wax drip on a copy is a drip too many. The Archive does not light candles in the deep halls, because the air there is still, and a flame wants moving air to be safe. Oren says candles are fine for the front hall, and for the copying room, where the window is wide. Ossa says she is glad to know where her light goes.
Sold to the Archive this year: three hundred and ten. Sold to the rest of Vell: four hundred and twelve. Ada's boy says that is more than the bakers. The bakers say it is more than the clerks.
%%
Fallon, spring notes from the hamlet
Spring of 208, Thaw to Swarm.
The first rain filled the road ditch. It held until the turn by the oak hedge, where the bank had slipped. I cleared the mud and put two stones in the gap. A cart came through before I had washed my hands. The driver said the road was good. I said he should come back after the next rain. He said he would, but he did not.

The old map was taken to the Archive at Vell for copying. Fen went with it and brought back a paper showing the same lines in a clerk's neat hand. The copy has our spring, our wall, the old path, and the mark for good stone. It also has the square marked "new store". The clerk left the square in place. I like that. A map should show what someone hoped to build, as well as what they built.

We have had poor harvests before, but the small grain bin still looks worse than it did last spring. Last year's wheat was thin. Beetroot was scarce, and we kept the best potatoes for seed. Tavin says we should plant more carrots. My sister says he should plant them himself. He has not answered yet.

A child from the hamlet helped me sort stones for the well curb. The child chose the round ones and called them eggs. I told the child stones do not hatch. The child said that was because we did not keep them warm. I put the round stones aside. They fit the curb better than the square ones.

The apprentice hauled blocks to the wall and brought water to the stone yard. It learned the way from the shed to the well. When Rell put the tool basket in the path, it stopped. Rell moved the basket. The apprentice went on. Rell says this is a fine way to make a path clear. I say a path is easier to clear when no one fills it with tools.

At the end of Swarm, we planted the last row. The bees worked among the flowers beside the field. The well curb stands firm. The road stones stayed in place through two rains. Tomorrow I will cut a step for Fen's new door. She says it must be smooth. I will make it smooth, and she will say it is too smooth.
%%
Fen's journal, a net and a road
Late Sowing. The Brown Hen, on the road home.

A road is a net. I have thought this for years, and tonight I have an inn and a fire to think it by.

A net is made of knots and the space between. A road is made of inns and the space between. Each inn is a knot. Each waymark is a small one. If one is missing, the whole thing sags.

I saw Dunn today, the waymark keeper, at the bridge below the Brown Hen. He had a chisel and a ladder and the look of a man who is about to say a number. He was recutting a 6 that had worn to a 5. He said: Fen, this is the whole of my work. I make sure that when a stranger sees a 6, it is a 6. I said: I do the same with holes. We shook hands. His hands were rough. So were mine.

I found a lost boy at the second waymark. He was about nine, and he was sitting on the cobblestone edge eating a heel of bread. He had followed a cart and the cart had gone. I did what the Rules say. I took him to the next inn. We walked slowly, and I told him about knots. He said knots were dull. I showed him the over-and-under. He learned it before the next waymark. He tied one in the lace of his boot, and I said that was a good start.

Marda gave him soup. His father came at dusk with a torch and a face I will not forget. I sat at the back and mended a net.

I am sixty this year. My hands ache in the cold. Marit says I should stop, and she is right. But the nets still tear. Someone has to be at the other end of the road when they do.

Dace's flood book, Sallow Ford
Written by Dace, mayor of Three Wells, who came down to Sallow Ford when the water rose. Kept for the Archive.
Reaping, the 12th day. Rain for three nights. The river came up over the ford stones in the dark and took the old fence down by the mill. Nobody drowned. Two sheep and a bucket went down the river. The bucket was found the next morning in the square at Three Wells, sitting by the well as if it had come to market.
Reaping, the 13th day. The mill wheel has stopped. The water is still high. We moved the flour up to the loft. The ferry runs on the rope now, slowly.
The ferry mind is in the small crystal. It still takes the rope the right way. I stood on the landing and watched it bring a cart of hay across the flood. It stopped halfway, a moment too long, and then went on. The hay came over dry.
Reaping, the 14th day. The water is going down. The tutor says the stop in the middle is the cost of the small crystal. Only I minded, and I had been wet since dawn.
Reaping, the 15th day. Written at the ferry house by the stove. Nobody is missing. The wheel is turning. The road to Three Wells is open. Good.
%%
Corin, the fair at Saltreach
Summer of 201. The fair at the harbor wall. A good wind. No rain. Hot.

Day one, the fair opens. The whole town is on the quay. Stalls of fish, nets, rope, tar, wool, salt. A man selling hats made of seaweed. Nobody buys one.
Day two. Boat race in the harbor. Six boats. The Heron came first, as always. The Gull came second. The red boat, ours, came third and was very proud. It was not last.
Day two, afternoon. The prize for the best model ship. I entered one, made of birch, with a real sail and a tiny ladder. The judge looked at it a long time and said it was very good. Then he gave the prize to a boy of eight who had made a boat from a boot. It floated. Mine did too, but it had no boot.
Day three. The net-mending contest. Marit won, with a net so fine you could see through it. She has won eleven times. She did not even smile.
Day three, evening. The prize for the biggest fish. A man from Lowmarsh brought a pike as long as a child. The harbor master said no, that is a pike, and we are at the sea. The man said it was a very long cod. They argued until midnight.
The prize went to the pike. Nobody could think of a rule against it.
Day four. The fair ends. Everyone is tired. I have a ribbon, a bruise and a bag of salt.
Next year I am entering a boot.
%%
Quill, flock log: the shearing, Swarm
Kept by Quill, shepherd, at Ferris End, through the shearing. Nine days of wool, and one of them with a sheep who did not want to be sheared and said so.

Swarm, first day. Sheep caught in the pen by the gate. Sixty-one counted in, sixty-one counted out, with fourteen lambs trailing behind like guests. Fen herds them in with his nose.

Swarm, second day. Shears set. The shearer from Lowmarsh has hands like a healer and a grip like a carter, and says both are useful. He took the first fleece in one piece, the whole coat rolling off onto the boards like a loaf coming out of the tin.

Swarm, third day. Muffin objected. She ran round the pen three times, and then stood in the corner with her eyes shut, daring us. We sheared her in the corner. She said nothing, which was worse.

Swarm, fourth day. Honey, the lamb, is nearly a sheep now. She was left out of the shearing because she is still too thin, and she does not forgive me for it. She stands by the wall with a look of great injury, and I told her she looked well. She has not forgiven me.

Swarm, fifth day. Wool counted: forty-two bundles, tied with string. Wool price at the Three Wells market is six emeralds the bundle, if you can carry it, and seven if you can carry it while talking about the price. I will carry it. I will not talk.

Swarm, sixth day. Juno came up for the wool and bought six bundles for the cheese cloths. She says the wool keeps the cheese dry in the cellar, and the cheese keeps Juno dry in the rain, and that is a fair trade.

Swarm, seventh day. The hooves were clipped. Each foot took a moment, and each sheep objected in its own way. One of the lambs bit me. I bit it back, gently, on the ear, and it stopped. Fen watched with great interest and then went to sleep, having decided it was not his business.

Swarm, eighth day. A traveler from the Long Road stopped at the gate, asking the way to Vell. I told him. He asked whether the sheep were for sale. I said no, not the one with the black face. He looked at Muffin for a long time, and then said, "She looks like she knows the road." I said she knows every road in Ferris End, and that is enough for anybody.

Swarm, ninth day. The last fleece is off. The flock is pale, and cold, and full of opinions. Counted: sixty-one, and fourteen lambs. Honey is in the top field, out of the wind, eating grass as if she had never been looked at by a shearer in her life.

Swarm, ninth day, evening. Wren came up the lane with the jar of honey, the same jar, the cloth tied over it. There was about a spoon left. She said it was for the one that needed it. I said the one that needed it was walking about the field with the flock, and was fine. She put the jar on the wall anyway, and said it would be there when I did need it. I believe her. She is a healer.
%%
Mira, letter from Ambrook
Sowing, 3. To Tam at Kettle Ridge.
I have been away from Northpoint for four nights. Do not worry. The keeper at Vell sent me south to help mark the relay line. I have seen more mud than snow, which is a change.
At Ambrook the market is held by the mill pond. The wheel turns from dawn to dusk, and the sluice stays shut. I asked Jory why it stays shut. He said the answer is in the town rule. Then he went to mend a bucket. I did not find the rule.
The road from Three Wells is better than the road from Vell. The crews have put stone at the steep places and earth between them. They set a torch post every twelve blocks, though I did not count each one. Pell counted. Pell counts everything that can be sold.
The tower site is on the hill above the pond. We carried tools there by hand. The old oak door from a small storehouse will be used for the relay house. It is heavy, and the hinges have not yet been found.
There was a wedding here last week. The bride and groom crossed the new bridge with the cake. The cake arrived whole. This is considered a good sign.
I return north when the builders have raised the first wall. Please send me apples next time. The north has none that taste as kind.

Elm, ferry log, the first frost
Kept at the ferry on the Long Road, where the river bends below the Brown Hen. Written by Elm, who keeps the ferry, the rope and this book, and who is certain that only one of the three is hard work.

First day of Frost. Wind from the north, sharp as a knife. Frost on the rope in the morning, so the rope is stiff as a bar. Crossings: six. Carts, two. Drovers, three. One man with a pig under his arm, who did not say why, and did not need to.

Second day. Still cold. Ice at the edges of the landing, as thin as paper. I put the boat's bow into the reeds, to keep it from freezing to the bank. Crossings: four. Fares: twenty-two emeralds in all, and the pig paid for half.

Third day. The first frost has come to stay. The river is grey and slow. The ferry rope is greased and hung on its pegs, and I am sitting by the stove with my boots off, the same as every winter.

Elm

Fen's journal, the mill track
Sowing, the ninth day. The Long Road, between Three Wells and the Halfway Inn.

The Long Road is shut. They are laying new stone, two carts wide, with a torch every twelve blocks. Carts go round by the old mill track, and so do I. It is mud on both sides. In the middle there is a different kind of mud.

At the top of the track I stopped to look. Two apprentices from Vell were hauling stone for the new road. They are made of copper and iron. They followed a pattern and laid each block where the one before had been. They did not stop. The masons stood by with their arms folded and looked pleased.

A child asked me if the apprentices get tired. I said I did not know. She said they looked tired. I said they looked the way they always look. She said that was what she meant.

By dusk I reached the Halfway Inn. The hearth was lit. The soup was on. I had two bowls, and the second was free. I had a third and paid.
