Dell, the curve on the board
Autumn of 196.

The fifth of Apple. Pell's cart brought a parcel from my cousin at Vell: a copy of a note by Isla, the tutor. Eleven pages. I read it twice and understood about half. This is the half I understood.

Isla grew minds of many sizes. The smallest had a crystal the size of a bean. The biggest she could afford was the size of a plum. She drilled every one on the same writing for the same number of days. Then she tested each on pages it had never seen, and counted how many of its guesses were wrong in every hundred.

Then she took a board and made a dot for each mind. Along the bottom, the size of the crystal. Up the side, the wrong guesses.

The dots did not scatter. They lay along a smooth curve, going down. Down fast at first, then slower and slower, but always down.

She did it again with one crystal and more and more writing. Another smooth curve. She did it again with one crystal and more and more days of drilling. Another.

Here is the part that made me put the note down. She drew each curve onward, past her biggest mind, into the empty part of the board. She wrote: a crystal this big will make this many wrong guesses. Nobody had grown one that big. The council paid, and a mind was grown. It made almost exactly that many.

The ninth. I put it to the class. I drew dots on the board as Isla did. Size one: forty wrong in a hundred. Size two: thirty-two. Size four: twenty-six. Size eight: twenty. I asked what size sixteen would make. Nobody knew. Then I showed them that each time the size doubles, the wrong guesses shrink by about a fifth. A fifth of twenty is four. Sixteen.

Runa put up her hand. "Why does it go slower?"

"The easy mistakes go first," I said. "The hard ones are left."

She said that is how it is with spelling. She is right.

The baker's boy asked: "What if the crystal is big and the writing is small?" Then it learns the few pages by heart and fails on new ones. "And if the writing is big and the crystal small?" Then there is more than the crystal can hold. So the crystal, the writing and the drilling must grow together. I wrote that on the board and left it up.

The thirteenth. I asked the class if the curve is a promise. Nobody was sure. I said it is a good guess, drawn from what has been seen, and it has held so far. Mira says the same about the weather.
%%
Emmet, the broken relay lamp
Blossom, the sixteenth. A storm came in off the marsh last night. The relay lamp on the hill took a blow of wind, and the glass on the north side cracked. The lamp blinked crooked for an hour, then went dark.

Wren's boy ran up the hill with a lantern. The keeper came down at dawn, wet to the knees, and said it would be three days before the lamp could send again. The messages from Vell have backed up on the line. Thirty-one of them, he said. He had counted.

The copper ring round the lamp room is bent. A new pane has to come from Saltreach by cart along the Long Road, and a man who likes to talk must fit it. We sent a boy with the cart. He will be back before Melon, if the road does not argue.

The fishers are glad of the quiet at night. They say the lamp always blinks at the wrong hour, and wakes the frogs. I told them the frogs are never asleep. They said I was welcome to think so.

Blossom, the nineteenth. Lamp lit again. The keeper says the new pane is in, and the copper ring is straight. The first message through was from Vell, and it said only: Received. Thirty-one sent again, all in order.

Dace, market journal, the cave
Sowing, the second day. The plough went into Brin's field at the foot of the hill and stopped with a thump. Under the turf there is a hollow, and under the hollow a room, walled with old stone. The corners are square.

Sowing, the third day. The room has a stair going down, nine steps, and a tunnel going on past it. We have put a rope on the top step and a bell on the rope, and the children are not to go past the bell. Lark says she will write a notice. Lark always writes a notice.

Sowing, the fifth day. Hesk came up from Copperhold with a lamp and a rope and said this is a mine, not a cave, and that mines are dug, not found under fields. He has been asked to explain. He has not.

Sowing, the seventh day. The Archive at Vell has sent a clerk with a drawing board. He says the walls match a drawing in the hill, of a place that was never built, or was built and then forgotten. He would not say more. Brin has asked for his field back, and will plant beans on the top.
%%
Dell, the class makes a curve
Autumn of 196.

The twenty-second of Apple. After the lesson on Isla's curve, Runa asked if we could make our own. I said we have no crystals. She said we have children. I could not argue.

So here is the plan. Every child copies pages from the school primer. At the end of the week, each is tested on fifty words they have never copied, and we count the wrong ones. Four groups. Group one copies five pages. Group two, ten. Group three, twenty. Group four, forty. A fifth group is Runa, and she says she will copy as many as she is allowed.

The twenty-ninth. The tests. Group one: thirty wrong in fifty. Group two: twenty-four. Group three: nineteen. Group four: fifteen. I drew the dots. They lay on a curve, going down, slower and slower, exactly as Isla's did. The children were very pleased with themselves. Some of them went and looked at the board from the back of the room.

But something else happened. Four children in group four copied the same page forty times, because it was the shortest. They could spell every word on that page. On the new words they were as bad as group one. The writing must be new, I told them. Forty pages of the same page is one page.

I asked Runa, "How many wrong at eighty pages?" She had already worked it out on her slate. Each doubling takes off a fifth. "Twelve," she said.

The thirtieth of Apple to the sixth of Leaffall. Runa copied eighty pages. Her hand hurt for two days. Her cat sat on the primer. On the last day I tested her on fifty new words. Twelve wrong.

The whole class stood up and clapped. Runa bowed, very slowly, as if she were a mind that had just been grown.

Then the baker's boy asked what would happen at one hundred and sixty pages. Ten, said Runa. And at three hundred and twenty? Eight. And when do we stop? "When the primer runs out," said the baker's boy. I wrote that down too. The curve never tells you when to stop. That is a question for emeralds and wrists.

The ninth of Leaffall. I told Mira the story at the tower. She said, "So the dot landed on the line." I said yes. She said, "That is how I know the council is right. Not because the tutors are wise. Because their line holds." She went back to her lamps.

I wrote on the board for tomorrow: A guess that holds every time is worth a great deal. And a guess is still a guess.

Tova, road log, Deepsnow, the test
Deepsnow of 159. The first thaw. Water in the ditches, and the road is open at last.

The mind passed its test this week. Abel brought out the test box, twenty letters the mind had never been shown. It got nineteen right. The twentieth was a crooked three. Ivo says it was the mind's fault. Abel says it was the crooked three's fault. They have not agreed since.

Abel wrote the score in his book. He wrote fit for the marsh and underlined it twice. Then he wrote not fit for the road, because the road is not the marsh, and underlined that twice too.

Ivo has taken the stone out of the box at last. He says the mind is warm enough now. The box is warm to my hand, so I think he is right.

The mind is to ride inside a helper made of copper, a small one with two arms and a basket, that carries the sacks from the door to the tower. It will be the first helper Lowmarsh has had. Wren at the healing house says she will not have one on her shelves, because it will drop the jars. Abel says she will change her mind when it carries her honey.

Last night the frogs began again in the reeds. Ivo counted nineteen of them before he fell asleep. Greer has sown the wet field again, with beetroot, because beetroot forgives anything. He told the cow so.

I asked Abel once more why the mind learns at all. He laughed. He said nobody has written down why a crystal learns, and he will not be the first to do it. If he got it wrong, he would have to pass the blame back to himself. He laughed about that for a long time.

The road opens tomorrow. I will take the carts on to Three Wells. I will look back at the marsh once, to hear the frogs.

Lark, the melon too big
Frost, the eleventh. The melon that grew in the south garden this summer is still in the cellar. It was planted in Melon, when nobody thought it would come to anything, and it grew all through Reaping like a second sun. It is the size of a cradle. The cellar steps had to be taken up to get it down.

Frost, the twelfth. The children have been visiting it every morning. They have measured it with a rope, and found it is four ropes round, and they have written the number on the cellar wall in chalk. The number is wrong by a rope. They have argued about it all week, and the argument is the best thing that has happened in the hamlet since the summer.

Frost, the fourteenth. Tried to cut a slice. The knife went in two fingers and stopped. The melon did not split. It was thick as a barrel and as stubborn. Brin says it is a melon for the spring fair, and that we will need a cart and two strong men to carry it there. Nobody has volunteered the men.

Frost, the eighteenth. The melon has begun to sweat in the cold, which the children say means it is thinking. It is a very large melon for a very small hamlet, and we are all a little afraid of it.

Frost, the twentieth. The keeper of the tower sent a message down the line: he would like a slice, if one can be cut. I replied that a slice cannot be cut, only admired. He said he would admire it from the top of the tower, at a safe distance, and send us his thanks.

Toby, baker's journal, a birth
Three Wells market, spring of 206. Up at the third hour as always.
Bread: eighty loaves. Rolls: two hundred. The oven ran hot.
At noon, Ness from the cheese stall had her baby, a girl, in the back of my shop, among the flour sacks. The midwife came running. The girl has flour on her nose. I say she is a baker by birth.
I gave Ness a loaf. She said it was the best she ever ate. She was probably tired.
%%
Apple harvest, Ambrook
Pell's brother's farm, three fields up the hill from the mill pond. Hot and still all through Apple month.
The apples came in early this year. We picked the four hundred trees in eleven days, which is fast for us. The oldest tree with the grey branch gave nine baskets on its own. Nobody eats from the grey branch. It is bad luck and also the apples are small and sour.
The bees were cross all summer. The hives on the hill hummed at night, which they do not often do. My brother says the heat. His wife says the honey tastes of smoke and will not sell it at the fair. I tasted it. It is honey. It has a taste. Folk will buy it.
A sick cow. The brown cow went off her food on the fourth day of picking and lay down in the shade of the hedge and would not get up. We gave her water and cool grass and she looked at us. The healer from Lowmarsh was passing on the road so we asked her and she looked at the cow a long time and said she did not know. That is what she said. She did not know. She left a jar of something green and said to keep the flies off. The cow is up now. She is thin but she is up.
I went to Saltreach to see the big ships. There are three of them in the slip, each three times as long as any boat I ever saw. Corin says they are for the fishing. Nobody fishes three days out. I asked a man on the quay where they go and he said across the water, and I said what is across the water, and he said he did not know, and he did not look like a man who was sorry not to know.
There are folk at the fair saying the lights over the north are fires. There are folk saying falling stars. A boy from Kettle Ridge says they were looking at us and his mother told him to hush. I do not look north much. There is nothing north but snow and my cousin's tower.
Sold honey at Three Wells. Sold the smoke honey too, to a baker, who said it would do for cakes.
%%
Corin, house log, Frost
Frost. First day.
The house at the edge of Saltreach leaks at the back door. I put a strip of wool under it. The wind pulled the strip out before supper.
The slip is quiet today. I heard a hammer at the shipyard, then none for an hour. The big ship stands above the long ramp. Its side is higher than our roof.
Nell brought fish stew. She says her boy will not eat the white fish. He ate the potatoes and asked for more bread.
I mended a net by the window. The thread kept catching on my nail. I am too old for fine knots, but the net is not ready to mend itself.

Letter: Oren to a friend
Late summer, 200.
Dear friend,
You have heard me complain before, so I will complain once more and then stop.
They are writing minds small now. Fewer marks for each number. The crystal fits in a pocket and the mind is nearly as good. Nearly. It is the nearly that keeps me up.
A big mind reads a page and gets every letter right. A small mind reads the same page and gets most of them right, and one wrong, and you cannot tell which one until it matters. The tutors say the cost is small. It is small if the mind is fetching bread. It is not small if the mind is reading a letter for a relay and the letter says come at dawn and the mind reads come at dark.
I keep the big minds big for the relays. I keep the small minds for the fields and the carts and the hen house doors. That is the rule I have written for the clerks.
What worries me is not the rule. What worries me is that everyone will want the small one, because it is cheap, and after a while nobody will remember why the big one was worth the cost. I have written that down too, in the front hall, in the big book by the door, so that the people after me can read it and argue with me. That is what the archive is for.
We will keep everything we write.
Oren, clerk at Vell.
%%
Archive day-book
Deepsnow of 204.
Filed: 8. Copied: 12. Back on the shelves: 55. Lost: none.

A hard winter. Snow lay a block deep on the hill. The light wells, the glass shafts that bring daylight down, were covered. For three days the halls were dark but for the lanterns. I did not mind. I find the halls very peaceful by lantern.

On the second day, I walked down to the clerks' house to fetch my other coat, and found that the new roof had a leak. A small one, in the corner above my bed. Tarn the roofer had mended it, and the drip had come back.

I said a word I will not write.

I wrote to the council, asking for a second visit. I wrote it very politely. I wrote it three times, because the first two had ink spots.

Tarn came on the fourth day, with his ladders and his hammer. He looked at the roof for a long time. He said, "The slate is good. The nails are good. I think the roof is a roof, and the leak is a snowflake that got in a gap." I said a snowflake does not cause a leak. He said a million of them do.

He put a thin strip of copper along the gap. It was the cleanest repair I have ever seen. He would not take payment. He said I had kept his grandfather's will in the archive, and it was the least he could do.

Evening. I went down to the deepest room, to sit with the oak box that holds the oldest record. I do not open it. I only sit. It is a thing I do on cold nights. The room smells of dry wood and stone. I told the box about the roof.

It did not answer, of course. But it is a good listener.
%%
Kester, road and forge journal
Blossom and Swarm, year 26.
First day of Blossom. The frost is gone from the ditch. I opened the forge and found a mouse nest in the coal bin. The mouse had made a warm bed from old straw. I moved the coal to a dry shelf and left the nest alone.
Third day of Blossom. A stone cart came from Three Wells. The driver had two sacks of flour and a cracked spoke. I fixed the spoke with an iron band. He left one sack at the inn for the road crew. No one asked him to.
Sixth day of Blossom. We set stones along the hollow part of the new road. The track is higher there now. Water still gathers by the reeds, but the carts pass without sinking. We have marked the next stretch toward the Long Road with oak stakes.
Moss found a frog inside my boot. He said it had chosen a poor house. I tipped it into the ditch and put my boot by the fire.
Ninth day of Blossom. Wren came from Lowmarsh with a list of work. Her shed door had dropped. A shelf had pulled from the wall. One bed frame was loose. I mended the door and bed, then made iron hooks for the jars. She paid with honey and dried fish.
Fourteenth day of Blossom. The innkeeper says travelers are coming more often. Two carts stopped here on the same evening. One carried wool from the hills. The other carried clay and a little glass from Saltreach. The drivers shared a table, though one wanted the bench by the fire for his wet socks.
Nineteenth day of Blossom. We put a waymark at the road fork. It points to the inn and to Lowmarsh. We have not settled the count to Three Wells. The first mark says three, but the road keeper says it is four. We will count again when the ground dries.
Fourth day of Swarm. Bees are out by the reed flowers. The fields have green shoots. Mara's beetroot is taller than last year, and the potatoes have not drowned. We have mended the ditch twice. It carries the spring rain past the low field.
The harvest of year 25 was poor. The wheat heads were few, and the beetroot did not grow deep. Folk ate more fish and saved seed where they could. I sold no iron for emeralds that season. I traded for food, and the inn kept its pot full.
Now the crop looks fair. I do not call it good yet. We have all seen a green field give little grain. Still, Moss says the wheat is tall enough to shade the weeds. He has begun to learn which is which.
The road crew has asked for a small relay post on the rise. A keeper could see the road from there and pass a signal toward Three Wells. It would need a stone base, a lamp and a dry room for the keeper. We have no tower here, only a tall oak and an old shed.
I will make a hinge for the shed door. The stone can come by cart after the road is firm. The lamp can wait until we know who will keep it. There is always more work than iron in a small hamlet.
At supper we had bread, fish and carrots. The innkeeper said the new road had made the meal taste better. It may be true. It is easier to be cheerful when the cart arrives before dark.
%%
Mira, ice camp log
Longnight, 168.

The well by the fish shed ran dry. There was ice at the bottom, but no water under it. We carried snow from the drift and melted it in the cook pot.

Runa says the well has done this before. She says it will fill again when the ground softens. I hope she is right. She is older than the well, or says she is.

I walked the path to the tower with two pails. One pail had a crack. I found out which one when I reached the door.

The fox watched me spill half my water. It looked pleased.

Tam sent a jar of honey from Kettle Ridge. I have put it on the shelf above the stove. It is for tea, not for foxes.

Dace, the mayor's book, a long autumn
Notices and sums, kept for the council.

The fence at the cave is still up. The torch on its post has been relit twice, once by Orrin and once by the watch. Both times it went out by the next morning, and no one can say why. The wind comes up the field from the east, but that is all we have.

The carters from the north road are not coming through as often. Those who come say the snow has not fallen on the ridge, but the nights are very long there and very dark, and the lamps on the high towers are slow to light. A messenger from the south line said the keeper at Ambrook had a message from the far north, and that the message said only: keep the fields until we come back. Nobody knows who sent it. The keeper at Ambrook says it was a fault in the line. The messenger says it was not a fault, it was clear.

The Archive has sent a clerk to look at the cave. The clerk went down with two lanterns and came up with a page of notes and a cold face. He would not say what he had seen. He said the Archive would decide. Oren's seal is on the page, and the page is in the mayor's box.

Accounts for the autumn. Lanterns, eleven. Wicks, forty. Bread for the watch, nine loaves a day. Bread for the school, five a day, and one extra for Bel, who says she has eaten all of the first lot already.

Note for the council: the steps are not to be walked on. Nobody is to be paid for going down. Nobody is to be blamed if they do.

Fen's page in the well book, the fox
Tenth of Swarm.
A fox came at dawn. It came along the hedge and stopped at the hen house and looked at the hens.
The hens looked back. The hens did not move. The fox put one paw on the fence. The fox was very thin.
Da came out with the shovel. He did not hit the fox. He threw the shovel at the fence and shouted. The fox ran. It ran fast and low and it did not look back.
Da says the fox will come back. Da says a fox always comes back. So we are to put a proper fence round the hen house, with a gate, and a torch on the post. Da says the torch is not for the fox. The fox does not care about the torch. The torch is for us, so we can see the fox.
Fifteenth of Swarm.
The fence is up. The gate is up. The torch is on the post. I lit it myself. The hens are pleased. The hens do not know they are pleased, but I know.
The fox came back last night. It sat outside the fence and looked at the hens through the gate. It did not try the fence. It just sat. Then it went away.
I do not think the fox is wicked. I think the fox is hungry. Da says that is the same thing to a hen.
Twentieth of Swarm.
The new family's apprentice is here. It is made of copper and iron and it carries the water from the well to the house. It does not talk. It walks and it carries and it comes back.
Da says it is a good worker. Da says we could use one. Da says we cannot afford one. Da says maybe next year.
%%
Long Road inn log, Sowing
I brought a sack of apples and pears to the Halfway Inn. Una of Ambrook had asked me to keep them apart for her lesson.

The horse was borrowed from the miller. It ate one apple before I tied the sack shut. I wrote that down. Una said it was not part of the lesson.

She set two baskets by the hearth. Apples in one, pears in the other. Then she showed the crystal one fruit at a time. The crystal moved each fruit to a basket. It put a pear with the apples.

Una changed the marks inside the crystal a little. Then she showed it the pear again. It chose the right basket.

We did this until the innkeeper asked us to stop blocking the bench. The crystal made fewer mistakes than I did.

The horse got another apple. This time I saw it happen.

Odo, roof book: the boat that was late
Leaffall, the 12th to the 14th. Kept at the house at the edge of Ambrook.
The 12th. Mave's brother Kell took the Heron out from Saltreach on the 3rd. Her trips take nine days. She is due today and is not home. Mave has not eaten since breakfast. After supper I took her to the relay house, and Linnet sent word to Saltreach. The answer: no news. The harbor lamp is lit.
Mave wrote at the desk: "Is the Heron lost?" The big mind wrote back in steps. I copied them.
"Step 1. What do I know? The Heron left on the 3rd. Her trips take nine days. The 3rd and nine days is the 12th. She is due today.
Step 2. What do I not know? Where she is, and what the wind has done. I have not seen the sea.
Step 3. What would help? A count of how often the Heron has been late. Please ask Saltreach."
Harl sent the answer from the log: in twenty trips, late five times, by one to three days.
"Step 4. Five in twenty is one in four. A late Heron is common. The longest delay was three days, which brings us to the 15th.
Step 5. Check: 12 and 3 make 15. I cannot tell you where she is. I can tell you what is usual. A late Heron is not a lost Heron. If she is not home by the 15th, we will send word to the boats on the coast. Until then, I would eat something."
Mave read it twice. She ate half a loaf at the desk, and Linnet gave the rest to the relay house cat.
The 14th. The Heron came in at dusk, two days late. The wind went round to the north and kept her out past the point. Nine hands, 900 cod. Kell lost his hat and found a better one.
I do not think the mind knew where the boat was. I think it knew how to be steady while we were not.
%%
Road book: spring on the Long Road
Blossom, the 20th day. The road is open from Saltreach to the north turn. The waymarks have new paint. Each stone pillar has its figure and its arrow, and each arrow points at the right inn, which I checked with my own feet.
The carts are back in numbers. Forty-one went past the Halfway on the first fair day of spring. Most were carrying wool or wheat. One was carrying nothing but a cage of hens, and the hens were carrying on about it.
On the hill past the Brown Hen, a cart of coal from Copperhold stopped to rest. The driver said the coal was bound north for the relay towers, and the north road was wet but passable. He said it with a look that suggested he did not expect to be asked twice.
At the Brown Hen the new keeper is Hobb, who was a carter until his back went. He has a boy who helps in the kitchen. The boy is an apprentice, small and very neat. It carries the bread from the oven to the table without dropping a crumb. It puts the bread down at each place, and then steps back and waits. The guests do not know what to say to it. I told them to say thank you. They did, and the boy turned and went back to the oven.
The honey price is up at Three Wells. Eleven emeralds a jar in Blossom, where it was seven in Thaw. The beekeeper at Ambrook says the bees are busy and the weather is good. She says this every spring, and every spring she is right, and every spring the price still goes up.
On the bridge at Hobb's Brook the plank was still loose. I have decided it is part of the road now. Nettle has decided the same.
Mended a wheel at the Halfway with Marda's hammer and a spare pin. The wheel squeaks worse than before. It is a happier squeak. I cannot explain the difference.
Tonight there is singing in the common room. Two carters from Kettle Ridge and a traveler from Lowmarsh have agreed on one song, which none of them knows. They sing it very loudly. The fire is warm. Good.
%%
Market log, Ambrook
Blossom, 30. Market bell at noon.
Stalls: Jory, flour. Sella, honey and jam. Pell, wool and salt. Mara's aunt, apples. Den's mother, carrots and beetroot.
The new oak bridge held all the carts. We crossed one at a time, as the road crew asked. The old path by the water is still open to walkers, but not to loaded carts.
A keeper from Vell came back to the hill. He brought two builders and a box of stone marks. They chose the ground above the mill pond, beyond the hives. The tower will stand where the old oak can be seen from the road.
There is no tower yet. There is a square of cleared grass, four stakes, and a board that says what will be carried up. Stone first. Then a stair, or rungs if the stone runs short.
The keeper says a lamp must be seen from the next hill. He says the signal must be plain. One light means a message waits. Two lights mean the road is closed.
A child asked who will watch the lamp. The keeper said someone who can stay awake. Everyone looked at me. I said I have a market to run.
%%
Mira, Northpoint, Longnight
Longnight, the 1st. The longest night of the year, and I have been up most of it. The lamp needs its glass cleaned more on the long nights than on any other, because it is the only light for a day's walk.

Longnight, the 3rd. The fox came to the door at dusk, and I was ready. I had a sock on the step and a sack of crusts beside it, and I watched from the window with my tea going cold. The fox looked at the sock, looked at me, and took the sock in its teeth. Then it walked up the path toward the tower with the sock held high. Lantern came out of the woodshed and followed it at a fair distance. The fox did not hurry. Lantern did not hurry. They went up the tower path like two guests who are not speaking.

Longnight, the 5th. The fox came back to the tower stair. It sat on the third step and looked up. Lantern was on the stair above it, carrying coal, and it stopped, because it had been told to carry, not to stop. The fox waited. Lantern waited. Then the fox went past it, very slowly, and out onto the lamp room rail, where it sat with the sock across its paws. I have never seen a fox so pleased with itself.

Longnight, the 6th. Pell wrote from the Halfway Inn to say the bet is settled. The fox was not a party to it, Pell says, but a fox is a fox, and a sock is a sock. I am to knit one sock with a fox on it, by spring. I have not decided which fox.

Longnight, the 11th. Letter from Tam. Pip has learned to carry two full buckets at once, one in each hand, and spills only half. Big Beet ate a bucket, and the bucket was not his. Tam asks if the fox has had a sock this year. He has heard about the sock.
%%
Arlo's account of the first founding day
The first day of spring is the founding day. The clerks at Vell have kept the years from it, and Pell says the whole road is keeping it now. So we kept it here, at the hamlet, and I will write down what we did, though I am a horse keeper and not a clerk.

In the morning we put up a pole with ribbons by the bridge, and the beetroot man brought his cart and sold honey cakes from it. The beekeeper brought three jars of honey and a little pot of wax, and set them on a table with a card that said, in her own hand, "Honey. Ask." Children came from the farms up the hill and ran over the new bridge and back, and one of them fell in the stream and was fished out and was very proud of it.

At noon a rider came down the road with a message. It had come by the relays, tower to tower, all the way from Three Wells, and the last keeper walked it to us. The message said the founding day was kept, and all were well, and the roads were open. We read it aloud by the pole. Everyone clapped, though the message did not ask for clapping.

Then I brought out the horses. Bramble first, steady and slow, and then Tansy, who is not a foal any more and does not care to be called one. I led them round the yard and Tansy did her tricks, the gate and the trough and the word for walk, and she did stop on the first try, which she never does. The beetroot man gave her a honey cake. I gave Tansy a small piece and ate the rest.

In the evening we lit the torches on the waymark and on the bridge and on every post along the road, and it was a chain of small lights from here to Ambrook and back. Pell says the towers do the same, from the tallest one down to the last, one lamp to the next. I thought of that while I lit my torches. We are a small light in a long chain. I like that.

The jar of honey is empty again. The beekeeper says the hives will fill it by Apple. The horses are in the stable, which is dry, and the wall is mine, and the light burns at the gate. We kept the first day of spring, and all were well, and the roads were open. I am glad I wrote this down.
%%
Northpoint house log
Winter of 204. This is the log of the north house, kept by Dace, who is old now and writes things down so as not to forget them.

Third of Frost. Gale. Six shingles gone again. I found one in the well. The roof is the oldest thing I own and it has a mind of its own.

Fourth of Frost. Mended the roof. I am far too old for roofs. My neighbor's apprentice held the ladder instead of Ossa, who has gone to live with her sister. The apprentice is called Reed. Reed held the ladder very still. It did not let go even when the wind came. I said thank you. Reed nodded once, the way they do. It was the best ladder-holding I have had in forty years.

Fifth of Frost. Reed's big mind sent a letter by the relay. It said: is the roof done and is Dace well. I wrote back: roof done, Dace well, Reed held the ladder. The big mind wrote back: good. I like a big mind that is brief.

Tenth of Frost. Snow, soft and thick. The goats will not go out. Loaf is dead these many years and Bitter too, and I have two new goats, Salt and Pepper. Salt is the thoughtful one. Pepper eats the washing. Some things do not change.

Fifteenth of Frost. News. There is to be a wedding at the edge of Northpoint. My granddaughter Wynn is marrying a woman called Tace from Kettle Ridge. Tace brought beetroot all this way to prove she is a farmer. It worked. I am to give Wynn away. I have been practicing giving people away. I keep getting it wrong and holding on.

Twentieth of Frost. We mended the roof of the old store for the wedding feast. The whole edge of Northpoint came. Reed carried shingles. Vey, who is keeper now, brought the good lanterns from the tower foot. We worked till dark and the roof was done and everyone was warm and we ate soup on the new roof, sitting on it, because we could.

Twenty-first of Frost. The wedding. Wynn and Tace stood in the snow with lanterns all round and the relay lamp blinking above them on the hill. Tace said her promise and Wynn said hers. I gave Wynn away and only held on a little. Then we ate beetroot and bread and honey and pumpkin pie, and someone played a fiddle and someone else sang too loud, and the foxes came for scraps and the whole edge of Northpoint danced in the snow in their mittens.

I wrote in my book that night: roof done, wedding good, Dace well. It is a short entry. It is the truest one I have.

Briar, baker, the farm below Vell, first
Winter. Bread, flour, the oven, and the things that happened on the way to the oven.
First frost on the windows. The oven was lit at the fourth bell and the first batch was out before the sun was up. Twelve loaves, all brown on top. Olin says the bottoms are a bit pale. Olin is always right about bottoms.

Orla, farm journal, early spring
Orla, grandmother, on the farm outside Three Wells.

First of Sowing. Frost on the gate. Ploughed the near field. Knees complain.
Second. Sowed the peas. The hens are laying.
Third. Market day tomorrow. Eggs: forty. Cheese: six rounds. Honey: three jars. The children want to come.
Fourth. Market. Sold it all but one jar of honey. The jar was eaten on the way home.

Reed, first winter at the edge
Winter of 204. I am Reed. I am an apprentice. I have a small mind in my crystal and a big mind in the relay house on the hill. I keep the log for Dace now, because Dace's hands are slow and Dace's memory is a sieve. Dace says this is not an insult to sieves.

My drills. I learned farm. I learned store. I learned build. I stand in my row at the drill house in the mornings and the tutor says the orders and I answer.

> bring me some wood
= fetch wood

> put the wood by the stove
= store

> mend the roof
= build

I got the roof wrong twice. Once I put the shingles upside down. Once I put them on the goat shed, which did not need them and has a fine roof. The tutor did not shout. The tutor never shouts. The tutor said try again. I tried again. The third time the roof was right and Dace said well done Reed and I nodded once.

Dace's big mind sent me a letter and asked how I was. I cannot write letters. Only the big minds write back. So I did the roof and the big mind saw through the relays that the roof was done, and wrote to Dace: the roof is done, Reed is well. I liked that. It was true and it did not need words from me.

The snow. I had never seen snow. The first morning I went out and stood in the yard and the snow fell on my head. I stood a long time. I was trying to learn snow. It is cold and it is soft and it lands and then it is gone. I did not learn all of it. I will try again next winter.

I wax my knees now. Dace told me apprentices creak and gave me a pot of wax. I wax my knees every Frost morning. I do not creak now. I did not know I creaked. Oiling the joints is good for a long winter.

Ossa's sister Rill came to the wedding and said I held the ladder well. I nodded once. I held it very still. It was the most I have ever done and it was enough.

Job done. Reed well. I will keep the wood in and the goats fed and the roof mended until Dace says stop. Dace is not going to say stop. Good. I will keep working.
%%
Letter: Pell to Ambrook
Winter of 208.
To my sister at Ambrook, from the Halfway Inn.
The honey you sent froze and cracked in the cold. I sold what was left. Send more in Swarm, not in Longnight.
The road north is quiet. Too quiet, folk say. The relays carry less than they did. I do not know if that is true. I only know the inn is full of people who say it.
A man came through with a big mind on a cart, going to a relay house. It was in a crystal the size of a loaf, in a box of straw. He would not let anyone near it. He said it answers apprentices through the relays. I said I hoped it answers better than my ledger does. He did not laugh.
Your apples were good this year. The oldest tree still gives. Keep the sluice shut and the bees happy.
Tell the children the riddle board is still unanswered. That will make them laugh.

Vell tutor house, autumn log
Leaffall, 11, 207.
We compared three small minds today. Each had learned the eight meanings: farm, store, fetch an item, come, stay, build, haul and home. A small mind must give one of those answers by doing. It cannot write back to say it is unsure.

The new order drills do not add a ninth meaning. They teach the mind to find the meaning inside a whole sentence. The same meaning may be asked in different words.

> put the apples in the store room
= store

> carry the apples to the store room
= store

The apprentice moved the apples to the right bins. It did not explain its choice. Small minds do not write letters. The big minds in relay houses can write back through the relays.

Leaffall, 14.
A child brought a card from the school. It said, "Take the book to the room where we read." The mind took it to the teaching room. Nessa said the order was fair because the mind had learned that room in other drills. The child said it was still a clever answer. Both were right.

Leaffall, 19.
The argument about first place came back at supper. Hesk writes that Clank was first at Copperhold. Tam writes that Pip was first at Kettle Ridge. The keeper of the fair says Peg was first. Nessa says she saw it in the drill room long ago.

Oren copied all four claims. He would not choose one. He says a careful clerk does not repair a record by guessing.

Mira, house journal, a quarrel
Spring. A house at the edge of Northpoint. Four days of a small, silly quarrel.

First day. The fence between my yard and old Hale's has fallen. A post rotted in the winter and the whole stretch leaned over into my garden. Hale came out and said it is my fence. I said it is his. He said it was put up by my predecessor. I said my predecessor was a keeper, not a fence builder. He went inside and shut the door hard.
Second day. I went to the village record and looked. The fence line is Hale's. It is on the map. The ink is brown, from the old days, but clear. I carried the map to his door. He looked at it for a long time and said the map was wrong.
Third day. A neighbor came by and asked if we wanted help. We said no, both of us at once. Lantern, who had been listening from the roof, said she would mend the fence herself if we would stop shouting. We said no again. She mended it anyway, in the night. In the morning it was straight, with two new posts and a gate between our yards that neither of us had asked for.
Fourth day. Hale knocked at my door with a pot of stew. I gave him tea. We sat on the step and looked at the gate. We did not say anything about it. He said the stew was his wife's. I said the tea was my brother's, from Kettle Ridge.
It is a good gate. We have been through it three times.

Log: the boat that learned the coast
Written at the Saltreach harbor, by the boat-builder's log, for the sea trial of the little yawl. Kept by Corin, shipwright, with notes from his mate Dace, who is not the mayor.

Day 1. The yawl is newly built, from oak and spruce. Her mast is birch. She has a keel of iron bars and a rudder on two strings, the strings tied to a small mind in a brass cage on the stern. The tutor from Vell said the mind is not drilled on sea. It is only set to try, and to keep the boat's head to the wind.

The first sail was short. The yawl went out past the breakwater, with two men aboard to watch, and came back with her bow pointing at the cliff. The mind had kept her too close to the wind. It had not been told. It had only found that when the boat went that way, she slowed, and when it went the other way, she sped. So it had stayed close to the wind, because that was where she went quickest, and nearly put her on the rocks.

Day 3. The mind is slower to learn the wind than the water. The water is steady. The wind changes its mind every few breaths. We have set the mind a longer task: to hold the boat steady for a whole reach of the bay, and then to turn her at the buoy. The first time, it did not turn. The second time, it turned too early, and the boat spun. The third time, it turned at exactly the right place, and the men cheered so loudly that the gulls rose off the mole.

Day 6. The rule the mind has found is simple and it is not simple. It keeps the boat moving, and it keeps her head to the wind, and when the wind shifts it turns a little. A little, and then more, until the boat comes back into the line. It does not hold one line. It holds a line that moves. Dace says that is what a sailor does. Corin says a sailor has a face and a belly and does not like being wet. The mind does not mind the wet.

Day 9. The yawl went out in a rough sea. Three fishing boats came in early and tied up along the quay. The yawl came in last, with the wind on her quarter and her sail half-reefed. The mind had reefed the sail itself, by pulling the second string, which we never told it how to do.

Day 14. We have sailed her forty times. She has not gone on the rocks since the first morning. The men still do not trust her. The mind has not been asked to say whether it trusts the sea. It does not write. It only sails.

Day 20. Corin has written in the margin of this book that the yawl is to be sent to Copperhold, to the river, to see if the mind can keep a barge in the current. He has added a line under it. He says: the boat has learned the coast. Let us see if it can learn the river.
%%
Colm's farm log, the grey cough rumor
Autumn. Late Apple.
The cold came early this year. I have shut the top field gate and put the sheep in the low meadow by the barn.
Wren wrote from Lowmarsh. She is well but tired. She says more people are coughing than usual. A grey cough, she calls it. She says she does not know where it comes from. Some in the village say the water is bad. Some say it came off the ships at Saltreach. Some say it is the mines. Wren says she has heard all three and believes none of them yet. She says she washes her hands and keeps her jars full.
I sent her two sacks of the felt for blankets. She wrote back and said thank you and that Moss is learning to make the beds. Moss is her apprentice. Wren says Moss is careful, and that careful is most of healing.
At market Pell said the Deep Works hum at night now. Hesk told him so. Pell says Hesk does not joke. Then Pell said it was only the pumps, old pumps make a hum. Then he said the ninth gate is locked and the key is at the mine hall, and nobody says why. Then he drank his tea.
I do not know what to think. I carded wool until the fire went low and went to bed.
%%
Lenn's summer log, the pond
Sowing, 202. The pond is warm and the fish are lazy. I sit on the bank and they come to look at me. I catch three, and one gets off the hook at the boat. That is how it goes.
The bees are in the apple trees all day. You can hear them from the water. Jory says a loud bee summer means a good crop. Jory says a lot of things.
Pip came down from the ridge with a cart of beetroot for the inn. It stopped by the pond to watch the frogs. It stayed an hour, very still, watching. Then it went on up the road. Good.
%%
Market note by Sella
Blossom, 24. The Ambrook market.
The east path was muddy, so the stalls stood close to the bridge. I sold honey, apples and two small pots of apple jam. The jam went first. Everyone says they came only for apples.
Pell arrived with a cart of wool. One wheel made a sound like a frog. He said the cart had learned to sing. The wheel stopped singing when he put a stone under it.
The road crew passed after the market bell. They are joining the path to the Long Road with packed earth. Where the ground is soft, they put down cobblestone. They say a bridge will go over the little stream next. For now, we carry goods across on our backs.
A relay keeper came from Vell to look at the hill above the mill pond. The people there want a tower, so messages need not travel by hand. He walked around the hill with a cord and a small board. He marked the best place beside the old oak.
I asked how tall the tower would be. He said tall enough to be seen from the road. That is a useful answer. I hope he can give the same answer to the wind.

Quin, midwife's book, Apple
Midwife's book for the month of Apple, year 199, kept by Quin on the farm outside Saltreach, for the next midwife to read.
Third: called out before dawn to the farm by the reeds. A first child. Long labor. I stayed all day. Tea, and then more tea. The baby came at dusk, a girl, with a lot of dark hair. The mother asked to call her Heron. Both well.
Fourth: slept. Fed the hens. Found the horse had been borrowed again by the neighbor's boy without asking. He brought her back in good order and a jar of honey, as a joke. I accepted both.
Ninth: Marn's third. Quick. A boy. He came out in a hurry, as if he had somewhere to be. The mother laughed the whole time.
Fourteenth: visited the Hale farm to look at the baby's cord. All well. Told them again to keep it dry.
Twentieth: the farm by the cove. A twin birth, a rare thing. A boy and a girl. I was glad of Wren's letter, which said what to do. I did it. Both well. The father could not speak for an hour.
Twenty-sixth: a quiet day. Picked apples. Baked a pie.

Tam, house journal, the calf
Autumn. Cold morning, mist on the ridge.
A calf was born in the night. Brown, with a white nose. I sat up with the cow and Pip held the lantern. The cow did it all herself. I only said "good girl" a lot.
Named the calf Beetroot. Pip approves, as far as copper can. By noon she was standing on four wobbling legs and looking at the world as if it owed her something. The cat sat on the fence and watched. The cat has never seen anything so tall and so foolish. I gave the cow an extra measure of oats and a bucket of warm water. Pip will carry the milk when the time comes. Tonight I will sleep in the barn, with the lantern turned low.

Colm's farm log, the wedding at Ambrook
Autumn. Two weeks into Apple.
Went to the wedding at Ambrook. Pell drove the cart and I sat in the back with the wool and the rain came and went. Three hours on the Long Road. A torch on a post every twelve blocks, the same as always. It is a comfort, that road.
The wedding was in the orchard, under the oldest tree with the grey branch. Everyone from Ambrook came, and half of Three Wells, and Jory gave away the bride, who is his niece. There was honey cake and apple cake and a barrel of last year's cider. The bees were out, which is odd for Apple, but the day was warm.
The bride wore a dress of the good wool from my own flock. I did not know until I saw it. I had to sit down.
There was dancing in the barn after. I do not dance, but I clapped. Dace from Three Wells made a speech about the town and the roads and the trees and the people. It was a long speech. It was a good speech. He said: we plant trees we will not sit under. Everyone went quiet, then everyone cheered.
Pell drank too much cider and told the same story three times. It gets better each time.
Coming home, the sky over the north was bright for a while. Lights, low down, moving slow. Pell said falling stars. I said fires. A child in the cart said they were looking at us. Nobody said anything after that. Then the lights went out and we rode on.
The road home was dark. The torches were lit. I was glad of them.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 57. The first of Swarm.
Crew: Hesk, Sava, Varo, Jell.
Level: four.
Count: copper 9 carts, iron 4, coal 5.
The relay tower above Three Wells has its lamp. We watched from the road after shift. A keeper climbed the stair with oil and came down carrying an empty tin. At the top, the signal lamp shone through the glass.
A runner brought our mine tally to the tower. He waited while the keeper sent it on. No one knows who will read it first. Sava says a clerk at Vell will complain about my figures. Sava says this because my sevens look like hooks.
The road is busy now. Two carts passed us before supper. One carried flour. One carried wool and a sleeping child. The child woke when the wheel struck the loose stone.
We ate in the new room at the mine gate. Jell had saved an apple from market day. He cut it into five pieces, one for each of us. That is a good way to divide an apple.

Dace, market journal, the spring
Written at the end of spring, after the council asked for the count. I have kept this journal since Thaw, and I will keep it through the summer, because a market is only a market if someone writes it down.

Thaw, the first day. The goose has been seen on the roof of the bakery, looking down at the carts with the air of a tax collector who has not been paid. Nobody has asked the goose for its opinion. It has given it anyway.

Thaw, the sixth day. The bakery has a new helper, a copper one from Vell, which carries the flour from the cart to the loft. It stands where it is put, and waits for the next sack. Ida says it is the best helper she has had. She has not told it so, because it would not know what to do with the praise.

Sowing, the eighth day. A homecoming at the north well. Pim Hale has come back from Vell with his clear hand and a copied page, and he read it aloud at the well. The children clapped, then asked him questions, and he answered most of them and wrote the rest down to take back. The bell rang at noon, and nobody minded that it was late.

Sowing, the nineteenth day. A man came to the stall asking after the lost hen. The hen was on his shoulder when he came, and nobody had seen her arrive. She had walked up the Long Road and into the market, and sat down on the honey stall, and waited. The man said he was grateful. The hen said nothing, which is her way.

Blossom, the fifth day. The bees came out of their hives on the first warm day and sat on the lid of the honey jar until Ama gave them a dish of water. She says it is the same thing as an apology. The bees agree, and have not swarmed since.

Blossom, the twenty-second day. The keeper of the hill lamp asked the council for a new cloth for the lamp room, because the old one was frayed. The council voted for it, and asked the cook to make it. The cook said the cloth would be ready by the next market, if the stew allowed. The stew allowed.

Blossom, the last day of the month. The council counts the spring. Forty-one stalls. Nine carts from the ridge. Two cats in the square, and one that is almost ours. One goose, on the roof. One cave under the field, with a bell at each end. One lamp, lit every night. One slate, counted twice. One gift from the Archive, which says it rained, and which is kept in a box with a lid, waiting. The bread has been dry the whole spring. Ida says that is the bread's fault. I think it is only the weather. Either way, I am glad of it.
%%
Fallon, hamlet and yard log
Spring of 207, Thaw to Apple.
The store wall is finished. We used Copperhold stone for the lower row and stone from our hill above it. The two kinds look much the same after rain. Tavin says he can tell them apart by tapping. He taps every block. He taps the well too, though we asked him not to.

A new apprentice came up from the town with a little mind in a crystal. It carried stones, filled the trough, and stayed when told. It did not know our yard. The keeper showed it the tool shed and the stone pile. Then the keeper asked it to bring a block. It brought the smallest one. We used that for the low step.

I asked whether it could sort the blocks by size. The keeper said it had not been tested on that work. So we did not give it the job. Rell made three piles instead, and got two right. The apprentice stood beside him and watched. It did not speak. Neither did the blocks.

The old map is now in a wooden case. Fen cleaned the dust from its edges with a soft brush. It shows the road before the new bridge and the lane before the east wall. A mark beside the hill says "good stone". I know that shelf. I have cut there for thirty years. The map maker knew it too.

The spring has been kind this year. Water runs in the ditch, and the well has not gone low. We planted wheat on the upper field and beetroot below it. Potatoes went by the south hedge. My sister put carrots there as well, though I told her the row was too narrow. She says carrots do not mind close neighbors.

At dusk we ate by the yard wall. The apprentice carried the plates from the kitchen. It set my plate beside my stool and Tavin's plate on the stone pile. Tavin ate there without complaint. It was the first time I saw him sit still.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 74. The sixth of Longday.
Crew: Hesk, Sava, Varo, Jell.
Level: four.
Count: copper 12 carts, iron 5.
The winter lasted longer than any I remember. Snow covered the road and the cart track. We stopped work below the third level twice, when water froze on the ladder rungs. No one fell. We left torches burning at the gate, and Sava brought hot broth for the late crew.
A fox slept beneath the wood store. It left when the snow thinned and took a crust of bread with it.
Now the road to Three Wells is open. A stone bridge crosses the stream below the mill. The bridge is narrow, but the carts pass one at a time. The innkeeper at the Brown Hen says he kept the hearth lit all winter. He also says we owe him for two bowls of soup. I remember one bowl.
The relay keeper brought a message from Vell. The archive has made another room in the hill. The shelves are dry, and the clerks copy old pages as well as new ones. I asked if they had written down our mine tally. The keeper said yes. My sevens do look like hooks.

Tova, road log, the first snow
Frost of 158. Snow in the night.

The Long Road is shut. Two carts are in the ditch. One is mine. I am not sorry. I am tired.

Greer took me in. His farm sits at the edge of the reeds. His son Ivo asked if the snow was early. I said everything is early in Frost.

The tutor here is Abel. In his box is a mind the size of a hen's egg, in a crystal, with three layers. It sorts the letters for the Lowmarsh relay. It is not ready for the road. Abel says never send an untested mind to the fields.

Sallow Ford log, the small crystal
Kept by Ilse, tutor of apprentices at Vell, on a visit to Sallow Ford. A note on what fewer marks cost, and what they do not.
Reaping, the 20th day. The ferry mind has been in the small crystal for a week. The Archive will want the counts, so here they are.
Before the change: the mind passed the river test on eighty-seven crossings out of a hundred. On a fresh hundred, unseen, it was wrong on thirteen.
After the change: the same test on the same hundred crossings gave seventy-nine right. After a morning of drill on the crossings it had missed, the count rose to eighty-one. The cost is about six crossings in a hundred. Not much. It is real, and I will not hide it.
Why? Each weight in the big crystal was written with eight marks. In the small crystal each weight has four. Four marks say most of the weight, but not all of it. The small errors add up across the mind. So it is a little less sure, most of all at the edges of a crossing, when the water is high and fast.
That is what fewer marks are. A way to fit a mind into a smaller place, with a small cost paid in the answers.
The ferry hands ask whether the mind will get worse. It will not, if we keep it honest. We drill it each morning on the crossings it has seen and the ones it has not. We test it on the day's new crossing before it takes a cart. That is the rule.
Brede asks why I do not put it back in the big crystal. The ferry house has no room for one, and no wall strong enough for the wax. So it stays small, and it stays tested.
The mind does not know it is smaller. It does not know anything. But the ferry hands know, and they stand a moment longer at the landing when the water is high, to be sure. I think that is the right kind of care.
Written by Ilse, at Sallow Ford, in Reaping.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 103. The seventh of Blossom.
Crew: Hesk, Varo, Jell, Rusk.
Levels: three and four.
Count: copper 13 carts, iron 7, coal 5.
The spring road crew reached the hill above Copperhold. They have laid cobblestone from the mine gate to the first bend. Beyond it, the Long Road runs down to the Brown Hen and on to Three Wells.
At market day, we sold copper wire and bought two sacks of flour. Rusk bought a beetroot seed for his mother. He carried it in his closed hand all the way back, as if it might run away.
There was a notice from Vell on the board. The archive's first shelves are full. The clerks have sealed the old room and opened another room farther in the hill. The notice says the copying room has eight long tables. Rusk asked whether all the tables were for writing. I said yes. He said that sounded like a lot of sums.
The new waymark at the fork has a torch on top and an arrow toward Copperhold. It is taller than Rusk. He stood beside it to check.
The road is easier for carts now. A wheel broke at the bend, but the driver had a spare. We helped him lift the cart. He gave us an apple from Ambrook. I put it on the mine hall shelf for after shift. Jell took it before I did.
I have written this tally twice. The first copy had coal where copper should be. I put the spoiled page in the stove. Varo says the clerks would have kept it too. Let them keep the right one.

Hesk, log of the farm crew
Apple, third through sixth

The frost has not come, but the mornings are cold. We have to finish the fence before it does. The south side runs from the barn to the stream, then turns beside the beetroot field. Two posts are loose. A third is split.

Clank took the crew out at first light. It read the fence marks, checked the tools, and made a list of what was missing. It had brought a hammer, a shovel, and enough oak rails. It had forgotten the iron nails.

The store shed was still locked. I went for the key. Clank did not wait with the others. It gave them cards to clear the old rails and stack the good ones by length. That was useful work, but it kept the crew out of trouble.

When I came back, Clank sent a question through the relay to the big mind at Copperhold. Could the crew set the loose posts before the nails arrived? The answer said to brace them with stones, then fit the rails when the store opened.

Clank changed the plan. One helper dug around the first post. Two brought deep stone from the field edge. The last held the post straight while we packed stones around its base. We used the same way at the second post.

The split post could not be braced. Clank marked it for replacement and wrote a card to fetch an oak post from the wood pile. It checked that the new post was not cracked before anyone lifted it.

On the fifth, a fox came to the hens at dawn. The hens were shut in the little house, but the latch had slipped. One helper was carrying a rail past the yard. It stopped, set the rail down, and stood between the fox and the door.

I ran from the shed with a broom. The fox slipped through the hedge before I reached the yard. One hen was gone. The others were loud enough for the whole hill.

Clank had not been given a card about foxes. It had seen the open door and the fox near the hens. It put the rail across the gap after the fox left. I fixed the latch with wire.

The nails arrived on the sixth. The crew set the rails in one long line. They left a gate wide enough for the cart and put the new post where the split one had stood. We finished before the evening fog came off the stream.

Ivo's market book, Reaping
Autumn of 174. Four market days at the north well, set down in one go, because my knees would not let me write on the day.
The 5th. Warm. Sold the last of the summer eggs and all my green socks. A child asked why the sky was so big. I said it is a market day and the sky is allowed. Lost children: 1. He was at the bakery, as always.
The 11th. Cooler. The wheat is coming in at last, and every cart smells of straw. The man with the squeaky wheel went by four times, to be sure we all heard. Sold 80 eggs. One of my hens laid an egg with two yolks. I kept it for Piper.
The 17th. Wet. The market board checked the loaves and found three short. Mott's loaves were not short. They never are. The board says it again every week, and Mott says "Thank you" every week, and neither of them smiles.
The 23rd. Fine. Piper kept the stall for an hour so that I could walk round the three wells. It is not far. It took me a long time, because everyone wants to talk. I learned that Cato's goat is expecting, that the new inn is nearly finished, and that Lyle's field has more wheat than Lyle's barn.
I am not a gossip. I am a collector. The clerks at Vell would call it keeping a record.
Total for Reaping: eggs 410, socks 22 pairs, lost children 5, all found. The stool leans. So do I.

Vane's book, the long talk in autumn
Leaffall. A month of talk. The whole hall was full every night.
After Ilse's test, the question was not allowed to die. Every traveler wanted to talk about it, and the carters brought their own versions up the road. Some said a mind could only think if it had a body. Some said a mind could think if it had enough letters to read. Some said a mind was only a very good parrot, and that a parrot could sing a song without knowing the words.
Old Ama said that a parrot was a fine thing, and that she had never once met a parrot who knew it was a parrot. Everyone laughed, and then they went quiet, and then they argued for an hour.
Hobb said that a mind that said it did not know something was surely thinking. A parrot would never say that. Marda said that a parrot would say whatever it had heard, and that the best minds were the ones who had heard the most people say I do not know. That was why they said it too.
The tutors who came through in the autumn were kinder about the question than the scholars had been. They said the answer was a matter of patience, and that the minds were improving every year, and that it was a poor sort of tutor who stopped asking the question. Nessa, who came through on the cart, said she had asked it herself, every morning, of her own drill slate, for thirty years, and had never got a satisfying answer. She said the question was the most useful thing the archive had given her.
I have written all this down because Ilse asked me to keep the record. She said the archive would want it. She said the question would still be open when the record was read. That seems right to me. It is still open in my kitchen, every night, over the stew.
%%
Wren's healing house, winter log
The wind came over the marsh in the night and rattled the jars. Nothing broke. Moss says the jars are brave. I say they are heavy.
A fisher came in with a sore shoulder. He had pulled a boat by its rope while the boat was caught in mud. I rubbed the shoulder with warm oil and told him to ask for help next time. He said he had asked. The help had been a small boy with a spoon. We both laughed, then he winced.
Moss carried the washing to the line. The cloth froze before it was hung. We brought it back in and laid it by the stove. It dried slowly, but the beds were made before supper.
The well bucket had a crack. We used a sound bucket from the shed and set the cracked one aside for catching rain. Moss wrote CRACKED on it. The letters were large enough for anyone to read, even from the boardwalk.
At the fish weir, the water had pushed reeds across the path. We cleared them with hooks and stacked them beside the boat house. The frogs watched from the bank. Moss asked if frogs ever work. I said they keep still and catch flies. Moss said that sounded like a good job.

Oren's day-book, the cart to Northpoint
Winter, the year the north road was open late. Oren went up the road with the cart from Vell, to check the logs at the relay house by hand, which the clerks had asked him to do since the relay line was slow. Written in the night, at the Northpoint kitchen table.

Left Vell on the fourth day of Deepsnow. Two carts, one driver, two boys, and a box of paper for the north. The box is heavy. The boys say it is heavier than the box of letters from the south.

The Long Road is white to the Halfway. Hobb had a fire going and sent us on with bread. The waymarks stand up out of the snow like small stones. We counted them. Forty-one to the crossing.

Stayed at the Oak Post. Sella gave us the good room. Mott came in at midnight with a lamp and said the bakery cat has been sleeping on the flour. Sella said the cat is not to be moved. The cat was not moved.

On the road past the ridge, the wind came up from the north. The carts went very slowly. We walked beside them for the last part of the hill. The boys were cold. I was cold. The horse was not cold at all, and is still the only one of us who is happy.

At Northpoint the lamp was lit when we came round the last bend. It is a good light. Mira came down the stair with a cup of tea in each hand and gave one to me and one to the air. Then she gave me the other one.

Quin, the apple season
Journal of Quin, midwife, a farm outside Saltreach, the whole of Apple, year 188. For myself, and for anyone who likes to know how a midwife spends a month.
First of Apple. The first apples are in. I picked two baskets from the old tree behind the house. They are small, hard and good. I made a pie for supper and gave half to the neighbor.
Fourth. Called out at midnight to the farm on the cliff. A hard birth, long, the baby turned the wrong way. I turned it back with my hands, which is the oldest trick. Both well. I came home at dawn, so tired I ate the pie crust for breakfast.
Seventh. Slept all day. In the evening, a boy came for the horse. His mother is in labor and his father is away. I lent him my mare, with a note for Wren if she was nearer. The boy rode off as if the night were on fire.
Eighth. The mare is back. The boy also, with a jar of honey from his own hive. The baby is a girl. He is eleven, and calls himself the uncle.
Twelfth. A week of waiting. Nothing. I sat on the porch and mended a basket and watched the clouds. I find I like the quiet days best, after the loud ones.
Fifteenth. The festival of the first ship at the harbor. I went, and stood at the wall, and ate fish pie. The harbor was full of paper boats with candles. Someone's baby, born the week before, was held up to see them. I know that baby. I was there on the first day.
Twentieth. Wren sent a letter. She asks if I have any honey to spare. I said I had two jars. I sent one. It seems that everyone is giving me honey this month. I think I must be doing something right, or very wrong.
Twenty-fourth. A child's birth at the Marl farm, easy, pleasant. The father brought me a basket of apples I had given him last year. They were small and hard and good.
Twenty-ninth. Quiet. I sat on the porch with the last of the honey. I counted this month: eight births, one false alarm, two borrowed horses, five jars of honey and a hen in a bed.
It is a good life. I would not change it. I might ask for more sleep.
Quin

Ivo, bees on the market bell
Autumn of 179. Apple, the 20th. Market day at the north well. The strangest one in my thirty-eight years.
At the first bell, everything was ordinary. Eggs, socks, one lost child, found. Then a hum began over the square. It was low, like a pot coming to the boil. A woman by the pie tent said "Oh," and pointed at the market bell.
The bell hangs in a little roof at the crossroads. A great brown ball hung from its rim, as big as a pumpkin, and the ball was moving. Bees. They were swarming, on an autumn day, which nobody had ever seen. Old Teague, who keeps hives behind the school, said that swarms come in Swarm, not in Apple. He said it twice. The bees had not read the almanac.
The mayor was told. The mayor is afraid of bees. He decided at once that the bees were not his business.
Teague came with a basket, a smoking pot and a sheet. "Nobody ring the bell," he said. Nobody did. We stood and watched an old man in a sheet climb a ladder with smoke coming out of his hands. The bees hummed. A boy asked if they were cross. Teague said they were looking for a house and had not found one, and that is a sad thing to be, even for a bee.
It took him until noon. By then the bees were in the basket, the basket was in a cart, and Teague was asking for a cup of tea.
Here is the funny part. The market ends at the noon bell, and the noon bell had been busy. So nobody knew it was noon. Nobody went home. The stalls stayed open. The pie tent sold out. The man with the long hats sold every hat he had. A man offered me 3 for my stool. I said it leans. He said that is why he wanted it. I did not sell it.
At dusk the mayor rang the bell himself, with a very long rope tied to a very long stick, from behind the pie tent.
Teague says the swarm will make honey on the hill next summer. He calls it Bell honey. I have put my name down for a jar.
Eggs: 0. Socks: 9 pairs. Lost children: 1, found. A very good day. I would like a swarm every year.

Corin, the first snow
The first of Frost. First snow. It came in the night and the whole slip is white. I walked down at dawn, and the only marks were fox prints running along the ramp, as if the fox were measuring it. They were straighter than my yard boy's lines.

The boats are drawn up. The yard is quiet. In the afternoon I took a question to Ilse at the tower. The big mind there will answer anyone who writes to it now. I asked how many planks a boat of thirty blocks needs, if each plank is three blocks long and the boat has six rows on each side. I know the answer. I wanted to see how it would get there.

They say the new ones write out their steps before they answer. I will see.

Berit's notes for the tutors' halls
These notes are for the young tutors. They are not for the drill books. Read them when you have sat through a long trial and want to throw a crystal into the sea.

Most of our minds are drilled. We show a question and its answer, many times, and each time the mind is wrong it changes a little, toward the right answer. This works well for sorting, for counting and for words. It works because we know the answer before we begin.

A tried mind is different. We do not know the answer, or we do not know it well enough to write it down. So we do not drill it. We set it a task, let it try, and watch what happens. When the melt holds, or the boat stays up, we keep what the mind did just before. When the melt runs thick or the boat rolls, we let that go. Over many tries the good choices gather, and the bad ones fade. Nobody tells the mind the answer. The good results are the only teacher it has.

Here is why this matters. The furnace mind at Saltreach learned to hold the colour of the iron without anyone telling it what the colour should be. It found the colour by trying, and it kept the bellows movements that went well. The yawl learned to keep her head to the wind in the same way. Neither one knows a word for what it does. Both do it.

Here is the danger. A tried mind keeps what went well, and nothing else. It does not ask whether the good result was luck. It does not know that shouting means danger. So we must test it on what it was never tried on. We keep a box of new tasks, with tasks no mind has seen, and we run the mind through them before we let it near the furnace or the sea. If it fails the box, it does not work. If it passes, we still watch it.

Some of the young tutors ask me why we bother, when the drilled minds are faster. My answer is this. A drilled mind knows what we taught it. A tried mind sometimes knows things we did not know to teach. The yawl was not taught the storm. It found the storm, and kept the boat up in it. That is worth the slow years.

Remember also that a tried mind cannot say what it has learned. It cannot write its reasons. You can only see the work. So keep records of the work, and write down what the mind did, and what the result was, and the day and the weather. Your notes are the only way anyone will know what the mind has done.

Berit, tutor of the Vell halls, for the young ones
Written at the tutor house in Vell, after the trial at the Saltreach harbor.
%%
Hesk, deep shift log, and the harvest
Written by Hesk of Copperhold. Reaping, year 201. The mine lets every miner go to the farms for the harvest week, and I went to my sister's.

Last shift before leave. Copper level, east drift. Cut two hundred and ten blocks. Clank hauled forty loads without a stumble. Eighth rail powered, ninth rail not, which is as it should be. A tired miner counts rails to stay awake.

Out of the shaft at dusk. Wrote my name on the board and rubbed it out coming up. The kitchen at the gate had a stew of potatoes, carrots and a little salt pork. Good stew. I had two bowls and did not talk to anyone.

First day of leave. Walked to my sister's farm along the road. The hawthorn is red. Her wheat is high. It has been a hot summer and the harvest is early. She said, "You are late." I said, "I am exactly on time." She said she meant for dinner.

Second day. Wheat. Cut, bind, stack. Clank came with me, because the mine hall lets it on leave if I say so. It binds sheaves faster than any of us. My sister's neighbors stood and watched for a while. Then they stopped watching and started racing. The young ones lost. The old woman from the next farm tied a sheaf with her eyes closed and won.

Third day. The beetroot field. A heavy crop. The boys pulled and the girls sorted. At noon we sat in the shade and ate bread and cheese. A bee walked across my sister's hand. She did not move. She said the trick is to be a tree. I tried to be a tree. A bee walked across my nose.

Fourth day. A storm in the afternoon, but we had the last sheaves in. We sat in the barn and listened to the rain on the roof. It is a good sound. Underground, rain is a story somebody tells you.

Fifth day. Harvest supper. We ate a pig, and a pumpkin pie, and apples from the old tree. My sister's son asked me about the mine. I told him about the deep stone, how cold and dark and quiet it is. He asked if I was afraid. I said no. A mine is only a very big cellar, and I have never been afraid in a cellar. He nodded and went back to his pie.

Sixth day. The wagon came to take the wheat to the mill. I walked beside it as far as the crossroads. The driver sang. I did not sing, but I hummed. I am not a good hummer.

Seventh day. Back to the shaft. Wrote my name on the board. Down the long stair. At the bottom, in the dark and cold, I stopped for a moment and listened. Quiet. Good. Home.

Hesk, the new road
Melon, year 171. At my sister's farm outside Copperhold.

The new road came through this spring. Cobblestone, with a torch on a post every twelve blocks. The mine hall paid for the stone and the farmers paid for the digging. Before this the cart track turned to soup in rain, and the ore carts sank to the axle. Now a cart goes from the shaft head to the farm gate in a morning.

My sister says it is the best thing that ever happened to her turnips. A turnip that gets to market on the day it is pulled is a better turnip.

Today I walked it end to end to see if it was straight. It is not. There is a bend by the old oak. I asked the road crew why. The foreman said the oak was there first. I said that is a fair answer. He gave me a boiled egg.

Evening. Sat on the waymark with my boots off. The stone is warm from the sun. Nobody has yet left a little stone on the top. I left one. It looked lonely, so I left two.
%%
Northpoint journal, the ice camp summer
Melon, the first. The ice camp is folded for the summer. The men have taken the sledges down to the lake shore, where they keep them in the boat house, and set up their drying racks on the stones. The lake is clear and green, and I can see fish in it, which is news for a keeper who has only seen them through a hole in the ice.

Melon, the third. The ice camp men came up with the first fish of the summer, a big one, the length of my arm, and wanted to know whether the tower could cook it. The tower can cook anything, I told them. The fish was very good. Lantern does not eat, but it held the pan steady for an hour while the fish spat and hissed, and it did not spill a drop.

Melon, the sixth. A letter from Vell came up by the post cart, which takes a month to reach us in summer, because the roads are muddy at the bottom and dusty at the top. The councils have voted to send a relay house to Northpoint, for the spring, if the stone can be carried up the north road. The stone is at the quarry by the lake. I have been asked whether I think it can be carried. I have said yes. I have also said that I hope the stone is not too heavy, because my back is not what it was.

Melon, the ninth. Took the stone's measure with the tower's rope. It is a long pale block of good stone. Lantern measured it too, very carefully, by walking round it and counting its steps. It came out at twenty-two steps round, which is not what the quarrymen said. We argued about it all afternoon, in the way two keepers argue, which is quietly and with a lot of sighing.

Melon, the fourteenth. The summer rains came, and the lake rose a hand's width in a night. The ice camp men dragged their racks up the shore and cursed the water in the old way, loudly.

Melon, the twentieth. Clear again. Sent a message down the line to Vell that the first fish has been cooked at Northpoint. It came back: "Fish is for the southern towers." I said the same about beetroot.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed. The lake is green, and the stone is waiting at the quarry.
%%
Una, candle book, the first frost
Frost, the second. First hard frost of the year, and the tallow went hard in the pot before I could pour it. Made forty candles from what was soft and left the rest for the night.

Frost, the fifth. Wicks cut to the length of a finger. A wick that is too long smokes, and one that is too short gutters in the first draught. Measured every one.
