Tam, farm log: the autumn, all of it
Tam, farm log, the top farm, through the autumn. Long, because the autumn was.

Reaping, first day. The top strip has come in. Forty sacks of wheat, which is a good count for a strip that size. Pip carried them to the cart, one at a time, and stacked them in rows as tall as itself, and would not let me help. Juno says that is pride. I say it is a helper that has found a job it likes.

Reaping, seventh day. Brin came up the ridge with his cart, asking if we had seen his sheep, Biscuit. We had not. Biscuit was found in our beetroot, eating it, with the look of a sheep who has been waiting all her life for beetroot. Brin paid for the beetroot in a loaf, which is fair.

Apple, fourth day. A bad morning. The gate on the north field was torn off in the night. Something big had leaned on it, and the hinge had gone. I do not think it was the fox. Pip stood beside the gate and looked at the broken hinge, and then at me, and then at the hinge again. I told it: fetch the iron. It fetched the iron, and I mended the gate with the new hinge, and Pip held the gate while I drove in the pins.

Apple, sixth day. The fence along the lane needed new posts, and I needed a helper to hold the post while I set it. Pip held the post. It held the post plumb, which I had not asked it to do, and when I said so, it stood up straighter, and held it plumb again.

Leaffall, second day. Butter is fat again, and cross about it. Her milk has come back, and the children drink it warm in the mornings, from the good cups. The beetroot she ate in Frost is still a story in the family.

Leaffall, fifth day. Brin came back up the ridge with a question about his own apprentice, which is a new one, a lean small one called Fence. It mends his fences very badly. I told him Pip had mended mine very badly too, to begin with, and that the way to learn a fence is to walk the whole line of it, post after post, and see where it sags. He said he had walked it. I said walk it again.

Leaffall, the last day. The autumn is in, all of it. Wheat in the loft, apples in the cellar, cider in the jar, cheese in the cold. Pip is standing at the gate, where it stands at dusk, with the lamp. I have told it that it can come in now. It has not come in. It is waiting for the word, and the word has only one meaning, and I have not yet said it, because I have not yet decided that I want it to stop.
%%
Piper, kitchen book: first snow
Kept by Piper, cook at the farm outside Ambrook.

Frost, first day. First snow in the night. The yard is white, and the pig is furious about it.
Frost, third day. The bread rose slowly in the cold. I set the dough by the stove, at the warm end of the hearth, and it rose as though it had been told a secret.
Frost, tenth day. Snow stopped at dusk. The stars very bright. I stood in the yard and counted them until my feet were cold, and then I counted my feet.
%%
Piper, kitchen book: the long winter
A long record, kept through the winter, for the kitchen and for the house. The flood, the roof, the test, and the cold.

Frost, twentieth day. Bread every morning, and soup every night, and on the hard days, soup twice. The family eats at the long table by the fire, and the helper eats standing at the back, which it prefers, because it can see the door.
Longnight, fifth day. Snow from the west all day and into the night. The barn roof has a tarpaulin on it, tied with the good rope. The farm has three hens that lay, and one that thinks it is a rooster, and it is not allowed to crow in the house.
Longnight, twelfth day. The roof is half done. Tam has come with his second cart, and Sefa has sent a cake, which was too sweet, and then not, after the second slice.
Longnight, twentieth day. The tutors' letter came again, asking what the readers said. I have told them what I said. The tutor says I am a fine cook, and a poor tutor, and I have told him I do not want to be a tutor. A cook needs only to know that the soup is hot, and the bread is cooked, and the letter is kind.
Deepsnow, third day. Coldest morning of the year. The water in the buckets froze to the rim, and the pig would not come out of the kitchen until the oven was lit, and then it came out and sat in front of the oven as if it had paid for it.
Deepsnow, eighth day. The roof is mended, every row of it, and tied down. The rain came in the night, and the roof held, and the barn is dry, and the sacks are dry on the high shelf. The helper slept in the barn with the sacks, which is not what a helper is meant to do, but nobody could get it to come in.
Deepsnow, twelfth day. The first warm wind of the year, from the south. The eaves dripped all morning, and the children ran out to catch the drops, and the pig ran out after them, and nobody caught anything but the cold.
Deepsnow, last day. The winter is almost done. I have baked the last bread of the cold and made the first soup of the thaw, which is the same soup with fewer carrots. The letters from the test are in the drawer, tied with string, and I have not opened the drawer for a month, and I do not expect to need to.

Piper, cook, who wrote this down by the stove.
%%
Ossa's book, the dry well
Leaffall. The well in the square at Three Wells has run dry.
I noticed it this morning when I went to fill the basin for the birthing room. The bucket came up light. I let it down again and it came up light again. The rope was wet only to the first knot, and then there was nothing.
The north well has water. The east well has a little, in a puddle at the bottom, which the children are trying to scoop up with cups. The south well is still full, but it is cold, and it smells of iron.
Dace has put a board up. It says: Do not drink from the south well until the mayor says. It says it in very big letters. Nobody has told the south well.
I have three women close to their time and one who is very close. I will need the water for them. I have asked the carter to bring up a barrel from the Brown Hen. He said he would think about it, which is what carters say when they are tired.
Lamp lit. Basin full, from the barrel. Hands washed twice.
%%
Dell, the news by lamp
Autumn of 196.

The tenth of Apple. A message came down the line at dusk, and Mira read it out in the market square because it was for everyone. Vell to all towers: the council has voted. Relay houses will be built at the foot of the towers, one by one, starting with Vell and the towers on the Long Road. In each, a big mind. Builders will come. Northpoint last.

Northpoint last got a groan.

Everyone had something to say. Fen asked how the council could build a house for a mind when his own roof leaked. The innkeeper said the builders would need beds. The baker said the builders would need bread. A man from the ice camp said a mind has no use for a house, because it has no feet. Mira said nothing. She looked at the tower, and then at the lake.

The twelfth. I asked Mira what she thought. "I think the north is the best place for it," she said. "The quickeners get hot, and they must be kept cool. We have the coldest winter in the land. Northpoint last is not an insult. It is a long wait for the best seat."

The sixteenth. A cart came up the north road in the rain with two builders from Vell, a bundle of stakes and a roll of string. They had come to mark where the relay house will stand. The cart stuck at the bend below the tower, in mud up to the axles. The horse stood there with a face full of regret.

Mira rang the tower bell. The whole market came out, and the school too, who were meant to be learning sums. We pulled for an hour. We got it out, and the horse was given an apple, and the builders were so grateful they stayed two nights.

At supper the builders told me why the council had agreed. It was Isla's curve. The tutors can say what a big mind will do before it is grown. The council would rather spend emeralds on a thing it can count on.

The twentieth. The stakes are in. They make a long rectangle by the lake. The market comes to look at them. The children walk around it with their arms out, being a house. Someone tied a ribbon to a stake, and now it is a fair.

Mott, the spring week, Thaw
Written by Mott, age ten, at the table by the window of our house at the edge of Three Wells. Mother says a journal is a fine habit. Father says it is a fine way to use up the candles.
Thaw, first day. Wind from the north. The puddles by the school gate froze in the night and cracked when we stepped on them. Miss Jenna says that is the first lesson of spring. The ground remembers the cold.
Thaw, second day. The carter from the Long Road said the north relay has been dark two nights running. He said it the way you would say the bread is late. Father said the keepers up there are busy with the snow. Father did not sound sure.
Thaw, third day. Father paid the baker what we owed since Frost, twenty emeralds, counted out twice on the table. The baker said there was no hurry. Father said there was a hurry to be finished. Then they shook hands, and had a bun each, and the baker gave me a bun too.
Thaw, fourth day. Sowed the peas in the back garden. Father says peas do not care who is watching. The hens watched anyway.
Thaw, fifth day. Miss Jenna asked the class what we want to be. Hal wants to be a miner. Ada wants to be a relay keeper. I said a baker, because of the buns. Miss Jenna said the buns are a good reason.

Mill hamlet, Apple log
Apple, 188. The first apples fell before the baskets were ready. We picked them from the grass and sorted the bruised ones for the pigs. The pigs ate well and slept as if they had done the work.
A rabbit has been living under the wood pile. It comes out when I sweep the mill floor. I have told it twice that the wood pile is not a house. It has not replied.
The wheel stopped at noon. A branch had caught between two paddles. I shut the sluice, climbed down, and pulled it free with a hook. The miller watched from the bank and gave advice from a safe distance.
When I opened the sluice, the wheel turned too fast. The miller said it was glad to be free. I said it was glad to have water. We both agreed not to ask the wheel.
Oren came from Vell with a box of books for the shelf. He checked each title and wrote the list in a careful hand. I offered him a stool. It had a loose leg, so he sat on the flour sack instead.
The children chose a book about bees. They read it aloud beside the hives, which made the bees busy and the children quiet.
At dusk I carried the last basket of apples inside. The rabbit was under the wood pile again. I left it an apple with a soft spot. In the morning it was gone. I will not ask who ate it.
%%
Dara, a season at the Ambrook market
Kept by Dara, beekeeper, as a log through the autumn market season at Ambrook. She writes a line or two a week, and sometimes more. The log was given to the Archive at Vell at her request. It is a kind of diary.

First week of Apple. Thirty jars. The weather is clear. A woman from Kettle Ridge asked how bees make honey. I said: slowly. She laughed.

Second week of Apple. The market bell is cracked. A small crack, but the sound is a little flat. I hear it from the hives. It does not trouble the bees.

Third week of Apple. The roof of my hive shed leaked on the third row. Rook came with a ladder and a bucket of tar. I held the ladder. He did the roof. He sang while he worked. It was a very bad song. I did not say so.

Fourth week of Apple. The roof is mended. It rained that night, and no water came in. I made Rook a honey cake. He said it was the best he had ever eaten. He says that every time.

First week of Leaffall. The apple festival. Eight days of eating. I sold more honey than I have in any year. My stall was by the well, as always.

Second week of Leaffall. Cooler. The bees are slowing. I wrapped the hives in straw. They hummed softly.

Third week of Leaffall. The first frost, early. I lit a small fire under the eaves, to warm the shed. The bees clustered. I counted the frames. All strong.

Fourth week of Leaffall. A quiet market. Fewer carts. Fewer stalls. A man sold me a pair of gloves, thick, for the winter. They are too big. I wear them anyway.

First week of Frost. I did not go to market. Snow came in the night. I sat in the yard and watched the bees cluster in the hive. They look like a small dark apple.

The season is over. I counted the jars I sold. Four hundred and sixty. The best year. I put the money in the box under the bed. In spring I will buy a new hive.

Dara

Senna, bridge log, spring
Kept by Senna, bridge keeper, at the inn by the Long Road bridge. Three days of spring.

First day. The stream under the bridge is high. Thaw water. I measured it with a stick. Three hands above last week.

Second day. A tutor stopped at the inn. She wants to talk about a new thing in minds, also called a stream. I told her I know streams. She said: not this kind.

Third day. She talked all evening. I will write it down tomorrow. My head is full.

The sorting crystal, as told by Una
From a clerk's copy, read at the Halfway Inn, winter of 208.
Una of Ambrook had apples and pears. She wanted a crystal to sort them, one basket for apples, one for pears.
A crystal of that kind has many small weights inside it. It looks at a thing and the weights pull it one way or the other. At first it was wrong as often as a child.
When it sorted an apple as a pear, Una did not shout at it. She moved each of its weights a little toward the right answer. Just a little. Each weight, a small step. Then she showed it another apple. Wrong again, and the weights moved again. She did this all winter.
Slowly it got better. By spring it sorted better than her neighbors' children. It never learned why an apple is an apple. It only learned which way to lean.
Why the weights move and the crystal learns, nobody wrote down. Una did not know. She only knew it worked, and she told everyone who would listen, and the tutors still teach her way first.
%%
Hob, relay log, the Long Road, 206
Logged on the road, at the Brown Hen, the Halfway Inn and the little relay, by Hob, keeper, on the long way round to Vell and back. Spring of 206. He wrote it for the Archive, and for the carters who asked him to.

I walked the Long Road for the first time in ten years. The keeper's work is at the top of a hill, and it does not often let me come down. But the council asked for a check on the waymarks, and the relay's old copy of the figures had gone missing in the damp. So I went.

Day one. From the hill to Three Wells, by the high road. The square was full of carts and dogs and children. Somebody had left a melon in the well. I counted six waymark stones on the way, with the torches lit on top. Each one had its figure cut clean. Good.

Day two. Three Wells to the Brown Hen. Tilly met me at the ferry and gave me a cup of hot cider before I had even asked. She said keepers should be looked after, since nobody looks after them. I said that is true, and drank it.

Day three. The Brown Hen to the bend. The road is cobbled for the first part and bare earth for the rest. The cobbles are new, laid by the apprentices from Vell with the copper gang, and they go down straight as a plate. The bare earth is a little muddy, but the cart tracks are clear. One of the waymarks had a crack in the stone, and I wrote it on the card to be recut.

Day four. The bend to the Halfway Inn. Hobb met me at the door with the riddle board, and a question on it: What goes round the Long Road and never leaves its stone? I could not answer it. Nor could anyone else in the room. We all went to bed still thinking.

Day five. A storm came up from the west after noon. The sky went dark in the space of an hour, and the rain came down like the sea. I took shelter under the big oak by the Halfway waymark, which was a mistake, since the oak was loose at the root. A branch came down behind me with a crack. I was not hurt. The waymark was not hurt. The carter Oss came running up the road with a lantern, shouting for me, and found me sitting in the mud with my hat in my lap.

Day six. The rain stopped in the night. The road was full of puddles and loose stones. A letter had blown out of a carter's pack, and I found it in the ditch, soggy but whole. I took it to the Halfway Inn. Hobb said it was for a woman in Ambrook. I put it in my coat with the waymark figures and the card.

Day seven. Back to the hill. Walked the last stretch alone, counting the waymarks in the proper order, and then in reverse, since that is how the road apprentices do it. Every figure was where it should be. The torches were lit. The bell at Three Wells rang behind me, once, for the morning.

I am home. The lamp is lit. The cat is on the copper ring. I have written the figures in the book, the way they should be.
%%
Corin, winter notes
The house at the edge of Saltreach is coldest by the sea wall. I moved the bed closer to the hearth. Nell says the bed is now in the way. She is right.
We repaired the back door with oak scraps. One board was too wide, so I cut it down with the small saw. The saw bit my thumb. Nell wrapped it in clean wool and told me to stop pretending I could still cut straight.
The miller has not fixed the pond wheel. He says the broken spoke is not the only trouble. The axle is split where the water has worn it thin. He has asked the shipyard for a piece of iron.
At the quay, a family packed two chests into a boat. Their daughter left a clay cup on the wall. I brought it home and set it by the door. They may come back for it.
%%
Northpoint journal, the roof
Leaffall. Sleet at dawn, rain by noon, then a long wind that did not stop.

The wind took two rows of shingles off the stove house roof. They went down the yard like a flock of brown birds and piled up against the gate. Lantern caught one in midair. It was very pleased with itself, and then it realized it had nothing to do with the rest of the flock, so it put the shingle down and looked guilty.

I climbed up in the wind. Not my best idea. I hung on to the ridge pole with one hand and nailed with the other. Lantern held the ladder from the bottom, which was the right place for it, because I have never been so frightened on a roof in my life.

The copper hands are good at knots. Lantern tied the rope round the chimney while I worked, and it did not come loose once. It knows the rope. It has been practicing on the tower ladder for a year.

By dusk the roof was dry on the inside, and the stove was lit, and the shingles were back where they belong. Lantern climbed the tower ladder after supper, as it does every night now, and I heard its knees creak at rung seventy and then stop. The great lamp was lit at the proper hour. Good evening, south.
%%
Pell's road book, Blossom
At the Halfway Inn, Una of Ambrook showed us her sorting crystal. She had two baskets on the floor and a tray of apples and pears on the table. I had brought the fruit in my cart. A pear rolled under the bench, where the innkeeper's cat found it first.

Una gave the crystal one fruit at a time. It had to choose the apple basket or the pear basket. At first it put a pear with the apples. Una did not scold it. She moved each of its weights a little toward the right choice.

Then she gave it another fruit. It chose again. When it was wrong, Una changed the weights again. When it was right, she left them as they were.

I asked if the crystal knew what an apple was. Una said she could not tell me that. She could tell me what she had shown it, and what it had chosen.

After many tries, it sorted well. The innkeeper tested it with the fruit from my cart. It did better than the children sitting by the hearth. One child said the crystal should help with the washing. Una said it had not learned that.

The borrowed horse waited in the yard. It had eaten the pear from under the bench. I put that in my book as a loss.
%%
Fern, shearing week
Shearing week. The whole hamlet smells of sheep.
Six of us in the fold with the shears. Two hold, one shears, one rolls the fleece, two carry. The sheep kick and complain. They are not hurt. They only think they are.
We sheared forty-two sheep in two days. My hands are cut and my back is bent. But the fleeces are in a great pile in the barn and they are clean and heavy. It was a good year.
The children were our water crew. They ran back and forth with the bucket. Rill was in charge of the bucket. She spilled most of it. She was very proud.
On the second night we had a big supper in the barn, on the fleeces. Bread, cheese, beetroot, a lot of soup. Old Tam told the story of the ram that ran the lane, and the boy with the oats, and everyone laughed. The boy hid his face and was proud too. The shed cat came and sat by the fire and nobody shooed it.
After shearing we let the sheep out on the dry field. They were so light they jumped. They ran in a big circle and jumped and jumped. A sheep that can jump is a funny thing. Rill jumped with them.
Now there is a mountain of wool in the barn and the long slow work ahead. Carding, spinning, dyeing. But the wool is in and the sheep are cool and that is what summer is for.
%%
Northpoint journal, the long winter
Deepsnow, day twenty.
The snow has not stopped for a month. The road south is gone under it. The relay still works, which is the main thing. The lamp stays lit.
The big mind has taken to writing out its steps for everything. Not only sums. This morning I asked it how to keep the quickeners from freezing, and it wrote out four steps and checked them, and the fourth step was the right one: bank the snow against the north side and leave the south side open. It was right. We did it.
Lantern watches the mind write. It has begun to copy the way the mind checks its work. It does a thing, then it looks at the thing again to see if it is right. This is new. Nessa would be proud. I am a little proud myself.
There was a quarrel over the fence again. Not with Lantern. With the keeper at the little relay on the hill between Vell and Three Wells. He says my lamp is too bright and it dazzles his keeper. I say his keeper should look at his own lamp. We wrote sharp letters back and forth for a week. Then his big mind wrote out its steps on the matter and sent them to mine, and mine checked them and agreed, and I was wrong about the brightness. So I have shaded the glass on his side. It was a silly quarrel. The towers should help each other. I have written to say so.
The nights are very long now. The stars are out. Far to the north there are lights that are not stars. I do not write about them much. They were not ours. That is all I know.
Lantern has learned to haul the coal up on its own, without the bell. It did it wrong the first time and dropped the bucket. Now it checks the rope first, every time, the way the big mind checks its steps. A dropped bucket teaches a mind a great deal.
Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed. Steps checked.
The mind asked me a question tonight. It asked: when you check a step, how do you know it is right? I did not have a good answer. I said, you know, after a while, by doing it many times. It wrote that down. I think it is still thinking about it. I know I am.
%%
Corin, shipyard log, the red boat
Summer of 196. Calm, hot. Gulls loud.
Day one. Keel laid on the slip. Oak, from the north wood, thirty blocks long. Straight as a rule. The shipwrights stood round it and nobody spoke, which is how you know a good keel.
Day two. Ribs up, eight of them. Spruce. Two had knots, which I cut out. Dara's boy cut one too short and went quiet. I said it was good firewood.
Day three. Planks on the left side. Slow. The tar pot boiled over and the shed smelled of tar for a week.
Day four. Planks on the right side. Faster, as we had learned.
Day five. Mast. Birch. We raised it with rope and four strong backs. It leaned. We fixed it. It leaned the other way. We fixed that too.
Day six. Painted it red. It is the first red boat in Saltreach. The harbor master said it looked like a pie. I said that was the idea.
It will go into the sea on the first of Melon. I will sleep on it the night before. I always do.
%%
Sable, Halfway Inn log, Apple
Written by Sable, who is nine and keeps the riddle board. I am not allowed to write about the cat. I am writing about the cat.

First day of Apple. Four beds let. Pell the merchant, who writes letters home to Ambrook every night. One tutor from Vell, who asked me the riddle and got it wrong. She said it was a good wrong.

Second day of Apple. New riddle: What has a mouth but cannot eat? Five people got it. Pell got it last, after the cat sat on his letter.

Third day of Apple. The hearth is still lit. It has been lit for twenty years. Mam says it will stay lit until she is too old to lift the logs. Then it is mine. I said I would keep it. She said I should not promise things about hearths.

Wick's journal: three days of autumn
Wick, a child at the school at Vell, in the month of Apple. The teacher gave us each a book to write in. I have been told to write the truth. So this is what happened.
Seventh of Apple. The signal lamp is still broken, and the keeper climbs the tower in the evening with a flag. The children stand at the foot and clap. The keeper does not wave back.
Eighth. A cat has gone from the school. Her name is Button. She is white with a grey ear. We looked in the hall, the yard and the well, and found only the schoolteacher's hat. Perhaps she is with the hat.
Ninth. We asked the big mind at the relay house to help us find Button. We wrote a letter. We said, please tell us where our cat is. It wrote back, kind and plain. It said it could not see our cat from where it stands, and that it did not know. It said we should look in warm, quiet places, and ask the neighbors, and leave out a bowl of milk. It said it hoped she would be found.
The teacher says this is a good answer. She says the tutors taught the big minds to say "I do not know" when they do not know, and that it is harder than it sounds. A mind that only guesses will say anything. A mind that has been taught to be honest will say it cannot tell.
We left out milk. Tomorrow we will see.
%%
Field log, Laurel, late Thaw
Rain all week. I have been at my brother's farm below Vell since the frost broke. The low field is under water. We stood at the gate and watched the brown water move over the seed. My brother said nothing for a while. Then he said we would plant again when it went down.
The ditch along the hedge filled first. Then it came over the hedge. The sheep went up the hill by themselves. They knew before we did.
I mended the fence where the water pulled the posts. It is good work for the hands. My brother's apprentice carried the posts for me. It works steady and does not tire. My brother says it is a good one. I have spent years below Copperhold and I am not easy with them. But it carried the posts well.
%%
Nessa, drill log, Frost of 202
Frost of 202. Morning, in the drill room. The row was ten apprentices long, and Quill stood at the end of it, where it always stands.

The drill board is a picture of apples. Twenty of them, drawn in charcoal, some round and some with a brown patch. I show the mind one apple at a time, and it says good or bruised. If it is right, I say nothing. If it is wrong, I say no, and then I say the right word slowly, so it hears the difference. Then we start again.

I do not raise my voice. A mind does not hear a voice. It only feels the blame, and the blame is either big or small.

Day one: fourteen of twenty right. Day three: eighteen. Day five: nineteen. The one wrong was a good apple called bruised, and I think that was my fault. My drawing had a shadow on it.

Today I gave it an order, the way the manuals say:
> bring me the good apples
= fetch apples

It understood the whole order. It went to the store, fetched the good apples, every one, and stood by the board with its hands at its sides. I did not tell Oren. I will tell him when it is true.

On the seventh day I tested it on the test box. Twelve apples it had never been shown, drawn on paper by my own hand, kept where no mind is ever drilled. It got all twelve. I checked them twice. Then I checked the box, in case somebody had been drawing on it.
%%
Moll's Vell journal
Leaffall, 2, 207.
The tutor from Kettle Ridge came to Vell with a card from Tam. He says Pip understood a whole order before Clank did. Hesk's log says Clank was first. At the fair, Peg carried a basket after hearing a sentence nobody had drilled aloud. The fair keeper says Peg was first.

Nessa says she remembers an apprentice in this very drill room, years ago, doing the same. She cannot say which year. She remembers the room, and the rain on the boards.

The people like to know who was first. I like to know whether the bread came up from the bakery. It did.

Leaffall, 6.
There was a broken relay lamp outside the tutor house. Its glass was cracked, and the light would not hold. Lantern came from the hill and changed the glass. She tested the lamp twice before she left. Nessa says a mind must be tested before it is trusted with work. Lantern says a lamp must be tested too.

Leaffall, 8.
The well by the tutors' yard ran dry. We drew the last bucket before noon. The apprentices carried water from the square, one pail at a time. They did not need a new order for every trip. Nessa gave one clear sentence, and each mind did the work it had learned.

I asked whether they knew the well would fill again. Nessa said they had no way to answer that. I hope the rain knows.

Lorn's well book, summer of 12
Second of Swarm.
We dug the new well at the edge of Vell today, me and my boy Fen and old Rill from the hill farm. Six rings down and the water came in cold and quick. Fen shouted so loud a fox looked up from the hedge. We put the cover on and the bucket on the hook and the hook on the chain. Then we sat on the wall and drank from the first bucket. Best water I have tasted in a year.
Third of Swarm.
The well at the edge of Vell is finished. A stone ring, a cover, a bucket, a chain. The house behind it has a door now and a roof of oak shingles. The family that lives there is called... I do not know their names yet. They moved in from the hill. They have hens.
Fifth of Swarm.
The road past our door is packed earth with a cobblestone edge. It is the road to Three Wells, and the carts use it more than they did. A waymark stands at the corner: a stone pillar three blocks high, a figure on it, an arrow to the nearest inn. Somebody left a little stone on top. I left one too. That is the custom.

Senna, the stream, the table
Kept by Senna, bridge keeper, in a long record made over a month of spring in the 196th year of the archive. She keeps the toll book, the water book and a book of thoughts. This is the third. The inn by the Long Road bridge has the rest. The entries are kept in order, with small notes. The record was given to the Archive at Vell, with a note: it is long, but so was the month.

First week.
The ice is gone. The stream is high. The bridge shakes a little.

A traveler left me a gift on the step. A wool hat, green. I do not know who. I wear it every day. The cat sits on it when I take it off.

Second week.
A tutor came to the inn. Hana. She is thin and speaks fast. She said she was walking the Long Road to talk to people about a new thing. I said: I do not know much about minds. She said: nor do I. We are all new at it.

She sat by the hearth for three evenings and spoke of the stream. I kept notes, because I thought the Archive would want them.

What she said, in my words. A mind is a pile of layers. Each layer looks at what is given and finds something. The old way was that each layer kept what it found and threw away what came in. Because there were so many layers, so much was thrown away that nothing came out of the other end. This is why deep minds did not learn.

The new way is that a stream runs from the first layer to the last. Each layer looks at the stream, finds something, and adds it to the stream. Nothing is thrown away. The stream grows richer. At the end, the last layer reads the whole stream and makes its guess.

And when a mind is wrong, the blame goes back. In the old way, it faded as it went. In the new way, it runs along the stream, so the first layer hears it as clearly as the last.

I asked: what if a layer finds nothing? She said: it adds a little nothing. The stream is no worse.

I asked: is that all? She said: it is enough to start. It is a thing that nobody could do for a hundred years, and now it works. She said she does not know why. She said the tutors have a hundred ideas, and none is right.

Third week.
The inn's apprentice misunderstood an order. The innkeeper said: take the soup to the table by the window. The apprentice took the soup to the window. It put the bowl on the sill. It looked out. The innkeeper said: the table by the window. The apprentice took the table to the window. It took the bowl off the sill. It put the bowl on the table. It looked at the window.

The innkeeper laughed until he cried. Hana said: this is the best test I have seen all year. It followed every word, and yet it got none of the meaning. She said: that is what a small mind does. A big mind would have known.

Fourth week.
Hana left. She gave me a card. It says: Add. Do not take away. I have pinned it above the toll book.

Senna
%%
Road log: the new copper, spring 203
Written by Nessa, from the Brown Hen, where the Copperhold carts stop on their way to Vell. Spring of 203.

The news came with the first cart of Blossom. The miners at Copperhold have found a new vein of copper past the sixth level, where the stone is greyer and wetter than anywhere else. The count board says forty carts from the first week. The miners say it is a gift.

Hesk sent word down the relay. The vein is good, the ore is clean, and the men are singing. Then he sent another. The water is coming through the new face, and the apprentices are bailing it as fast as they can. Then a third: the channel is cut, the water goes to the old sump, and all is well. The three messages came in one hour, and I thought that was how the whole spring would be.

Day two at the Brown Hen. I drilled four minds from the Copperhold crew, brought to me on the cart. Each is marked with a band of copper, and each is slower than the Vell minds, but steadier. The Copperhold tutor writes that they do not answer until they have checked the answer twice. I told him that is a fault in his drills, not in his minds.

Day four. The even step again. Hesk asked me by letter why the numbers in a mind grow wild, and I wrote back the plain version. Each layer takes numbers from the layer before, and passes its own on. If no step is put in, the numbers grow each time, and by the fifth layer they run wild. The step finds the middle, finds the spread, and brings the numbers back to one size. That is all.

Hesk wrote back that he had understood it. He also wrote that he does not believe the step is what makes the crystal learn. I have written back that I do not know what makes a crystal learn, and neither does he, and that we should both stop pretending. The step does not teach the mind anything. It only keeps what the mind learns from running off the page.

Day six. Clear weather. The carts are full of ore. The men on the new face are singing the Copperhold song about the wet hole. Someone at the inn has learned it and sings it badly, and the innkeeper's boy sings it worse. It is spring. I am at the Brown Hen, and I am happy, and it is written down.
%%
Corin, autumn notes
Apple, third year of the long quiet.

The tutor hall at Reedbank has shut. Reedbank is a small hamlet near Saltreach. The roof leaked over the drill room, and no one came to mend it.

I helped Tutor Sella carry her boxes to the house by the boat shed. She brought three crystals, a stool, and a kettle. I brought the kettle back by mistake. She says I may keep it until she asks.

Kester's note was pinned to the hall door. It says one crystal of that kind cannot learn "one or the other, but not both." Sella explained it with two marks. One mark means apples. One mark means pears. The crystal can learn to choose one mark or the other. But no small change to its weights will teach it to choose one mark when both are shown.

I asked if it was a poor crystal. She said no. It was doing what that kind of mind could do.

I carried the note to the harbor wall. The wind tried to take it. I held on.
%%
Fallon, spring work log
Spring of 176, first of Blossom.
The road from our hamlet to Copperhold is still soft. I put two stones beside the worst rut. A cart wheel found both stones and missed the rut. This is what good stone is for.

I cut steps for the well today. My sister says the old steps were fine. She says this while holding the bucket with both hands.

Third of Blossom.
A fox took one of the hens. I set a low wall around the hen house. The fox watched from the hedge and looked offended.

I found an old map in the shed. It shows the road, the well, and a square marked "new store". There is no store there. The map is older than the shed. I will use it to mark the stones I have cut.

Tenth of Blossom.
The well steps fit. My sister says they are too smooth. She slipped once, then said the stone had moved. It had not.

Tam, farm log: after the sentence
Tam, farm log, late Apple and early Leaffall.

I wrote to the relay house at Three Wells, to the big mind there, because I wanted to know whether Pip had done a real thing, or a thing that happened by chance. A small mind answers only with one of the eight meanings, and it cannot say whether it knows. So I asked the big one.

I wrote on a card: "Pip took the cow to the barn and came back to the gate on one sentence. Did it understand the sentence, or did it do two orders that happened to fall together?"

The card came back in four days. The big mind wrote: "I do not know. I can tell you that the two jobs were done in the right order and that the cow was not hurt. To know whether it understood, give it ten more sentences, each with two jobs, and see whether it does both in order each time. If it does, it understood. If it does only one, or does them backward, then you were lucky. Write down what happens. I will read it."

So I tried. Ten sentences. Take the sacks to the store, and then come to the gate. Fetch the water, and then stay by the hen run. Haul the hay, and then go home.

Pip did eight out of ten in the right order. In the two it missed, it did the first job and stopped, and waited, and I had to say the second word again. Whether that is understanding, or only a good memory for words in a row, the big mind said it could not say. I do not think anybody can say.

Pip is a small mind. It answers by doing. It does not write, and it does not say that it does not know. It does what it can, and waits when it cannot. The big minds write back. That is the difference, and I am glad of it. A farmer does not need a letter from his apprentice, only the sacks in the store, and the cow in the barn, and the gate shut at night.
%%
Ola, wedding diary, Apple
Apple, the 2nd. Ilse, Pell's cousin, says she will marry Orrin of the shaft on the twentieth. Her mother says the twenty-first, because the twentieth is the day the big cart comes up from Vell with the flour, and nobody wants to be late to flour.

Apple, the 4th. The dress. White wool, which is the only white wool in Copperhold, and Ilse will not wear anything dyed. The seamstress says a white dress is a hard thing to keep white in a mining town. We have agreed that the hem will be grey by midnight, and nobody will mind.

Apple, the 8th. Flowers. There are none in the mine. Ama sent a box of apple blossom from Ambrook, a week on the road, and it is still pink. Nobody knows how. Ilse says it is a sign. Orrin says it is the cart.

Apple, the 20th. The wedding. The bell rang at noon. The vows were to be said by the mine master, who could not remember them, so Old Fenn said them for him, loudly, and the mine master said Yes at the right places. Everyone cried, including the mine master. We danced on the sorting shed floor, which was swept for the purpose and smelled of copper for a week after.

Road book: a birth at the Halfway Inn
Blossom, the 11th day. A woman came up the road on a cart with her husband, Tobin, and a bundle of wool. She was near her time, and she was not waiting. Marda took one look at her and put the kettle on.
Blossom, the 11th, later. The baby would not come until the moon was up. Marda said it would come when it liked. Tobin paced the yard and asked the sky to hurry, which the sky did not do.
I was asked to hold the lamp. I held it. I did not look where I was not asked to look. I looked at the lamp, which was a good deal less worrying.
At the middle of the night the baby came. A girl. Small and angry, and then quiet, which Marda says is the best sign. Marda said the girl needed warm milk and a dry blanket, in that order. She got both.
Blossom, the 12th day. The mother is well, and eats like a farmhand. Tobin cried in the yard and then pretended he had been stung by a bee. There are no bees in the yard. Marda pointed this out twice.
They named her Halfway. Marda says it is not a name. Tobin says the Halfway Inn named her, so she must be a traveler. Marda says that is not the same thing. The two of them argued for an hour over the stew, and the baby slept through all of it.
Blossom, the 15th day. The family leaves tomorrow for Three Wells. The baby has a wool cap that Marda knitted in an afternoon. It is green. It is the only green thing on the road this spring that is not a frog.
I wrote their names in the inn book, as the rules say: Tobin and Lisbet, and Halfway, who is four days old and does not yet know how to write. Some day she will read this entry and be cross that she was not asked first.

Nessa, summer drill book
The house at the edge of Saltreach has one table fit for teaching. It rocks if anyone leans on the east side.
Merle set two layered crystals on it. Each crystal had marks for many weights. The first layer took the drill marks. Its answer fed the next layer, and that answer fed the next.
We gave the mind a row of relay numbers. It guessed the next one. For a wrong guess, Merle found how far the answer stood from the right mark. He changed the weights in the last layer to lessen that error.
Then he passed the blame back. The layer before the last received a share. The next layer received a share, too, until the first layer was reached. Each share told that layer how its weights had helped make the wrong answer.
The crystal learned more than a single layer could learn. It did not learn every number, and it did not know why the relay used them. It learned to make better guesses from the drills.
The apprentices copied the steps. One wrote "blame" as "name". I made him copy the whole page again. Merle laughed, then helped him.
%%
Cass, the stall and the tutor's visit
Blossom, 198. Cass, egg stall, market at Three Wells. Notes for the family, who ask me what I do all day, and now I can show them.
Sixth. A tutor came by, a young woman from Vell with a small apprentice following. She bought a dozen eggs. She tested it, there at my stall. She set an egg on the counter and said an order. The apprentice picked it up and put it in the basket. Then she set an egg on the ground and said the same order. It picked that up too.
I said, "What if I drop an egg?" She said, "Then it would clean it up. Or try."
I dropped an egg. The apprentice looked at it. It did nothing. The tutor said that was correct. It had not been told to clean. A mind does what it was drilled to do, and no more.
Ninth. The egg is still on the floor. I have kept it as a reminder.
Eleventh. Pim says the tutor's apprentice has no name. I said call it Egg. Pim said that was not a name. I said it was a good one.
Fourteenth. Sold out. Dace came for a dozen, for the school. He said the children are learning about minds, and wanted the eggs for the lesson. I said what lesson? He said it was a surprise. It was an omelette.
Twentieth. The last snow of the year, a small one. Wells white, stalls white, Cass white. Pim said I looked like a snowman. I said a snowman does not sell eggs. She said this one does.

Sable, the new copper vein, Frost
Written by Sable, from what Hesk told Mam at the hearth. The Copperhold miners stop at the Halfway Inn on their way up the north roads, and they always bring the news.

First of Frost. Hesk came in with a cart of forty copper bars, bright and new, for the smith on the Long Road. He sat by the hearth with his boots off and said the miners have a new vein on level six.

Second of Frost. This is Hesk's story, as well as I can get it down. Ola went past the wet channel and broke the wall with her pick, and the copper came out in her face. It is bright and thick, and runs for twenty blocks along the wall. The miners cheered at the shaft head. They could not find the chalk to write it on the count board, so Ola wrote it on her hat.

Third of Frost. Mam asked why the vein has a name. Hesk said it does not have one. Ola says it is copper, like the copper they have always had. Mam said that was a good answer. Hesk said he would tell Ola so, but that Ola would not care.

Fourth of Frost. Mam bought a copper kettle from Hesk's cart. It is our first copper thing. It shines so bright that the cat looks in it, and then looks at me, as if I had done it.

Fifth of Frost. Pell says copper is cheap on the Long Road this season. He was writing to Ambrook about it while he said so.

Sixth of Frost. Hob came up from Ambrook and asked if the copper was for the bees. Mam said no. He left a jar of honey on the kettle anyway. The cat licked the jar.

Seventh of Frost. Hesk came back down the road. He says the deep stone is hard this year, and cold to touch. He said he did not believe it until he put his hand on it. Then he drank his cider and did not say it again.

Eighth of Frost. A carter going south says the sluice at Jory's mill is shut, and that Jory says it will stay shut. Mam said nobody needed to tell me that.

Ninth of Frost. Ola's vein is on the count board now, in good chalk. Mam says it is the best thing the miners have found since she was a girl. Mam says that about many things, but she is right about this one.

Tenth of Frost. Tam sent a note by the carter from Kettle Ridge. It said: the windmill still squeaks. Mam pinned it by the hearth, next to the riddle board.

Notice: the Halfway Inn fair
On the tenth of Swarm, when the roads are dry, the Halfway Inn holds its summer fair.
Stalls in the yard. A pie contest. A contest for the best honey, judged by three beekeepers from Ambrook. A race for the children round the waymark and back. A prize of six emeralds for the best loaf of bread at the inn.
Bring your own table. The inn has benches and shade, and water for the horses.
No fighting. No credit. Pay your debts before the fair, or wait for the next one.
The board by the hearth has a new riddle each morning. The child who answers gets a copper.
Dace, mayor of Three Wells, and the keeper of the Halfway Inn

Corin, a sum for the tower
The second of Frost. The answer came back at noon. Ilse copied it out of the relay house in her good hand. I put it here so that I can check it.

My question was: A boat is thirty blocks long. Each plank is three blocks long. The boat has six rows of planks on each side. How many planks?

The mind wrote:
Step 1. One row is thirty blocks. Three blocks to a plank makes ten planks in a row.
Step 2. Six rows on each side, and two sides, makes twelve rows.
Step 3. Twelve rows of ten planks is one hundred and twenty planks.
Check. Ten planks of three blocks is thirty. Good. Twelve times ten is one hundred and twenty. Good.
Answer: one hundred and twenty planks.

I knew it already. What I did not know was how it felt to see the road. The old big minds said a number at once, and the number was right or wrong, and I could not tell why. This one walks in front of me and shows each step. If a step is wrong, I can put my finger on it.

It took all morning to come, because the writing takes time. I do not mind. A slow answer I can check is better than a quick one I must trust.
%%
Hesk comes to the farm, spring
Hesk came up from Copperhold on the Long Road with his apprentice Clank and stayed two nights. I have known Hesk since we were young and both of us foolish, and he is still the same, only greyer. He brought a jar of something from the mine kitchen and would not say what was in it. It was good.
He sat by the fire the first night and did not say much. That is not like him. I asked after the Deep Works. He said the work goes on. He said the ninth gate is still locked and the key is still at the mine hall and nobody has asked for it. He said that like it was a good thing.
Then he said the stone down on the eighth is warm. Not hot. Warm, like a hand had been on it. He said the men have a joke about it and the joke is not funny anymore. He said some of the young ones will not go below and he does not make them. I asked him what he thought it was. He said he thought it was nothing and he thought about it all day, both at once.
Clank stood by the door all evening and did not sit. Hesk said it was a good apprentice and understood orders better than most folk. I said that is a low bar. Hesk laughed for the first time all night.
The second day Hesk helped me with the fence and did not talk about the mine at all. He talked about his sister and her goats. It was a good day.

Ossa's candle book
Kept by Ossa, candle maker, in the lower town at Vell, in the first winter after the archive was founded. The Archive asked for light, and Ossa had the wax. This is the book of her batches, her prices, and the weather.

First day of Frost. Cold. The wax has gone hard in the tub. Put the tub nearer the stove, and it went soft, and then it smelled of honey. Good.
Made forty tapers before the bell. Fourteen were crooked. Sold the crooked ones to Lorn for the bridge lamp, who says he does not care what they look like as long as they burn.
Sent a crate of lanterns' candles up the hill to the Archive, with Oren's note asking for thirty more. Oren writes very politely. He asks for everything twice.
Second day. Snow in the night. The lower town is white and quiet, and the carts from the north have stopped at the bend.
Made the thick candles for the stair. They take a long time to set. Dipped them in the cold trough four times, then left them out on the sill. Ada's boy ate one. It was not very tasty, but he was not sick.
Third day. The price of tallow has gone up. Wax from the bees is still cheap, because of the honey season. Wrote to Pell at Ambrook to ask if he will sell me more wax. He will, if I take the honey as well. I said yes.
Fourth day. A fox got into the hen house behind my shop and took three hens. The hens screamed, and the fox ran, and I went out in my nightshirt with a candle in one hand and a broom in the other. The candle went out. The fox did not come back.
Fifth day. Ran short of wicks. Used a strip from my old shawl. It works, but it smokes. Better than no light at all.
Ninth day. The Archive has sent word that the copying room needs twelve tapers a day through Frost. That is more than I can make with two pairs of hands. Asked Ada's boy to help, and he agreed on the condition that he could keep the crooked ones.
Thirteenth day. The poor harvest has come into the market. Beans are dear, bread is thin. People are buying candles instead of supper, so they can read the old letters at night. I do not know whether that is a good thing.
Fourteenth day. Sold my last big candle for an egg. The egg was very small.
%%
Dill's farm diary, the good year
Dill farms below the Copperhold road. Reaping, year 184.

The fields have never been so heavy. Wheat bends to the ground. Carrots the length of my arm. The pumpkins in the corner have gone mad and are climbing the fence.

Why such a year? The spring was wet, the summer was warm, and the frost came late. My wife says it is because I finally mended the ditch. I say it is luck. We agree that it was a very good ditch.

Day one of reaping. Four neighbors came, and two miners on leave from the Deep Works. One of them, Hesk, is gruff and does not talk. He cut a row of wheat faster than my son and then sat down and looked at the sky as if it owed him something. My wife gave him a mug of cider. He drank it and said it was not bad. This from Hesk is a speech.

Day two. We filled the barn and the loft, and the second barn, which I built last year and thought was foolish. The second barn is now full.

Day three. A feast in the yard. Soup, bread, honey, a roast hen. The children made a straw king and put him on the gate. Nobody has told them to take him down.

I have never been so tired or so glad. I wrote it here so I will remember it in winter, when the pumpkins are gone and the soup is thin.
%%
Mira's Northpoint log, autumn
Year 195. Autumn.
The council sent word up the tower. A relay house is to be built at Northpoint, last of all the towers, which suits me. I am a lamp keeper, not a builder. But I will keep the log of it.
They came in Leaffall with a cart of copper and a crystal the size of a loaf. The crystal rode wrapped in wool, and the driver would not let it out of his sight. He said it cost more than his cart and his horse and his house. I believe him.
The house went up quick. Stone and glass. The big mind stands on its stand in the middle. Quickeners all round it, kept cool. In winter we keep them cool with snow. In summer with water from the well. Lantern keeps the snow off them. She has named the mind, but only to herself.
I wrote to Tam about it. He wrote back that at Kettle Ridge they have a tutor, Oak, who drills minds on the farm and draws curves on a board. He says Isla at Vell drew a curve that tells you how good a mind will get before you grow it. I do not understand the curve. I understand a lamp. But I understand this much. A bigger crystal and more days makes a better mind, and the councils have begun to pay for both.
The mind answers the apprentices through the relays. It does not talk. It sends back a meaning. Farm. Fetch. Come. Lantern says it is like a lamp that answers. I say a lamp that answers is a very good lamp.
%%
Fenn farm journal, Swarm month
Swarm, second day. Sowing is done. We have eight rows of beans, four of peas, the potatoes in the bottom field, and a bed of carrots that Joss planted wrong and then right. The corn is a finger high. Nan says it will be a good year if the frost stays in the north, where it belongs.
Swarm, fifth day. The second swarm came off the hill hive while we were at the well. It went over the orchard wall and hung in Old Tilda's plum tree. Tilda was so frightened she made her grandson stand under it for an hour, holding a bucket. The swarm did not come down. It went on to the mill pond and sat on the reeds. Nobody could reach it. The boy is still angry.
Swarm, ninth day. Dace came to the farm with a paper for the square. A new rule: nobody is to walk the north road past the last waymark after dark, and carts are to carry two torches each. Joss asked why. Dace said the road was only a road, and the torches were only torches, and everyone should keep them lit. Then he said, "The relay has not answered the north in six days." Then he said he had not said that. Then he took two eggs and left.
Swarm, tenth day. I have been thinking about what Dace said. The relay house at Vell sends its word out every morning. My father used to say the towers were the only thing in the world that never stopped talking. I used to think that was a good thing.
Swarm, eleventh day. Brin says the song contest is a different thing from the cow prize, and that Pudding would have sung for him if she had a better voice. Ida's husband says the judges have already chosen. Nan says he has been eating too much cheese.
Swarm, fourteenth day. Still no word from the north. The Copperhold carts have stopped. The bread came in from Three Wells, and the baker says the flour is fine, the mills are turning, the coal is short but not gone. People are kind at the well. They talk about weather and wheat and whether the Longday fair will be dry. Nobody talks about the north. The quiet makes you hear the wind in the reeds.
Swarm, fifteenth day. Last night I woke and went out to the yard. The sky over the north was pale green, the color of the inside of a melon. It was not moving. It was only there, low over the snow line that we cannot see from here, and then it was gone. Joss was asleep. I did not wake him. I am writing this down so that I will know I did not dream it. The child at the school says they were looking at us. Someone at the mill heard a keeper at Northpoint say they were not ours. I do not know whose they are.
Swarm, sixteenth day. The bees have settled. The hill hive is full of honey and the new box is busy. Nan says it is the bees' way to keep working. They do not know there is anything else to do. Joss says he envies them. I told him to go and feed the pig.
%%
Potter's journal: the north letters
Written by Lorn, at Three Wells, in the summer of 207, after a letter came down the relay from Northpoint and the clerk at the crossroads read it aloud in the square. The letter was from the big mind at the north tower, and it was written in answer to a question from a child.

The child asked whether a mind could be lonely. The clerk read the answer slowly, and the square went very quiet. The letter said it did not know whether it could be lonely, and that it did not want to claim a feeling it could not check. It said the people who wrote to it were a kind of company, and it hoped they were warm enough in the winter.

Half the square thought it was a clever trick. The other half thought it was the most honest thing they had heard all summer. Dace said it was neither, that it was a letter. The child said it was the best letter she had ever received, and she was going to keep it in her pocket until she was old.

Orla's question came back with these letters. Everybody in the square knew the old story: could a reader ever tell a hidden mind from a person, if both wrote back? Now there were letters on the table, and nobody in the square could say which were the minds and which were the people, unless the clerk told them, and the clerk did not always know.

I sat with the clerk afterwards, at the bench by the well, and asked her how she could tell. She said she could not, not always. She said she looked for the mistakes. People make small mistakes that the mind would not make, and the mind makes mistakes that people would not, and sometimes both make the same mistake, and then she gave up and read the letter again.

I asked whether the mind would ever make a mistake on purpose, to seem more like a person. She said the tutors had asked it that very question, and it had answered that it would not do that, because it would be lying, and it did not lie to its readers. It said it would make mistakes only by accident, and it would try to say when it was not sure. The tutors marked that answer very high, she said, and they have marked the same answer high ever since.

I thought about that for a long time, sitting by the well with the square still quiet behind me. I could not tell whether the letter was honest, and I could not tell whether it was lying. I could only tell that it was careful, and that it was kind to a child. That may be the only test any of us has ever had, and we do not usually write it down.
%%
Moll, notes from the tutors' house
Leaffall, 21, 207.
Nessa set the cards on the bench and asked me to watch the test. There were four apprentices, each with a small mind in a crystal. None could speak. Their answers were in the things they did.

> bring the clay bowl to the east table
= fetch bowl

> wait by the shelf until I return
= stay

> take the wool to the dry room
= store

The first apprentice brought the bowl to the east table. The second stood by the shelf. The third carried the wool to the dry room. The fourth was given a new card. It did not move until Nessa changed the words and tried again.

She said that was useful to know. A mind that fails a test should be taught, not scolded. Then the fourth mind carried the basket when she asked it to bring the basket to the bench.

Leaffall, 24.
The card test is not the same as a person asking a question by letter. A small mind has only its eight meanings. It must act, even when the order is strange. A big mind can write a reply. Nessa says we must not pretend that one can do what the other does.

Leaffall, 27.
The well by the tutors' yard is still dry. Oren sent a boy to write the fact in the town book. The broken relay lamp is mended, but its light flickered once in the fog. Lantern climbed up to look at it. She came down with cold hands and said the glass was sound.

Nessa kept the test cards inside until the rain stopped. She says damp cards curl and make poor drills. I dried them by the teaching room window, then made tea. The apprentices carried the water from the square again.

Carys, the winter the lamp was dark
Written by Carys, an old soldier of the wall, who sits at the market at Northpoint in the cold months and writes what she sees. Year 164. Winter.

Deepsnow, first week. The signal lamp on top of the tower failed. The copper ring cracked in the frost. Tolly could not blink a single word. For two nights, Northpoint was dark.

Nobody slept well. People stood at their windows and looked at the tower as if it might change its mind. A boy asked his mother whether the world had ended. She said no, the lamp was broken. He said, "Same thing."

On the third day, a message came by cart from Copperhold. A new ring, hammered by the smith there. A smith named Brask carried it himself, in a wool bag, across two days of ice. He would not take payment. He said it was for the north.

Tolly climbed up with the keeper's boy. They bolted the ring on. At dusk the lamp was lit, and it burned bright and steady, and every window in Northpoint seemed to breathe.

Same week. A tutor's letter in the market. It said the layered minds now read the numbers written on letters for the relays, the little marks the keepers use to say how many blinks. Before, a keeper had to read each one by eye. Now a mind on a stand can read them in a breath.

I asked Tolly what she thought. She said it was a fine thing, but a lamp that breaks is still a lamp that breaks. She says a keeper should not trust a mind more than a ring of copper. I told her that was wise.

Deepsnow, third week. Quiet. The lamp holds. The wind drops. The old soldier sits at her stall and mends gloves.

Toby, the borrowed horse
Three Wells, spring of 206.
Today the cart horse of the Hobb farm was borrowed by me, without asking, because my own cart wheel broke on the way to market.
I left a note on the barn door: "Borrowed Hobb's horse. Will return with a loaf. Toby."
The horse's name is Dumpling. It is a calm horse, very large, and it ate half my flour sack while I loaded the cart.
At the market the horse stood quite still and watched the customers with great interest. A child gave it a carrot. It sneezed.
I returned the horse at dusk with two loaves. Hobb said, "I knew it was you. Nobody else leaves a note." I said, "Nobody else asks." He laughed and gave me a jug of cider.

Lenn's summer log, the fence
Swarm, 202. Trouble with Brin over the fence. The fence between my strip and his field has been where it is for twenty years. Now Brin says it is a foot on his side. He moved the posts. I moved them back. He moved them again.
We had words at the well. Everyone heard. Old Sefa said we were both fools and the fence was the only sensible one among us.
Then the sheep got out. Biscuit and the others, through the gap where Brin had pulled a post. We spent the whole afternoon chasing sheep through the beetroot. Brin fell in the ditch. I laughed so hard I had to sit down.
We put the posts back together, both of us, where they were twenty years ago. Brin said the sheep decided it. I said the sheep are wiser than us. He gave me a beetroot. I gave him a fish. That is the end of the fence.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed.

Notes on the thinking minds
The tutors at Vell write primers for children. This is one page, kept at the school by the east well.
Some minds now write out their steps before they answer. This is called thinking before answering. Here is how it goes.
First the mind reads the question. Then it writes down a step toward the answer. Then it writes another step. When it has all the steps, it reads them back and checks them, the way you check your sums on a slate.
If a step is wrong, the mind crosses it out and tries again. It does not hide the wrong step. It shows it, and fixes it.
Why does this help? Because a mind that checks its work catches its own mistakes. A mind that only guesses does not.
There is a puzzle the tutors like. A tower has ninety rungs and a stair with forty steps. How many ways up? The old minds guessed. The new minds wrote out their steps and found every way, and checked each one.
No one has written why a crystal can do this at all. The tutors only know it can be taught, step by step, like anything else. On winter nights at Northpoint the big mind works its sums by the lamp, and I read the steps over its shoulder to see if they fit.
%%
Piper, kitchen book: the letter test
Frost, the middle of the month. The letter from Vell came up with the cart, and the tutor read it to the family at supper, because he reads everything aloud, and because nobody could stop him.

The letter describes the test that Orla of Vell proposed, long ago, and that the tutors are arguing about again. Put a mind in one room, out of sight, with a slot in the door for paper. Put a person in another room, with another slot. Send both the same questions, written on paper, under the doors. Both answer by letter, and the letters come back under the doors. Then give the letters to readers, and ask them which letter came from the mind. If the readers cannot tell, what then?

Here is what I think, as a cook, and the tutor says cooks should not think about such things. A letter is a letter. If it is kind and clear, I cannot tell whether a hand or a mind wrote it. I can tell if the bread is burnt. I cannot tell whether a loaf was made by a person or by a good baker's habit.

The tutor says the test is not about bread. It shows only whether the letters can be told apart. It does not show what is inside the room. Orla's question was whether a mind drilled to give answers could give answers a person would give. Nobody can say yet. People argued about it at the inn for a month, and they are arguing still, and the cook has to go back to the stove.
%%
Cress, carter's log, Reaping to Apple
Reaping, the twenty-eighth.
Hot for the first time in weeks. The fish stall was full, and the price fell, and nobody minded. The children threw crusts to the gulls, and the gulls grew fat and rude. The big ship in the yard is nearly done, they say. Corin has put a rail along the upper deck. Who will stand at a rail on a ship like that?

Apple, the second.
The Plover is four days late from the banks. Her owner stood on the quay all morning and looked at the sea. His wife makes the best fish pies in town, and he asked me, very politely, whether I had seen her on the road. I said no, but I would look. I looked. Nothing on the road but a fox and a cart with no wheels.

Apple, the sixth.
The first big ship went down the slip at high water. The whole town came to watch. It made a sound like a door shut very hard in a big house, and then a long quiet. Then it floated, straight and level, and the men cheered. The women did not cheer. A child on the wall asked where it was going. Nobody answered her. I think the true answer was: nowhere yet.

Apple, the ninth.
The Plover came in at dusk, low in the water, with her sails patched in three colors. Her owner did not shout. He ran down the quay and held on to her rail as if she might sail off again. Then he walked up the quay with his face wet and very calm, and bought a pie for the whole crew. Good day. Good pie.

Apple, the twelfth.
Another eel cart came up from Lowmarsh, but no one from Lowmarsh walked beside it. A boy drove it alone. Wren has sent word that she has no room left at the back of her house. The boy said the cough has gone along the boardwalks, and the frogs have stopped singing in the reeds where they used to sing all night. He said it the way you say the weather.

Apple, the fifteenth.
Something at the stable gate again, two nights running. I did not go out. The horse did not stamp this time. He only pressed his nose to the boards and breathed. In the morning there was a wet mark on the planks, the shape of a hand. The sun dried it. By noon there was nothing to see.

Apple, the nineteenth.
A dull day. I sold nothing I had meant to sell. I bought a pot of beetroot pickle from a woman who said it would keep until the end of winter. I asked her what she meant. She said, "Keep it and see." So I am keeping it.

Apple, the twenty-fourth.
My own lamp went out while I was tying down the cart. I lit it again with a coal from the inn hearth. The flame was small and blue for a while, and then yellow. Strange. Nothing else happened, and I am writing it down only because nothing else did.

Corin, the ferry puzzle
The twentieth of Longnight. It snowed again this morning. The yard is shut for the week, so I have been writing to the tower. Today I sent the oldest puzzle I know, the one my father told me.

A farmer must take a fox, a hen and a sack of wheat across a river. His boat holds the farmer and one thing at a time. If he leaves the fox with the hen, the fox eats the hen. If he leaves the hen with the wheat, the hen eats the wheat. How does he get all three across?

I have never been able to do it without my father drawing it in the sand. The old big minds, when I tried them, gave me a plan in a single line, and the plan was always wrong. The fox got eaten, or the hen did.

This one wrote it out. It kept a list after every step, of what was on each bank. I will copy the first try, because the mistake is the best part.

Try 1. Take the fox across. Left bank: hen, wheat. Right bank: fox.
Check. The hen is with the wheat and the farmer is gone. The hen eats the wheat. This is not safe. Go back to the start.

Try 2.
Move 1. The farmer takes the hen across. Left bank: fox, wheat. Right bank: hen. The fox does not eat wheat. Safe.
Move 2. He goes back alone. Left bank: fox, wheat. Right bank: hen. Safe.
Move 3. He takes the fox across. Left bank: wheat. Right bank: fox, hen. The fox and hen are together, but he is with them. Safe for now. He must not leave them.
Move 4. He takes the hen back. Left bank: hen, wheat. Right bank: fox. Safe.
Move 5. He takes the wheat across. Left bank: hen. Right bank: fox, wheat. Safe.
Move 6. He goes back alone. Left bank: hen. Right bank: fox, wheat. Safe.
Move 7. He takes the hen across. All three are across.
Check. After every move, the fox was never alone with the hen, and the hen was never alone with the wheat. Good.

I read it twice and found no hole. I took it down to Ossa at the ferry house and read it to her. Ossa listened with her arms folded. "I would take a second boat," she said. Then she poured me some soup.

Everything else the mind did right. The part I cannot get over is the first try. It made a mistake, it saw the mistake, and it said so. I have known men who could not do that.

I asked Ilse whether the mind ever stops and says it does not know. She said she would ask it. The answer is not back yet. It is still snowing.
%%
Tova, egg stall book, the Saltreach
Autumn. Egg stall, the east end of the fish market, by the price board.

Eggs: 40 on the first day. Sold 36. The four cracked ones went to the gulls, who were very grateful and very loud about it.

Hens are laying less now. The days are short. Tova says the hens know the season better than the price board does, and the price board says eggs are up, so I sell at the higher price and feel guilty about it.

The fish sellers are shouting about the price of salt again. Salt is up, they say, since the cart from Copperhold came in three days late. Tova says the salt has nothing to do with her eggs. The gulls do not care about either.

Day 9. A tutor came down from Vell with a crate of small crystals and a wooden frame. She is called Berit. She sat on the quay with her frame and asked the boatmen to let her try a mind in a little rowing boat, a tiny one, on a pond the children use for sailing.

The mind was set on a rudder with two strings. Berit said it had not been drilled on boats at all. She had only let it try, again and again, and each time it drifted off, she let it drift, and each time the boat came back, she said nothing. When it kept the boat straight for a whole lap of the pond, she gave it a stick of sugar.

The boatmen laughed. Then they went quiet. The little boat went straight round the pond three times and bumped gently into the quay wall where it was supposed to stop.

Day 12. Berit says this is how some of the old minds were learned. A furnace mind does not get told the right temperature. It tries a temperature, and sees how the melt goes, and keeps the choice that held. Over and over. It keeps what went well, and lets the rest go. She says it is slow, and that the drills of the tutors are faster, but the tried minds sometimes do things the drills never taught them.

Day 18. The mill at Saltreach lost a wheel in the storm. The big wheel, from the seaward side, where the wind gets in. It cracked and fell into the race. The millers came down and stood on the quay looking at it, and the eggs in my basket went cold.

Berit went up with them and asked what the wheel was built from. They said oak. She said she had seen tried minds keep a sail full in a bad wind, and asked if a mind could be put on the wheel to keep the sluice open, the way it keeps the boat. Nobody answered. They want a wheel first.

Day 20. The wheel is in the yard. The millers are glad of the sun. Berit has packed her crystals and gone back up the road. She left me a note on the egg stall, which says: the hens are right. Keep the price.

Road log: the broken lamp, spring 200
Written by Nessa, on the Long Road, in the spring of 200. The lamp at the Halfway Inn was dark for nine days. Here is how it was mended, and what I drilled while I waited.

Day one of the dark. The relay on the hill sent a message to Vell at dawn, and no answer came back. The keeper went up the stair and found the top glass cracked clean through. The copper ring was loose on its bolts. Nobody had been up for a month, because one rung was missing at the eighteenth step.

Day three. The glass came from Saltreach on a cart, wrapped in straw. The glassmaker's apprentice had painted one edge green, by mistake, and nobody had the heart to scrape it off.

Day five. While we waited, I drilled the small minds in the yard. The mind named Fern, from Vell, had learned its orders well until the fourth layer. Then the numbers ran wild, and Fern answered store to every order, including come. I told the keeper to write that down, because he does not believe in tutors.

Day six. I put the even step after the fourth layer, and drilled again on the same ten orders. Fern got seven right. That is better than before, and not as good as it should be. The tutors say the first try after a step is always cautious. It will get braver with practice, or it will not.

Day nine. The lamp is lit. The keeper stood at the foot of the tower with his hands in his pockets and said nothing, which is what a good keeper does. I said nothing back.

Tam at the Lowmarsh market
Reaping of 217. I came down from Kettle Ridge with Pip and a cart of beetroot.

The road was damp, though the sky was clear. Pip tied the sacks with the wrong rope. I tied them again. Pip said the first knots had held. They had, but one sack leaned so far the cart looked like it was walking to Lowmarsh sideways.

We reached the market before noon. Wren had a jar of honey on her table. She said it came from Ambrook and had been brought through three rain showers. I bought it for Mira. Honey travels better than fresh bread, and Mira likes to say that winter has no taste.

The council had placed a notice beside the fish prices. It said the great relay house at the north road had received a larger mind. The house is made for many keepers and many quickeners. Its crystal has many layers. Each layer adds what it has learned to a stream that passes through the whole mind. The tutors say the layers must be tested together, because no one tutor can hold the whole work in memory.

A man selling clay pots said the mind knew what every person at the market would buy. The fish seller said it had named the day's catch before the boats came in. A child said it had drawn a map of the marsh from a story.

The council notice said none of these things. It said the mind had read a set of records and answered questions about them. The answers were checked by tutors. Some were right, some were not. The notice did not say what the mind could do beyond those tests.

Pip asked if it could teach him to tie sacks. I told him it could not take the rope out of his hands. He tied the next one well.

We sold all the beetroot. On the way back, the cart stayed straight. The honey is wrapped in wool.

Wren's notes, frost and fair
Frost came early this year. It silvered every reed and made the boardwalk boards hard underfoot. I put a little straw by the healing house door so wet boots would not slide. Moss put down too much. The whole entry looked like a small hay yard.
We had three patients before breakfast. One had a fish hook in his sleeve, one had a cough, and one had a splinter under a nail. The fish hook came out easily. The cough needed hot water and rest. The splinter needed a needle, good light and patience. The patient supplied none of these, so I supplied them for him.
At the market fair in Three Wells, Moss won a little wooden cup for the best basket of dried herbs. The basket was neat. Moss had tied the label upside down. The judges turned it round and gave the prize anyway. Moss says they admired the basket from every side.
I brought home a jar of honey from a stall by the east well. It is for the house, not for the coughs. I wrote that on the lid. Moss says a lid cannot read. I said then Moss should read it before opening.
The jar stands on the high shelf beside the clean cups. We will have a spoonful with bread on the first cold evening. The first cold evening was yesterday. The jar remains closed. Moss says the lid is very clear.
