Corin, three days of gale
Winter of 202. Longnight, the 5th to the 7th. West wind, very strong.
The 5th. The wind began before dawn. By the noon bell the spray was coming over the harbor wall, and the boats in the harbor were knocking together like teeth. Nobody went out. The nets were mended by lamp at the quay. Marit mended faster than she talked, which is saying something.
The 6th. The worst. The Heron's mooring rope parted at midnight. Kell and four of his crew rowed out in the dark with two lanterns to tie her to the pier again. I stood on the harbor wall and counted the lanterns. Two going out. Two coming back. Count the crew going out, and count them coming in. It was a long hour. Kell came up the steps wet to the bone and said only, "She is tied." Marit gave him tea. He put three spoons of honey in it, and shook.
Three boards off the yard shed roof. A bench gone into the sea. The slip is under foam. A boat on the blocks, under the roof, is safe.
The 7th. Calm. Grey and calm, as if nothing had happened. The harbor is full of weed and broken wood. Kit found a boot, a bench plank and a fish in a bucket. He wants to keep the fish. I said the fish has a family.
Everything that was tied stayed. Everything that was not tied went to sea. That is the rule of the sea, and it has no exceptions.

Quin, the long autumn journal
A longer record kept by Quin, midwife, on a farm outside Saltreach, through the three months of autumn of 195: Apple, Leaffall and Frost. She kept it, she says, because Oren the clerk asked for a midwife's book, and no one had ever written one.
APPLE
The harvest was good. I brought in four baskets of apples from the tree, and made jam. One jar of the jam is going to Wren, who wrote that she wanted to see what Saltreach apples are like. I put a note in. "Eat it on bread. If you do not like it, give it to Moss."
Births this month: five. Two boys, three girls. All well.
Horse: my own is old. I borrowed Pell's mare for a night call. She is better than mine. I mean to say so. I do not want to say so to my horse.
LEAFFALL
Rain, for nine days. The cove farm flooded in the lower field. They were moved into the barn. A baby was born in the barn, on the straw, between two cows. I named her, for the occasion, Hay. The mother said she would think about it.
Births this month: four. A girl at the cove, in the barn. Two boys at the ridge farms. A girl, very early, whom I stayed with all night. She is small but fierce.
Honey: I was given three jars this month by grateful fathers. I have decided that honey is the currency of births. I could pay for a boat with it.
FROST
The first frost came on the second. The apples that remain are sweet. The geese are flying south. The sea has gone grey.
Births this month: three. A boy and two girls. One, a twin, I did not expect. I was caught with only one blanket. We tore up a cloth.
Visit to Wren. I went to Lowmarsh to see her, on the borrowed horse. We talked of babies and herbs. She says the grey cough has not come back. She gave me a bundle of willow bark and a letter for Oren.
The borrowed horse knew the way to Lowmarsh better than I did. She stopped at the right gate. I gave her an apple. Later I found the whole bag was gone, and she had a look.
Summary of the season: births, twelve. Borrowed horses, two. Jars of honey, nine. Wet nights, twenty. Cups of tea, uncountable.
Next year I will buy a horse of my own. A good one, a patient one. I will name her Apple.
Quin

Lorn, the Quills go, Deepsnow
Deepsnow. The snow came in the night and lay on the harbor like a sheet. The first big ship is ready. It is longer than the wharf and taller than the mill. The Quills came down to the pier at first light with everything they owned on two carts.

Mara Quill had been my neighbor for twenty years. She bought my last two fleeces before I could sell them. She paid in full, in emeralds, and counted them out on my table. "We will not need wool across the water," she said, and laughed a little.

"What is across the water?" I asked.

"Land," said Mara. "Corin says so. Corin has never seen it."

Corin was on the pier with his hands in his coat. He did not say anything. He only nodded at the ship, the way a man nods at a horse that may kick.

The Quill boy, Ash, is six. He had his nose pressed to the side of the cart. He waved at me the whole way down the pier. He had his cat in a basket on his knees. The cat did not wave.

The loading took all morning. Two apprentices from the harbor carried the chests up the ramp, one step at a time, and set each one down where the man with the list pointed.

> take this chest up the ramp
= haul

They did not hurry. They did not stop. When the chest was on the deck, they came back down for the next one, as if nothing in the world were stranger than a ramp.

The ship has three decks and a bell on each. At the second bell the Quills went up the ramp. Mara turned at the top and looked at the town, the harbor, the wall and the sea. Then she went inside.

Nobody on the pier spoke for a while. Then a woman from Lowmarsh said she had heard the ship would go to a place with no winter. A man by the fish stalls said the ship would come back in a year. Someone else said it would never come back. Then he said they should not have said that out loud.

The Quill house is empty now. Their door was left open. The snow has blown in across the floor. I went in and shut the door. I did not know what else to do. The hens are with me now. The Quills did not take the hens.

Fallon, spring journal
Spring of 188, from Thaw to Longday.
Thaw came late. The hill paths stayed hard, and the cart from Copperhold brought flour in a sack with a hole. We lost some to the road. I swept it up. The chickens ate most of it before I could put it in the bin.

I spent the first days mending the wall by the east spring. The old map showed the wall in a straight line, but the stones bend around a birch stump. The stump is gone now. The stones have not moved. I think the map maker drew the line by eye and had cold hands.

My niece Fen found the map under the bench. She asked if the square marked "new store" was our house. It was not. Our house is drawn as a small box near the well. Fen said the map maker had made our house too small. I told her the map maker had never seen our kitchen table.

In Blossom I cut a step for the lane. The rain came while I worked. Water ran over the stone and carried my chalk mark away. I made the mark again. Rell held the board over my head. He kept the rain off the stone and let it run down his neck.

The spring is low this year. We saved water in the trough and gave the sheep a turn. Tavin forgot his turn and filled his cup first. He says he was thirsty. So were the sheep.

Last day of Longday.
The wall is mended. Fen says the old map should go in the archive. I wrapped it in cloth and put it on the shelf above the tools. It is not the archive, but it is dry.
%%
Tove's book: why the steps help
Tove, age eleven. Grandfather Corin says I should write down what the tutor said, because I understand things faster than he does and my writing is better. Both are true.

The tutor is Linnet, from Vell. She came because Grandfather has asked the big mind at the tower so many sums that Ilse wrote to Vell to ask if it was all right. Linnet said it was very all right. She said it is what the tutors are most pleased about this winter.

This is what she told us.

A mind like this reads a page one piece at a time and guesses what comes next. When you ask it a hard sum, it has to begin its answer at once. There is no room to work the sum out inside. So it guesses, and often it guesses wrong.

But if it writes its steps first, each step is small. It can do a small step in one go. And each step is on the page. When it comes to the next step it can read what it wrote. The page is like a second memory, Linnet said. It is like doing a sum on a slate instead of in your head.

So the mind has not become cleverer inside. It has learned to use the page.

Then comes the checking. After it writes the answer, it reads its steps again and looks for one that does not follow from the one before. If it finds one, it goes back and does it again.

"Does it always find the mistake?" I asked.

"No," said Linnet. "Sometimes it checks, and it is still wrong, and it is sure. It is not magic. It is a mind that works on paper."

Grandfather asked if that meant it thinks. Linnet looked at the snow for a long time. "It writes steps," she said, "and the steps are right more often than they used to be. What that is, I would rather not say yet."

I wrote down everything she said. Grandfather says I should be a clerk. I said I would rather build boats. He said that was also a good answer.
%%
Nessa, late summer record
There are six of us in the Saltreach house now. The house is too small for six, two layered crystals, and a wall of drill cards. We moved the bread basket to the window shelf.
Merle's work has drawn tutors from other towns. Some ask if the layers make a mind wise. He says no. A mind learns what its drills and its tests can show. A relay number is a mark to guess, not a promise that the mind knows who sent it.
Today we drilled a row of marks, then a new row with the same sort of pattern. The first layer changed the marks into a stream for the next layer. The stream went onward, layer by layer, until the last layer gave its guess.
We compared each guess with the answer. If the guess missed, we found the error and changed the last layer. We then passed the correction back through the other layers, from the answer toward the first. Each layer received its share, based on how it had helped make the mistake.
The first crystal improved on the drill cards. The second improved more slowly. Neither learned every card. We tested both on marks they had not seen. One did well. One did not. Merle refused to hide the poor result.
Pell the road merchant came to settle a debt for lamp oil. He counted out seven emeralds on the table. One rolled under the bread basket. We found it in the morning beside the egg.
%%
The Vell lower town, midwinter
Kept by Ossa, candle maker, lower town, Vell. Midwinter, the year the bread was thin. The Archive gave out a bushel of barley for every street, and the bakers of the lower town had to decide what to do with it.

Midwinter, first light. The bell from the hill was late, because the clerk who rings it had a cold and could not reach the rope. The bell rang at last, twice, and the whole lower town came out to see if anyone was hurt.
Nobody was hurt. The clerk was in bed with a shawl over his head.

The barley came up the road at the third bell in two carts, with a clerk walking alongside each one counting sacks. There were forty sacks for our street. Lorn said that was too many for the twelve houses, and the bakers said it was too few. They argued in the square for an hour, and then they split the barley by the number of mouths in each house, which everyone agreed was the only fair way and nobody liked.

Ada's house has five mouths and a dog. The dog was counted as a mouth, and then as half a mouth, and then as the dog's share, which is an extra loaf on the step.

The bakers' oven at the corner is hot all day now. The smell goes all the way up the hill. The clerks in the Archive are said to be very happy with it.

Later, a traveler came down from the Long Road with a cart of pumpkins, and sold them all for barley. He said he had heard the lower town was hungry, and he was not wrong. Ossa bought four and made a pie, and burned it on the bottom, and ate it anyway with Ada's boy and the dog.

Candles, this week: twenty-four made. Sold: all. Given to the Archive: twelve. Kept back for the stair: four, because the stair is dark at the top.

Corin, house log, Longnight
Longnight. Third day.
The mill wheel broke at the pond beside our house. One spoke came loose, and the wheel turned half a circle before it stopped. The miller tied it with rope so it would not fall into the water.
I took two oak boards from the shed to help him. The boards were damp, but sound. We will fit them when the wind drops.
A fox came up the road after dark. It stood by the wall and watched me carry the boards. It looked warmer than I felt.
The harbor bell rang once. No boat came in. The keeper says a rope struck the bell in the wind. I believe him. Still, I checked the latch twice.

Wynn, clerk, the cold weeks at the tutor
Deepsnow, the coldest month. The tutor house has been shut to visitors for nine days, and the clerks who go up to it come back with their faces red and their stories short.
I went up with the bread on the sixth day. The yard was white, and the torches round the drill yard had been wrapped in straw to keep the wind off. The apprentices stood in their row at the drill yard wall, very still, with snow on their shoulders that nobody had thought to brush off.
Nessa met me at the door. She looked tired in the particular way of a tutor who has been awake for three nights. She said the drill had gone well. She said the test had gone badly. She did not say which part she meant by which.
Then she told me what they had found. The tutors have been looking inside the crystals for a month, with Hesper's lenses and a great deal of patience. They have found the part that knows edges and the part that knows sacks. They have found a part that lights up at the sound of a bell. They have not found the part that joins them. She said that was the question, and she said it as if it were a joke she had heard too many times to laugh at.
I asked her what she thought the joining part was. She said she did not know. She said the crystal might be joining everything in a way nobody has words for yet, or that there was no single part, only the whole of it, spread out. She said Orla would have had a good answer, and then she laughed and said Orla would have had a question instead.
Before I left, Nessa gave me a copy of the note the tutors have pinned to the drill room wall. It reads: We know more than we did. We know less than we thought we did. Keep drilling. Keep looking.
I have pinned it to the wall in our kitchen. My father says it is the most honest note he has ever read from a tutor house, and he has read a great many.

Tova, road log, Abel's drawing
Longnight of 158. Snow, then more snow.

Abel drew the mind for me in the snow, with a stick. He says a child could follow it. I could not, at first.

He drew three rows of dots. Each dot is a weight, a small number the mind keeps. The first row looks at the reeds, the frogs and the water. Each dot adds up what it sees. The second row takes those sums and does the same with them. The third row says one word: frog, or not frog.

"And when it is wrong?" I asked.

He tapped the bottom row. "Then the answer is wrong by some amount. We find out how much each dot in the last row helped make that wrong answer. Those are the blame numbers. Then we pass the blame back. Each dot in the second row is told how much it helped the third row go wrong. Then the first row is told the same way."

"That is a great deal of blame," I said.

"It is the only way," said Abel. Merle of Copperhold found it, when he grew his crystals in layers. The first row had to know, too.

"Everyone gets a share," I said.

"Everyone gets a share. And each dot moves a little, against its share of the blame. Toward a smaller blame. Not all the way. A little. Then we show it another frog, and do it again."
%%
Kettle Ridge mill log, the broken axle
Summer of 200. The dry month.
The wheel broke on the eleventh. I heard it from the yard, a crack like a branch, and then the wheel turned once more and stopped.
The cause was the dry wood. The pond is low this summer, so the wheel turned fast and the axle dried in the sun and split. That is what I told Tam and that is what I will write here.
We took the old axle out with wedges and a great deal of pushing. The new one is oak, cut from the north hedge, and it is a hand thicker than the old. We set it in and greased it and the wheel turned again at dusk. It squeaks on every turn now, the way it always has. I have missed the squeak. A mill that does not squeak is a mill that is not turning.
The tally board stands at the door. Wheat 40 sacks. Beetroot 12. Potatoes 9. Carrots 6. Honey 4 jars. The honey was a good year. The bees were busy and the flowers were early.
I keep the board with a piece of chalk and a wet rag. I rub out the old week and write the new one. Jory says the board is the true record of the ridge, more than any book. Perhaps it is. A book can say what the miller hoped. The board says what the miller got.

Briar, baker, the apprentice
Deepsnow. A bad week for orders.
On the first morning I said to the apprentice, "Fetch the sacks from the back." It went to the back. It fetched the sacks. It fetched every sack in the store, including the three marked for the pig, and then it fetched the pig, which took some time to persuade.
I said, "Store them." It stored them. It put all the sacks in a line along the wall of the bakehouse, one behind the other, very neat, very straight. I could not get past the wall to reach the oven. Olin had to climb over the sacks with a lamp.
The next day I wrote the order on the card, as Nessa taught me. Fetch flour, and then haul it to the oven. I wrote the meaning under it: fetch flour, haul. The apprentice read the card and fetched the flour. Then it hauled the flour to the oven, and it hauled the flour to the oven again, and again, twenty times, because the card had said haul and the apprentice had not been told to stop.
I stopped it by saying "stay". It stayed, with a sack of flour in its arms, looking very sorry.
I have written in the house book that I am sorry too. The card was my fault. Nessa says the meanings must be short, and mine was not short enough. She says a card is only as clear as the keeper who writes it.

Ivo, the north well, one market day
Reaping, the 15th, in the autumn of 171. Fine, with a wind.
Eggs: 96, at 12 for 1. Got 8. Socks: 7 pairs, at 2. Got 14. Lost children: 2, both found at the pie stall. Lost hats: 1.
I sit at the north well on a stool my husband made. It has one short leg. I lean to the left, and after thirty years so does the market.
Brook's boy asked if my eggs come from happy hens. I said the hens have a cat to talk to. He bought a dozen.
A man from Saltreach wanted salt for his eggs. I sent him to the east well. He came back with fish. I do not know how that happened.
Home by noon. Knees good. Back not good. Tea.

Lorn, the autumn of the play
Leaffall, 207. A long log, from the house at the edge of Three Wells, where Lorn the ferry keeper now lives without a ferry, as the ferry is mended for the winter, and writes instead.
First of Leaffall. The ferry is in the yard, on blocks. A board is cracked, a rope is frayed. I have a month of nothing. I will write down what comes.
Fifth. The school came to the house. A dozen children, in a line, with Quill at the back. They wanted to ask if I would be in the autumn play again. I said I was old. They said I was the river. I said a river can be old. They agreed. I am in.
Eighth. Rehearsal. The play is bigger this year. It has an apprentice in it. Not a real one. A girl in a tin hat with a bucket. She walks across the stage and does what she is told. The audience is meant to laugh. They do, a bit. Then they stop. Quill says that is the point.
Tenth. Walked to the east well. The song contest is on. This year's theme: songs about work. A man sang about a mine. A woman sang about a net. Two girls sang a duet about the ferry. I stood at the back. I did not cry. A little.
Twelfth. The play. The hall was full and warm. The apprentice in the tin hat crossed the stage and put the bucket down. The fox, a boy, said, "What does it want?" The bridge, a girl, said, "Nothing. It is just doing the job." I stood in my blue sheet, in the back, and I thought of forty years of crossing. I rippled.
Fourteenth. The song contest. The ferry song won. It was written by the girls, and nobody told them what to write. It goes: "Over and back, over and back, the river is wide and the rope is slack." They sang it twice. I knew the words. I had not heard them before.
Sixteenth. Home. Tea. The yard is full of leaves. The ferry is on its blocks. I will mend it in the spring.
Eighteenth. Wrote this. I think it was a good autumn.

Quill, flock log: the jar of honey
Kept at Ferris End. Written after the storm, when the wall was mended and the reed fold was dry enough to sit in.

Blossom, the day after. The jar of honey is on the wall by the gate, with the cloth still tied over it. I have not opened it. Wren said for the chilled ones, and I am not sure whether that means me or the lambs. The lambs look chilled. I am not.

Blossom, third day. One lamb is not right. It is the smallest of Muffin's twins, and it stands with its head low, and it does not follow the others to the grass. Its ears are cold. Its nose is cold. I carried it up to the house in my coat and set it by the stove.

Blossom, third day, evening. Wren came up the lane to see how we were. She looked at the lamb for a long time. Then she took the cloth off the honey, put a spoon of it into a cup of warm milk, and stirred it with her finger. "Honey in warm milk," she said. "Before bed. Every night for a week. Do as I say. I am a healer."

Wren gave me the spoon and the cup. I held the lamb's head. The lamb did not like it, and Fen did not like it either, and Wren said that neither of them was the one she was worried about.

Blossom, fourth day. The lamb has stood up. It walked to the gate and back, twice, and then sat down in the straw and looked very pleased with itself. Its nose is warm. I put my hand on it, and it did not move away, which is the first time it has done that. I named it Honey. Muffin does not like the name, but Muffin does not like anything she did not choose herself.

Blossom, fifth day. The honey is half gone. Half to the lamb and half to me, because I was sitting up with it, and because I had a cold too, and nobody had told me I could not have some.

Blossom, sixth day. Wren came up again and sniffed at the jar and said I was not to eat the honey meant for the lamb. I said it was for both of us. She said that was a lie, and she was right, and I said so. She laughed for the first time since Frost.

Letter: Colm to Mira, Northpoint
Mira,
It is Apple here. The leaves are turning and the mornings are cold. The flock is sheared and the wool is in the loft. I hope your winter is not too long this year.
Pell came by with news from the road. He says the relays have gone quiet up your way. He says it is nothing. He says it twice. I thought I would ask you straight instead.
If the tower is well, write and say so. If it is not, write and say that too. I will not fuss. I only want to know.
Do you still have the fox that comes to the foot of the tower? Tam says you feed it. Tam says you named it. Tam says you will not tell him the name.
The wedding at Ambrook is in two weeks. Pell is going. If you can leave the lamp with Lantern for a few days, come down. Bring the fox if it will ride.
Your friend,
Colm
P.S. I have a sack of the good wool set aside for you. It is soft as a cat.

Orla, the winter fair at the tower
Winter fair at the foot of Northpoint tower, the first market day of the year. Everybody came up the snow road: the ice camp men on their sledges, the foxers from the north, and three families from the bottom farm with a sledge of cheese.

The stalls were boards on barrels, with blankets over the top. The apples from Ambrook were gone by the first hour.

Lantern carried sacks from the sledges to the stalls all morning, one at a time. It stopped at the end of each row to see whether anyone needed it. Nobody did. It fetched another sack anyway. The sack men asked whether it could also carry the lamp oil. It could.

The children held a sledge race down the snow road. Little Ned won with a sledge made from a barrel lid. He said he had built it himself, and then went quiet when his father said he had only sat on it. The mayor of the tower, who is Mira, declared it a draw, because she was the only one who could see the finish.

By dusk the snow road was full of lanterns going south. The last thing I saw was Mira at the top of the tower stairs, waving her lamp, the way she does at the end of every fair. I waved back. Somebody on the road waved too.

Lowmarsh market, spring ledger
Longday of 216. Notes from the stalls.

The market opened late because the boardwalk by the fish weir needed new posts. The water had eaten the mud around two of them. We carried oak from the shed and set the posts before the first carts arrived.

Wren brought jars of dried leaves and honey. One jar had a bee in the wax at the top. She said it had been there since the honey was poured. I said it had chosen a fine place to rest. Wren said it had chosen a jar.

At the notice board, Dace read a message from Vell. A new relay house had been built beside the Long Road. The council says it is larger than Three Wells. It has sleeping rooms for keepers, a store room, a kitchen, and a cool hall for the big mind. The quickeners are kept cool with water and ice. The keeper checks them each turn of the lamp.

The council has sent tutors from Vell and Copperhold to test the mind. One tutor gives it old market pages. Another keeps the test box and makes sure those pages were never used in its drills. A third checks the answers against the books.

Pell says the mind can find a name in every record ever written. Wren says that sounds like searching, not wisdom. Pell says it can also tell which cures will work. Wren said she would like to see the test for that.

A traveler claimed the mind had read the rain from the clouds. Another said it had asked for more pages. The keeper's letter says only that it answered the questions put before it.

I sold three baskets of potatoes and bought fish. The fish were small. The seller said they were young. I said they were small. We settled on the same price.

Fen's journal, the Brown Hen
Thaw, the sixth day. The Brown Hen, on the Long Road.

Marda says the stew is the same as last year. It is better. I did not say so, because she wrote down what I said last year, and I said it was good, and now she expects it.

I have lost a letter. It was Marit's, for her sister at Ambrook. I put it somewhere safe, which is the worst place to put a thing. I looked in my pack. I looked in my coat. I looked in my hat.

It was in my left boot. I had walked on it for two days. It is flat, but it can be read.

Cress, the tally at Leaffall's end
The count, to keep myself honest. Written at the end of Leaffall, by the lamp.

Carts up the Long Road this month: thirty-one. Carts back down: twenty-nine.

Mothers from Lowmarsh asking for clean cloth: four. I sold it to them at cost. Wren would want that written down.

Families gone out on the morning tide: nine that I know by name. More that I know only by the cart.

Gifts: three. A carved blue fish, a twist of dried apples, a jar of honey. I am still saving the honey.

Zombies at the stable gate: four nights. The horse has stopped stamping, and that worries me more than the zombies do.

Relay lamp on the harbor wall: dark for five nights. The keeper says it is the cold.

Pell writes that the inns along the road are full of people going the other way. Nobody asks them where. Pell says he has stopped asking too.

Things I do not understand: more than I can count, so I have not counted them.

Lamp lit. Horse fed. Boards nailed. Crust kept for the gulls, who have been very quiet lately.

Wynn, clerk, the glass crystal
Winter. The tutors came up the hill road today with a box of glass lenses and a great deal of patience.
They are from the house below the Archive. Three of them, two men and a woman named Hesper with a grey cloak and very cold hands. They wanted to look inside a crystal. A used one, from an apprentice that has been taken out of service at the Vell fields.
I held the lamp for them in the drill room. Hesper set the crystal on the table and put a lens over it. Then another. Then she sat back and said nothing for a long time, which is how tutors show that something is wrong.
She said the crystal has many small parts. Each part answers to a small thing: a line, a corner, the edge of a wheat stalk. She found one part, near the top, that wakes whenever it sees an edge. She showed us. When we held a sack of grain in front of the crystal, the part lit up like a window.
She could not find what the whole thing does. Only the pieces. She said the rest of the crystal is a sea of small changes, and she can see that it moves, but not where it is going.
I asked her if that was a failure. She said no. She said it was like finding the hinge on a door and not knowing the room behind it.
She wrote three lines in her book before she left. I copied them down, because clerks copy. The first line says: one part knows edges. The second says: one part knows the sack. The third says: most of it is still unknown, and this is not new.

Rudd's diary, the autumn of the rains
Sallow Farm, near Copperhold. Kept in the back of the book, in Rudd's hand, for the autumn of 205, a wet one. He says he wrote it to stop Gwen from saying he never writes anything down.

Rain came on the first of Apple and did not stop until Frost. The brook came over the lower stones twice. Gwen crossed the bridge every morning with her boots tied round her neck, and cursed the mason, who was not at fault, since the rain is not his.

The wheat in the top field was cut in a dry spell that lasted one afternoon. We got all of it in, and then the sky opened and the field went to mud, and the carts sank to the axle. We carried the sheaves on our backs. Three of them went to the pigs, who were very grateful.

Pip came in the first week of Leaffall. Not Pip. A new apprentice, from Vell, named Dib. A thin one, with a long face. It was tested on sorting. It sorts the beans by size better than any child I have ever met. The trouble is that it does not know when to stop. Tell it to sort the beans and it will sort the beans, the sacks, the sheds and the cat's bowl. We are working on the word stop.

The hens stopped laying in the wet. Fourteen eggs in a week, which was more than I expected. Ivy says she counts them every night, and that the count is the best part of her day.

We had a visit from the mill keeper at Copperhold, who came up the valley in a boat, because the road was gone. He had a message for Gwen from her sister at the coast. She had written it on a card. It said: "The boats are big here now. Come and see." Gwen cried a little. Then she laughed and said her sister always made boats sound better than they were.

Last day of the rains, a rainbow over the Copperhold hill, very bright. The children ran out in their nightshirts to see it. Ivy said it stood on the ridge like a bridge, and that you could walk across it if you were brave. Gwen said it was not a bridge, it was a rainbow, and that bridges were her business, and that she would look after the bridge and the rainbow would look after itself.

Frost came on the fourth of Frost. The brook went quiet. The frost on the top field was white and clean, and the sheep stood in it looking very surprised. We are all glad the rains are done. We are all glad Dib knows the word stop now. Mostly.
%%
Tova, road log, the poor wheat
Frost of 158. Grey light, short days.

Greer lost half his wheat this year. Rain fell every day in Melon, and the wheat went black at the root. He cut what was left in a field of mud. He has not said a word about it since. He pats the cow when he passes her, instead.

Abel's mind has sat on the windowsill all autumn. This week he tested it. He keeps a test box of drawings: twenty reeds and frogs that the mind has never been shown. No mind is ever shown a drawing from that box, not once, or the test means nothing.

It got fourteen right. Of the six it got wrong, two frogs were called reeds, and four reeds were called frogs.

Abel said that was not good enough to send anywhere. Greer said it was better than his wheat. Abel said that was not the point.

"The point," said Abel, "is that it must be right on what it has never seen. Otherwise it has only learned the drill by heart."

Greer said he did not know what learned by heart meant. Abel told him to go and look at his cow.

So we drill it again. Twenty more reeds and frogs, and the blame goes back up through the layers, as before. Ivo keeps the box warm with a stone from the hearth. Each night he wraps the stone in cloth and puts it by the box. Each morning it is cold again, and each morning he wraps it again.

Greer's wheat is lost. The mind is not. Greer says that is a poor trade.

Deep Works log
Winter of 207. Night shift.
Crew: Hesk, Ola, Bram, old Fenn, Clank. Dunn from the watch, who asked to come down.
Level: seven, the north wall.
Count: copper 6 carts, iron 3, coal 4, deep stone 12 for the wall builders.
Water: dry.
Hurt: nobody, except Dunn, who dropped a lantern on his foot.
Notes. The north wall at the seventh level is cracked. It has been cracked since spring. A creeper came up the old tunnel in the autumn and hit the wall, and the stone bulged out, and the men said it was only the wind. Tonight the crack is longer. The rubble on the floor is warm.
Dunn says the rubble is warm because the lanterns are close. Old Fenn says it is warm because the stone is warm. Hesk says the lanterns are on the other side of the tunnel, and he can feel the heat in his hand.
We put our hands on the stone at the crack. It is warm, the way a loaf is warm when you take it from the oven and wait a moment before you cut. Clank put its hand on the wall too. It did not say anything. It stood with its palm flat on the stone until Hesk told it to come away.
We built the wall up again with deep stone, in the pattern. Cobblestone, cobblestone, deep stone, and up. Clank laid the bottom course on its own, and laid it true. We did not put a torch in the crack. Hesk said a torch in a crack that warm would only show us what we did not want to see.
The rubble is in the cart. The cart is at the lift. The lift is for tools and for sick men. Nobody went below the wall.
All up. The wall is up. The stone is still warm when you put your hand on it.

Dara, the roof log
Kept by Dara, beekeeper, who sells honey at the Ambrook market. A week in autumn, when the roof of her hive shed was mended. In her own short words.

First day. The roof of the hive shed leaks over the third row. Water on the frames. Moved the hives to the dry end. The bees were cross. I wore the hat.

Second day. Asked Rook for his ladder. He said yes if I help him pick apples. I said yes.

Third day. Picked apples all morning. Rook's ladder in the afternoon. Found three loose shingles and one hole. Patched the hole with tar. The tar was warm. I got it on my elbow.

Fourth day. Rain at night. Lay awake listening. Not one drip. I think the roof is mended.

Fifth day. Market day. Sold twenty-two jars. The honey stall stood by the well. People asked about the roof. I said it is the best roof in Ambrook. Rook said it is only a roof.

Sixth day. Moved the hives back. The bees found their own frames in an hour. They hum differently when they are happy. A lower sound.

Seventh day. Rest. I sat in the yard and listened to the roof not leak.
%%
Orla, the long nights, a season
Longnight is the longest. The sun comes up at the start of the day, looks at us over the snow, and goes down again before the stove has warmed. We keep the lamps lit from breakfast to bed. The horses sleep more. So do the foxes, I think, though they do not stop coming to the gate.

Mira's lamp is the thing I look at most in the long nights. From the bottom field you can see it on the hill, small and steady, going on and off at the times she sends. She sends the weather to Vell and to the south towers, and long letters to her brother at Kettle Ridge. Some nights the lamp answers from up the line. It is a kind of conversation. The horses do not join in.

Lantern has learned the stairs. It goes up and down the tower twenty times a day with a bucket or a sack in its hands, and it never once falls. Mira says it does not know how to be tired. I say it has never had a long winter before, and nobody has told it that it is supposed to be.

The fox that comes to the gate has grown fat on our hen scraps. I have started calling it Gilly. It does not answer to the name. It only comes.

The ice camp men came up twice, with fish and news and the price of fish. The second time they said the lake had frozen to the bottom at the shallow end, and the fishing was good. They were pleased about that, the way men are pleased by anything near and certain.

The horses are sound. The long barn is full of hay that will last, if the winter is as long as Mira says. She says the cold has come early, and the snow is coming down the north road like a line of carts. I told her to write that down. She said she already had.

Lantern is standing by the barn door with the new lamp, waiting to be told what to do with it.
%%
Ida's log, the small crystal
Written by Ida, farmer. Spring. The tutor from Vell came up the lane with a smaller crystal for the hamlet's apprentice, Tibby, who has waited since Thaw to be given work.

The tutor is called Hale. The big crystal would not fit the apprentice's mind slot, he said, so he had brought a small one. It is the same as the big one, except that fewer marks have been written in it.

I asked him what that meant. He sat on the step and explained it, slowly, with his hands.

"A mind is a great many weights," he said. "Each weight is a number, and each number is written as marks in the crystal. The big crystal writes each mark finely, so a weight can be exact. The small crystal writes each mark more coarsely, so the same weight is only close. The mind is trained with that coarseness, and it learns to work with the rounding."

"Does it cost anything?"

"A little. Each answer is a little less exact. In the fields you will not see the difference. In a hard sum, you might. Test it before you trust it."

Then Hale set the small crystal into Tibby's slot. The first drill was simple: bring the hoe. Tibby brought the hoe, and then it stood very still, waiting to be told what to do next.

Hale nodded and wrote in his book: fewer marks, the same hoe.
%%
Mira, Northpoint, a winter in one book
Written up at Northpoint from the log, at the end of Deepsnow. For Tam, who asked for all of it.

Frost, the 16th. A cart came up the north road in the snow, which nobody does. It was Pell, with a box of honey from Ambrook, a sack of letters from Vell and a face like a wet sack. He had come the whole Long Road in six days. He drank a bowl of soup, read every letter by the hearth, and left before I could find the mitten I had knitted for him. He took it anyway, which is the most a merchant can do for a mitten.

Frost, the 20th. I knitted one mitten, for one hand, for Lantern. It is grey, the color of the tower in the snow. Lantern held it up to the lamp and turned it over. Then it put it on the hand nearest to it, and kept it there the whole day. It did not say it was pleased. It did not need to.

Longnight, the 2nd. A pane of glass cracked in the lamp room. The frost got in from the outside and split it in a neat line, like a page folded wrong. We keep one spare in the store, wrapped in straw and wool. I fitted it myself, with Lantern holding the lamp. The room is colder than it has ever been, and the lamp is clean again.

Longnight, the 5th. Counts. Potatoes 6 sacks. Bread 40 loaves. Coal 9 sacks, and the bin is much too quiet. Lamp glass 4 spares. Lamp oil enough for a year, if nobody gets ideas. Foxes at the door 3, fed 3. Oren must not hear of it.

Deepsnow, the 3rd. I thought about the bees today, which is foolish, because there are no bees within ten days of here. My father kept three hives on the ridge when I was small. In Swarm the first hive threw a swarm, and the swarm went up the windmill and hung from a sail. He climbed the ladder with a box and a cloth, and came down with the bees and not one sting. He laughed for an hour. I laughed too, which I was not supposed to do, because it was his work.

Deepsnow, the 9th. Lantern climbed the tower ladder without being asked, touching every rung. Then it stood in the lamp room and looked south for a long time, the way it does when it is working out a sum. I did not ask. It came down and set the lamp, and the lamp was fine.

Deepsnow, the 18th. Message from Vell, short. The Archive is out of shelves again and has begun a new room in the hill. The clerks are glad. Oren is not glad yet, but he will be, once he has seen it. A copy of this book goes to Vell in spring, Oren says, to be kept.

Deepsnow, the 30th. The last page of the book. Lamps lit. Glass clean. Pell's honey is gone, except one jar, which I have kept for the next cart.
%%
Dara, the Apple Fair at Three Wells
The Apple Fair was held on the square, the way it always is. Dace opened it with his speech, which was short this year, because the cold came early and the children were stamping their feet.

The stalls were full. Ambrook sent barrels of cider and a cart of red apples so big that Pell had to tie his own cart to the back of it. Kettle Ridge sent its pumpkins, which sat in a row by the north well like a crowd of sleepy heads. Saltreach sent dried fish and rope. Everyone said the rope was the best rope they had ever seen.

Sorrel's school had a stall of its own, with paper cranes and little boats made of bark. The boats floated in a tub of water, and the children took turns to push them along with sticks. Bel had made the biggest boat, and it sank, and Bel said it was the boat's own fault.

There was a lantern walk after dark. Everyone carried a lamp from the square to the east well and back, and the children sang the old road song all the way. Orrin came, and sat at the back, and did not sing. He held his lamp very still. When I asked him why, he said he was watching the dark between the lamps. Then he laughed and said he was being foolish.

A traveler stopped at my inn that night. He had come down the north road, he said, with two carts and a boy. He said he had seen lights in the sky above the ridge, the night before the snow. Green and white and moving. Some of the drivers said they were stars falling. Some said they were fires far off. The boy said they were looking at us.

I did not like that. I put him in the room by the stove, and I gave him a second bowl of soup, and I did not ask him to say it again.
%%
Kester, notes from the marsh
Blossom, year 20.
The thaw has left the lower path soft and full of holes. The cart from Three Wells lost a sack of clay there. We found it by a post, with frogs sitting on top as if they owned it.
The crop was poor last year. The beetroot roots stayed small, and the potatoes went soft in the wet ground. Some families took fish for supper most nights. We did not starve, but our bins look bare.
Wren says the reeds are growing well. She cut a bundle for the roof of her shed. I mended her knife and she gave me a bowl of stew.
At the inn, folk spoke of a road on higher ground. It would pass the hamlet and join the Long Road before the marsh. I think it can be done. I have iron enough for the first picks.

Corin, the zombie at the gate
Winter of 203. The 9th of Frost. Clear, bitter, no wind.
Kit woke me at midnight. "There is a zombie at the yard gate." I said that was not unusual in winter. Kit said it had a hat.
I went down with a lantern and a plank. The zombie stood at the gate in a hat, bumping gently into the post. It was not trying to get in. It was trying to find the gate. It never did.
We watched it until the sun came. At first light the sun burned it, as it burns them all. The hat was left on the ground. It is a good hat. It hangs in the shed now. Nobody has asked for it.
Torches lit. Gate shut. Kit sleeps like a stone.

Senna, the tutor and the stream
Kept by Senna, bridge keeper at the Long Road bridge, in spring, in the 196th year of the archive. A tutor from Vell stayed at the inn for two nights and talked at supper. Senna wrote down what she could, in her bridge log, between the notes on the water.

Evening one. The tutor, Hana, sat with her bowl and said she had a new thing to tell. I said: tell. She said: you keep a bridge over a stream. Good. I will talk about a stream.

She said a mind is made of layers, one after another. Each layer looks at what comes in and finds something. In the old way, the layer passed on only what it had found. In the new way, it adds what it found to a stream that runs through the whole mind.

I said: what is the difference?

She said: think of your bridge. Suppose the road ran through a hundred bridges, one after another, and each one swallowed what came in and let out only a little. By the end, there would be nothing. Now suppose the road runs on along the whole length, and each bridge only adds a toll booth. The road stays whole.

Evening two. I asked her why it mattered. She said: because when a mind is wrong, each layer must learn how much blame to take. The blame is passed back from the last layer to the first. In the old way, it faded. In the new way, it runs back along the stream, and every layer hears.

I asked: how do you know it works? She said: the minds with a stream learn when they are deep, and the minds without do not. That is the test.

I asked: do you know why? She said: we have a few ideas. But nobody has written down a final one.

The gift. She left me a small card at the end. It said: Add. Do not take away. I have pinned it above the bridge hut door.

The wrong order. In the morning the inn's apprentice carried a bucket of water to the bridge and poured it in the stream. I asked why. The innkeeper said: I told it to bring water. It misunderstood. It took the water to the water. Hana, who was eating porridge, said: that is how a mind with a stream learns, too. It does not stop for nothing.

Senna
%%
Abel, an apprentice and the word
Winter. A bad day.
We have an apprentice at the farm, a small one from the tutor house. It knows farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul and home. It does not talk.
This morning my wife said, "Take the plough to the barn." The apprentice was in the yard. It took the whole yard to the barn. Not the plough. The pig pen gate, a bucket, two logs and a sack of barley. It carried them across one at a time and set them in a row.
We watched. My wife laughed so hard she sat down in the snow.
Here is what I think happened. The order has two parts. Take means haul, and plough is a thing. The mind picked the meaning haul, which is the right one, and then it hauled whatever was near. It did not know which thing was the plough. The tutors say it is a small mind. It has few words as places.
I said the order again, slowly, and pointed. "Plough. To the barn." The apprentice stopped, looked, and put down the barley. It went to the plough and began to haul it.
That is how a small mind works. One of eight meanings, always. It cannot say it does not know.
The big minds in the relay houses are different. Ask one a thing it does not know, and it will say so. I have seen it. My cousin asked what lay below the ninth gate of the Deep Works. The big mind wrote that it did not know. It wrote that it would not guess, because a guess might put someone in danger.
I like a thing that says so when it does not know. My apprentice will be good at hauling. For the rest I have the relay.
%%
Una, candle book, a homecoming
Frost, the twelfth. Hesk came home from the lower levels today, the first time in a month. He stood in the door with his hat in his hands and his face black with the mine. Then his wife dropped a bowl, and the cat ran under the bench, and Hesk said: Well, the candles are still here.

They are. I lit one in the window every evening, so he could see it from the last bend of the path. He says it was the only light on the hill he was sure was not a miner's lamp.

Frost, the fourteenth. He is up, and cross, and home. He says one candle a night is a waste of tallow. Then he stood at the window and looked at the stub of the last one for a long time.
%%
Innkeeper's winter book
Reaping brought the fruit carts through the Long Road. Una of Ambrook came with them, carrying a crystal in a wool wrap. She asked for a table, two baskets, and a quiet hour. We had none of those things ready, but the bread was done and the benches were clear.

She set apples on one side and pears on the other. Then she showed the crystal a fruit. It had to choose one basket. It chose badly more than once.

Una explained the lesson while she worked. The crystal had weights that could be changed. Its answer came from those weights. When the answer was wrong, she moved every weight a little toward the right answer. Then she tried again with another fruit.

I asked why she moved all the weights, even the ones that seemed to have done no harm. She said the answer came from them together. Each had some part in it. She could not point to one and say that one alone had made the mistake.

We watched the weights change by small amounts. A man from the road asked whether she was teaching the crystal the names of fruit. Una said she was teaching it to choose between the two baskets from the examples she gave it. She did not claim to know what the crystal understood.

For several days she brought the same lesson to the inn. She kept some fruit aside and did not show it to the crystal until the end. That was how she checked whether it had learned the task, rather than just repeated the last answer.

The first time, it missed a pear. The next time, it sorted every fruit right. The innkeeper's son was cross because he had sorted the same tray by hand and missed two. I told him the crystal had been drilled many times, and he had been asked once, after carrying water.

A borrowed horse stood at the stable while Una worked. Its owner had gone to Ambrook with a cart and left the horse in my care. In Frost, the first snow came during the night. In the morning the horse pushed its nose through the half open door and took a mouthful from the hay basket. I shut the door and gave it a proper feed.

The snow covered the road edges but not the waymarks. A traveler came in wet and said the ridge had gone white. We hung his cloak by the hearth. Una wrapped the crystal in wool before she left. She said the cold was not good for it, though she did not know why.

Years later, a tutor from Vell asked me what Una had done. I told him what I saw: she showed the crystal apples and pears, and each time it sorted one wrongly, she moved each weight a little toward the right answer. The crystal learned to sort better than the children who had not been shown the lesson.

The tutor wrote it down. I hope he also wrote about the horse. It was a good horse, though it knew the latch better than I did.

Senna, the gift and the wrong order
Kept by Senna, bridge keeper, at the inn by the Long Road bridge, over two days of spring. In short.

Day one. A traveler left a gift on my bridge hut step: a wool hat, green, and a note. The note said: for the keeper, with thanks for the dry feet. I do not know who sent it. I wear it.

Day two. The inn's apprentice misunderstood an order. The innkeeper said: bring me some bread. The apprentice brought him a barrel. The innkeeper said: no, bread. The apprentice put the barrel down and fetched a second. Innkeeper gave up. We rolled the barrels to the cellar. They were empty. Nobody knows why they were on the shelf in the first place.

Note: I have no idea what the apprentice was thinking. The tutor says it is not thinking. It is matching. She says the next one will do better.

Ossa's book, the miners' talk
Frost. A quiet month.
The miners came up from the Deep Works at the end of the shift, and they did not go into the inn. They stood by the shaft head in the cold, all of them, in a row. Their lamps were out. I walked past on my way to the new house on the hill, and one of them, an old man named Pell, called me over.
He said the deep stone is warm now. He said it the way you would say a bad bruise is warm. He said that if you put your hand flat on the wall at the ninth level, it is like a loaf from the oven, and it has been like that for a month.
I asked if it was the forge. He said the forge is nowhere near. I asked if it was the water. He said the water is cold, and it runs away from the wall. I asked if anyone had been hurt. He said no. He said they had all stopped going below the sixth gate. He said it quietly, as if he were not sure he should have told me.
Then the other miners started talking about a humming. They said it was not loud. They said it was like a bee in a jar, very far off, and it came up through the rock at night. They said it stopped when the wind came round. Some of them said it was the wind. None of them said that.
I went home and sat with my hands in my lap. My own dog was lying by the stove, and his ears were up.

Oren, market log, the apple bet
Longnight of 202. Cold. The market in the square under the hill.

I came down at the first bell to buy apples for the copying room. Nessa's apprentice was at the apple stall with Wick's boy. The apprentice's name is Quill. It sorts the apples into three bins: good, bruised, and for the press.

Nessa says Quill will sort seven baskets in a row without one mistake. I say it will not. I say a mind that cannot tell a bruise from a good apple has no business in a market.

Nessa said that was a rude thing to say, in front of the apples.

So we have a bet. A jar of honey from the Ambrook hives. If Nessa is right, I buy the honey. If I am right, she buys it. We shook hands.

This morning Quill sorted a basket of forty apples. It got nine of them wrong. It put a bruised apple in the good bin, very sure of itself. It put a good apple in the press bin, equally sure. Nessa says that is only the first day.

I think we will keep this bet, too.

Fern, healing house, Ambrook
Apple month, 195.

Day one. A letter came from Vell today, from the tutor Nessa. She asks if we have room for two clerks who need quiet to copy. I said yes. We always have room. The roof still leaks over the east bed but the west one is dry.

Day two. The clerks came. Their names are Unna and Beck. They carry a box of pages and a small mind in a crystal, wrapped in wool. Unna says the crystal must not get cold. I put it on the shelf by the hearth, above the jars.

Day three. Beck explained the rule of growth to me while I pounded honey and thyme. He said a mind is like a child in one way. A bigger crystal holds more, and a mind drilled on more writing for longer grows better. Isla at Vell found it. She drew a curve on a board before the mind was grown, and the mind came out on the curve. Like marking a child's height on the door, but the child is not born yet. I did not follow all of it. Beck said nobody follows all of it. He said the councils are building relay houses now because of the curve. Bigger minds need a house.

Day four. Unna read to the crystal from an old record about bees. She says the mind reads one letter at a time. It guesses the next letter. When it is wrong she does not scold it. She only shows it the right letter and goes on. It is like teaching a child to read, she said, if the child never got tired.

Day five. A zombie at the gate after dark. Old Jory's dog barked and Jory took his hoe and the zombie was gone before I found my boots. We keep the torches lit along the mill pond now. The apples are heavy this year. The oldest tree by the bridge dropped three windfalls on the path.

Day six. The harvest fair is set for the ninth. Dace from Three Wells is coming with a cart of pies. Unna says they will finish copying before the fair and go home to Vell. I will miss them. The crystal has learned the bees well. It reads the word honey now without stopping.

Dell, first frost
The first of Apple. First frost. The school path was white this morning, and across it ran a line of fox tracks so neat that Runa said somebody had drawn them with a ruler. Mira says the foxes know it is the dry way round the pond. She says they are only being sensible.

We walked to school in their tracks. Nobody fell. The foxes had chosen well.

Perrin, festival day log
Festival at the relay house today. The snow has gone from the road, though it remains in the north ditch. The keeper hung wool flags between the house and the tower. The wind turned them around until every flag faced a different town.

The tutor came from Vell with two apprentices and a box of pages. She showed us how the reading mind is drilled. It reads from left to right, one letter at a time. Its window holds about sixty letters. When it reaches a new letter, it can look back at earlier letters that matter. It uses them to guess the next letter.

The mind has many layers. Each layer passes along what it has found, so the later layers can use the earlier work. The mind's weights change as it is drilled. If a guess is wrong, the drill compares it with the right letter and changes the weights a little. The mind does not keep a list of every page. It learns patterns in the writing.

The tutor gave it a fresh page from the school. It guessed the next letters until it made a sentence about a lost mitten. Bel laughed and said the mitten belonged to her. Then the mind wrote a reply to the tutor. It was polite and clear. Some people said no clerk could tell it from a person's letter.

I asked if that meant it could think as we do. The tutor said it brought Orla's question back, but gave no answer. I liked that answer better than a grand claim.

There was bread, honey, and beetroot soup. Lantern carried bowls to the old folk. It carried one too many to the tutor, who ate it without complaint.

Vane's book, the lost goat
Leaffall. Rain at night, fog by the water.
Our goat is gone. She was tied at the boat house on the far path, and when the rope was found in the morning, it was chewed through at the end. Nobody saw her go. Nobody heard her either, which is strange for a goat.
The boys went out with lanterns. They called her by her name, which is Brisk, and she came to nobody. They looked in the reeds, in the old boats, under the boardwalk. They found a frog that had been sitting on a sack of barley all night and looked very pleased with itself. They did not find Brisk.
At dusk the mother of the boy who brought the melon came in and said she had seen a goat on the road to the Brown Hen, going fast in the fog, with a bell round her neck and a torch in her mouth. Nobody believed her. Then Hobb's carter came in and said the same, only he said the torch was a carrot top.
We are not going to look again tonight. The fog is too thick. We will leave the lamp in the kitchen door and the gate open, and in the morning someone will go to the Brown Hen.

Gwen, farm log, a week of Apple
Sallow Farm, the valley road outside Copperhold. Written by Gwen, who keeps the stone bridge over the Sallow brook in the morning and the farm in the afternoon.

Day one. Cold in the mornings, warm at noon. The brook is low. I crossed on the bridge at dawn, counted the arches, and found the third one wet on top. The mason will need to see it. Wrote it on the bridge slate for Ferris.

Day two. Pressed the apples in the yard. Forty baskets. The press squeaked like a gate, and Rudd sang along to it. The children ate more than they pressed. Juice on everyone. Juice on the cat.

Day three. Rain, all day, light and grey. The hens would not come out. We left the door open anyway, so they could think about it.

Day four. Took the cider to the Copperhold market in the cart. Sold nine jugs, two to the foreman at the Deep Works, who paid in copper, which is heavy. Carried the copper home in a sack. My shoulder is still complaining.

Day five. Dib, the farm apprentice, had its first full week on the baskets. It is good with weight and bad with words, so I told it: Carry the baskets to the cellar, and put them in the row by the stairs. It carried them. All forty. It put them in the row by the stairs, one above the other, and then went to bed in the cellar with its arms round the top basket. Nobody knows why. Rudd says it is still learning what a row is.

Day six. Sold two sacks of late potatoes to the bakery. Sold the good barrel of apples to the inn at the bridge. Kept the bad ones for the pigs, who think they are very fine.

Day seven. Dib came to me this morning and asked, very politely, if it could have a new order. I said of course. It said: stay with the baskets until they are not in the cellar any more. I said that was a very good order. I did not tell it that the baskets would not be in the cellar until we ate them. It will find that out soon enough.
%%
Oren, notes from the west hamlet
Apple, 193. I left Vell after the first meal with three record boxes and a clean cloth. The road was bright with fallen leaves. At the hamlet, I found the miller weighing apples and his daughter trying to weigh a cat.
The cat had climbed into an empty basket. It was not helpful as a measure. The daughter said it weighed one cat. The miller said it weighed one cat and a basket. They were both correct.
I put the new seed list on the shelf above the flour bins. The old list had a dark mark across the page. The miller said it was a beet stain. His daughter said it was a map of the marsh. I wrote beet stain in the shelf book. The map may be true as well.
The wheel ran slowly in the morning. The stream was low, and leaves had gathered at the sluice. We pulled them out with a rake. The miller pulled from the bank. I pulled from the bridge. The cat watched from the warm stone by the door.
A fishing boat from the little pond came home late. It had gone out before dawn to set nets where the water meets the reeds. At dusk, the families began to worry. The boat returned after dark with two fish, a torn net, and one oar tied to the side.
The boatman said the wind had pushed them toward the far bank. He had not lost the way. He had lost the afternoon. His wife took the fish and left him the torn net to mend.
We lit the lamps by the mill door and checked the boat for leaks. There was one small crack. The miller packed it with oakum and wax. The boatman promised to mend the net before breakfast. His wife said she would believe him after breakfast.
I copied the day's notes before going home. Oren, if you are reading this, the shelf is dry and the cat is not a book.

Dace, market journal, the gift
Sowing, the eleventh day. The gift from the Archive came up the road in a box, sealed with the archive seal. It is a copy of the first page, for the school. It says: We will keep everything we write. It also says that it rained.

Sowing, the twelfth day. Ama gave the mayor a jar of honey from the first comb of the swarm, and said it was a gift for the market and not for the mayor. The mayor thanked her without a speech. Ama says that is the first time that has happened in her life.

Sowing, the thirteenth day. Garrow gave the cave diggers a whole cheese. The diggers ate half on the stair. The other half went down the rope in a basket, as a gift for whatever is below. The basket is sealed with wax, and the wax is good.

Sowing, the fifteenth day. A carter from Ambrook gave the school a bell. It rings like a spoon. The children hung it on the rope at the cave, so the cave now has a bell at each end, and the children say it is very well equipped.

Sowing, the twentieth day. The gifts are kept in a box with a lid, and the children may look at them on the second day of each week. The box is not full. The box is only waiting.

Sable, the Ambrook apple carts, Apple
Written by Sable at the Halfway Inn. Mam says the carts count as news, so they count as writing.

Apple, fourth day. Nine carts came down from Ambrook before the bell. Each cart had a hundred apples and a boy on top to keep them from falling off.

Apple, fifth day. We bought four carts at eight emeralds each. The boys counted the money twice, out loud, to be sure.

Apple, sixth day. Pie, forty-one. Mam says the travelers eat pie the way the riddle board eats questions: all at once, and then quietly for an hour.

Apple, seventh day. Hob came down with three jars of honey and a drawing of his bees. He says the quiet hives are the ones to watch. I did not know what to say back, so I wrote it down.

Apple, eighth day. Rain. The windmill on the ridge road does not turn in the rain. Its sails hang like a tired cat's ears.

Odo, roof book: the wrong step
Leaffall, the 9th. Rain.
I asked the mind about another roof, a long one with a bay. It wrote its steps again. At the third step it wrote: "Wait. I wrote that 7 times 13 is 81. Check: 13 times 7 is 91. I was wrong. Correct it. The answer is 182."
I stood at the desk for a while. I have worked forty years beside men who write 81 and never look again. Here is a mind that writes 81, looks again, and says it was wrong. Linnet the keeper says it does this every time. I said that is more than most of my crew. I have put the page up on the wall of the shed. Under it I have written: Look again.

Tam, farm log: the whole sentence
Tam, farm log, the top farm. Apple, the eleventh day.

Today Pip understood a whole sentence. I want to set it down plainly, because the tutors will ask me, and I will get it wrong in the telling if I wait.

We have had Pip for two years. It knows eight words for orders: farm, store, fetch, come, stay, build, haul, home. Each order is one of those words, and each word is one job. Fetch the water. Come to the gate. Haul the sacks. That is all it has ever been given.

This morning I said, out loud, not on a card: "Take Butter to the barn, and then come back to the gate."

That is two jobs in one sentence. Take the cow, and then come back. I said it once. I did not show it the words written down, and I did not say it twice.

Pip looked at me. Then it went to Butter, put a hand on her halter, and walked her to the barn. It went slowly, because she is slow, and it did not hurry her. In the barn it let go of the halter, turned round, and walked back across the yard to the gate, and stood there.

It did both. The cow in the barn first, and then the gate.

The tutors call this the next thing: a small mind that understands a whole sentence, and not one word at a time. The texts disagree on who did it first. The Copperhold book says Clank, at the mine gate. The fair book says Peg. Nessa says she remembers one in the drill room at Vell, when she was young. I do not know who was first. I know Pip did it today, on my farm, and that I was the one who said the sentence.
%%
Jem, the tower on the hill
Summer, the twelfth year of the Archive.

The first of Melon. Men came to the hill above our farm with stakes. They are going to build a relay tower. It will be the tallest thing for half a day's walk. Father says it will make our hill famous. Gran says it will make our hill noisy.

The third. They dug the footing. Stone comes up the road on carts from Copperhold, grey and square.

The ninth. A helper came from the tutor house at Vell. It is made of copper and iron, and it knows how to carry stone and nothing else. It takes one stone from the pile and walks up the hill and puts it down where the builders point, and walks back for another. It does not stop. It does not say anything. Ree called it Hod. At dusk the tutor opened its chest and took out the crystal and put it in a box, and Hod stood still. In the morning he put the crystal back, and it woke and began again.

The fourteenth. The wall is up to my head. Hod has carried six hundred stones. I counted. Hod cannot count. It just carries.

The twenty-second. The top. The glass for the lamp room came wrapped in straw, every pane in its own box, and a ring of copper to hold them. Six men carried the panes up the stair, one at a time, and nobody breathed.

The twenty-fifth. The keeper came. Her name is Dell, like the girl in my class, but older. She has a desk at the foot of the tower with a red line running down to it from the lamp. She showed me the lever. She said I may not touch it. I did not touch it. I looked at it very hard.

The twenty-ninth. First light. The whole farm stood at the foot of the hill at dusk. Dell moved the lever, and the lamp lit. It was so bright that Gran said, oh. Then it blinked, and far away at Vell, another lamp blinked back. Dell said the message was all well. It had gone to Vell and back in the time it takes to boil an egg.

Gran said, in my day that took a whole morning. Father cried a little. He said it was dust. There was no dust.

Hod stood at the foot of the tower the whole time. I think it looked up. I know it did not. But I think it did.
%%
Orla, the market and the farm, spring
Orla, grandmother, the farm outside Three Wells. Written in the evenings, a few lines at a time, through the first month of the year's work. For my granddaughter Pia, who will want to know.

First of Sowing. A thaw, at last. The ditch is full. The lane is mud. The hens came out to look at it, and went back in.

Fourth. Ploughed the near field. The horse limped, and I walked him to the pond and soaked his foot. By evening he was better. By evening I was not.

Seventh. Sowed peas and beet. Planted a row of beans by the south wall, where the sun is warm. Pia helped. She planted a bean upside down. I did not tell her. We shall see what it does.

Tenth. Market day. I was up before dawn. Eggs: sixty. Cheese: eight rounds. Honey: four jars. Cloth from the loom: two lengths. We loaded the cart and went in by the Long Road. A good morning, cold and clear.

At the market, a stall by the east well was selling blue ribbons. Pia wanted one. I said it was a waste. She said it was for her bean. I bought it.

The lamp on the tower was dark. The keeper was up on the stair, mending the glass. People stood below and called up advice. None of it was any use.

Sold everything but two cheeses. Came home with an empty cart, a blue ribbon, and a pocketful of emeralds. The emeralds are for the new pump.

Eleventh. The bean has come up, upside down. It has found its way. Pia is delighted. I am less surprised than I pretend.

Fourteenth. A message came by runner. The lamp is lit again, but the keeper says it blinks slowly. She says she will mend it again after the market. Some say it is the glass. Some say it is the keeper's hands, which are tired. I say it is spring, and everything is a little slow.

Sixteenth. Pea shoots. A frost last night, and the shoots held. I put a cloth over them. In the morning, the cloth was white, and the shoots were green.

Eighteenth. A letter from my sister at Kettle Ridge. She says the wheat is early. She says Tam has a new field. She says his Pip has learned to wave. I do not know what to make of that. I put it in the journal anyway.

I am tired but well. The farm is awake. It is a good time of year.
%%
Una, candle book, midwinter
Longnight, the twentieth, which is the shortest day, and the longest night of the year. The house is full of candles. Every window has one. The children have made a ring of them on the kitchen table, each with a little paper flag, and they say it is the sun, lit for the dark part of the year.

Longnight, the twenty-first. Midwinter. The whole of Copperhold came up the hill with lanterns, past the old mine gate and down the new road, and into the square. The miners carried their own candles, which I made, and they lit them from the big fire in the square, and the square was all light, the first time I have seen it so. The children sang the short song. The old men sang the long one, and got the words wrong, and nobody minded.

Hesk stood beside me with his candle lit, very straight, and did not sing. He had a look I have not seen in him before. When I asked him what it was, he said it was the look of a man who had come up from a long way under the ground and found the square still there.

On the feast night the old men told the story of the first candle in Copperhold. It was lit in a cave by a child who was afraid of the dark, and it burned so long that the child forgot to be afraid, and by morning the child had found the way out. Nobody can say whether it happened. The story is told every midwinter, and the children always ask whether the child was given a candle to keep, and the old men always say yes.

Longnight, the twenty-second. Cleaning up after the feast. Forty stubs in the square, and a single candle left burning on the step of the old mine gate, which nobody will put out. The foreman says it was lit by a child, and that the child was right to light it, and that the candle will burn itself out by dawn. I think it will last longer than that.

Deepsnow, the first. The new year's first sun, weak and white, came up over the ridge at mid-morning. The candles in the window went out one by one, and nobody lit them again, because it was day. I counted the stubs on the table. One hundred and twelve. Each one a night of light, somewhere in Copperhold, for somebody who needed to see the stair.

Deepsnow, the third. Made a fresh batch of wicks for the lower stair. Measured every one, finger-length, neither too long nor too short. The wax is clean, the tallow is rendered, and the candle house is warm. Pim, the cat, has found the warmest shelf in the house, which is the one above the kitchen. He has been given a candle of his own, unlit, which he is sitting beside with great dignity.
%%
The Tarn house book, first months
Second of Blossom.
We moved into the house at the edge of Vell today. It is ours. It has a door and a roof and a well. The well was dug by Lorn and his boy Fen and old Rill. The water is cold and quick and good. We drank from the first bucket.
Nell said the house is too big for three. I said we will grow into it. We have a cat now. The cat is called Moss. Moss is grey and Moss does not care about the house.
Tenth of Blossom.
The hens are here. Six of them. They are brown and they are loud and they do not care about the house either. The cat looks at the hens. The hens look at the cat. Nothing happens. It is a good arrangement.
Second of Swarm.
An apprentice came up the road today. It is made of copper and iron and it is shorter than me. It was sent by the tutors at Vell. The note says it is drilled on carrying and on the well. The note says to test it first. We tested it. It carried a bucket of water from the well to the door and did not spill a drop. We told it to keep going. It kept going until the barrel was full. Then it stood by the barrel and waited. It is a good worker. It does not talk. I do not mind.
Twentieth of Swarm.
I told the apprentice to bring the hens in. I meant at night, because of the fox. It brought the hens in at noon. All six of them. They were not happy. Nell laughed for a long time. The apprentice stood in the yard and waited. I think it did not know it was wrong. I think it did what I said. I will say it better next time. That is what the tutor's note says to do.
First of Longday.
The apprentice is called Tarn now. Nell named it, because it lives here. It does not answer to the name. It answers to an order. But we call it Tarn and it comes. That is the same thing, mostly.
The summer is good. The water is cold. The fox has not been back. The cat and the hens have an arrangement.
%%
Ambrook market, Blossom 18
Blossom, 18. Ambrook market.
I brought three jars of honey from the hill. The jars were warm from the sun. A boy asked if honey could be put on beetroot. His mother said it could, if he ate it himself.
The market stands by the mill pond today. Jory's wheel turned all morning. The loose plank on the bridge still lifts at one end. I stepped on it and got one wet boot.
Pell came in on the Long Road with salt and wool. He says the road to Three Wells is firm now. They laid cobblestone at the worst bend. A new waymark stands there, with a torch on top and an arrow to the Brown Hen.
I bought apples for Tam. He will say they are too soft, then eat them all.
%%
Lenn's summer log, the mill pond
Reaping, 203. Hot. The pond is low and the fish go deep where it is cool. I catch what I need and sit in the shade of the willow.
Jory's wheel turns dawn to dusk. It squeaks at the top of every turn. You stop hearing it after a while and then you hear it again. The sluice stays shut, like always. Nobody opens the sluice. Jory says it once and everyone knows it.
A fisher from Saltreach came up the Long Road with a cart of salted fish. He looked at my pond and laughed. He said it is not a sea. I said the sea is just a big pond with a bad temper. He liked that. We shared a pie.
