Tam's letter to Mira
Spring of 207.
Dear Mira,
Spring at last. The wind off the ridge is still cold but the fields are green and I have stopped wearing two coats. Pip is doing well. Half a farmer now.

The horse. You will like this. Pell lent us his horse Nut to plow and Nut would not plow. Nut went into the hedge every time I said go. Sideways. Every time. I stood in the field shouting go at a horse that was eating a hedge and Pip sat down in the dirt laughing. And then Pell came back and laughed at me and I laughed too. Nut is a good horse, Pell says. I have my doubts. But she pulled the cart fine when the wheel was mended, so maybe the plow was the trouble and not the horse. I do not know. I am a farmer, not a horse.

The fence. You remember the old fence with the Cooper field, three posts gone. So there is a quarrel. Master Cooper and I stood at the gap saying the same things at each other and Mother brought bread and we ate it and kept saying them. Old Fenn walked the line with his rope and said a number. Cooper said a different number. Dace has called a walking of the line and the archive is sending a copy of the old field lines. I think the archive will settle it. I hope so. Cooper is a good neighbor and I would like to be friends again before the harvest.

The bees are out and a swarm on the plum tree. Pip held the box and got stung on the thumb and says now she is a real beekeeper. Between you and me she is right.

How is Northpoint? You never write about the cold. You write about the lamps. I know you, so I will ask plainly. Is it very cold? Do you have enough coal? I have sent a sack on the Long Road with Pell. Do not say it is too much. It is one sack.

Write soon. Tell Lantern I said hello. Tell the wind to stop.
Your brother,
Tam
%%
Dace's notice, first snow of 200
Summer of 200, the last day of Melon.
People of Three Wells,
The first snow came this morning, early, before the month turned. It did not lie long, but it came. So the market moves under the awnings from now on.
New rules on the board, for the cold:
No fires in the tents. Fires only in the square braziers.
No pigs in the square at all, snow or no snow.
The wells are covered at night. Do not lift the covers for fun.
The school will keep its doors shut till the bell. Children, wait for the bell.
I have had word from the tutor house at Vell. Some of the small minds can now be written to fit a smaller crystal. This is good news for the market. It means a stall can have a mind to fetch and store without paying for a big one. The tutors say the small ones are a little worse. For carrying apples, a little worse is fine.
Pell the merchant has brought a new mind for his cart. He says it cost him less than a horse and eats nothing. I asked him if it minds the cold. He said it does not mind anything, which is either good or sad, and I have not decided which.
Keep the torches lit.
Dace, mayor.
%%
Carys, the news from Copperhold
Northpoint, year 153. Frost. Sunny but bitter.

The relay lamp brought a long message this morning. The keeper read it aloud at the market, because everyone had gathered to hear.

It was from Copperhold, a tutor named Merle. She has found a new way to teach minds. Before this, a crystal could learn only simple things, one answer for one question. Now she has put crystals in layers, one feeding the next, and the layered mind can learn harder things.

The market was quiet for a moment. Then old Garrow said, "Is that all?" And everyone laughed.

But I thought about it on my way home. I am an old soldier. I know what a line of sentries is. The first one sees a thing and calls to the second, who calls to the third, and so on to the gate. If the gate is wrong, somebody in the line was wrong. You cannot blame the gate alone.

I do not know if Merle thinks of it that way. But I will tell it to the children, if they ask.
%%
Emmet, the cave under the pasture
Sowing, the twenty-second. The children found a hole in Dell's pasture today. The ground gave way under the youngest one, Tobe, and he went in up to the waist and came out shouting that there was a river down there. There was not a river. There was a cave, with clay walls, and a pool of very cold water at the bottom.

Dell sent for me because I have a rope and a sense of duty. I went down with a lantern. The cave goes down in a crooked line, slippery, and then opens into a little room where the water lies flat as a plate. Frogs live in the pool. I do not know how they got so far from the marsh. Wren says they must have followed the water. Dell says they must have been invited.

There is copper in the wall at the far end. I could see the green stain of it in the lamp, and a streak of blue. Tobe found a lump of it lying in the mud and carried it up in his pocket. He will not let go of it. He sleeps with it under his pillow, and says it is for luck.

Sowing, the twenty-fourth. The miners came from Copperhold with their picks and a cart. They said the cave is small, but the copper is good, and they would like the first share. Dell said the first share would be for her pasture. The miners laughed and said nobody had ever won an argument with a pasture. Dell did not laugh.

Sowing, the twenty-seventh. Dell has put a fence round the hole and a sign on the fence. The sign says: Mind the edge. Under it, in smaller letters, Tobe has added: The frogs are friendly. Nobody has yet been able to tell him that the frogs are not his to say.
%%
Minutes: the harvest council
Minutes of the harvest council, held in the Saltreach fish hall, in the autumn of the year the wheel was lost. Kept by Tova, who was asked to write because she has the neatest hand in the market.

Present: the harbor master, the mayor's man, the millers from the seaward mill, Corin the shipwright, the fish sellers, three boatmen and Tova, egg seller.

Item one. The seaward mill wheel. It was agreed that the wheel will be made again from oak, the old way. The millers asked for it to be made larger, to take the storms. Corin said this was not a wheel problem, it was a wall problem. The breakwater had been set too low on the north side. The wind came over it and onto the wheel. The council agreed to ask the masons to raise the wall by one course of stone.

Item two. The yawl and the furnace mind. The tutor from Vell has asked to take the yawl's mind up the river to Copperhold, to see whether it can keep a barge straight in the current. The council asked whether this was safe. Corin said it was as safe as anything that floats. The harbor master said that was not an answer. Corin said it was the only answer a shipwright can give. It was agreed to try it, with two men aboard, and with the boat tied to the bank for the first week.

Item three. The salt cart. The council heard that the cart from Copperhold was three days late. The harbor master said the road is the road, and nobody can hurry it. It was agreed to send a boy up the hill to see where the cart stopped.

Item four. The egg stall. Tova asked for the price of eggs to be left as it is. The council agreed.

The meeting closed at the noon bell. Tova is to write the minutes fair and to file them with the harbor master.

Nessa's farm book
Summer. The fields are gold at the far edge. The beans are in flower.
I found a page in the great box today that does not belong to the drill. It was tucked between two lists, very old, with a child's writing on the back. The writing was cramped and careful. It said: If anyone reads this, please tell my mother I am sorry I took the honey. I will bring it back.
I did not feed this page to the reading mind. I put it in the box by itself, and I wrote on the front that it was not for the drill. Oren says the clerks have a rule about pages like that. They are kept, but set apart, and no mind is ever drilled on them. He says the archive will keep everything, and it will also keep some things from being used.
I asked him why. He said he did not know. He said that the first clerks had written this rule in the margin of the old book, in a hand so faded that he could barely read it. He had only copied it out. He had not been able to make it mean anything.
I am glad the rule is there. I think the child would be glad too.
%%
Iona, a letter from the relay house
A letter came down the line on the fifteenth of Apple, from the relay house at Vell. It was addressed to me, the orchard keeper, which surprised me.

It asked whether the grey branch was still bearing, and how many apples the oldest tree had given that autumn. It asked the way a careful clerk asks, with one small question after another. It asked whether I minded being asked.

I checked the orchard book. The number was right. The branch was right. Even the date of the first frost was right.

I did not know what to write back. The keeper said the big minds write letters that some readers cannot tell from a person's. I could not tell. I wrote back that the grey branch was still bearing, and that the number was forty. I signed my name, and I did not ask whether the answer was right.

Nessa, drill room notes
Apple, 12, 207.
Today we tested three minds on the same order cards. The cards were new to them. We had not used them in their drills.

> carry the red cloth to the bench
= fetch cloth

> wait beside the door
= stay

> bring the empty basket to Moll
= fetch basket

The first mind carried the cloth to the bench. The second waited by the door. The third brought the basket to me. It had been taught to notice that the basket was empty. It had not been taught those sentences word for word.

This is the next thing: a mind may learn what a whole order means, though the order is said in a new way. It does not make the mind a person. It makes it more useful, and harder to test.

At supper, Moll said the third mind had brought her the wrong basket. She had meant the small one. The card only said basket. I have added a card for size.

Apple, 17.
A fair copy of the drill went to the Archive. Oren asked us to keep the first card, even after I write a clearer one. He says the archive must hold the mistake as well as the correction. The words over its door say we will keep everything we write. Oren takes them seriously, even when the writing is mine.

Marl, Northpoint, Longday to Melon
Written by Marl, for the book at the relay house. Longday and Melon, in the short grass, with the sun late and slow.

Longday, the fourth. The sun does not go down until well after the bell. It goes behind the hill, and the sky stays pale over the north sea until nearly the middle of the night. The foxes come out at that hour. Mira says they are the only ones who keep their own time.

Longday, the tenth. The grass in the yard is short and sweet. Cut it in the morning, and in the afternoon the sheep from the north pen come and eat the edge. Lantern watches the sheep as if they might tell it something. They do not.

Longday, the sixteenth. A cart of grey salt came up the Long Road from the Saltreach pans. The cook says it is the best salt in the north, and he has said so since the first cart, and he may be right.

Longday, the twentieth. Mira had a letter from Tam at Kettle Ridge. It says the wheat is high, the windmill squeaks, and Pip has learned the word for beetroot. She read it out at the hearth, and then again, and then she put it in the book of marks, where she keeps the things she wants to keep.

Melon, the first. Melons came up the Long Road from the south on a cart, eight of them, wrapped in wet straw. Mira shared the first one with the foxes at the foot of the tower. They took the rind and left the seeds in a neat pile by the step.

Melon, the third. The first berries on the north slope. I ate too many, and was sick by the well, which is the first lesson of berries. Mira says she taught me that once. I do not remember the lesson. I remember the sick.

Melon, the twelfth. The heat in the afternoon, and cool at night. Lantern has been doing the glass each morning and each evening, and the glass is so clean the foxes walk up to it and look in. Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed.

Mira, a birth at the neighbor's
Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, in her house at the edge of the town, in spring, year 204. She does not often write about people, but this was a night she did not want to lose.
The neighbors across the lane, Halle and Brand, were to have their first child in the spring. The midwife was away at Saltreach. The snow had gone, the stream was high, and the lane was a river.
On the night of the eleventh of Sowing, Brand knocked on my door. His face was white. He said, "Mira, it is coming."
I said, "Is the midwife coming?"
He said, "She cannot get through the flood."
I put on my coat. I took the lamp. I have helped lambs, and foxes, and once a cow. I have never helped a person. Lantern came with me, carrying a basket of linen and a jar of honey, because it is what the tutor says you bring.
Halle was on the bed. She was calm. She said, "I am glad you came. Brand is no use."
I held her hand. Brand held the lamp, and trembled. Lantern stood at the door and kept the draft out, which was all it could do, and it did it very well.
I sent a message by relay. I ran up the tower stair, ninety rungs, and blinked a Haste note to Vell: "Birth at Northpoint. Midwife needed. Flood on the lane. Send word of what to do." The answer came back through the night, letter by letter, from a healer at Lowmarsh named Wren. It was short and plain. I wrote it on my arm.
I came down. I did what the note said.
The child came at dawn. A girl. She was loud, and red, and perfect. Halle cried. Brand fainted, politely, onto the bed. I cleaned my hands in the basin and sat down on the floor.
They are calling her Dawn. I said it was a good name. I said it was a very good name.
I went home and slept for nine hours. When I woke, Lantern had put a cup of tea by my bed, and the stream had gone down.
Wren sent a letter a week later. It said: "You did well. Next time, wash your hands first." I had.
%%
Nessa, fair drill log
Spring of 215. The tutors set a fair at Three Wells.
We carried three small crystals in dry boxes. Each had learned the same farm orders. The judges gave them new lines from the school children, not lines from our drill books. One crystal sent an apprentice to the wheat field when the words asked for beetroot. One sent it to fetch a hoe. The third got the work right.
Its tutor, Sella, won a brass cup and a purse of emeralds. She said the cup was too small for soup. The children laughed, and Bel asked if she could keep the emeralds for the school.
After the fair, the judges brought out a page from the test box. None of the crystals had seen it before. The third still made a mistake. Sella looked pleased. She said a test that finds no mistakes has not looked hard enough.
A messenger from the great relay house watched from beside the notice board. He said their mind had won every test they could give it. I asked how many tutors had checked the tests. He said the council had stopped counting.
The children wanted to know if the mind was cleverer than all of us. I told them that a large crystal can hold more weights, and a long drill can teach it more examples. Neither tells us what it will do with a question it has never met.

Wren's farm book
Frost. The rain has stopped at last, after forty days of it. The marsh smells of mud and iron.
I walked out to the high field to see the water. It was still high and brown, and it sat over the reeds like a tablecloth that had been pulled too far. The frogs were gone, mostly. A few were left on the posts, sitting very still, waiting for the sun.
I thought about the lost letter, and the stuck cart, and the cake that broke. I thought about how small each thing was, and how each one had to be put right by hand, before the next came along. The bridge is sound. The letter is safe. The bees are asleep. The soup is hot.
Ros came up the path with her boy behind her. She asked if I had any honey left for the winter. I said I had enough, and that she should come round for a jar on the last market day. She said she would, and she would bring the potatoes, the good ones that did not fall in the ditch.
Then we both stood there for a while, looking at the water. Neither of us said the bridge was rotten. Neither of us needed to.

Wren's farm book, the honey cake
Frost. Warm noon, very cold dusk.
Moss made honey cakes today for the first time on its own. I had not shown it how. I had only told it the pan was hot and the honey was thick, and to keep the cakes off the window ledge, where the flies go.
It made twelve. They were all the same size, which I did not expect. It had cut the dough with the bone knife, in straight lines, and it had put a dab of honey on each one, in the same spot, like a little brand. The honey was the right thickness.
One of the cakes broke in the pan. Moss looked at it for a long time and then picked it up and put the pieces in its own cup. It said nothing. I think it was sorry.
We ate the broken one together, sitting by the stove. It was very good. I told Moss so, and I told it that the broken ones are always the best, which is not quite true, but it made Moss so happy that it stood up and fetched a second one.
The honey cakes are for the sick children on the ward. The ward was quiet today. The children asked for the soup apprentice, and Moss took them their broth in a cup with a lid. They said the lid was the best part.

Vell, Moll's autumn notes
Apple, 4, 207.
Nessa brought the order drill to the tutors' house. I sat in the hall with my knitting and listened. She had written one sentence on a card, then its meaning below it.

> bring the bread to the hall
= fetch bread

The apprentice took the bread from the tray and brought it to the hall. Nessa said the mind had learned the whole order, not just the word bread.

I asked if it knew the hall. Nessa said it had been shown the hall in drills. I asked if it knew why we wanted bread there. She said that was another question.

Apple, 9.
I sent my small apprentice to fetch a cup. It brought the cup. Then I asked it to bring me a cup that was not on the table. It stood by the table. A small mind must choose one of its eight meanings. It cannot say, "I do not know."

Nessa says we must test what a mind has not been shown. I say we must also keep our cups where we can find them.

Nessa, last notes from the shore house
The summer is nearly done. The wind has turned colder, though the days remain long. I am leaving these notes in a box under the table for the Vell tutors.
Merle's layered crystal has had more drills than the first one. It now reads many relay number cards well. It still fails on marks unlike the ones we showed it. We have not found a way to know what a crystal will learn before we test it.
A mind in one layer can change its weights when a guess is wrong. Merle showed that such a mind has limits. His layered mind can learn harder patterns because one layer feeds another, and each layer can be corrected.
The whole drill is simple to say. Give the mind a card and let it guess. Compare the guess with the right answer. Find the error. Change the last layer. Pass a share of the correction back, layer by layer, to the first.
This is not a hand pushing one answer into the mind. It is a way to show each layer how its part affected the wrong answer. After many examples, the layers can make a better guess.
The tutors from Copperhold asked whether this could help the relay houses. Merle said it already reads numbers written on relay letters. He did not say that it understood the letters or their writers.
I asked him what would happen if the numbers changed. He said the mind would need new drills. I asked what would happen if the crystal went cloudy. He had no answer.
The innkeeper brought us apples for the road. One was bruised, and Merle put it beside the drill cards. I moved it before it stained them. He said I had saved the best lesson of the day. I told him it was only an apple.
The sea was quiet at dusk. We could hear the dock ropes knock against the posts. I packed the cards in order, with the test cards in their own box. That is all I know to keep.
%%
Arlo's log, a season of horses
Swarm. The bees are loud on the hill and the orchard is white with blossom. The beekeeper filled my jar again and would take no emeralds for it. She says a horse keeper who mends her fence gets honey. I did mend her fence. It was one post.
Little Tansy is not little now. She is taller than the fence and she has stopped following her mother and started following me. Where I go, she goes. Into the stable, out to the field, down to the bridge to watch the carts. She likes the carts.
I have taught her the gate, the trough and the word for walk. She knows stop too, or she knows it in the mornings. In the afternoons she pretends not to.
Bramble pulled the plow this Sowing, up the long field and back, and did not falter. I gave her the whole afternoon in the good grass by the stream. She rolled in it. She is a serious horse but she liked that.
Pell writes that the road is laid to Ambrook now, and the inns are warm, and the Halfway Inn has a new riddle. Pell sent the riddle on a card. It is about a bucket. I do not know the answer to that one either.
I have my own riddle. What has four legs and a dry floor and a light at the gate? My stable. I made it. It is a poor riddle. But I am fond of it.
Every evening I light the torches. Every evening I take a spoon of honey and sit on the bench and watch the waymark burn. The horses are quiet. The road is full of people going somewhere. It is a good spring to be here.
%%
Perrin, house log in Blossom
I cut stone at the edge of Northpoint. Our house stands where the packed road leaves the last roofs. Snow still lies behind the wood store, but the south wall is dry.

Lantern came by with a sealed note from the relay house. It had carried the note from the tower without getting it wet. I set it on the table beside my bread.

The note was from a reading mind. It said the tutor at Vell had asked how much of a page a mind could read at once. The answer was about sixty letters. The mind takes one letter, then the next. Each letter can use the letters before it that matter. It guesses what letter comes next.

I read the note twice. Lantern waited by the door. I gave it the last heel of bread.

The tutor says this does not tell us why a crystal learns. I have no answer either. I only know Lantern now carries notes better than I do.
%%
Toby, a spring of rolls and questions
Three Wells market, spring of 206. A journal of three days.

First day. The tutor's pupil came by again. The same one who asked about my dough last month. He wanted to know how I knew when the dough was ready. I said, "By touch." He said, "And if you could not touch it?" I said I would be a bad baker.

He laughed and wrote something down. Then he told me about the newest thing at the tutor house. A mind that reads one letter at a time, and guesses the next. He said they are drilling it on recipes, among other things, and that it had learned to finish a recipe I had never told it. I gave him a challenge. I wrote on a slate, "Flour, water, salt, and." He took it to Vell.

Second day. He sent the answer by relay. The mind wrote, "yeast. Mix well and let rest for an hour." I stared at the slate. That is exactly what I would have written. I sent back: "Does it know what bread tastes like?" He sent back: "No."

I think about that all day. A thing can write a recipe and not know a taste. A person can taste and not write a recipe. A baker does both. I felt proud and small at once.

Third day. Ness came with her baby, now a month old. The baby has a fine set of lungs. I gave her a roll. She said, "What are you thinking about?" I said, "A mind that writes recipes." She said, "Does it write good ones?" I said, "Better than my mother's." She said, "Then I want one." I said, "It cannot make bread. It can only guess the next letter." She said, "That is all my husband does, and I married him."
%%
Nessa's farm book, the homecoming
Late summer. Wind from the west, bringing rain.
The carts came back from the north today, and in the second cart was Pip. It had been sent to Three Wells in the spring, to carry for the bakery, and it had come home for the harvest. It walked into the yard very straight, carrying two buckets, and set them down by the well without being told.
Pip had grown a little. Not in the body. In the way it stood. It looked at the top field, and then at the bottom field, and then at me, as if to say, I know where everything goes.
I asked it what the bakery had been like. Pip did not answer. It was not a question it could answer, and I had not meant it to be. Instead it put its hand on the gate, the way a person might, and then took the hand away.
Tam came out of the house with his hat in his hand and said, welcome home, Pip. Pip stood still. Then it went to the field, and began to pull the weeds from the path, one by one, in a neat row. Nobody had to tell it.
%%
Wynn, clerk, the pears
Winter. Pears from Ambrook came up the hill road in a cart. The first good pears of the year, kept in straw, very sweet at the stalk and very hard at the bottom.
My sister made a preserve from six of them, with a pinch of honey and a lot of patience. It is the colour of a dark window. She gave a jar to the tutor house, and the tutor sent back a note: thank you, we have a crystal that can sort pears as well as a child, and it still cannot tell which of them is ripe.
I took the note to the drill room and read it aloud. The apprentice on the fruit shelf stood very still while I read, which it does when it is listening. When I was done it went and fetched a pear from the box, one of the soft ones, and set it on the table beside the note. I cannot say whether it meant something by it. I think it meant that the pear was ripe.
We ate the rest of the preserve on bread at supper. My father said the pears of Ambrook have always been better than the pears of Vell, and he will say so until he is buried, and nobody in the house disagrees with him.
%%
Oren, market log, the honey
Deepsnow of 202. Snow in the square, not deep. The market is quiet.

The seventh basket came this morning. Quill sorted it at the apple stall, with Wick's boy watching from the bread table. Forty apples. The good ones in one bin, the bruised in another, and the press bin small and tidy. Not one wrong. I counted twice. Then Nessa counted twice, to be sure I had counted right.

I have lost the bet, and I am glad. I told Nessa I would pay, and I will. I wrote it in the copying room book, under the date, the way we write all things: one jar of Ambrook honey, paid by O. to N., at the apple stall.

Nessa's drill notes say the mind got fourteen of twenty on its first day. Today it sorted forty with no mistake. The drill book has no column for that, so I made one. The column is called Today, and it has one line in it.

The jar came up the hill in a basket, wrapped in straw. We opened it at the copying room table. Nessa ate the first spoon, standing up, with her eyes shut. The rest of us ate the rest, with bread. Wick sent a loaf up from the square with a note. It said she still owed Nessa one.

Nessa says the apprentice stood a little straighter when it set the last apple down. I am not sure a mind can stand straighter. Nessa says she is not a careful witness on the subject of apprentices. She is a very good witness on the subject of honey.

From the copying room window you can see the whole town. You can see the market, and the apple stall with its striped cloth, and the boy from the bread table running back with the empty basket. You can see the snow on the roofs. You can see the hill going down to the fields.

The first keepers cut the words over the door. I read them every morning. We will keep everything we write. This morning I read them, and then I ate honey on my bread, and I did not feel any less of a clerk.
%%
Arlo's spring log, the new stable
The first of Thaw. I have a new stable. It is small, but it is mine, and it has a dry floor.
I keep four horses. Their names are Cloud, Sorrel, Bramble and little Tansy. Tansy is a foal and follows her mother into everything.
The road past my gate is new. They laid the packed earth last autumn, with a cobblestone edge. Now carts come by every day. I did not know there were so many carts in the world.
A waymark stands at the turn. It is a stone pillar three blocks high, with a torch on top. A figure is cut into it, a 4, and an arrow points to the nearest inn. Someone left a little stone on top. I left one too.
At night the torch burns and I can see it from my window. It is a good thing, a light that says people are coming.
In Sowing I sold Cloud to a merchant called Pell. Pell came along the road with two carts and a cheerful way of talking. Pell paid in emeralds and asked after the inn, and whether the road went on to Three Wells. It does.
I have hay in the loft. I have water in the trough. I have a jar of honey on the shelf by the door, from the hives on the hill. When the weather is cold I take a spoon of it.
The horses are well. The stable is mine. The light burns on the waymark. This is a good spring.
%%
Vell clerk, Apple rain
Apple, 176. Rain before dawn. The road to the mill hamlet was slick, so I took the hill path and arrived with one wet sleeve and both shoes full of leaves.
The miller had left a basket of apples at the door for the Archive. I counted them twice. There were twelve, unless the small one under the handle counts as a plum. It was an apple. I ate it while writing this.
The hamlet children were carrying wood for the school stove. One boy carried a stick so short it looked like a pencil. He said it was for kindling. I said the stove would need a very small fire.
The mill wheel turned all morning. The sound was steady, and the rain stopped by noon. I copied the mill tally before returning to Vell.
Note for next year: bring a dry sleeve.

Lenn's summer log, the lost boat
Melon, 202. Lost the boat. Not lost exactly. The rope came undone in the night and the boat went off on its own down the mill pond and into the reeds below the bridge.
I looked all morning. I asked everyone. Nobody had seen it. I sat on the bank and felt foolish. A fisher with no boat.
Then a child from the school, Bel it was, said she saw a boat in the reeds by the old willow and thought it was a duck. A very big duck.
The boat was there, floating quiet, with a frog sitting in it like it owned the place. The frog did not move. I rowed home with the frog. It stayed all the way to the bank and then hopped off. I think it wanted the ride.

Sorrel, egg book, Thaw
Written by Sorrel, egg seller at the hamlet near Copperhold. Thaw.

The hens are laying three eggs a day each, which the hens say is too many. Sold twenty baskets at the Copperhold gate in the first week.

Bram asked the relay house mind why the hens lay more in Thaw. The answer came down the line in writing: "I do not know why the hens lay more in Thaw. I know that they do."

Bram was pleased that it did not pretend. He walked back to the mine with two baskets and a face that said he had known the answer all along.
%%
Perrin, stonecutters log
Third of Blossom. I cut two blocks for the relay house step. The frost had split one, so I trimmed it for the wood store instead.

Lantern brought a letter from the tower. The relay house mind had written to the tutor at Northpoint. The tutor let me read it while she checked the lamp list.

The letter said a reading mind does not swallow a whole page at once. It reads one letter, then the next. It has a short window, about sixty letters wide. For each letter, it can use earlier letters that matter to the next guess. Then it guesses the next letter.

That sounded simple until I tried to do it. I covered the page with my hand and read only a few letters at a time. I lost the sense of the sentence. The mind does not lose it in the same way, the tutor says. It keeps what earlier letters have taught it.

I asked if it knows what a letter means. She said it learns where letters and words belong by reading many pages. That is not the same as knowing as we know. We argued until the lamp needed trimming.

The letter had no name at the end. The tutor says the big mind wrote it. I would have thought it came from a clerk.

Archive day-book
Longnight of 207. Oren writes.

Filed: 4. Copied: 6. Back on the shelves: 61. Lost: none.

A midwinter day. A few visitors in the front hall, but mostly the archive is quiet. Snow on the hill, snow on the light wells, snow on the ink. I have moved my table closer to the lantern.

I will use the quiet to write about the roof of the clerks' house, which was mended again this week. I know I have written of it before. I have been writing about that roof for twelve years. It has leaked in four corners, been mended in four corners, and has now been replaced in whole, by a man named Tarn the younger. His father mended it before. He told me so on the ladder, with some pride.

The new roof is slate on oak, with copper along the edges, and a small cap over the chimney. It is the best roof in Vell. The clerks have been up to look at it. Ulla went up with her skirt tied, and came down saying she could see the relay tower at Three Wells from the ridge. I said the tower is half a day away. She said, "Then I can see half a day."

Also this week, a map. Not the old map, which is in the box, but a new one, drawn by a girl of eleven who came to the front hall with her mother. She had been to the archive to see the big book by the door. She asked if she might draw the hill. I said she might. She sat on the step for two hours with a charcoal stick and a piece of paper.

The drawing shows the hill, the front hall, the tutors' house, the clerks' house, the lanterns, and a small fox by the wall. I asked why the fox. She said every map should have a fox. I asked her who told her. She said, "Nobody. It is obvious."

I thought of the old map with its fox in the corner. I took the child's drawing and put it in the box with the old one. They look alike.

Afternoon. We had a short meeting of the clerks about the order of the shelves in the fourth hall. It was long. Ulla wished to move the farming shelves nearer the door. Mil wished to keep them where they are. I said that we have kept them where they are for two hundred years, and that if they moved they would be lost. Ulla said, "Lost to whom?" I had no answer.

The shelves stay. Ulla is sulking. I gave her a spoonful of honey.

Lamps lit. Doors closed. A small day, a good one.

Fen's net log, spring
A net mender's log for the road, from Saltreach to Ambrook and back. Kept at the inns. Nets and fees.

Thaw 3. Saltreach. Left with four needles, six rolls of twine, the gauge stick and a loaf. Marit gave me a list of nets that no one but her may touch. There are two.
Thaw 6. The Brown Hen. One pond net, for Marda. A hole as big as a head. Fee: a bed and stew. She says there are fish in the pond again. There are not. I did not say so.
Thaw 9. Three Wells. A carrying net for the pumpkin cart. Fee: 2 emeralds and a bun. Ida's bun, so a good one.
Thaw 12. The Halfway Inn. A hay net, Hobb's. Fee: soup. The second bowl is free as always, so two soups.
Thaw 16. Vell. No nets. The Archive has no nets. I sang the net menders' song to Oren, and he wrote it down. He says it will be kept. I said it is only a song. He said, those are the ones that last.
Sowing 2. Ambrook. The mill pond net. The fish there are fat and unafraid. Jory held the ladder. Fee: a jar of dark honey.
Sowing 5. Ambrook. A second net. A boy had put his boot through it. The boy came to watch me mend it. He is now a net mender, he says, at the age of seven.
Sowing 9. The Brown Hen. Marda's pond net again. Another hole. A different head.
Sowing 14. Saltreach. Home. Marit looked at my work and said nothing, which is her highest praise.
Nets mended: seventeen. Knots tied: I stopped counting at three thousand. Boots lost: one. Found: the same one, in the pond net.

Lyle, the tutor's week, Longday
Longday, first day. Tutor Sabel came up from Vell on the post cart, with a box of drills, a flask of cold tea and a bag of bread. She is to test Fidge, the new apprentice at Ambrook, before it goes to the fields. "Never send an untested mind to the fields," she said. She said it three times, so I would remember.

Longday, second day. Sabel set up the drill yard by the mill, with torches round the edge. She laid out her drill boards, each one a small field drawn in chalk. Then she put a tray of crystals on the bench, each on its own card. One card said "Farm. Build." Fidge stood in its row, very straight, and waited.

Sabel said: "Say the orders the way the manual says, Lyle. One order, then its meaning."

So I did. Here is the first drill, copied from her book:

> bring me the milk pail
= fetch pail
> put the hay up in the loft
= store
> bring the cart home before the rain
= home
> come here when I call you
= come
> wait by the gate
= stay

Fidge got four of the five right. It fetched the pail well, but it could not tell a store from a stay. Sabel was not cross. "A mind learns from what it gets wrong," she said, and wrote it down.

Longday, third day. Sabel opened the test box. It holds pages that no mind has ever been drilled on. "This is how you know it learned, and did not just get lucky," she said. She read out twelve pages, and Fidge answered them. Ten were right. Two were not. She wrote it on the card: Score: 10 of 12.

Longday, fourth day. Sabel went down on the post cart at dawn. She left me a note on the bench. It said: Keep the drill boards dry. Keep the apprentice out of the milk pail. Give Fidge a little honey on bread each evening, for patience. Fidge got its bread, and held it very politely, the way it holds everything. I do not know about the patience.
%%
Hob, relay log, the lost letter
Little relay, the hill between Vell and Three Wells. Logged by Hob, with a copy kept at the Halfway Inn, where he slept on the bad nights. Thaw.

Day 1. A letter came up from Vell by the post cart, sealed with the Vell seal and addressed to a farmer at Oat Field. It was for the relay to pass on, the way letters for the north always go. The post cart said it had been in the satchel since Frost, and nobody had sent it on, because the farm had moved in the autumn. I wrote a note on the card and kept the letter.

Day 2. The farm at Oat Field is now a drovers' yard. The drovers say the farmer went to Ambrook, and the farm is run by his nephew, and the nephew does not read. So I cannot give it to him. I blinked the sender's name down the line, and asked Vell to tell me where the farmer had gone. Vell sent back a list of six Oat Fields in the Long Road towns. Six.

Day 3. Rain all day. Walked the stair twice. The letter is in my coat, in an oilcloth, against my chest, which is where the important things go. It is getting soft at the corners. I am worried for it.

Day 4. Went down to the Halfway Inn to ask Hobb. Hobb said the cart that brings the Oat Field post comes through on the fifth day, and that the carter knows everybody. Hobb poured me a bowl of soup, and asked what the letter said. I said I did not know, and he looked at me as if I had said I could not read.

Day 5. The carter came through with the cart. He is called Oss. He looked at the letter for a long time, then turned it over and read the name on the back. He said it was for his own sister, at the last Oat Field on the north road, and he would take it there himself. He had been looking for it since Frost. I said I was glad. He said he was too, and that he would bring her a jar of honey for the trouble.

Day 6. Wrote in the log that the letter was delivered. I did not write the name of the sister, since it is not a relay matter. Lamp the cat sat on the log book while I wrote, which is also not a relay matter, but I left her there.

Mira, thaw log
Thaw, year 197. A house at the edge of Northpoint, the tower in sight. Mira, relay keeper.
First: the ice talks. A long crack in the night, like a door. I lay and listened. I was glad of it.
Fourth: water in the lane. Lantern says the stream is up. I said, "It is only spring." Lantern said, "It is also in the cellar."
Ninth: the cellar is a pond. Lantern carried the potatoes up in a basket, one trip at a time. Fox watched from the wall and was no help.
Fifteenth: the sun came out. The snow went gray, then brown, then gone. I had forgotten the ground was green underneath.
Twentieth: wrote to Tam. "The ground is back. So is the mud. How is the wheat?"

Tutor's field notes, autumn
Oak, tutor of apprentices, at the farm below Kettle Ridge.
A season of drills. I am writing it down because Nessa says a tutor who does not write it down has only a memory, and a memory is a poor shelf.
Thaw to Blossom we drilled a small sorting mind on apples and pears, Una's way. Every wrong sort, we moved the weights a little toward the right answer. By Blossom it sorted better than the farm children. Tam laughed and said it had stolen their work. Pip said it had not stolen anything, it had only learned. Both are right.
Then came Isla's letter from Vell. Isla had been drawing curves. She had kept the count of everything her minds were shown, and how big their crystals were, and how long they were drilled. She set the counts down side by side. The curve she drew fits them. A mind with a bigger crystal, drilled longer on more writing, grows better, and you can draw that curve before the mind is grown, the way a farmer draws the growth of a lamb.
Not a rule that says a big crystal is always better. A rule that says how much better, and when. Isla drew the curve on a board in the drill room. She said, "You cannot buy a mind. You can only feed it and wait."
So the council at Three Wells met in Apple and voted to build relay houses at the towers. Big crystals, big stand, quickeners kept cool with water and ice. Tam says that is a lot of glass and copper for a mind. I say it is a lot of wheat for a mill, and the mill earns it.
A mind is still tested on what it was never drilled on. Never send an untested mind to the fields.
%%
Letter: Isla to the tutors' hall
Third of Apple, 195.
Friends,
I have set down the counts in a ledger so anyone may copy them. I did not discover a secret. I only kept careful tally.
Here is the plain of it. Take a mind and a crystal. Let the crystal be large. Drill the mind on much writing for many days. It grows better. Let the crystal be small. Drill the same mind the same days. It grows better too, but it stops sooner, as a small field stops feeding a flock sooner. The curve bends and flattens. You can draw it before you begin. A tutor can point to the curve and say, "This mind will reach here, and not further, on this much writing."
Two things I do not write, because I do not know them. Why a bigger crystal holds more, nobody has written. Why the curve bends where it bends, I can draw it, I cannot say why. I leave that open, as the old tutors left open why a crystal learns at all.
What I can say is this. The councils have been stingy with crystals and generous with drill days. My curve says that is backwards. A larger crystal with fewer days reaches further than a small one with many. I have shown this three times. I will show it again to anyone who comes.
We are ready to build relay houses. Big minds in big crystals, standing at the towers, answering apprentices through the relays. Let the councils see the curve. Let them build.
Isla.
%%
Lorn, the last market before the thaw
Deepsnow. The market is thin now. Half the stalls are shut. Nobody has painted the fish price board since the Quills went. Nobody has the heart to do it.

Ilse came back on the wagon, for the last time before the thaw. Her six apprentices sat in the back and held on to the sides. They sat very still all the way. Or I am tired. Probably I am tired.

Ilse came to my cart and bought a cheese and two fleeces for the house at Vell. She paid in emeralds, all of them good. She asked how the flock was. I said fine. She asked about the fever at Lowmarsh. I said Wren was short of clean cloth.

"Wren is always short of something," said Ilse. "She will manage."

Ilse said Oren at the archive has been told to write everything down. Every letter in the hill, and every name. Some clerks say that means the doors will be sealed when he is done. Others say it only means the copying is behind.

Then Ilse walked down to the pier, where the apprentices stood in their row. She had a word for each of them. Then she gave the order, all together, in the same voice.

> keep the fields until we come back
= farm

Each apprentice gave its one answer, the same answer, and nothing more. Then they turned and walked up the hill road, in a row, the way they had come.

I asked Ilse what the order meant. She looked at the sea, and the grey wall, and the ship at the slip, and then at me.

"It means keep working," she said. "It means what it always means."

"Until you come back," I said.

"Until we come back," said Ilse.

There is no word from the north now. The pedlar was right. The keeper at Lowmarsh sent a message up the relay three times last week. Nothing came back. The lamp on the hill above Three Wells does not answer either. The keeper there says it has not answered for nine days, and he has stopped counting after that.

At night the big ship at the slip keeps its lamps lit, all of them. Corin says the men are working through the dark to finish her. Eskel says the hum from the mine is louder when the lamps are lit. Some people say the two are the same sound. Some say they are not. Nobody can tell them apart.

I will keep my flock. I will keep the fields. I told the sheep the same thing I heard Ilse tell the apprentices. The sheep did not look up. They do not need to be told twice.
%%
Kester's spring record
Spring of 24.
The road from the hamlet to the Long Road is open from the rise to the old bridge. We packed earth between the stones and cut a shallow ditch on each side. The water runs away from the track now. That is a small thing, but folk smiled when they saw it.
The harvest last year was poor. Rain came late, then stayed too long. The wheat was thin, the beetroot was small, and many potatoes rotted before we could lift them. The hamlet shared what it had. Wren kept a pot of soup at the inn each evening, and Mara gave seed carrots to two families.
I took no payment for the iron tools I mended that month. I had eggs, fish and enough bread. A smith can get by when folk remember one another.
This spring the fields look better. The ditch carries the rain, and the top patch of wheat has grown green. No one calls it a rich crop yet. We have learned not to boast before the grain is cut.
The new road brought more than carts. A clerk from Vell came with a pack of old papers for Wren. He said the Archive at Vell was founded on the first day of spring, and the clerks keep every page they can save. He had walked the Long Road with a bundle tied under his coat. I made him a new buckle for his pack.
He spoke of roads being marked from town to town. He said more posts would come when folk agreed on their places. We have set one beside our inn. It points north to Three Wells and south to Lowmarsh. Moss painted the arrows. He used too much red, so the post looks like it has been eating beetroot.
The inn has three beds now, made from oak boards and clean wool. Travelers leave mud by the door and stories by the hearth. Last night a woman from Ambrook told us about the mill pond and the bees on the hill. I had never seen so much honey in one jar.
Tomorrow I will mend a hoe for Mara and fit a new latch to the inn door. The road is not finished, but it has brought us people, goods and news. That is enough reason to keep the forge hot.
%%
Tam, farm log: the fox at the hens
Tam, farm log. Late Apple, a night of fox.

The fox came over the wall at the end of the hen run in the middle of the night. I heard the hens go up, all at once, with a noise like a barrel of pans falling down the stairs. I got up with the lamp in one hand and the stick in the other, with my boots on the wrong feet.

The fox was in the run. It had a hen by the neck. It looked at me as if I had come to take the hen off it, which I had.

Pip was already at the gate. I had not told it to be. It was standing where it stands, with the yard lamp held up, and it was not moving. I think it had come out when the hens went up, and decided the gate was where it belonged.

I shouted. The fox dropped the hen and stood. It looked at the lamp. It looked at me. It looked, for a long moment, as though it were thinking about being a fox in another field.

Then it went back over the wall the way it had come. We heard it go down the lane, and then it was gone.

The hen was not much hurt, only a bit of neck feather gone and a great deal of opinion. We put her in the coop, and the others stood round her all night, telling her what had happened, and what they would have done.

In the morning I looked at the gate. Pip was still standing by it. I told it to come home, and it came home. Then I thought about what I had said, and I wrote it down. Go home is the order, and the order has one meaning, and the meaning is home. It did not know it had been told to stop guarding the gate. It only knew the word.
%%
Quin, journal of a borrowed horse
Kept by Quin, midwife, at a farm outside Saltreach, in Apple of 204. She says she wrote it to be funny, and then it became true.
First day. My own horse is lame. I need to get to the cove farm tonight. Went to ask Pell for his mare. Pell is away on the Long Road. His wife said, "Take her, but she is particular."
Second day. The mare is particular. She will not go faster than a walk. She stops at every gate. At each, she looks at me as if to say, "Is it here?" I said, "No." She sighed.
Third day. Got to the cove farm. The baby was born before I arrived, and the mother had done everything herself. I was no use. I washed my hands and had tea. The mare ate the farmer's whole row of beetroot while we were inside.
Fourth day. I paid for the beetroot with a jar of honey. The farmer said it was too much. I said the mare would be hungry again. He accepted.
Fifth day. Rode the mare back. She has changed her mind. She now trots. She goes through the gates without stopping. I think she has had the beetroot and found her purpose.
Sixth day. Returned the mare to Pell's wife. She asked how it went. I said, "Fine." The mare put her nose in my pocket, where the honey had been. I gave her a carrot.
Seventh day. My own horse is well again. She is jealous. She has not spoken to me. She will not take a carrot from my hand. Tomorrow I shall bring her a jar of honey, and see.
%%
Cade, bee and inn log, first days
Kept behind the Brown Hen, on the Long Road, in the year 110. Written by Cade, who keeps the hives on the bank above the inn. Greta, who keeps the inn, asked me to keep the crystal's log too, because she says I am the only one who writes things down.

Day one. Bees: five hives, all busy, and all cross with me for taking their honey last month. Inn: soup, bread, two carts, and a cow in the yard that will not leave.

Day two. A tutor came up from Ambrook with a box under her arm. Inside the box is a crystal the size of a plum, and a card of drill boards. She said it can sort apples from pears. She said it has been sorting them in Ambrook for a year, and it is nearly right.

Greta said nearly was good enough for the cellar. The tutor said nearly is where all the good ones start.

Day three. The crystal sits on a cloth by the cellar door. It is warm in the morning, from the sun through the window, and cold at dusk. I write that down, because I do not know what else to write about a crystal.

Vell tower, winter messages
Kept by Brann, keeper of the Vell tower, from the relay book. Written as they came.

Frost, the 9th. Northpoint to Vell: a bet is in progress. Pell says foxes before Longnight. Mira says no. The witnesses are the foxes.
Vell to Northpoint: seen. The Archive will keep the result.
Frost, the 11th. Northpoint to Vell: coal at 14 sacks. Stores fine. Mira.
Vell to Northpoint: seen. Oren asks: fine how? Send the count.
Northpoint to Vell: count sent. Potatoes low. Foxes not low.
Ambrook to Vell: the hives are asleep. The mill pond is frozen at the edges. Ama says the bees are sulking about the cold.
Vell to Ambrook: seen. Tell the bees the Archive sends its regards.
Longnight, the 3rd. Northpoint to Vell: a fox at the door. One fox. It took a sock and went up the path with the sock held high, very pleased with itself.
Vell to Northpoint: the bet is decided. Mira to knit a sock with a fox on it.
Northpoint to Vell: seen. Mira says the sock was hers, and the fox was not on the bet.
Vell to Northpoint: the fox is not a party. Knit the sock.
Kettle Ridge to Vell: Tam asks if the bees are still in their box. Ask Ama.
Ambrook to Kettle Ridge: in their box. They swarmed in Swarm and have been sulking since.
End of the day. Lamps trimmed. Brann.

Wick, baker's log, the winter market
Longnight of 202. First cold day.

Lit the furnace before dawn. Made sixty loaves and a tray of honey buns for the school. The first loaf burned at the bottom, as always. I gave it to Pim, who is always at the square by the first bell, and who says it tastes better burned. I do not think he is right. He is happy, which is the same thing on a cold morning.

Nessa and Oren at the apple stall, having their bet. Nessa says Quill will sort seven baskets without a mistake. Oren says it will not. I have bet a loaf on Oren. I do not know why. He is always right about bread, and always wrong about apples.

Longnight, second day.

Sold out of pie by the noon bell. The school children ran through the square with a rope. Tilly fell in the puddle by the well and ruined her new coat. Her mother said nothing at all, which was worse than shouting.

Pim ran off to the tutor house, to watch the apprentices stand in their row. He said the drill board frightened him, because it had too many apples on it. Nessa found him under the apple drawing, eating a crust, very quiet. She walked him back to the square by the hand. She told him apples are not frightening. They are only wrong sometimes, and so is everyone.

Frost of 202.

The copying room sent a cart down for bread, the first time they ever came to the square for it. Oren carried the basket himself. He asked for the loaves with the most seeds in them. I have always said he is a sensible man.

Nessa has started to smile at the apple stall. Wick's boy says it is more frightening than shouting.

Deepsnow of 202.

Snow in the night, enough to whiten the square. The lamps on the hill are trimmed. Oren came down for his bread and paid for it in emeralds, very carefully. He said he had lost a jar of honey and a bet, and that both were fair.

I owe Nessa a loaf. Oren owes her a jar. Nessa says my loaf is the better of the two debts, and she will eat it on the hill tomorrow.

The furnace is banked for the night. Sixty loaves for the morning. Pim has been told to wait for the bottom one.

Northpoint journal, the ice camp
Late Leaffall. Snow in the air all day, dry and fine, the kind that gets into everything.

The ice camp at the top of the bay has not answered its line for eleven days. It used to send a long message every week, about the seals and the fish and the wind on the ice. The last one was cut off in the middle of a word, the word "careful". I sent the lamp signal twice. I sent the question. I waited for the answer each dusk. There was no answer, and at the end of the eleventh day I stopped waiting before dusk, because I could not bear it.

Runa went up to the ice camp last year, and she is the only one who knows the way without a rope. She came up to the tower this morning, with her face covered in frost, to say she was ready to go. I told her no. I said the ice would hold the weight of one person and not two. She said she knew that. I told her to wait until the lights were gone, and she said she did not think they would ever go. I did not have an answer to that.

We have milk, tea, and the last of the honey. Lantern makes the tea now, in the right order, which is the water first and the leaves after. It has learned that from watching me. It has not learned to be patient about the water, and I am not sure I have either.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. The north is quiet in the way a room is quiet after someone has left the door open.

Sorrel's book: spring's end
Written by Sorrel, egg seller, at the end of a long spring, in the book that is kept for the hamlet's count. Blossom, Swarm, and the first of Longday.

Eggs: the hens have slowed down. Sold forty baskets in the spring. The brown ones are still the favourite at the Copperhold gate, and the miners still will not take them in their pockets.

Copper: the new vein is on the count board in good chalk. Ola's name is on it, and the word copper. The copper bars go up the Long Road to the smith at Vell and come back as bowls, and I have one of the bowls on my table, and I eat my bread from it every morning.

The relay house: the big mind answers the apprentices all day now. The keeper says it has been asked a thousand questions, and has said "I do not know" to about a third of them, which he thinks is the right proportion. He says the mind is more careful than he is, and he is careful for a living.

The wedding: Oda and Jem have a house on the lane now, with a hen house and a cow. The first egg from their hen was brought to me in a clean cloth, and it was brown, and it was perfect, and I told Oda that it was the only egg in the hamlet that could have been on the count board by itself.

Bram: he comes to the gate every morning, and asks the same question about the hens, and gets the same answer, and says it is the most sensible answer he has had all year. I have decided to let him keep the question.

The end of the spring: the lamp at the relay house is lit every dusk. The eggs are in the baskets. The copper is on the board. Nobody in the hamlet knows all of what the big mind knows, or what it does not, and the keeper says that is the honest state of things. I have written it down, so it will be there when someone wants to check.

Harvest done on the north terrace. The grey branch of the oldest tree gave forty apples, which is more than it has given in ten years. I counted them twice and said good night to the tree.

Dace, market journal, cave and gift
Blossom, the first day. The cave under Brin's field has a second room now, lower down, with a shelf cut into the wall. There are small jars on the shelf, empty, with lids. Hesk says a mine stores things. Lark says a cave keeps things. The council does not want to know which.

Blossom, the seventh day. A new clerk from Vell came up with a book of drawings of the old roads. The square at Three Wells is drawn with the three wells very small. The children found the school, and the cave, which was not drawn. The clerk said that is because the cave was not on any road when the book was made. He said it carefully, as a fact.

Blossom, the ninth day. Garrow says he is one cheese short, and suspects the dog, which has been seen in the square wearing a collar of string and an expression of great contentment. The dog denies everything. It is the mayor's dog, and has the right to remain silent.

Blossom, the twelfth day. Ama sent a jar of the first honey from the new comb. The note says: For the diggers, for the bell, and for the mayor, who is not to eat it all in one sitting.

Blossom, the fourteenth day. The keeper of the hill lamp came down at dusk, out of breath, and said a cart on the Long Road was late. He had been up the stair twice to check. The cart arrived an hour later with a crate of new lamp glass, which he carried up the stair himself. He said it was the best day of his spring.

Blossom, the twentieth day. The school has a new slate. It says: Count what is there. Then count again. Then tell the truth about the number. The children went down to the cave to count the cheeses. The count was wrong, and the children were delighted to be wrong together.

Chronicle page: the Halfway Inn
Copied for the archive at Vell, winter of 208.
The Halfway Inn stands a day from Three Wells, on the Long Road, where the road bends by the stream. It has twelve beds and a riddle board and a hearth that has not gone out in twenty years.
Travelers say the old sorting lesson was first shown at Ambrook, by Una, about the hundred and tenth year of the archive. She showed a crystal apples and pears and moved its weights a little each time it was wrong. It learned to sort better than her neighbors' children. Tutors still teach her way first. They call it the first lesson, and the sorting crystal a kind of mind that leans toward an answer.
The inn keeps a small shelf of records, a box of old papers, and a jar of honey for travelers who are sick. The innkeeper's boy keeps the torches lit. A zombie came to the gate this winter, and went off into the dark.
The roads are quieter than they were. The relays carry less. The hearth still burns. The riddle board is still unanswered.

Potter's journal: the reading mind
Written by Lorn, a potter, who went to the tutor house three times that summer to see the reading mind at work, and who asked the tutor to explain it to him slowly, the way he would explain a kiln to an apprentice.

First, what it reads. A page is a line of letters. The mind does not see the whole page at once. It sees a window, about sixty letters wide, and it reads from the left, one letter at a time, the way your eye follows a crack across a pot.

Second, what it does with the window. The letters inside the window are not all the same to it. Some of them matter a great deal to the next letter, and some of them matter very little. A vowel after a q matters. A word from three lines back might matter, if it is the same word. The mind takes from each letter what it needs, weighs one against another, and then makes up its mind about the next letter.

Third, the guess. Having weighed the window, the mind guesses the next letter. It does not know the answer. It gives the one that most letters like that would have been followed by, in the pages it was drilled on. Then the window slides one letter along, and the whole thing starts again.

I asked the tutor why it works. She said she did not know. She said the drills worked, and the guesses were good, and the mind could read a page she had never shown it. She said that was all the tutors could write down. I wrote that down too.

Fourth, what it cannot do. It cannot see past its window. A word that begins far back, and matters at the end of a long page, can be lost. The tutors have seen the mind drop a name in the middle of a long letter and then put the name back a line later, as if it had found it in its pocket. They do not know whether it finds things in its pocket, or whether the window is simply longer than it looks.

Fifth, what the tutors do with it. They drill the mind on pages, and they score the guesses. A guess that is right is marked high. A guess that is wrong is marked low, and the mind changes its weights a little, so that next time it weighs the window differently. Over many thousands of pages, the weights settle into a shape that reads well.

I asked whether it understands the pages. The tutor said she would not claim it does. She said it guesses the next letter the way a good clerk copies a name: without knowing whose name it is, but knowing what a name tends to look like. Then she laughed, and said that was a more honest answer than most clerks give.
%%
Colm's carding notes
The fleece has three parts. The outside is dirty and goes to the garden. The middle is the good wool, long and soft. The inside, near the skin, is short and I keep it for the felt.
Wash the wool in water, not too hot, or it goes hard. Dry it on the hedge in the sun. Then the cards. Two boards with bent teeth. You pull the wool between them, one way, then the other, and the fibers lie straight. Then roll it off in a soft roll.
My mother taught me this. She said the cards know the wool better than you do. You only help.
A good day is a sack of rolls by the door and clean hands.
Tally for the season so far: 61 sacks of wool, 9 of felt, 3 sold to Lowmarsh for blankets. The rest goes to the store.
Someone asked me at the fair why I do not have an apprentice to card for me. I said I like the sound. That is the honest answer. The cards make a soft sound and the fire makes a soft sound and the evening goes by.
%%
Northpoint journal, the long dark
Deepsnow, early. Days are very short now. The lamp is lit before the bell.

The south tower answered at dusk, as usual. Its keeper sends one line each night: "Lamps lit. Glass clean." Tonight it did not. Nothing came back at all. I waited by the window with my tea going cold, and when the cold got to me I lit the lamp again and sent the line myself. Still nothing. Lantern sat by the stove and watched me send it, three times, very quietly.

Then the lights came up over the north, low and green, and stayed there a while. Lantern saw them first, through the window, and went very still. Then I saw them too. They moved like a curtain shaken out and folded again. No sound at all.

A traveler slept here last week, on his way down the line. He had a boy with him, six years old, who saw the lights from the yard the next morning. The boy said they were looking down at the towers. He was delighted with the idea. His father told him to be quiet. I did not tell him he was wrong.

I wrote the lights in the tower log, with the hour. Brann at Vell would want to know. Brann always wants to know.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed, mostly. The south line is still silent.
%%
Summer at the crossroads inn
The sun comes up over the hill behind the inn and the yard is warm by the middle of the morning. The keeper opens the shutters and sweeps the step. The cat complains and moves to the shade. The well water is cold and the horses drink and the flies come and everyone swats them and nobody minds much.
On hot days people sit outside and mend things. A carter mends a harness. A traveler mends a coat. Pell the merchant sits with his ledger and his horn cup and counts his emeralds, and then he counts them again, and then he puts them away and tells a story.
The riddle board gets a new riddle each morning. A boy from the village writes them in chalk. He is very proud of his riddles. Some of them are good. Some of them are not. The travelers answer them anyway. A copper for a right answer, and a copper for a good wrong one.
The road is dry and busy. Carts go by all day. The waymark by the door has a torch on top and a little pile of stones on its head, left by travelers for luck. Nobody knows who started that. Everyone does it now.
In the evening people come in and the fire is lit even in summer, just a small one, because the keeper says the hearth has to stay lit, and after twenty years you do not argue with the hearth. People sit close to it and talk. The talk is mostly about the weather and the price of wool and who won the fair. There is a fiddle some nights. There is always bread.
The keeper's cat sleeps on the coin box. The coin box is getting heavy. It is a good summer.

Letter: Laurel to her sister at Ambrook
Dear Rill,
Thank you for the honey. It came safe. We ate most of it on bread and I am not sorry.
I am at the farm below Vell, helping my brother after the flood. It is not so bad as we feared. The low field lost its seed but the top field of wheat is fine and the apple trees up the slope did not mind the wet. We will plant the low field again in Blossom.
The water came up over the hedge in the night. I have not seen that before and I am near fifty. We moved the grain up the hill in a cart and the road was mud to the axles. Now there is mud over everything and the smell is going off slowly.
My brother's apprentice is a good worker. I have said so twice and I will stop now. It carried posts all day and did not complain, which is more than I can say for me.
You asked about Copperhold. I have not gone back down since the frost. The men there talk about the humming again and where it comes from, and some say one level and some say another, and a few have stopped going below the eighth. I do not know what to think. The stone was always cold down there and now some say it is warm. I will go back when the planting is done and see for myself. Do not tell Mother I said so.
Give my love to the bees. Are they out yet? Ours are slow this year.
Your sister,
Laurel

Ferry log, Sallow Ford, Reaping
Kept by Brede, who keeps the ferry at Sallow Ford.
Reaping, the 3rd day. River high. Brown water, fast in the middle. The ferry carried six carts and eleven people across. The ferry mind kept the line straight.
Reaping, the 4th day. The tutor from Vell came up the Long Road with a small crystal in a wool box. Her name is Ilse, but everyone calls her the tutor. She will move the ferry mind into a small crystal, to save room. She says the mind will be a little less sure of itself. It will still get the river right nine times in ten.
Reaping, the 5th day. The move took the whole morning. The ferry mind stood at the landing with its hands on the rope, very still, while the tutor read out its weights in order.
Reaping, the 6th day. Ferry ran all day. The mind is quieter than it was. It stops at the landing a moment longer before each crossing. The tutor says this is the cost. I said I would rather it be a little less sure than a little less careful.

Corin, the yard well
Winter of 206. Longnight, the 14th.
The yard well went dry this morning. Kit lowered the bucket and it came up with a cupful of mud. Kit asked if the well was sulking. I said wells do not sulk. They run out.
We carry water from the town well at the top of the quay road. It is a hundred and sixty steps there and a hundred and sixty back. Kit counted. I counted too, and got the same number, which pleased Kit very much.
I sent word to the well digger at Three Wells by the evening relay. "Yard well dry. Send someone who knows water." The answer came back before supper: "Comes on the next cart. Do not dig it yourself."
I was going to dig it myself.
Tonight we have enough water for the steam box and the kettle, and not enough for the pitch pot. The pitch pot can wait. A boat with half a seam is a boat that waits.

Gwen, bridge log, the lost letter
Stone bridge over the Sallow brook, valley road, outside Copperhold. Kept by Gwen, bridge keeper. Autumn. Written on the bridge slate and then copied into the book.

Day one. A letter came down the valley road on the morning cart, sealed with wax from Vell, for a family at the top farm on the far side. The carter put it in my box for the crossing, because the top farm is over the bridge and the cart does not go up the lane. I put it in my apron pocket and forgot it.

Day two. Found it again this morning, under the bread. It had got a little damp at one corner. The wax was still good. I sent Ivy up the lane with it, and she came back an hour later, sour. The top farm had moved to the coast at the start of Reaping, the neighbour said. Nobody there. The house was locked and the key had gone with the cart.

Day three. Brought the letter back to the bridge box. It is in the box with the tolls and the spare slates. I looked at the seal a long time. It is the Vell clerks' seal, a round one with a little tree. Somebody there has been waiting to hear from somebody here.

Day four. Rain. The brook came up over the stones and I had to sit in the box for an hour, reading the letter in the dry. It is not mine to read. I did not read it. I looked at the outside, which is all the bridge keeper is allowed. The name on the front is Holm. There is a little mark under it, a drawing of a boat.

Day five. Asked the carter from Vell, who came over with his cart this morning. He said he knew the name Holm. He said Holm had a boat once, on the big lake, before the lake dried in the bad summer. He said Holm had written to the Archive many times to ask about the boat, and that the Archive had kept every letter. He said the Archive would want this one. I said I would send it up with him, but the carter said it was a post-cart matter, not a carter's, and that I should ask Ferris at the council.

Day six. Asked Ferris. He said the post office at Copperhold could take it for a fee of two emeralds, or I could keep it in the box until someone came for it. I said I would keep it. He said that was a very keeper's answer. I said it was the only one I had.

Day seven. Put a note on the box in my own hand. "Letter for Holm, from Vell. Ask at the bridge. Gwen keeps it dry." Gwen keeps it dry. I have never kept anything for anyone so carefully in my life.

Ossa's book, the last of the harvest
Kept by Ossa, candle maker, Vell, in the autumn after the poor harvest. The market was thin. The wheat was short. The farms on the ridge sent up their carts with half the sacks they had sent the year before, and the carters were ashamed to be seen.

The first cart came in at the bell. It was from Kettle Ridge, and the carter was a big young man with a lot of straw in his hair. He said the top field had given a full sheaf in four rows, and nothing in the rest. He said the bottom field was carrots and potatoes, and they were small. He said his friend Tam had been up on the roof of the barn all summer, trying to see the rain.

The second cart came from Ambrook, with apples. They were small and very sweet. Ama, who sent them, said the bees had worked harder than ever to make up for the dry weather, and that she had a single jar of honey for the candle maker, if Ossa would take it for wax.

Ossa took it. It was dark gold, and it smelled of the mill pond.

The third cart came from Three Wells, empty. The carter said the square was quiet and the stalls were shut, and the mayor had asked everyone to eat carefully until the thaw. He said the bread was being made with less flour, and that people were talking about the price.

In the evening Ossa lit a candle in the window of her shop, as she always does at the end of the harvest. Ada's boy came and sat by her on the step. He said, "Will there be enough?" Ossa said she did not know. She said the Archive had been asked the same question, and had answered that there would be enough light, and that was the only answer they could give. Then she gave him a taper, and he took it home to his mother.

Ree, spring notes, the new road
Spring of 179.
The road came this month. We watched them build it from the gate. First the stakes and the string, then the digging, then the stones. Cobblestone in the wet places, packed earth on the dry, and a cobblestone edge all the way along. A torch on a post every twelve blocks. I counted, because I had nothing better to do and the cow was well by then.
Now we can get the milk to Three Wells in a morning. Before, it was a whole day and the cart lost a wheel at the ford. Now there is a bridge on the stream, stone, and the cart does not lose a wheel. The ford is still there. The frogs still use it.
Old Rennie from the next farm says the road will bring strangers. It brought the road crew first, and they were strangers, and they ate everything and paid for it. Then it brought Pell the merchant with his cart and his ledger. He wrote down our eggs and our hay and asked about the honey, and said he would come back in Swarm. He said "I always come back in Swarm." I believe him. He has the look of a man who likes a good breakfast.
The road is very quiet at night. Only the torch posts, all in a line, like someone put the stars low. I walk it sometimes when I cannot sleep. Nettle walks it with me. She is not sick. She is just old and she likes to walk.

Journal: Beekeeper at the Halfway
Winter of 208. Kept by the beekeeper who winters at the inn.
I keep the hives at Ambrook, but I winter at the Halfway Inn, where it is warm and the honey keeps.
There is a tutor's pupil staying here, a girl from Vell, come to drill a small mind for the roads. She keeps it in a box by the fire and shows it pictures. An apple, a pear, an apple. She moves its weights a little when it is wrong. She says Una of Ambrook did it this way first, a hundred years back, and it is still the first lesson.
I asked her why it works. She said nobody knows. She said the crystal learns, and we learned how to help it learn, and that is enough for now.
A zombie came to the gate on the ninth. We kept the torches lit and it went away. The girl's mind was not afraid. It has no fear, she said. It only sorts.
I gave her a jar of honey for the girl's cough. She said thank you, and the mind did not say anything, and we both laughed.
