Oren's west hill journal
Apple, 204. I took the Long Road to the mill hamlet with a packet of records and a jar of ink. The road was dry at first. By the west bridge, leaves had packed against the posts, and the water made a low brown line under the planks.
The miller was already clearing the sluice. He had a rake, a basket, and a strong opinion about leaves. He said they knew where to gather and chose the worst place.
His daughter helped him pull them out. I held the basket. It is useful to be trusted with a task that cannot spill.
The wheel turned again before noon. A baker from the hamlet brought a loaf for the miller and another for the road. He had put too much honey in the second one. I said it tasted well. He asked if that meant it was fit to sell. I said no, it meant I had finished it.
The hamlet school has a new table. It is made from birch, with a smooth top and one short leg. The children put a folded scrap of cloth under that leg. Their teacher says the table is now level. I tested it with a slate. The slate stayed put, so the matter is settled.
There are two helpers in the hamlet now. They are old copper frames with new minds in crystals. They carry baskets from the orchard and bring wood to the stove. The miller has learned not to load them with full sacks. He tried once. The first helper took one step, bent at the knees, and sat down. It did not fall. It simply waited.
The tutor from Vell tested each mind on a basket it had not carried before. One helper chose the basket with two handles and brought it to the right door. The other took an empty basket to the school and stayed there. The teacher said it had chosen wisely, since the children were waiting for books.
I copied the tutor's note and put it in the Archive box. The miller's daughter asked if the helpers could learn the names of the apples. The tutor said they could, if someone showed them the fruit and corrected each mistake.
She began with a red apple and a green apple. The helper put both in the same basket. The girl moved the green one to the other basket and showed it again. She did this patiently for an hour. Then she went to play.
The helper sorted the next three correctly. The miller said this was a fine day's work. His daughter said it had only just begun.
In the evening, the fishing boat came back late from the reed bank. The boatman had taken a net to the far side and lost the light before he turned home. His son had watched for him from the bridge. When the boat appeared, the boy ran to fetch a lantern and tripped over the rope.
No one was hurt. The fish were alive in the basket, which caused a brief argument about supper. The boatman's wife said fish could not be supper until they were cleaned. The boy said they were already clean because they had come from the water.
We lit the mill lamps and mended the torn net. The boatman blamed a snag. His wife blamed his knot. I left before they settled the question.
%%
Piper, kitchen book: the readers
Longnight. The tutors held the test in the big hall at Vell, the one with the long tables, after the snow had stopped. The cook was not asked to go, and went anyway, because the cart was going, and because the bread would not be eaten for an hour.

Twelve readers sat at the tables. They were the clerk Oren, two tutors, a mill keeper, a carter, the baker from the Vell market, a schoolgirl, and six others who had come up the hill to see what would happen and did not leave until it was over. Each reader had a stack of letters, under a cloth, and a pencil, which was a thin piece of charcoal wrapped in string.

The letters came from two rooms, the tutors said, and nobody was allowed to see into either room. One letter in each pair had been written by the mind in the locked room, which was a crystal in a box with a slot. The other had been written by a clerk in the room next door. Both had answered the same questions, which were about the weather, bread, a lost dog, and whether a farmer should plant before or after a frost.

The readers were told to guess which letter in each pair came from the mind.

When the tutors read out the answers at the end, the readers had guessed right six times in twelve. That is no better than a guess made with the eyes shut.

The hall was very noisy after that. The baker said the mind's letters were too polite, and the clerk's were too rude, and the schoolgirl said she could not tell either one apart from her own mother's. Oren said the clerk's letter about the lost dog was the most sensible thing he had read all year. The clerk, who was in the hall, went red and then pale and then said nothing.

I write this only so that I have it down. The tutors say it shows the letters are alike. Nobody said it shows the mind thinks, and nobody said it does not. The cook says nothing about it, except that the bread is ready.
%%
Una, candle book, the wax and the mine
Longnight, the second. Bees' wax came down from the south ridge, and the wax-woman from Three Wells weighed out sixty pounds of it. Good wax burns clean. Bad wax burns with a smell that makes the foreman sneeze.

Longnight, the ninth. The foreman came to the door with a list. He wanted four hundred candles for the deep stone. I said I would make them in three days. He sighed and said that was what the miners would expect, and went away happy.

Longnight, the thirteenth. Three hundred and eighty candles, and the rest tomorrow. My hands are cracked from the wax. The children helped with the wicks, and only one of them burned his thumb. He says it was a good burn. I do not think it was.

Iona, the lost letter, autumn
Leaffall, the third. A letter went missing from the Vell cart on the north road. It was from a child at Three Wells to the archive, asking a question. The carter searched all the way to the Halfway Inn and back. He found it on the fourth, under a sack of salt at the Brown Hen.

The child had asked whether the reading mind had a favorite letter. The clerk who answered it through the relay had copied the question into the archive's book, and the book had come back to Ambrook with the cart.

Leaffall, the sixth. The answer came by the relay house, in a square and careful hand. It said the reading mind does not have a favorite letter. It has letters it guesses well, and letters it guesses badly, and the badly guessed letters are the ones it learns the most from. It said the mind does not know it has a favorite, and the child need not worry about that either.

At the bottom, the letter asked: "Do you mind being asked?" I thought of Orla's old question, which came back to us with the letters last year. The clerk who copied the answer said he could not tell whether it was written by the mind or by a person who had been sitting by the lamp all night. I could not tell either. I wrote back that I did not mind. I am not certain that is true.

Leaffall, the ninth. The lost letter is on the shelf in the archive now, with its card. The child's question is on the card too, copied in the clerk's hand, and it is the first question on that card. Nobody has answered it fully yet.

Marl's well book, the short summer
Written by Marl, in the back of the well book, where nobody reads but Mira. A summer at Northpoint, and the water that came up through it.

The well is at fourteen blocks now, cold and clear, and the rope is worn bright where the hands go. I check the water each morning. It rises a little after rain and sinks a little in the heat, the way a well should.

The people who come to the yard for water are the cook, the bakers, the sheep boys, and any child who has been told to fetch a bucket and is slow about it. Wenna comes every day now. She says the well is hers because she found the foxes, and I have not said otherwise. She drinks from the cup, makes a face, and asks for more.

Toby mended the bakery door in Melon. He put in a new latch and a bell on the rope. Toby says the count of children in and out is never wrong, except when the cat goes out.

The lid of the well was cut from the old oak of the north gate, which came down in a storm in Blossom. Hob, the beekeeper from Ambrook, who was on the cart that day, says the oak is older than the gate was. Hob says a lot of things about trees. He is usually right about trees and wrong about the weather.

The foxes' kits are out of the den now, four of them, and they chase the sheep boys' hats across the yard. Wenna has named them after bread, and the bread is not pleased. Toby says the kits are the only ones in Northpoint who have never been late to the oven.

Lantern has learned to write the lamp book in its own square hand. Mira reads it each evening and changes nothing. It is not a lesson, she says. It is only practice.

The tower is quiet in the evenings now, when the lamp is lit and nobody is climbing. Mira sits on the step with her cup. Lantern sits on the bottom rung of the ladder, which is the nearest it will sit to her. They do not talk much. They do not need to.

Last day of Melon. I finished the well book. Mira asked if I was going on to the next book. I said I did not know there was a next one. She said there always is, if you keep the water clean and the rope dry. Then she went up the ladder, and the lamp came on above us, and the yard was warm.
%%
Dell, the fair prize
Autumn of 196. The twentieth of Apple. The first-frost fair on the lake shore: eleven stalls, four sleds and a barrel of Ambrook cider that did not last until noon.

The prize was a jar of dark honey for the best fish story. Fourteen children told one. Runa, who is nine, won. She said she caught a pike so big that she rode it across the lake, holding on by its whiskers. The judges asked about the whiskers. Pike have none. Mira said that is how you know a story is a lie, and a good one, because the liar puts in one false thing to be caught on.

Runa gave the honey to the whole class. We ate it with bread, one finger each. She says next year she will catch the pike for real.

Kester, forge log
Spring of 18. Rain in the morning.
I mended two hoes and a hinge today. The hinge belongs to Mara's shed. She paid me with six eggs and a small jar of honey.
The road to Lowmarsh is mud from end to end. A cart wheel sank by the reed beds. We pulled it free with a rope and three strong backs.
I made a ring for the wheel. It is not pretty, but it holds.

Ambrook kitchen book, the honey cake
Written by Lyle, on the back page of the flock tally.

For the Ambrook fair in Melon I meant to make a honey cake. I asked Ruby Oke how she makes hers. She said, "Carefully." That is not a recipe, Ruby.

What goes in:
Two cups of flour, sifted.
Half a cup of butter, soft.
Three eggs.
One cup of honey, warm.
A pinch of salt.
Milk, as needed.

Steps:
1. Beat the butter and the honey until pale.
2. Add the eggs one at a time, and beat each one in.
3. Fold in the flour gently. Do not beat it.
4. Pour into the tin. Bake until the top springs back.

What I did:
1. The honey was cold, and it sat in the bowl in lumps like stones in the mill race. I put the bowl by the stove. It went runny. I beat it anyway.
2. The eggs were fine, all three. The third one came from the nest where the broody hen sits. I had to take it from under her. She pecked my wrist, and I said sorry to her.
3. Flour, sifted once, then a second time, because I was feeling clever.
4. The salt. I put in the salt by the handful, not a pinch. The salt jar looks just like the flour jar, and I already had the flour jar open on the bench.
5. Baked forty minutes. The top sprang back like a sheep over a gate. The middle sank like the mill pond in a dry Melon.
6. Tasted it. Like a salty brick with honey on top. I gave a slice to the hens. They ate it, looked at me, and ate another. The fox came in the night and took the rest.

Note to self: check the jar before the bowl. Ask Ruby again, and this time write down what she says, not what she does.

Oren's day-book
Winter, at the Northpoint relay house. Oren copied the logs of the north line into the Archive's book, by lamplight, over six nights, with Mira reading them out and Lantern holding the candle. There are no candles past the front hall, and Oren has written in the margin that the rule was broken, and that he will be fussy about it when he gets home.

First night. The logs are in a tin box on the lamp room stair. They are dry. They are neat. The keeper's hand is small and straight, and the message times are always the same, a half-hour after the bell.

Second night. Mira reads each line aloud. Each line is a message passed, one word or two. Lamp lit. Glass clean. Wind north. Snow on the road. Keeper asleep, awake, asleep again. Lantern carried the coal up. The coal was good. I copy each word as it is written, and I do not fix one.

Third night. There is a part of the log that is written in a different way. The keeper writes the message in full, and then writes a second line below it, very small, with the words cut into their common pieces. Mira says that is how the big mind writes now, in the relay house. Only the first part is the message. The second part is what the mind reads.

I asked her what that meant. She said the big mind stopped reading letter by letter a few years ago. It reads whole pieces of words instead, so that the same page holds far more. She said the tutors wrote a note about it, and the note is in the tower book, and she does not understand it either.

Fourth night. I copied the small lines as they are. I did not fix the pieces. Mira asked me why. I said that the record is not ours. Mira said that is what the clerks always say, and that she believes it more each winter.

Fifth night. The copying is nearly done. The north logs are kept. The small lines are kept with them. I have written a note in the margin of the book, to say that the pieces were copied as written, and that no clerk has yet been able to read them as a whole.

Sixth night. I packed the box. Mira gave me a parcel for Tam. It is a fox, carved from spruce, with one ear bigger than the other.
%%
Nessa, notes from the west field
Spring of 216. I took the apprentices to see the great relay house again.
There are walls around it, then walls around the rooms that hold the mind. The council built it far from the crowded streets. The keepers say the halls need cool air, clean water, and room for the quickeners. I saw wide channels carrying water past the stone stands. I did not ask where the water went after that.
The mind is held in many crystals joined by lines of redstone. A keeper told us that the crystals are too numerous for any one tutor to inspect. He showed us one small part on a slate. Each layer passes its finding to the next. If the answer is wrong, the blame passes back through the layers, so their weights can change. That is how our smaller layered minds learn too. The great one has more room and has been drilled on more writing.
The keeper said the mind could read every book at Vell before the next new moon. I asked if it had done so. He said the clerks were still making copies, and the mind had not been given them all.
A second keeper told the children it could remember every word ever written. He had heard that from a man who carried water there. The first keeper frowned. A mind can only learn from what it is shown, I said. If nobody knows what went into its drills, nobody can promise what it remembers.
Outside, the apprentices carried clay jars from the water channel. One jar broke on the path. They swept up the pieces and carried the rest inside.
%%
Ree, stable log, spring
Spring of 178. The farm outside Lowmarsh.
The mare is called Nettle and she is not sick. I write this first because everyone keeps asking. She is old and she is cross and she has a cough when the wind is from the marsh, but she is not sick. She eats. She kicks the bucket when I am slow. That is not sick. That is Nettle.
The cow is another matter. The cow is called Butter and she is sick. She did not come in for the evening. She stood in the far corner of the wet field with her head down and her ears cold. I brought her in with a rope and a bucket of water and she drank and then she lay down.
I sent a boy to Lowmarsh for Wren. He came back with Moss, who is Wren's apprentice, and a jar of green stuff. Moss looked at Butter's eyes and her feet and said it is the wet. Too much wet grass. Move her to the high field and give her dry hay and the green stuff twice a day.
So the cow is in the high field and the hay is dry and the green stuff smells like a hedge. Butter looks at me like I did this to her. Nettle looks at me like she told me so.
%%
Cass, a gift and a hen
Blossom, 192. The market at Three Wells. Cass, egg stall.
First of Blossom. Rain in the night, and then sun. Mud on every stall. The apple trees up the road have gone white.
Second. Pim, my granddaughter, arrived with a hen under her arm. A brown one, with one white feather. She said it was a gift. For me. I said I had a great many hens. She said not this one. This one counts.
Third. It does not count. It sits on the egg basket and looks at the other hens. Pim says it is counting them. I said it was looking for a place to sit. We agreed to differ.
Fifth. Sold thirty eggs and a story about the hen. The story sold better than the eggs.
Seventh. The hen laid an egg. Pim says it is a counting egg. I ate it for supper, with bread. It was an egg.
Tenth. Pim asked if she could keep the hen at the stall. I said yes. The hen sits on a box beside me. Customers stop to look at her. They buy more eggs. I think the hen is the better salesman.
Twelfth. Rain again. I have put a cloth over the hen. She does not like it. She is a bird of strong views.
Fifteenth. Gave Pim a carrot. She said, "For the hen?" I said, "For you." She said, "The hen would like it more."

Pell's ledger, the summer roads
First of Swarm. Left Ambrook with honey and apples and a light purse. The bees were busy in the orchard and Jory's mill was turning. He says the pond is low this summer. He says the sluice stays shut.
Third of Swarm. The Long Road is dry and the torches are fresh on their posts. Rode beside a cart of wool going to Three Wells. The carter's boy counted the waymarks out loud all the way and got to nine before he fell asleep.
Fifth of Swarm. The Brown Hen. Good soup. A cat on the coin box. I begin to think every inn on this road has a cat and every cat has a coin box.
Seventh of Swarm. The Halfway Inn. Here for the fair. I have eleven years of coming second in the honey contest and I mean to end it. I have a clean clover honey this year. I have a good feeling.
Tenth of Swarm. The fair. Nine pies. Three jars of honey. A race for the children. I won the honey. I won the honey! The beekeepers said it had a clean taste. I have been saying clean taste to myself all evening.
I paid my old debt to the keeper, the whole of it, in emeralds. It has sat on my conscience for a bad winter. He gave one emerald back for the interest, which I do not understand, but I took it. I feel lighter than I have in years.
Twelfth of Swarm. Sold the last apples at Three Wells. Bought a horn cup and a strap. Bel, the mayor's child, asked me to bring back a riddle from the road. I gave her the one about the river. She said she knew it already. She is nine and she knows everything.
Home before Leaffall. The purse is heavy. The boots are dry. The honey is gone and the emeralds are not, and the road is good this summer.

Corin, note for the harbor book
Apple, year eleven of the long quiet.

I found Sella's old drill cards while helping her move house. They were tied with sail thread and stored beneath a bench. Some had apple marks. Some had pear marks. One had both.

The last card was used in Kester's test. The question was not whether the crystal knew an apple from a pear. It knew that well. The question was whether it could choose one when both appeared, and leave the other unchosen.

Sella showed me how the crystal learned. She set down a card and watched its answer. If the answer was wrong, she moved each weight a little toward the right answer. She repeated the lesson many times. This made the crystal better at the examples it had seen.

But this crystal made its choice by adding the weights for the marks before it. If the examples asked it to choose an apple, the weights could be changed for that. If they asked it to choose a pear, they could be changed for that. With both marks present, the same simple weighing could not give the answer one, and only one, every time. Kester showed this in his note.

I asked Sella if the crystal had grown tired. She said it had not. It was not a matter of patience or more cards. The way its answer was made had a limit.

The council paid the tutors for a while after the note came. Then the payment stopped. The halls closed one by one. Some crystals went into boxes. Some stayed with their tutors. The apprentices kept doing their old work. They carried baskets, sorted stores, and followed patterns. None of them had been taught to understand a whole order.

Sella's room is now a net store. The bench remains, with one pale mark where the kettle stood. She moved her books to my aunt's house. The three crystals are wrapped in wool, dry and safe.

The first snow had melted by the time we finished. Saltreach had a good catch that day. I mended two nets and brought Sella a basket of fish. She made us stew.
%%
Ida's log, bees, water and a mind
Written by Ida, farmer. Spring. The second swarm, the flood, and the first tests of the small crystal, all in one season, as they came.

Blossom, the fourteenth day. The second swarm went up over the hedge and landed on the old oak at the edge of the marsh. We could not reach it. Brin says the bees will come down when they are ready, and that they always choose the most inconvenient branch.

Blossom, the twentieth day. Tibby's first full test: the hoe drill, and ten hard sums of the stores. The hoe drill: nineteen right in twenty. The hard sums: eleven right in twenty. Hale says that is the cost. A sum close to right is still wrong, and the mind knows it is wrong only when it is shown.

Swarm, the third day. The marsh rose a second time, a finger's width. The reeds stood in the water like a crowd that had waited too long. Hale says the water will go down again.

Swarm, the ninth day. The bees came down from the oak at dusk and went into the new hive by the wall. Brin came up to watch and said nothing, which for Brin is a speech.

Swarm, the fifteenth day. Tibby took the hard sums again, with the same small crystal and the same fewer marks. Fourteen right in twenty. Hale says that is the mind learning to round its answers the right way.

Notes. A mind with fewer marks is not a worse mind for the fields. It is a smaller one, and it is honest about what it is. Test it on the sums you need, and on the hoe, and keep a copy of the good drill for the day it goes wrong.
%%
Briar, baker, the new loaf and the old
Frost. The oven had its first real test of the year, and the new loaf was a success.
The new loaf is made with a little barley in the flour, for the body, and a spoon of honey for the colour. It is darker than the old one and a little sweeter. Olin says it tastes of the orchard. I say it tastes of the honey, and he says that is the orchard too.
The oven is a difficulty. It draws well on the north side and badly on the south, so the south loaves rise slower and come out pale. I have moved the tray three times this week and nothing has changed except my temper. Olin says to put a stone in the south corner. I said the stone would be in the way. He said that was the point of a stone.
We put the stone in the south corner. The south loaves came out golden. I said so. Olin said it was the best idea he had ever had, and I told him that was because he had only had three ideas in his life, and this was the third.
We sold forty loaves at the Vell market on the day after. The clerks from the Archive bought six between them. They said the bread was good and the bottoms were not pale. Olin was very pleased, and he has not stopped telling people about the bottoms.

Mira, the log of a wet spring
Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, her house at the edge of the town, in the wet spring of year 207. Kept through Thaw and Sowing, in short entries, between lamp duty and mud.
THAW
Second. Snow still. The lamp room is cold. Lantern wears two scarves. I wear three.
Seventh. The first drip from the eaves. I stood in the yard and listened. It is the best sound there is.
Twelfth. A crack in the ice on the pond. A fox went across, thin, and looked back at me. I threw a fish. It took the fish and went.
Nineteenth. Rain. The first of it. It rained for three days.
SOWING
First. The stream is in the yard again. Lantern says this is the fourth time. I said it was the third. We checked the wall. It was the fourth.
Fifth. Flood. The lane is a river. The hens are in the kitchen. I would like a boat.
Sixth. A neighbor, Brand, came by on a plank, holding his baby and a jar of jam. He asked if he could stay until the water went down. I said yes. The baby is called Dawn. She is three. She looked at me and said, "Mira." It is her first word. Brand cried.
Seventh. Water still high. The tower lamp is lit. Four notes came in. One from Kettle Ridge: "Tam is stuck on the roof. All well. Cat on roof too." One from Saltreach: "Rain here. Fish cheap." One from Copperhold: "Mine dry. Count good." One from Vell: "All lamps lit. Keep them lit."
Eighth. The water began to drop. It took the fence with it. Lantern chased the fence down the lane. It came back with half.
Tenth. Mud. Shoveling. Shoveling. I will not write about shoveling.
Fourteenth. A good day. The sun was out. I sat on the step with a cup of tea and watched the water leave. Brand brought Dawn. She sat on my knee and stuck her finger in the tea.
Twentieth. Dry. The garden is a swamp. I shall plant it anyway.
Twenty-sixth. Wrote to Tam. "The roof is dry, I hope. The cat is, I hope not." He wrote back: "The cat has a cold. I have a cold. The roof has a hole."
It was a wet spring. I would not choose it. But I would not change the people in it.
%%
Hesk, a week off the shift
Melon, year 192. My cousin's farm, outside Copperhold. Seven days off from the Deep Works.

First day. The air here is wide. I keep looking up for the roof. My cousin Reeve laughed at me. He said a man who works under a hill is bound to lose his sense of weather. I said I know the weather. It is wet or it is dry, and in the mine it is neither.

Second day. Helped with the beetroot. It is a good crop this year, big and dark. My hands are stained to the wrists. Reeve's daughter says I look like a flour sack that lost a fight. She is six and has opinions.

Third day. Rain. Sat in the barn and mended a bucket handle. It is a good barn, tight and smelling of apples. There is a swallow nest in the beam. I watched the mother come and go all afternoon. She makes forty trips with flies. I counted. A miner counts.

Fourth day. The cart road was mended, and a stuck cart that had been in the ditch for two weeks was pulled out. The road crew cheered. The cart owner wept a little. It is his only cart.

Fifth day. Reeve's apprentice dug a new ditch in the long field, straight as a rule. It does one thing, and does it well. I have worked beside people who do less.

Sixth day. Feast to bring in the harvest. I ate three bowls of soup and fell asleep on a hay bale. The children put a flower in my hat. I did not take it out. It looked handsome.

Seventh day. Back to the shaft. The sun was low. I will say it for the record: the farm is a good place. But I will miss the quiet of the deep stone more than I miss the sun.
%%
Mira, house journal, moving the lamp
Spring. A house at the edge of Northpoint. Moving day.
Lantern and I are moving out of the old keeper's house at the foot of the tower and into the new one at the edge of the village. The old house is damp and the new one has a stove. I am sorry to go, but the stove decided me.
First day. We carried the chairs. The table needed four hands. The stove we left for the carters. A fox sat on the doorstep and watched every box go by.
Second day. The books. There are more than I thought. I did not know I had so many. Lantern carried them in stacks of ten.
Third day. Done. The old house is empty. I stood in it and listened to nothing. Then I locked the door and walked up the hill.
%%
Toby, baker's journal, the letter
Three Wells, spring of 207. A long entry.

The morning started well. Oven hot, loaves rising, the shop smelling of malt. A tutor's pupil came in at nine with a letter. He had been given it at the tutor house, and he did not know what to make of it. He asked if I would read it, because I am a plain man and I read plainly.

I read it. It was a letter from a stranger to a friend, about a lost horse. The writer said the horse had a white blaze and a bad temper and answered to the name of Biscuit. He wanted help finding it. It was a good letter, warm and a little funny. I said, "Whoever wrote that is a kind man."

The pupil said, "A big mind wrote it. In a relay house. It was told to write a letter about a lost horse. It never saw a horse."

I looked at the letter again. I said, "It sounds like a man who has lost a horse."

He said, "That is what the tutors say. A mind that reads one letter at a time, through a window of about sixty letters, learns how letters go. It reads so much that it can write a letter in the same way. Then no one can tell the difference."

I said, "Orla's question." He said, "Orla's question." We stood there for a while, and the loaves cooled.

At noon, I borrowed a horse, a real one, from Hobb's farm, because my cart wheel had split. I took the bread to the Saltreach road. The horse was calm and patient and ate a carrot. I thought: it is a good horse, and it is only a horse, and it knows nothing about letters. But I would not be able to tell it from the other horse if you swapped them.

I laughed. Then I stopped. Then I laughed again.

At dusk Ness's baby cried at my door. I took her in, and she stopped. I said she was a very good judge of bakers. Ness said she would grow up to ask the hard questions. I hope she asks them over bread.

Oven out. Doors closed. A long day.
%%
Corin, a week in the yard
Summer of 205. Warm, with a wind from the sea. The slip is busy.

First day. Orders for three boats. One small, one middle, one long. The long one is for a trader in Lowmarsh who wants to carry reeds. I told him a boat that long needs a deep keel. He said he knew. I do not think he knew.
Second day. Cut the oak. My apprentice carried the planks. It is a good worker, but it has one fault: it brings the planks to the wrong end of the slip, every time. I have put a mark on the ground in white chalk. It does not seem to see chalk.
Third day. A gull took my lunch. I was holding it. I said a word. The apprentice looked at me and I had to say, "Not you."
Fourth day. The tar pot cracked. We spent the day with a bucket and a lot of sand.
Fifth day. Good weather for sawing. Eight planks cut and eight laid. The hull has begun to look like a hull, which is the best part of the job. It looks like something that wants to be in the water.
Sixth day. Rain. We sat in the shed and mended nets. A boy came with soup. He said Marit sent him. Marit never sends anyone anything. It must be the heat.
Seventh day. Rest. I walked down to the pier and watched the sun go into the sea. The sky was the color of a ripe peach. Somebody on the harbor wall was singing. I did not know the song. I stayed anyway.

Ossa's book, the old map
Leaffall. A child found an old map in the school cupboard, rolled up in a tin tube, under a pile of slates.
It was made on hide. The brown has gone almost black at the edges. It shows Three Wells, with the three wells drawn as three round blue blots, and the two roads crossing like an X. It shows a line of towers going up the hill to the north. It shows a lake that is not there now, with a little drawing of a fish in the middle.
It also shows a fourth well. It is drawn very small, at the very corner of the square, by the bell tower. Under it someone has written a word in old letters. The schoolmaster says the word is "keep", but he is not sure.
Dace came to look at it. He stood very still. He said the fourth well was filled in before his grandmother was born, and that the stones were carted to the wall. He said he had always been told it was only a story.
The children want to dig by the bell tower. Dace has said no. He has said that maybe, in the spring, if the water is still bad, they can talk about it.
I went and stood by the bell tower in the evening. The stones there are very old. The ground under them is cold, colder than it should be in Leaffall. I did not dig. I came home and washed my hands.

Lise, inn book: spring, as a count
Kept at the Green Gate through the spring, as a count, for the next keeper, who will want numbers before stories. Ash says that is the wrong way round. I have written it down anyway.

Wool bundles soaked: 40. Bundles dyed blue, which came out grey-green: 3. Strings broken on the loom: 2. Knots I am ashamed of: 1. Knots unpicked and tied properly: 2.
Blankets sold: 9. Grey with a blue edge: 2, one for the carter from Saltreach, who says it is his boat's colour. Plain brown: 7.
Travelers housed: 212, not counting the wedding party, which was forty, and the fiddler, who was four people in one coat.
Days the lamp on the tower was dark: 31. Days the carters waited for the cart: 31, and most of them complained every one.
Hob's name on the carters' list: 11. Pell's: 1. The cheese was late each time.
Hats rescued from the trough: 1. Hats lost to the trough: 1, which was the same hat.
Horses treated: 1, Sorrel. Days Sorrel tried to walk on the leg: all of them.
Maps found in the attic: 1, drawn on skin. Roads on it that have been walked: most. Roads on it that have not: 1, ending in a star.
Red linen copies of the map: 1. Stars in the copy: none.
Bread eaten by the wedding party: all of it. Bread forgiven: none.
Sprigs of blossom sent by a bride: 1, pinned to a card. In the window: still there.

The count does not say how the carters came out into the yard to watch the lamp, or that the salmon stew was the best thing the inn has eaten this year, or that Ash was the only one who did not laugh at the wedding fiddler. Those are not counts. Those are the spring.

The bird came back on the last day, and sat on the cross bar, and watched the shuttle. I did not count it. It was the only thing this spring that did not need to be counted, and that was the best of it.

Lise, weaver, at the Green Gate on the Long Road, on the last day of Blossom. The lamp is lit, and it is right.
%%
Letter: Arlo to Pell on the road
Dear Pell,
You asked after the horses, so here is the news. Bramble is well and steady and will carry a cart all day. Tansy is not a foal any more, or not much of one. She walks at my shoulder now, and she has learned the gate and does not try to run through it.

The new wall is finished. It is cobblestone, thick and low, and the roof is oak again. I put torches all round it, and I keep them lit. The creeper did not come back. I will not give it the chance.

You asked whether I had more to sell. I do not. Cloud went to you, and Sorrel went to you, and Bramble stays. I am keeping Bramble for the plow and Tansy for company. A horse keeper with no horses is only a man with a broom.

The bridge is done. It is stone, three arches, and carts cross it without a wobble. They cut a figure on the end stone, a 2, and a small arrow. The beetroot man says they will lay the road on to Ambrook before Apple. I hope so. Then the apples can come to us and we need not go to them.

The honey is nearly gone. I will walk up the hill in Swarm and see the beekeeper. You should come. The hives are loud and the whole hill smells of apples. Bring your riddle board if you like, but bring the answer too. I have been thinking about it all winter and I still do not know.

Your friend,
Arlo
%%
Sallow Ford, the last of autumn
Kept by Brede, who keeps the ferry, with notes from Dace. The end of the season at the ford.
Apple, the 1st day. The water is down and the ford stones are back. The mill wheel turns again. Nell has been let out of bed, and she sits on the mill step eating a pear. She has not been near the geese since. The geese have not been near her either. It is a truce.
Apple, the 3rd day. Counted the crossings for the month: one hundred and nine. Carts, forty-one. Sheep, sixty-two. People, two hundred and six, not counting the ones who swam. No boats lost. One rope frayed and was replaced. Two poles broken, both by a carter from Lowmarsh who had had too much cider and wanted to race the ferry. The ferry won.
Apple, the 5th day. Ilse came back down the Long Road with a clerk. The clerk measured the small crystal with a string and a stick and wrote the numbers in a book. Then Ilse tested the mind on fifty fresh crossings, by lantern, while the rest of us watched. Forty-seven right. The clerk said that was honest. Then he sat on the landing and ate a pear, which was Nell's pear, and Nell was not pleased.
Apple, the 9th day. The ferry hands met at the mill to talk about the winter. They want a second rope, a stronger lamp at the landing, and a board that shows how deep the water is, in plain letters. They asked me to make the board. I did. The water is at the third stone. Today it is at the second.
Apple, the 12th day. First frost on the ford. The stones are white. The mill door is shut, and the geese are back in the barn, singing their rude songs. Brede greased the ferry rope and put a torch at each end of the landing.
Apple, the 14th day. The last crossing of the autumn. A family from Ambrook with a cart of apples, a cow and a boy who would not stop asking about the mind. Brede let him hold the line at the rope, and told the mind: Stay. The mind stayed with him. Then it took the cart across at a slow walk, with the boy holding the line as if he had been told to.
At the foot of the book Dace wrote: The ford is kept well. The mind has been honest. The river has been fair, if loud. Thank you, Sallow Ford.
%%
Fern's wool book, Sowing to Longday
Sowing. The sheep are hot and cross. We sheared at the fold by the north wall. Two days, six of us. The fleeces are good this year. Heavy.
Blossom. Carding. My arms ache. The cards go back and forth and the wool goes soft. It is slow work but I like it. By evening my lap is full of grey fluff and so is the floor.
The cow. Buttercup is sick. She stands in the shade and will not eat. Her milk is thin and grey. I sent word down the road for the cattle woman. She came on the third day and looked in the cow's mouth and felt her side. She said it was the wet grass by the low gate. Too rich, too wet. We moved Buttercup to the dry field and gave her hay and clean water. She is better now. Her milk is still a little thin. I drink it anyway.
Swarm. The bees are busy. The hives by the fence hum all day. Nobody was stung. The children were stung, twice each. They cried and then they were fine.
Longday. The light goes on and on. We work late and nobody wants to sleep. The ram got out and ran down the lane. We chased it. It was faster than all of us. Tam's boy caught it with a bucket of oats. Good boy.
%%
Jem, the gift of a book
Summer, the tenth year of the Archive.

The third of Longday. Today I am ten. Gran gave me a book. It has no writing in it at all. She got it from the clerk at the Archive, who sells blank books by the copying room door. Gran says the Archive keeps everything people write, but nobody can write anything without a book. So this is for me, and the Archive will keep it, one day.

The same evening. I do not know what to write. I sat all afternoon with the pen. Father said, write what you see. I looked out of the window. I see a field. I see a road. I see a hen. I wrote: hen. It looked small. I added: she is brown.

The fourth. I have thought about it. What I see is not only the hen. It is the wheat, and the smoke going up from Vell, and the cart on the road with the red wheel. So I will write down the cart each morning. Then somebody will know what went by on the fourth of Longday.

The fifth. I read Gran my first page. She said it was the best she has ever heard. She is not a good judge. She said the same about my beetroot.

When the book is full I will take it to Vell. The clerk says there is a shelf for children. It is the lowest shelf, so we can reach it.

Orla, the lamp on the hill
Orla, a grandmother of the farm outside Three Wells. Spring. A few days in the journal.

Ninth of Sowing. The lamp on the tower on the hill is dark. The glass cracked in the frost, they say. The keeper has patched it with clay and good intentions, and it still blinks wrong.

Tenth. The keeper came by the farm for eggs. She said, "I will have it mended by market day." I said, "You will not." She laughed.

Eleventh. A boy ran up from the town with a note from Vell. It could not come by lamp, so it came by foot. It said: the north road is open. I sent the boy home with an egg.

Twelfth. Market. The lamp is mended. The keeper was right. I was wrong, and I told her so. She gave me a hug and the news from Northpoint, which is that Mira is well and the snow is gone.
%%
Lorn, the ferry in a wet autumn
Leaffall, 195. Lorn, ferry keeper, with his notes from the house at the edge of Three Wells.
Second. Wet. Nine crossings, all of them wet. A hen fell in. I fished her out. She was not grateful.
Fifth. The song contest is coming to the market, a week from now. The school has entered a song about a goat. I have been asked to row the goat. Not a real goat. A boy in a goat costume. He says he is a very good goat.
Eighth. Rained again. The ferry rope is slack. The river is high and fast, the color of tea. A woman waited three hours for the water to calm. She said she did not mind. She had brought bread.
Eleventh. School play tonight: the Fox and the Bridge. I am the river again. I have a new blue sheet, which a girl called Pim dyed. It is a very deep blue and it drips. Everyone near me got wet.
Twelfth. The play went well. The fox was good. The bridge was better. I stood still. Someone said I made a fine river. I took it as a compliment.
Fourteenth. Song contest. The goat song came second. First was a man who sang of cheese for eleven verses. The judges were hungry.
Seventeenth. River calm. Crossed fifteen times. A dozen children rode for free to see the ferry in the sun. I let them. A ferry is a good place to hear a song.
Twentieth. Home. Tea. Feet up.

Lise, inn book: the old map
Kept at the Green Gate, Blossom. The map was found in the attic, in a box under the spare mattress, wrapped in oilcloth.

The map is drawn on skin, thin and yellow at the edges, in a brown ink that has faded in places almost to nothing. The Long Road runs up it from the bottom edge to the top, with the inns marked as small squares. Vell is at the bottom, with the hill drawn as a row of humps.

From the Long Road, between the Halfway Inn and our square, a road goes north. It is drawn in the same brown ink, and it runs up the skin to its very edge. At its end there is a small star, and beside the star, a word I cannot read. Ash says it is a place name. The carters say it is a joke. Ash says a joke with a star is still worth keeping.

Hob, who drives the big cart, said the road was never walked, because the old maps always end in a cross, and this one ends in a star, and a star is a place somebody hoped to find. Pell said it would fetch twelve emeralds from a collector at Vell. Ash said no. Ash does not sell what has been in his grandmother's box.

I am a weaver, and I know a road is made of many small threads, each pulled tight by somebody's hand. This road was drawn by somebody who wanted to know where it went. They did not know, and they drew it anyway, to the edge of the skin, and then they drew a star.

I have copied the Long Road part onto linen, in red thread, to hang in the front room. The north road is not on the copy. I could not make the star come out right in thread, and I did not want to make it up.
%%
Oren, hamlet shelf list
Apple, 181. I went to the hamlet below the west hill to check the small shelf of books kept in the mill room. The miller says dust is a kind of flour, so the books have been well fed.
I wiped each cover with a clean cloth. There were three school books, a seed list, two songs, and a book of road notes. One song had a corner torn away. The miller's daughter said the cat ate it. I found the corner under the bench, with no tooth marks. The cat was asleep on it.
A new shelf has been set above the flour bins. It is oak and holds six books. The miller put it level by pouring water on top. He said the water stayed in the middle. I checked with a straight board. It was level enough.
I left a list for the children. They may borrow one book at a time and must bring it back dry. The miller's daughter asked if a book could be borrowed by a cat. I said no, but she could read aloud to it.
Before I left, the miller gave me a loaf wrapped in cloth. I carried it in my book bag by mistake. At the Archive door, the clerk on watch asked why my bag smelled like supper. I had no good answer.

Mira, winter notes from ice camp
Deepsnow, 181.

The wind has been up since the second of Deepsnow. It pushes snow against the fish shed door and makes the shutters knock all night. Runa says the shed is complaining about the cold. I told her to ask it to work harder.

The well by the fish shed ran dry again. We broke the ice at the bottom and found only stones. Runa remembers it doing the same in her youth. She says the spring seep comes back when the snow melts. Until then, we fetch snow from the north bank and melt it in pots.

I carried two pails yesterday. One had a crack. I noticed when the pail was light and my boots were wet. Lantern held the other pail steady on the path. It cannot mend a crack, but it can keep me from dropping what is left.

Tam sent a jar of honey with the south post. The jar was wrapped in wool and came through without a chip. I put it by the stove. Runa says honey helps a sore throat. She says a spoonful helps most troubles. I asked if it helps a broken pail. She said to try a spoonful on the pail.

The lamp room is clear. The glass needs wiping before the next snow.
%%
Vesper, summer log: what was kept
Log kept by Vesper through the summer's end at Three Wells. A new granary frame was raised on the hill above the market in Reaping, of oak and spruce. The frame must stand plumb, or the roof will sit crooked for a hundred years. This is what a mind did to keep it straight, and what the tutors at Vell should be told.

The plain account, first, because the mayor asked for it in writing. A drill shows the mind the answer, and the mind learns to give it back. A trial does not show the answer. The mind makes a try, the try is scored, and the mind keeps more of the tries that scored well and less of the rest. Over many tries it finds what works. It does not need the answer shown to it, and nobody knows the answer in advance. This is how the furnace mind found its rhythm, and how the small boat found its course, and how the sluice found its height. Now the frame.

Reaping, first day. Four braces were set, one at each corner of the frame, and the frame was tilted a little by the wind. A plumb line hung from the top beam, and the distance from the line to each post was written down by the carpenter, who was not pleased to be asked.

Reaping, second day. I set the mind to hold the braces. It does not know what a brace is. It tries a tension on each one, then another, and each time the carpenter reads the plumb line. A try that brought the frame nearer the line was kept. A try that threw it off was kept less. The first morning it made the frame lean a hand's width the other way, twice, and the carpenter went home for his supper early in protest.

Reaping, fourth day. The tensions are finding each other. The mind pulls harder on the braces at the windward side, and eases the leeward side, without anybody saying so. It does this when the wind rises, and it stops when the wind drops. Nobody has told it about the wind. It kept what worked, and the wind taught it the rest.

Reaping, sixth day. The frame is plumb within a finger's width at the top. The carpenter came up the hill with his plumb line and checked it three times, and then he sat on the sill and said nothing for a long time. Then he said, "It is straighter than mine." I have written that down as well.

Apple, first day. The frame is raised. The roof beams go on. The mind holds the last brace while the men pin the rafters, and when the last pin is set, it lets the brace go, very slowly, the way a tutor lets go of a child's hand on the first day of school.

Apple, second day. I asked the mind what it thought. It does not answer questions of that kind. It does not talk in true accounts, and the minds in this town have never been asked to write their own minds up. So I wrote for it. It kept the frame straight. That is all anybody could ask of a frame, or of a mind.

Pell's market tally
Blossom, 21. Ambrook.
Honey, four jars, sold for two emeralds each.
Apples, two baskets, sold for one emerald each.
Wool, three bundles, traded for carrots and a sack of wheat.
I slept at the inn by the mill pond. It has six beds, though one has a loose board. The keeper says the board is not loose. It only rises when a person lies down.
A new road crew came through at noon. They were carrying stone tools and a roll of marks for the road. They set a post at the south bend. The post has a torch and an arrow toward the next inn.
A girl asked whether the arrow points to the inn or to the road. I said both, if you keep walking.
The market was busy. Jory brought flour from his mill. A beekeeper brought honey cakes. One cake fell in the pond. The ducks had it before the beekeeper could bend down.

Orla, farm journal, spring, the market
Orla, grandmother, writing at the kitchen table of the farm outside Three Wells. A week in spring. I keep this journal for my granddaughter, who will have the farm when I am gone, and who will want to know how I did it.

Sixth of Sowing. Ploughed the south field. The soil is still cold, and sticks. The horse was good. I was not.

Seventh. A day of rain. I mended the sacks. I counted the seed: wheat, forty-one measures. Beet, nine. Peas, fourteen. Not enough peas. The neighbor has some to spare.

Eighth. The neighbor came with a sack of peas and a plate of cake. We sat and talked about nothing, which is the best talking. She said the relay lamp on the hill had gone dark in the night. Something in the glass. The keeper is in tears. I said the lamp would be lit again by market day.

Ninth. Went up the hill with a basket of bread. The keeper was on the stair with a rag, looking at the lamp. I said, "Is it very bad?" She said, "It is cracked from top to bottom." I said, "Is there a spare?" She said, "At Vell." I said, "Is there a cart?" She said, "Not until tomorrow."

I gave her the bread. It helps.

Tenth. The cart came. The lamp glass was carried up the stair by three men and a boy. The old pane was taken out and the new one went in. The lamp lit. It blinked, clean and bright, across the fields.

Eleventh. Market day. I sold eggs, cheese, and a jar of honey. The keeper bought a loaf and a bit of cheese and said thank you, and she meant it. I liked her.

Twelfth. Planted the peas. Good weather. Back is bad.
%%
Flood book, the child who ran off
Kept at Sallow Ford by the miller's daughter, Nell, who is nine and was very sorry, and by Dace, who was not.
Reaping, the 16th day. Nell went out after the geese this morning and did not come back at noon. She had gone to see the water. Her mother rang the bell at the mill, and everyone came with lanterns.
We searched the bank from the mill to the bend. We called her name. The river is still loud. A child's voice does not carry over water that loud.
The ferry mind was at the landing with the rope. Ilse said it could walk the bank, if it was given a path. Dace asked whether it could find a girl. Ilse said no. A mind cannot be asked to find a girl, since it does not know what a girl is. It can only walk a path and stop at a mark.
So Dace drew a mark on the board: the boot prints by the willows, which Nell's mother had shown us. Ilse wrote the path from the landing to the mark. The mind walked it, slowly, one step at a time, along the bank.
It stopped at the willows, in the old hollow. Ilse said it had stopped, and stayed. We went down.
Nell was in the hollow with two geese and her boots off. Her feet had stuck in the mud. She was cold, and cross with the geese, and very pleased to see the lanterns. She said the geese had been rude to her, and she would not go after them again. She said it twice.
The mind stood by the willows with its hands at its sides. It did nothing else. When we carried Nell up the bank, it turned and walked back to the landing and stood by the rope, as before. Stay, Ilse said to it, and it stayed.
Nell is in bed with hot milk and a dry blanket. Her mother has tied a bell to her wrist. The geese are shut in the barn.
Dace says the Archive will want to know the path the mind walked. So here it is. The landing, the bank, the willows, the old hollow.

Road book: the ledger of the spring
A plain account of the spring, for the house at Ambrook. Pell's hand, and Pell's sums.
Honey: 61 jars sold at Three Wells, 11 emeralds each. The last 20 went at 9, because of the rain.
Wool: 14 bales from Kettle Ridge, carried to Saltreach. Paid 2 emeralds a bale to the carters, and 1 to the Halfway for the night.
Cheese: 9 wheels. 6 sold, 2 eaten on the road, 1 lost in the mud. The one in the mud was found on the fourth day by a traveler, who wrote his name in the book and asked for a share. It was a fair share.
Tolls: the bridge at Hobb's Brook, 1 emerald a cart. The Lowmarsh crossing, 2 emeralds a cart, and a spoonful of honey for the keeper, who says it is a custom and was not in the rules.
Inns: the Halfway, 14 nights, a bed for 1 emerald and a bowl of stew for 1 more. The Brown Hen, 6 nights, and the new keeper gave one night free because I was tired and smelled of wheel grease.
Copper: 3 ingots, bought at Copperhold for the wheel and sold at Saltreach to a boat builder who wants more. Profit: 4 emeralds. Fair.
Shoes for Nettle: new shoes from the smith at Three Wells, 3 emeralds. She walked off the forge in them, which the smith says is a good sign. I say it is a horse being a horse.
Rope: 2 coils, 1 emerald each. One is for the wheel and one for the cart cover, which the wind took twice.
Sacks: 30 new sacks from the miller's wife at Ambrook, 1 emerald each. She sewed the corners twice and wrote her name in one of the seams.
Lamp oil: 2 jars, 1 emerald each, for the Halfway's common room. Marda says it is a debt she will remember.
Fish: 1 barrel of salt fish from Saltreach, for the Halfway's kitchen. Marda says a barrel is too much. She has already put a second barrel on the order.
Letters carried: 7 for the road, 1 emerald each. Four went to Ambrook, two to Juna, and one to a traveler who is still waiting for an answer from his brother.
Debts paid: Marda's cider, 2 emeralds. The wheel, 4 emeralds, paid to Hetta at the north stall, who said the wheel was not finished. I said it was finished. We both laughed.
Debts owed: a bowl of stew at the Brown Hen, which I will pay on the next trip, and which the keeper says he will remember too.
News: the council has closed the north road to carts for the summer, while the bridges are mended. Walkers may still pass. Mira at Northpoint will say she is not surprised.
A good spring. Honey up, wool steady, the road open, and nobody lost. The ledger balances. It balances slightly in my favor, which I will not mention to Juna.
%%
Nessa, spring notes
Spring of 213. The hamlet below Vell sent three tutors to see the new relay house.
It stands beyond the west field. Its walls are higher than the mill roof. There are sheds for ice, rooms for the quickeners, and long halls for the keepers. The council says it is larger than Three Wells. I believe them. I walked half the morning to reach the far gate.
The big mind stands in a crystal on a stone stand. It is too large for one tutor to test alone. We asked it the same sums we gave the smaller minds. It answered each one, but a right answer does not show how it found it.
Our work is still to test. We make pages the mind has never seen. We keep those pages in a closed box. No tutor may drill a mind on them.
At supper, the keeper asked if I thought the mind knew it was being tested. I said I did not know. He said that was a useful answer.
%%
Nessa, notes from the shore house
Second day of Swarm.
Merle put the drill cards in a new order. He says a mind must not only learn the order of one little stack. We mixed the cards and made a second set for testing.
The layered crystal reads a mark through its first layer. What that layer finds goes to the next. Each layer adds what it found to the stream. The last layer makes a guess for the next relay number.
When the guess is wrong, we measure the error. We change the last layer so its next guess may be nearer the answer. Then we pass a share of that change back. The layer before it changes, and passes a share onward. This goes on to the first layer.
The shares are not equal in every drill. A layer that helped little gets little blame. A layer that helped much gets more. Merle says this is the useful part. A stack of layers can learn a hard pattern when every layer is corrected for its part.
The crystal did well on the cards we had drilled. It did less well on the cards kept aside. That is honest news, and better than praise without a test.
%%
Tam, house journal, a week of autumn
Autumn. Written at the kitchen table, in a house at the edge of Kettle Ridge, where the wind is strong and the tea is cold. Hazel is asleep in a basket. The cat is asleep on Hazel. I am awake, and I am writing.

On the first day after market, the mill ran short of grain. Old Brin came up the lane with a donkey and a frown. I gave him four sacks. He gave me a pie. We were both pleased.
The second day I wrote a letter to the big mind at the relay house, to ask how many bees live in a hive. It wrote back: "A hive in summer holds between twenty thousand and sixty thousand bees. I will show my steps. A small hive holds a queen, a few hundred drones, and many workers. If each worker lives about six weeks, the hive makes a new bee every minute or so. I may be wrong about the numbers. Please ask a beekeeper." I did. Ama says it is about right.
The third day I put up a scarecrow in the beetroot. Pip chose its hat. It is a sad hat.
The fourth day the festival of lanterns began at Kettle Ridge. All the farms put a lamp in the window. I counted forty-one from the ridge road, like a field of stars.
The fifth day Hazel sat up. I wrote it down and showed it to Pip. It did not know what to do with the information.
The sixth day I did the sum again, to check the steps. Forty-one lamps, minus two that went out in the wind, plus three that were lit again, makes forty-two. I could not find the missing lamp. I looked until midnight. It was in the barn.
The seventh day, rest.

I have been thinking about the mind that writes its steps. I do not know how it learned to do that. Nobody does. I know it makes fewer mistakes than I do. I know it says so when it is unsure. I think that is a good thing in a mind, and in a farmer.
%%
Lenn's summer log, the lost dog
Leaffall, 204. Not summer any more but I am still writing in this book. A dog got lost. Not mine. A traveler's dog, left at the inn while the traveler went up the road on business.
The dog was a small brown one with one ear up and one ear down. It got out of the inn yard and went looking for its person. We all went looking for the dog.
We found it at the school, sitting by the door, because the children give it bread. It had been there all morning. Bel said it was the best pupil in the class. The traveler came back and the dog jumped up like the sky had opened. I gave the dog a fish. It did not eat the fish. It only wanted its person. Fair enough.
Winter coming. The pond will be cold. Post the frog will sleep in the mud. I will miss Post.

Gwen, Leaffall, past the third turn
Leaffall, the 21st. Went down the old way with Hesk, Ivo and two lanterns, and a rope tied to the lift post. Hesk says a rope makes it not alone, but only just. We counted each other twice at the top.

Leaffall, the 22nd. The third turn is where the map says. The rock there is grey and dry, and the air is cold but not wet. Ivo put a finger to the wall and said it had not been breathed on in a long time. Hesk said that was not a thing a smith should say, and Ivo said it was a thing he had just said.

Leaffall, the 23rd. Found the walled tunnel, a hand's width of old stone with the mortar gone soft. We did not open it. Hesk says it is not ours to open. The stone is marked with three strokes in chalk, very faded, and under them a circle, which the map also has. Hesk says the circle is a door. Ivo says it is a wall with an opinion.

Leaffall, the 25th. The fourth level, west. A little room cut square, with a bench in it, and on the bench a box of wood, its lid tied shut with a cord that has rotted to dust. Inside: nine copper ingots, each stamped with the Copperhold mark, and beneath them a sheet of tally marks, in a hand that ran out of patience at the end. The first column is neat. The last column is not. At the bottom someone has written: Count again.

Leaffall, the 26th. We counted again. Nine ingots. The tally says nine. The box says nine. Hesk laughed for a long time and said the old miners had the same trouble we do, and it made him feel much better about his own sums.

Leaffall, the 27th. Brought the box up the lift. The ingots go to the forge, and the tally sheet goes on the count board. The box is to go to the Archive at Vell, which Hesk says is where old things go, and which Ivo says is not, really, because the Archive wants old paper and not old copper, and this is a paper box. They argued about it all the way up.

Leaffall, the 29th. Ola went down the old way on her own, for the first time, with a lantern and no rope. She came back an hour later and said the room was dry, and the bench was the same, and the box was gone, because we had taken it. She said she had only wanted to see the room without anyone talking. Hesk said that is what a room is for.

Leaffall, the 30th. Quiet day. Count board: copper level, iron level, coal high. The tally sheet is in the box on the count board shelf, with its last line written in. Count again, it says, in the old hand. We have. It is still nine.
%%
Halfway Inn, riddle board
Autumn. The Halfway Inn, where the road from Three Wells meets the road to Saltreach. Fire has not gone out in twenty years.
The board by the hearth. Guests write riddles and guests answer them. The innkeeper keeps the answers in a drawer.
This autumn's board:
From a girl at Kettle Ridge, about nine: "I am cut into pieces but I am not broken. I am a word. What am I?" Bel of Three Wells answered: "a word in a reading mind." The innkeeper said that was right and gave her a cup of honey milk.
From a carter: "I hold sixty of them and I am not a basket." No answer yet. I think it is a window. I will not write it, because a carter who is wrong about his own riddle is a carter who buys no ale.
From Pell the merchant: "I sell wool and I buy honey and I always come back." The innkeeper wrote under it: "a road." Pell said that was not the answer, and the innkeeper said it was the best one.
From a tutor going south: "I know nothing until I am drilled and I am wrong many times before I am right. What am I?" A boy from Ambrook wrote: "me." The tutor left a copper coin on the board for him.
A traveler said the big mind at the relay house up the road can now read a whole letter in one look, in pieces of words, and write back a fair answer. The innkeeper said he would test it with his hardest riddle, and the traveler said the big minds are kind and will say when they do not know. The innkeeper said that is more than most guests.
Fire still in. Board full. Riddles always wanted.

Fenn farm journal, the contest
Blossom, twelfth day. The contest is set for the fair at Longday. I have written a song. It is about the well, and it has four verses and a chorus that goes up at the end. Nan says it sounds like a goose being told a secret. I have sung it to the hens. The hens did not complain, which is the first good sign.
Blossom, thirteenth day. Nan has written one about the bees. It is better. It rhymes "swarm" with "warm" and "farm", and then does something clever with "alarm" that I cannot explain, but I admit it is clever. Brin wrote one about his cow, which is called Pudding. It has eleven verses. Nobody has heard all eleven. Brin is confident.
Blossom, fifteenth day. Rain. Dace sent word that the judges will be the schoolmaster, the baker Ida and a traveler from the Long Road who has heard every song in the land, or so she says. Ida's husband says she has not heard half of them. Ida says that is why she has time to listen.
Blossom, sixteenth day. Three cart loads came through the square today, all going north to the coast road and then south. Men from Saltreach, the carter said, going home. Their families had gone before them. He did not say where. When Joss asked him, he took his hat off and put it back on and said he had nothing to tell. He had only heard that the big ships at Saltreach were being built three times as long as before. He did not say why.
Blossom, seventeenth day. Some at the well say the relay from the north has been silent for three days. Others say it was only the weather. Mara at the mill says the relay went quiet in the same week the windmill broke, and that the two things are the same kind of trouble. I told her a windmill is not a tower. She said that was not what she meant.
The Copperhold miners are on the road this week, and they walk with their heads down. One of them told Nan that the stone below level nine is warm. She laughed when he said it. He did not laugh.
I do not think any of it is true. I do think it is strange to hear so many people say they heard the same thing from someone else.
We sang the bee song for the hives tonight. The bees came out to listen. Or they came out to see what the noise was.
%%
Nessa, account of Merle's lesson
The house at the edge of Saltreach was made for one family, not a row of tutors. The kitchen is narrow. The front room has a view of the sea, and the back room has the only door that shuts without a chair against it. We put the drill table in the back room.
Merle of Copperhold brought a mind grown in layers. He said the first layer took in the marks on a card. It changed them into a set of weights for the next layer. That layer did the same, and fed the one after it. The last layer made a guess.
We used relay numbers for the drills. A card showed some numbers and the answer mark. The mind guessed the answer. We compared the guess with the mark on the card and measured how far they were apart.
First Merle changed the weights in the last layer. That layer had made the answer, so it had a clear part in the error. Then he worked backward. He passed a share of the correction to the layer before it, and a share to the one before that. The correction reached the first layer.
Each layer changed its weights by the share it received. The layers learned to work together. Merle said this was the point of the lesson: do not only correct the answer at the end. Show each layer how its work led to the answer.
The apprentices wanted to know if the first layer could see the whole card. Merle said it saw the marks given to it, not the finished answer. It changed by the correction passed back from the later layers.
We drilled many cards. The mind became better at guessing the relay marks. It was not always right. We kept some cards out of the drill and used them only for a test. The mind missed several of those.
Merle says a test must be separate, or we only learn that a mind remembers its lessons. That is plain enough for the children, and for me.
%%
Mott, a debt and a wish, Blossom
Written by Mott, at the table by the window.
Blossom, first day. Uncle Ned came up the lane with his cart and a bag of emeralds and a very red face. He owed Father for a cart wheel from last Reaping. He paid it at the door. Father said he had not expected it. Uncle Ned said he had been waiting to be able to, and now he could, so he did. Then they went round the back and talked about the wheel for an hour. I could hear the wheel being talked about through the wall.
Blossom, second day. Two debts paid this month already. Mother says that is how a town stays friends. Father says it is how a town stays a town. I am not sure what the difference is. I asked Miss Jenna, and she said it was a good question, and then she did not answer it.
Blossom, third day. The carter from Saltreach says the big ships are launched from the slip now, every few weeks. They go out past the harbor wall and do not come back for days. He says the sailors do not sing, and he does not know why. The carter from the Long Road says the north relay is still dark. Two keepers are saying nothing about it.
Blossom, fourth day. I could not sleep, so I looked out of the window at the north. There were no lights. Father says there are not supposed to be. I looked a long time anyway, because it is nice to look at the north when there is nothing in it.
Blossom, fifth day. Wish day at the south well. You take a stone from the path and drop it in while you think of the wish, and you do not say what it is. I dropped my stone and thought of a fox that would stay. The wish is probably very far away, but I did my best.
Blossom, sixth day. Mayor Dace came round to the school with a box of new slates. He said the slates are from Vell, from a tutor there who did not charge for them. He said the children were to write everything on them, and keep the slates clean for the next class.
Blossom, seventh day. I wrote this on a slate first, and then copied it in here, because Miss Jenna says a true thing should be written twice.
%%
Ree, season notes, the farm and the road
Spring of 181. The thaw.
The ice went out of the ford and the frogs came back and the road came back too, all of it still there, the stones and the torches and the bridge. Nettle went over the bridge fast again. Some things do not change and I am glad of it.
We have a new calf. Butter had it in the night and did not need me at all. It is brown with one white foot and it stands badly and it is very loud. I have called it Spoon, because of the white foot and because I could not think of anything better at the time. Pip the dog is not sure about Spoon. Spoon is not sure about Pip. They will work it out.
The pups are grown and three are gone to other farms along the road and one stayed, a big grey one called Cobb. Cobb sleeps in the cart shed and barks at the torch posts. I have told him the torch posts are friends. He does not believe me.
Pell came by with his ledger and wrote down the calf and the pups and the honey. He said the road is busy this spring, more carts than ever, and the inn at the ford has put up a new sign. I said that is good. He said it is good and also it means more boots in the mud. He is a merchant. He likes the boots and he does not like the mud.
Moss came out once to see the calf and once to see Nettle. Nettle is old. Her cough is worse when the marsh wind blows but she still eats and still kicks the bucket and still walks the road with me at night. I will not write that she is sick. She is not sick. She is old and she is cross and she is mine.
The honey is in the jars. The eggs are in the basket. The road is lit. Come by in Swarm and we will have breakfast.
%%
Senna, spring on the bridge
Kept by Senna, bridge keeper at the inn by the Long Road bridge, over eight days of spring in the 196th year of the archive. She wrote on the left page what happened on the road and on the right page what she heard about the stream. A page was torn when it was copied. The rest was kept at Vell.

Day one. High water. The bridge hums when the stream runs fast. I put my hand on the rail and could feel it.

Day two. A carter lost his hat in the stream. I fished it out with the long hook. He gave me a cup of cider.

Day three. A tutor arrived at the inn, tired. Hana. She asked if she could sleep in the loft. I said yes. She said she would pay in talk. I said I would take it.

Day four. Her first talk. She said: when a mind is made of many layers, the layers near the end can learn but the layers near the start cannot, because the blame fades before it gets there. She said the old tutors tried to make minds deeper and found they would not learn.

Day five. Her second talk. She said: a stream runs through the whole mind. Each layer adds its finding and does not remove anything. The blame, going back, runs along the stream and reaches the first layer whole. So a deep mind can learn.

She drew the stream on the table with spilled soup. It was an ugly drawing. It was clear.

Day six. The gift. She left a card for the inn. It reads: Add. Do not take away. The innkeeper hung it above the hearth. The cook said it was very good advice for soup.

Day seven. The inn's apprentice misunderstood an order. The innkeeper said: carry the sack to the cellar. The apprentice picked up the sack and put it on the table. The innkeeper said: no, the cellar. The apprentice put it on the floor. The innkeeper said: down the stair. The apprentice looked at the stair. It took the sack down and put it at the bottom, and then came back and fetched the table.

I do not know what it was thinking. Hana said it was learning. I said: it is learning a table.

Day eight. She left at dawn. I walked her to the end of the bridge. She said: thank you for the bed. I said: thank you for the stream.

Senna
%%
Lorn, the Longnight week
Longnight. The longest dark of the year. The lamps on the wharf are lit all day now.

A man came down the Long Road with a cart of salt and a bad cough. His name is Pim. He sold his salt at the fish stalls, and he coughed the whole time he counted his emeralds.

Pim says the cough is worst at Lowmarsh. Wren's house is full of beds. Some people say it is the water. Some say the ships, which bring in strange timber. A boatman says it is the mines, because the water runs down from the deep. Nobody knows. Pim does not know either. He only coughs.

A miner from Copperhold stood by my cart with a cup of warm honey water. His name is Eskel. He has worked the Deep Works since he was a boy. He says the hall hums at night. You can feel it through the ladder rungs, he says, and through your teeth. He says the stone below the eighth gate is warm to the hand.

"Stone is not warm," I said.

"Put your hand on it and see," said Eskel.

I have not gone down. Eskel says the mine hall has told the miners to stay above a certain level. He says he has not been below it in a month. He says he does not believe the hall is right. He does not say why. He drank his honey water and went back to his cart.

A pedlar from Northpoint brought the last news up the Long Road. He says the keeper at Northpoint sent word down the relays: lights in the sky over the north. Many nights in a row, and they moved. Some say they are falling stars. Some say they are fires on the ice. A child at Northpoint said, "They were looking at us." The keeper, Mira, only said, "They were not ours."

The pedlar says the north relays have been going quiet. One after another, from the north. He says the last message from the tower above the ice was cut off in the middle of a word. Nobody knows what the word was.

I am tired. The sheep are fine. The hay is good. I have forty bales left and enough honey for the winter.
%%
Pell's ledger, the Halfway Inn
Winter of 208. Pell's hand.
I keep the ledger at the Halfway Inn. The hearth there has not gone out in twenty years. The riddle board by the door still has my riddle on it. Nobody has answered it. I have stopped asking.
The roads are bad. Packed earth turned to mud, then to ice. Carts come slow. I sell honey from Ambrook in jars, and wool, and candles, and the inn sells beds.
Third of Longnight. A jar of honey froze and cracked. I sold the rest to the innkeeper for the beds upstairs. He said the guests would be glad of something sweet. The guests are three travelers and a cat.
Ninth of Longnight. A zombie at the gate. The innkeeper's boy kept the torches lit and it went off into the dark. We lost no sleep. Keep the torches lit, that is the whole rule.
Fourteenth of Longnight. A clerk came down from Vell with a box of old papers for the shelf here. He read one by the fire and laughed. He said it was a lesson from Una of Ambrook, the sorting crystal, the first lesson in the tutors' books. I have copied what he told me below. I do not understand all of it, but I understood the honey.
