Ossa's book, the last of autumn
Late Leaffall. Cold, still days. Almost no wind.
The new house on the hill is finished. The Meers have moved in, with their two girls and their goat. Their old house in the square has shut its door, and the children have put a torch in the window, the way you do when someone is away.
Many of the young ones are going away this year. They go to Saltreach, and some say to the sea. A cart went out of the square each day of the last week, piled with trunks and chairs. The people in them wave to the people left behind. The people left behind are quiet.
I was asked by the schoolmaster why, and I did not know what to say. He said the ships at Saltreach are bigger every year. He said a family he knew had gone down to the quay and watched a ship go out, and had come back and said nothing for two days.
I delivered a baby on the last night of the month. Tall, fierce, with a cough like a wet dog, and very angry about it. The mother laughed at her and said she would be a fisherman. I said she would be a baker. We all agreed it would be fine.
Lamp lit. Basin full. The water from the north well is still good.
%%
Pip's week at Kettle Ridge
Spring of 207.
Tam keeps a tally board in the mill and this week he let me keep it. So this is my tally week. I am Pip. I am Tam's apprentice, but he says I am half a farmer now and should learn the board.

First of Blossom. Wheat in the top field is up. Beetroot in the long field is up. The ditch down the middle of the field is running and I cleared the leaves from it. The oak hedge on the north side has a gap where a branch fell, and I mended it with two posts. The torches along the edge need new ones. Two are out. I wrote it on the board.

Second of Blossom. Hens laid fourteen eggs. The pig pen needs mucking and I mucked it. I do not like the pig pen. The pigs do not care.

Third of Blossom. This is the day of the horse. Pell came by on the Long Road with his cart and his horse Nut, and he had a bad wheel and asked to leave Nut with us while he got it mended at Three Wells. Tam said yes. So we had a horse.

Nut is a good horse. Pell says so. Nut would not stay in the yard. Nut went through the fence to the beetroot and ate a row and a half before I found her. I pulled and she pulled and we went in a circle. Tam came and laughed so hard he had to lean on the gate. Then he got Nut out with a bucket of carrots, which is what I should have done. The beetroot row is thinned now, by Nut. Tam says we will call it thinning and not say more.

Fourth of Blossom. Pell came back for Nut and paid us two coins for the keeping and one for the beetroot. Tam said the beetroot was worth more and Pell said no and they settled on three coins and a bag of apples from Ambrook. The apples are very good.

Fifth of Blossom. Beets by the flowers and a swarm on the plum tree. I held the box. I got stung on the thumb. Tam says now I am a real beekeeper.

Sixth of Blossom. Market in Three Wells. Sold eggs and honey. The price board says eggs up and honey up. Pell says never trust the price board on a fair day.

Seventh of Blossom. Rain all day. Mended the hen house roof where it leans. Slept.

The board says all fields up, hens laying, pigs mucked, two torches to mend. Tam says a good board is a short board. Mine is long. Next week shorter.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 18. First day of Blossom.
Crew: Hesk, Noll, Sava.
Level: two.
Count: copper 5 carts, coal 3.
The road from Copperhold to Three Wells is packed earth now. Two men laid cobblestone at the steep turn. Noll says carts will stop breaking there. Noll says many things.
The new inn is open at the turn. It has one room and a bench. The keeper served us beetroot soup. It was hot enough to make Sava forget his sore foot.
We carried the ore up before dusk. The road holds. The soup was good.

Nessa, Saltreach house notes
Summer of 158.
I am staying in a small house beyond the Saltreach fish market. Its door faces the sea. Its back wall faces the road. The wind gets in through both.
Merle came from Copperhold with two layered crystals in a box. He says each layer feeds the next. I asked him to show the apprentices the old sorting drill first. He did.
We showed one crystal marks for the relay numbers. It guessed the next mark. When it guessed wrong, Merle changed the weights in the last layer. Then he passed some of that change back to the layer before it.
He says each layer must take its share. If only the last layer changes, the first layers learn nothing useful. I wrote that down twice.
The second crystal did better after many drills. It still made mistakes. Merle says that is why we drill again. The sea wind has blown the cards off my table. I have found three under the bed.
%%
Letter: Ree to the Lowmarsh market
Spring of 180.
To the market at Lowmarsh, from Ree at the farm on the north road.
You asked for eggs and butter and honey. We have eggs, twelve a week, and butter, six pots a week, and honey from the hives on the hill, but the honey is not ours to sell until Swarm. The bees are busy and I do not argue with bees.
Butter the cow is well. She had the wet two springs back and Moss gave her the green stuff and she got better. She gives good milk now. Nettle the mare is old but she pulls the cart to the road and back and she does not like the new bridge. She stops at the bridge and looks at it and then goes over it fast. I do not know why. The bridge is stone. It is perfectly good.
We are near the second torch post past the ford, the one where the post leans a little. You will see the gate. You will hear the dog. The dog is called Pip and she is not for sale. People ask. She is still not for sale.
Send word by the road now that the road is here. A boy can carry it in a morning.
Ree.
%%
Northpoint log, Mira
Frost and Deepsnow, 190.

Frost, tenth. The path to the ice camp is hard underfoot. Runa walked it faster than I did, though she stopped twice to tie her boot. She says a keeper should not race an old woman. I was not racing. I was carrying the lamp oil.

The camp well has run dry. Runa lowered a bucket and heard it strike stone. No water, only a little black mud. We will use snow until the ground softens. Lantern carries the snow in a basket. It leaves a neat trail of little spills behind it.

Frost, seventeenth. The wind turned. It blew the snow off the tower steps and into my doorway. I cleared the steps. Then I cleared the doorway. Then I cleared the steps again. The wind is a poor helper.

Tam sent a jar of honey from Kettle Ridge. He says Pip packed it in wool and made a list of every person who touched it. The list is longer than the road note. I opened the jar for Runa's tea. She took one spoonful and said it was good. She took a second to be sure.

Deepsnow, third. The lamp burned well. I cleaned the glass and checked the redstone line at the keeper's desk. The south tower sent a message about snow on the road. I sent back that we have plenty here, and would be glad to share the extra.

Runa laughed at that. We have more snow than pails.

Notice on the fold gate
To everyone who keeps sheep at the north fold. The low gate field is wet and the grass is too rich there. Move your animals to the dry field until the ground dries out. Buttercup got sick on it, and she is better now, but we were lucky. Keep the gate shut. If your animal is sick, tell me or tell the cattle woman down the road. Do not wait.
Signed, Fern.

Lowmarsh market, spring prices
Sowing of 214. Market day.

The fish were dear today. The river ran high, and the weir caught less than usual. I sold carrots, two baskets of apples, and a jar of honey from Ambrook. The jar had a crack in the lid. Nobody noticed until the honey reached the boardwalk.

Wren wiped it with a reed cloth and laughed. She said the honey was still good. I bought it back for half a measure of fish. I shall take it home before the lid cracks again.

At the far end of the market, the council reader stood by the notice board. She had come from Vell to speak about the big relay house. She said its crystal was too large for one tutor to test by hand. The tutors test parts, then compare the answers. They also send unseen pages through it. A mind must not be trusted just because it gives a neat answer.

A man from Saltreach said the big mind had counted every boat in the harbor without being told. A Lowmarsh fisher said it had named a storm three days early. The reader said neither tale was in the test book.

The market bell rang while she was speaking. Everyone turned toward the fish. No storm came. No boats were counted.

I bought fish, honey, and a reed mat. The mat is crooked. It will still keep my feet dry.
%%
Corin, house log, Longnight
Longnight. Twelfth day.
The miller fixed the wheel at last. The iron band held, and the wheel turned slowly through one full round. Then the lower spoke cracked. We stopped it before it struck the stone lip.
We had tea in the shed while he looked at the break. Nell brought honey. The miller said honey was not a tool. He ate two spoonfuls.
A crew from the shipyard came by our house to carry boards to the slip. Their new ship is nearly ready. I asked when it would leave. One said after the thaw. Another said no one had set a day.
The fish market was thin today. A basket of cod, some small fish, and two crabs. The price board had more empty spaces than words.
At dusk, Lantern's message came through the relay house. Northpoint is cold, the keeper is well, and the lamp is lit. No more was written. My sister read it twice, then put it in the bread box so it would stay dry.
A traveler says the lights above the north came down close to the snow. He swears they moved against the wind. The harbor keeper says he saw no lights at all. I did not ask which of them was right.

Sable's book: Reaping to Frost
Written by Sable, for the Archive, because Mam says a whole season should be in one book and not in pieces. Reaping to Frost.

Reaping, the first. Harvest carts from the north field. Mam counts the sacks herself and shouts the number to the cook, who shouts it back. Thirty-one sacks. The cook says it is the best since the year Mam was born, and Mam says the cook was not born then, so he cannot know.

Reaping, the fourth. Pell came back down the road with his ledger under his arm. He stayed three nights and wrote a letter every night to Ambrook. He read the first one aloud to the hearth. It said: "Dear Sen, the beds are dry, the stew is hot, the road is long, and the riddle board has a new riddle I cannot answer." On the fourth night he got the answer, and he wrote it to her, and he said it was the only letter he had ever written that made him happy.

Apple, the ninth. Patch still says come to everything. Nessa says it is learning, and I believe her, though I have not yet heard it say anything else. The cat has learned to come when called, which is more than Patch has.

Leaffall, the third. Leaves came down the road and got into the cart wheels. The carters swept the road with their own brooms, and they did not complain, because it was their road. Mam sold the cider at the old price. She says that is not kindness, but good sense, because the carters will come back.

Leaffall, the twelfth. A different tutor from Vell, with a box of crystals packed in straw. She asked the riddle and got it wrong. Then she got it right. Then she said it was wrong again, because she liked the answer better the first time. She told me not to be sad about it. I was not sad.

Leaffall, the sixteenth. We have copper bowls on the table now. Four bowls and a ladle from Copperhold. The copper shines in the lamplight. Mam says it makes the stew look richer than it is, which is the best thing a bowl can do.

Frost, the first. The first frost came in the night. The windmill on the ridge road stopped turning. In the morning the grass was white, and the cat would not go out. He sat by the hearth and looked at the door, and then at me, and then at the door again.

Frost, the fourth. Hesk again, with news. Ola's vein is on the count board. He says the copper is better in the cold. Mam says everything is better in the cold, as long as you have a hearth.

Frost, the seventh. The hearth is still lit. Twenty years, Mam says, and ten more to go. She counts them on her fingers. I counted too. I am nine. I will be grown when the hearth is much older, and I will still keep it, if Mam lets me.

Frost, the last. Eleven new riddles on the board, all in my handwriting. The answer to the last one is a hearth. Nobody has got it yet. I hope they do not. I like a riddle best while it is still waiting.
%%
Corin, house log, winter
Frost. Fifth day.
The mill wheel is still broken. The miller found a sound oak spoke, but the iron band will not sit flat. He has laid both beside the pond and covered them with canvas.
I went to the shipyard for a nail and stayed to watch the big ship. Her deck is taller than the roof of our house. A man on the yard called her a safe ship. Another man said the sea does not care what we call a ship.
My sister sent a pot of beetroot soup. She put too much salt in it. I told her so. She said the sea must have taught me to complain.
At supper, a traveler said the grey cough has reached the farms beyond Lowmarsh. A fish seller said it comes from the mines. A boatman blamed the marsh water. No one at our table knew.
I covered the fish and closed the shutters. The wind has not eased.

Wren's winter journal
The first snow covered the reed beds and the roofs of the boat houses. It did not cover the boardwalk for long. The wind pushed it into heaps by the posts, where it stayed until Moss brought out a shovel.
Moss cleared a path from the healing house to the well, then from the well to the fish weir. The last path went to the shed. Nobody had asked for it, but the shed door was hard to reach, and the shovel needed a dry place.
I carried hot water to Noll, who had a fever and a sore throat. He drank the water, made a face, and asked if we had anything better. I gave him broth. He made the same face, but finished the bowl.
His daughter came to sit by his bed. She brought a book from the school house and read aloud. Noll fell asleep before the end. His daughter said the book was dull. I said it had done good work.
A woman from the boat house came with a swollen thumb. She had shut it in the store door. Moss held a cold cloth on it while I wrapped it. She thanked us and went home with one hand held high. Moss said she looked like she was greeting the whole marsh.
The boardwalk was slippery again at dusk. We put down sand and brought the empty pails inside. Moss left the shovel by the door. I moved it to the shed. In the morning Moss found it there and said the shovel had gone home by itself.
%%
Dara, the same inn in a late autumn
Frost, the third week, and the square is white at dawn. The lamps are lit at the usual hour, but the grey is so thick that the carters do not see them until they are on top of them.

Pell came in with his cart and sat by the stove. He had been to the north of the Long Road for the first time in years, and he looked old. He said the towns along the road are quieter. Not frightened. Quiet. He said he saw children at every inn who had stopped playing their games and were just watching the road.

Tilly asked him what he had seen. He said he had seen a fox sitting on a gatepost, looking north, and it did not move when his cart went by. He said he had never seen a fox sit still for so long. He said it was the strangest thing on the whole road.

Later, when the others were in bed, Pell told me that he had not seen the lights. He had heard about them from everyone. He said every story was different. Green, white, red. Falling, or still. One said they were fires, and one said they were eyes, and one, a boatman from Saltreach, said they were ships, very high and very far off, with lamps along their sides. Pell said that was the story he liked least, because it was the only one that sounded like a plan.

I gave him a bowl of soup and told him to sleep. He said he would, once the lamp was out. I left it lit for him, as I always do. He did not notice, I think, that I had also left the door unbarred, which I never do.
%%
Saltreach house log, by Nessa
First day of Swarm.
The house is small, but the windows face the water. Salt dries white on the sill. Merle says it will not harm the crystals if we wipe it away.
We began with relay numbers written on cards. The first layer took the marks. The next layer used what came from the first. The last layer gave a guess.
Merle showed us how to test the guess against the answer on the card. If it was wrong, we found the size and direction of the mistake. The last layer changed first. Then part of the change went back through each layer to the beginning.
He called this passing blame. He does not mean that a layer is naughty. He means each layer must be shown how it helped make the wrong answer.
After many cards, the crystal guessed more numbers correctly. It still failed on a card it had not seen before. Merle says that is a reason to keep a separate test box.
At supper, the innkeeper brought fish and bread. The table rocked so hard that Merle caught the bowl with both hands.
%%
Tutor's log: the even step, spring 197
Written by Nessa, at the Halfway Inn. The keepers on the Long Road asked me to write the step down plainly, so they can teach it to their own minds.

The step goes after every layer. A mind is many layers. Each one takes the numbers from the layer before, mixes them, and passes them on. If nothing is done, the numbers grow with every layer, until they run wild and every answer looks the same.

So the step finds the middle of the numbers, and how far they spread out. Then it scales them back to one even size. The next layer starts from the same place it started from at the beginning. That is all the step does.

It is simple to say and easy to get wrong. A keeper who leaves it out will see the mind drill well for a day, and badly for a month.

Why a crystal learns at all, I do not know. I have asked the other tutors, and none of them has written it down. Do not ask me to guess. The even step only keeps the learning from running away.
%%
Briar, baker, the lamp in the window
Deepsnow, the long night. The little lamp in the window of the bakehouse blinked a strange thing for an hour after dark, and the whole farm came out to look.
It was not the usual pattern. The usual pattern is for the tower on the hill to call across to us, and we answer with the short flash, and that is all. Tonight the lamp in our window was blinking on its own. A long string of short flashes, then a pause, then the same again.
Olin said it was the wick, which had been trimmed too short and was guttering in the draught from the door. He went to fix it. The lamp went on blinking. Olin said that was a very strange wick.
Then the boy Pim said it was the cat, sitting on the sill, tapping the glass with her paw. We looked. The cat was asleep in the flour bin. So it was not the cat either.
Then I saw it. The apprentice had gone to the window to look at the snow, and it had its hand on the sill, and every time the snow fell heavier on the glass it tapped the frame with one copper finger. It did not know it was making a message. It was only keeping time with the snow.
We all laughed about it for a long while. Then I went to the window and saw that the flashes, when I counted them, made a pattern that was almost a word. It was very nearly a word the tower would know. I do not know what the word was. I did not write it down. I said goodnight to the apprentice and told it to stop tapping, and it did.
%%
Abel, how the big minds learned manners
Winter, many evenings by the stove. I asked my grandmother, who knows tutors, how the big minds learned to write back so kindly. She told me this and I wrote it down so I would not lose it.
First a big mind learns to read, by being drilled on a great deal of writing. It learns what usually comes next. That is all it does, at first. It can finish a sentence. It cannot hold a conversation, and it has no manners. Ask it for a joke and it may give you the next ten lines of a ledger.
Then the tutors wrote questions, many of them, the kind a person might send by relay. They had the mind write answers. Then they scored the answers. A kind and true answer got a high mark. A rude or false one got a low mark.
Then the mind was drilled again, on its own answers and their marks. It was nudged toward what scored high, and away from what scored low. Round after round.
In the end the mind answered well even to questions it had never seen. It learned to refuse what would harm somebody. It learned to say when it did not know, instead of making something up.
My grandmother said the hard part is the scores. A score is only what the tutors thought was good. If they were wrong, so was the mind. So the tutors read each other's marks and argued about them. They still do.
She also said nobody knows quite why it works. The tutors know the steps. They do not know what happens inside.
I wrote that last part twice, because it was the part I liked.
%%
Yarrow, the thin year, a spring log
Yarrow, who fishes the mill pond at Ambrook. Spring. Written a little at a time, over a month, in the back of the market ledger.

Third of Sowing. The ice is gone. The pond is quiet. I have a good feeling about this year.

Eighth. I was wrong. The market cellars are empty. Last year's harvest was poor, as all know: the apples were small, the wheat was thin, and the beets rotted in the ground. The market has little to sell. I have trout, and honey, and bread that is mostly crust.

Twelfth. The council of Ambrook met at the mill. They asked every stall to say what it has. The list was short. It went up to the relay tower and was sent to Three Wells and Kettle Ridge. Kettle Ridge sent back: we have beetroot. Three Wells sent: we have cheese. Both will be sent in carts.

Fifteenth. The school play. It was the first thing in a month that made the market laugh. It was a play about a very hungry pig who ate the whole harvest in a dream and woke up to find it was true. The children wrote it themselves. The pig was Fern's boy, who has a round face and a loud voice. He said, "Oh, no! I have eaten the apples!" and the whole market roared. Some of them had tears in their eyes. I think they were thinking of the real apples.

Twentieth. Beetroot arrived from Kettle Ridge. Twelve carts. The council shared it by the house count. We got a sack, and made soup, and ate it with trout. It was the best meal I have had since the autumn.

Twenty-fourth. A letter from my cousin at Saltreach. He says the boats are doing well, and the fish are plenty. He offers to send salted cod. I wrote back: yes, please, and a barrel of kelp.

Twenty-eighth. The pond is full of fry. I see them at the edge in the evening, flickering. I will not fish the small ones. The old fishers say: leave the small, and next year you will have the big.

Thirtieth. End of the month. I have caught sixty-one trout. I have sold fifty-four. I have eaten seven. I have given eleven away, so I count more than I caught, but I do not mind. A thin year is a year to be generous in.

I put the last of the cod in the pot tonight. The cat watched. It is a long spring, but I think the summer will be better.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 42. The ninth of Blossom.
Crew: Hesk, Noll, Varo, Sava.
Levels: two and three.
Count: copper 11 carts, iron 3.
Market day at Three Wells. We took copper wire, a sack of coal, and the bent pan from the kitchen. We came home with carrots, a wool blanket, and a pan that is only a little bent.
The square was full of stalls. The bell rang before the bread was ready. Dace said the bell was for the market, not for the baker, but the baker heard it anyway.
I saw the relay tower on the hill. Its stone walls are up to the keeper's room. The builders say they will fit glass panes next. A stair lamp hangs at every turn inside. At the foot, torches burn in the wind.
Varo bought a small wooden horse for his sister. He held it all the way home and would not let me carry it.

Tam, farm log: the sick cow, Frost
Tam, farm log, the top farm above Kettle Ridge. Written by the lamp.

Frost, first day. Butter, the brown cow, will not eat. She lies in the straw with her head down, looking at her own feet as if they had said something rude. Her nose is hot. I put my hand on her side, and it was like a stone that had been by the fire.
Frost, second day. The healer at Three Wells sent a paper of herbs and a note: give it in warm water, a cup at a time, keep her warm, and do not let her stand in the wet. Butter drank one cup and looked at me as if I had insulted her mother.
Frost, third day. Pip got the water. I told it: fetch the water from the trough. It fetched the water in the bucket, carried it very carefully across the yard, and did not spill. It held the bucket to Butter's mouth while I held her head. She drank the whole of it.
Frost, fifth day. Eating again. She ate a whole beetroot, which I think she took for a pear. Juno says the cow was saved by her stew. I say it was Pip and the bucket.

Quin, night call log
Apple, year 191. Quin, midwife, a farm outside Saltreach.
Second night: called to the Marl farm at the third bell. A girl, heavy as a good loaf. Mother tired, well. Father cried. I did not count how long.
Fourth night: a false alarm at the Hale farm. A hen had got in the bed. I went home.
Sixth night: a boy at the Brook farm. Fine, loud. Left a jar of honey for the mother.
Horse: I borrowed Pell's mare. She is a good mare. She stopped at every gate.

Mott, a season at the edge of Three
Written by Mott, age ten, for Miss Jenna, who asked us to write everything down this spring and not to leave anything out. I have tried. It is a lot of everything.
Thaw. The puddles froze and cracked. The north relay went dark two nights, and the carters said it was the snow. Father paid the baker twenty emeralds, counted twice. The peas came up.
Sowing. The lantern day. Hal said red is for miners. We walked round all three wells, and every one of us put a hand on the stone and said thank you. Father says families are going over the sea from Saltreach in the big ships, and he does not know where to. Ada's cows did not want to go.
Blossom. Uncle Ned paid for the cart wheel from last Reaping, at the door, with a red face. The big ships are launched from the slip every few weeks, and the sailors do not sing. The north has no lights when you look for them, and the carters say nothing about why. The south well ran low, and the men went down to it with buckets at dawn and dusk. Father says it always comes back. Father says it twice.
Mayor Dace read a letter with wax on it, and put it in his pocket. Mrs. Lark says the letter was about the fair. Hal says it was about the north. Ada says it was about nothing.
Miss Jenna says the Archive has asked every school in every town to write everything down this year. Write everything down, the letter says. Miss Jenna says that is not the same as writing down what happened, and she did not say what she meant.
So this is everything, as far as I know it. The wells are full again, and the swallow is in the bakery eaves, and Mother says we will have a good summer if the peas hold. Hal still wants to be a miner. Ada still wants to be a relay keeper. I still want to be a baker, because of the buns, and because a baker is the one who stays up at four in the morning and makes bread anyway.
Miss Jenna says that is a good reason. I think she is right. I think it is the best reason there is.
%%
Nessa's farm book
Late summer. The heat came back, dry and white.
The reading mind had a bad week. It was given a box of pages from the year of the great floods, and the pages had been left out in the rain. The ink had run. Many of the letters were gone, and the words had lost their edges. The mind guessed wildly. It guessed the same word over and over, and got it wrong each time, and then changed a little, and guessed again.
Oren came in the evening with a lamp. He read the pages aloud, one line at a time, and the mind guessed each line after him. Some lines it got right. Some it got wrong. It did not give up.
I asked Oren what he would do with the wet pages. He said he would copy them. Each clerk would copy one page, with a gap where a letter was too far gone, and write in the margin what he thought it had been. Then the copy would go in the box, and the wet page would go on the shelf. The mind would learn from the copies, and the copies would be our record.
That is how the archive works, Oren said. Nothing is thrown away. But nothing is taken as the only truth, either. Two copies, and a margin.
%%
Quill, summer notes on reading
Longday, 194. Hot days, cool nights.

The hens have found shade under the oak hedge. I set a pan of water there. The wind keeps turning the farm gate, and I tied it shut with a length of wool.

Nessa visited with two apprentices from Vell. They were carrying a crystal box between them. One apprentice walked well. The other stopped at the ditch and put both feet in the water. Nessa said it had been told to stay beside the box. It had stayed beside the box, but the box was on the cart. We all stood there while it dripped.

At the well, Nessa explained the reading mind again. A person can read a whole page and know the sense. The mind in the crystal works a smaller step at a time. It sees a stretch of letters, about sixty at once, and guesses the next letter. Then it sees that letter with the others and guesses again. The drill shows it many pages. When it makes a wrong guess, the tutor changes its weights a little. Over time, it makes better guesses.

The Archive at Vell sent copied pages for these drills. Clerks took out spoiled pages and pages copied twice. They kept some pages in a test box, so no mind was drilled on them. That is how Nessa said the tutors could learn whether a mind had really improved.

I asked if the pages came from the whole land. She said she had heard bundles arrived from many towns. She had also heard that some tutors gave their own letters. Nobody could show me a full list. It seems the clerks did not write down every bundle's beginning.

"We will keep everything we write," Nessa said. It is cut above the Archive door, and she says it often. I like the sound of it. I do not know if my egg tallies are among the pages. I will keep writing them anyway.

Today's count was eleven eggs. One went to Moss for her sick bed. Three went to market. The rest are in the cool room. Pip carried the basket there and put it on the floor, not the shelf. It did not break any.
%%
Perrin, spring notes at Northpoint
The house at the edge of Northpoint has a new stone step. I cut it from a block that split cleanly. The old step had a hollow place where rain gathered, and the keeper slipped there twice.

The relay house mind sent a letter to the tutor. The keeper read it aloud at supper. It asked for a copy of the lamp list and said the north glass needed wiping. The writing was plain. The keeper thought it was from Vell until she saw the relay mark.

The tutor says the mind reads writing one letter at a time. Its window is about sixty letters wide. It does not look at the whole page at once. For each letter, it can use earlier letters that matter, then it guesses the next one. It has learned from many pages kept at Vell. During drilling, a wrong guess changes its weights toward a better guess.

I asked whether the mind sees words as we do. The tutor said it keeps places for letters and words, and things used alike sit near each other in those places. She said this is one way to describe what the mind learns, not a tale of tiny words living in the crystal.

The old questions came up again. Orla had asked whether a hidden mind could answer like a person. We read the same questions to the mind and to the keeper's sister. Some answers were easy to tell apart. One was not. The tutor wrote both answers in her book and did not mark which was which.

I bet the keeper a copper piece that the sister had written the answer about the cracked glass. The keeper bet it was the mind. We showed our slips to the tutor. She said we had both lost. The answer had come from a clerk at Vell, who had written it down years before.

At the spring festival, the tutor brought a drill board to the yard. Children stood in a row and read short pages aloud. Lantern carried the board from the school house to the relay house, then brought it back with no corner chipped. It did better than the children at carrying. It did worse at remembering where we had put the honey cakes.

The wind kept knocking the flags against the tower wall. We moved them lower. The keeper says a festival is meant to be heard, not endured by the cloth.
%%
Ossa, letters to my sister in Three
Winter of 159. Dear Rill. It is cold here at the edge of Northpoint. I write by the stove.

You asked about Dace. Dace is well. Dace mended the roof again. I held the ladder. Dace is too old for the roof and does it anyway, which I have stopped arguing about. I just hold the ladder now. It is easier.

The goats are fat. Loaf ate the washing. Bitter watched Loaf eat the washing and did not tell us. I have never trusted a goat that thinks.

The foxes come for scraps. There is one with a torn ear. Dace talks to it. Dace talks to the goats too, and to the stove, and to the relay lamp on the hill. Dace is a person who talks to things. It is not a bad way to live. The stove never argues.

We had a bad night in Leaffall. The wind came off the ice and the lamp on the tower went out for an hour. Vey the keeper climbed the ninety rungs in the dark with a torch in the teeth. Vey has strong teeth. The lamp came back. The south tower had been blinking the whole time, worried, I think, though a lamp cannot worry. Vey says a lamp can worry. Vey would know.

I am knitting. Socks, mostly. Everyone here has cold feet. I am knitting socks for Dace, Vey, the woman at the store, and the goat Bitter, who does not want socks but will get them.

Rill, I miss the market at Three Wells. I miss the stalls and the noise and the bell. Here the loudest thing is the wind and the second loudest is Dace snoring. But the sky at night is very big and very full of stars and the lamp blinks and I am content. Do not tell our mother I am content. She will worry.

Your sister Ossa. P.S. Loaf ate the post again. If your letter is chewed, that is why.

Cress, carter's log, the north quay
Leaffall, the fourteenth.
Took the cart up to the shipyard for timber. Corin's yard is full of new ribs, curved like the ribs of a great horse and three times as tall as a man. Corin said good day. He looked tired. A man who has built boats all his life does not look tired on a good morning.

Leaffall, the sixteenth.
Two families went out on the morning tide. Four carts, a cow tied behind, and a girl on top of it all with a hen in her lap. The men on the quay said good luck and did not look at each other. Nobody said where. They said "over the water" and let it lie there. Pell says the sea is only a wider road, and roads end where you stop walking.

Leaffall, the nineteenth.
The harbor relay has sent no weather for three days. The keeper's boy came down to say the lamp is cold and the glass has gone white on the inside. The keeper says it is only the cold. At the market some say the north towers have gone quiet, one after another, so the messages stop halfway down the line. That is a rumor. Rumors are for the inn. I sent my weather to the fish market by hand. The fish man said it was wrong.

Leaffall, the twenty-sixth.
Tobe from the net sheds gave me a twist of paper with three dried apples in it. His mother dried them in the loft, he said, and they are good for the cold. I ate one at the cart. It was sweet and tough, like a good boot.
%%
Briar, baker, the last batch of the year
Deepsnow, the last day. The last batch of the year came out of the oven at the fourth bell, and it was the best batch we have made since Thaw.
Twelve loaves, all golden. Two sweet buns for the children. A small loaf, not meant for selling, shaped like a fox, which Pim made from the dough left in the bowl. It has ears. Olin says it looks like a cat. The cat says nothing.
We ate the first loaf in the bakehouse with butter and a little honey, standing up. The apprentice stood by the door and watched us eat. I thought about giving it a piece, and then I thought of the sacks in the line along the wall, and I did not.
Next year I will write the cards shorter. I will tell the apprentice stop when I say stop. I will put a stone in the south corner from the first day. The lamp in the window will be trimmed longer, so that it does not blink. The sacks will go back on the shelves, where they belong, and the pig will be left alone.
Lamp lit. Oven warm. Bread cooling. Everyone home.

Jem, the road comes
Summer, the eleventh year of the Archive.

The fifth of Longday. Men came with stakes and string and walked right through our top field. Father went out with a hoe and asked what they were doing. They said, drawing the road. Father said, through my wheat? They said no, along the edge of the field, and they would pay for the stakes. Then they walked around the wheat as if it were something precious. I liked them for that.

The ninth. They are digging. I counted seventeen men and one cart. They pack the earth down with big wooden rammers, and one man sings. Along each edge they lay a line of cobblestone. Ree and I carry water to them. They gave us each a nail. I keep mine in a box.

The twelfth. Torches. On every twelfth block a post, and a torch on top. A boy my age walks along with a bucket and lights them at dusk. They look like beads on a string. I stood at our gate and counted forty before the road turned.

The sixteenth. A bridge over our stream. Oak, because the stone has not come yet. The head man says, stone if you can, oak if you must. Father says that is a good rule for most things.

The twentieth. The first cart came by. The carter had a load of barrels, and he stopped at our gate and asked if we sell eggs. Father said we had never sold eggs to a man on a road. The carter said, well, you do now. He bought eighteen. Father says we are on the road now, not beside it.

The twenty-fifth. The road reached Vell. All the town went out to walk on it. I walked from our gate to the hill gate and back. It took no time at all. Gran says that when she was young the same walk took half a morning.

I think the road will keep growing. Father says it will go all the way to the sea.
%%
Quill, flock log: the storm
Kept by Quill, shepherd, at Ferris End. Written up after, because during the storm there was no time, and no paper that would stay dry.

Blossom, the day it came. The sky went the color of a bruised plum at midday. The birds stopped singing. Then the rain came sideways, and the first thunder hit the far hill like a dropped barrel.

We got the flock into the reed fold at the bottom of the field, which is the one place the wind cannot reach. Counted as they went in: sixty-one, and fourteen lambs. Counted again: sixty-one, and thirteen lambs. The fourteenth was under Fen, who was sitting on it out of kindness.

Then the rain. It came through the reed roof like water through a sieve. I stood in the middle of the fold with a lamb under each arm and the other two under my coat.

The lightning struck the old oak at the top of the field. It split down the middle. One half fell across the north wall, and the other half stood there, black and smoking, looking as surprised as the rest of us.

In the night the wind went round to the east. The reed roof lifted at one corner, and we held it down with our bodies and four sacks of wool until the dawn.

In the morning the rain had stopped. The sun came up pale and watery over the marsh. The lambs were all alive. Counted: sixty-one, and fourteen. Fen was asleep in the mud.

Wren came up from the healing house with a basket and a face like the sky. She had seen the oak from the boardwalk. She gave each of us a cup of warm milk with a spoon of honey in it, and told us to hold still. Then she set a jar of honey on the wall, with a cloth tied over the top. "For the chilled ones," she said, meaning the lambs and me. "Honey in warm milk. Do not argue."
%%
Northpoint journal, the creeper
Autumn. Wind from the north, then no wind at all, which is worse.

Last night a creeper came up out of the dark behind the woodshed. I did not see it. I heard it, the hiss and then the bang. Then the south wall of the woodshed was not there any more. Stones lay across the yard like a dropped bread basket. The woodshed roof sagged on one side and looked embarrassed.

Lantern came out with the lamp and stood in the wreck, looking at the hole. It said, "Creeper." I said yes. It said, "Noisy." I said that was the general opinion.

We have fourteen torches round the foot of the tower and two by the door. We have none behind the woodshed, because the woodshed was thought to be safe. Note for the book: torches for the back of everything.

This morning I rebuilt the wall. Lantern carried the stones and I laid them. It knows the pattern now. It has not forgotten a single stone, and it is quicker than I am at putting the flat ones on top. We finished by noon. The wall is a little crooked. It is my wall. It will stand.
%%
Vesper, trial log: the broken wheel
Trial log kept by Vesper, tutor of apprentices, at the Three Wells mill in Longday, while the wheel was out. Written in the evenings, at the market board, by lamp.

Day one. The wheel broke at noon. A cross-beam split where the old oak meets the new, and the sluice that feeds the wheel could not be shut in time. The water went over the wheel and the wheel went round too fast, and then it stopped. The miller says he has seen this before. He has not, but he says it firmly.

The mill has one apprentice, a small mind that carries sacks.

Day two. I did not show it the right opening for the sluice. I do not know the right opening. Nobody does, for this wheel, since it is new. So I let it try. I set the sluice at a height and the apprentice holds it there. The wheel turns, or it does not. I write down the turns, counted on the mill's tally stick.

Day three. The apprentice tried one height after another, and found the middle. I did not tell it to. I only kept the score. Each try that turned the wheel well, it keeps more of. Each try that did not, it keeps less of. By evening it held the sluice where the wheel turned smooth and quiet, and nobody had told it which height that was.

I should say what this is, plainly, because the tutors at Vell will ask. In drill, the answer is shown to the mind. Here nobody shows the answer. The mind tries, the try is scored, and the mind keeps more of what went well. That is all it is.

Day four. The wheel is mended. The apprentice is still holding the sluice, because I have not told it to stop.

Kester, forge and road book
Spring, year 23.
First of Blossom. Rain before dawn. The stream is loud, and the boardwalk is slick.
I opened the forge late. The bellows had taken in water through the roof. Moss held the door while I patched the leak with clay and a plank. The fire would not catch until the noon sun came out.
A cart from Lowmarsh brought a broken wheel rim. Its driver had used the new road twice and trusted it enough to carry a load of fish. He says the dry rise has saved him a day of pushing through mud. I mended the rim and took two fish for the work.
Fourth of Blossom. Clear sky.
We laid cobblestone at the turn above the hamlet. There is not enough stone for the whole road, so the middle stays packed earth. The children brought stones in baskets. One basket held three frogs and no stones. The owner said the frogs were guarding the road.
Mara has planted carrots by her shed. Wren has set herbs behind the healing house. The old path is still used by folk on foot, but no cart has taken it since the rain.
Eighth of Blossom. Wind from the sea.
The inn has a new roof of reeds. I made six iron pegs for it. The innkeeper paid me with a bowl of beans and a promise of a place by the fire when I travel. I will remember the promise. He has forgotten two such promises already.
The first waymark stands at the fork. It has an arrow cut toward Lowmarsh. There is no number on it yet. We have not agreed how far the next inn is, and the men from Three Wells count their steps differently from us.
%%
Hob, relay log, the storm on the hill
Little relay, the hill between Vell and Three Wells. Logged by Hob. Late Thaw, the night of the great wind.

Dusk. Sky to the west gone the colour of a bruise. Wind still light. Checked the lamp, the stair, the rope on the door. All good. Put the cat in the lamp room, where she will not be happy but cannot be blown away.

Second hour after dusk. The wind came over the ridge with a sound like a cart on stone. The tower shook. Not much, but enough for the wax to crack on the stair rung. The lamp did not go out. The glass held. I was very glad of Mira's advice about the glass, which was to clean it hard and never let the frame rust.

Third hour. Rain came in sideways. It found the gap under the lamp room door and ran down the stair in a river. I put the sacks along the bottom step and tipped the bucket on the top one. We got most of it.

Fourth hour. A message came from Vell, short and urgent. It said: keep the lamp lit at any cost. I blinked back: The lamp is lit. The glass is good. The keeper is wet. It came back: Then stay wet, and keep it lit. I laughed at that, out loud, all alone on the stair.

Fifth hour. Something hit the tower hard. We all heard it, the cat too. A branch from the old oak at the bottom of the hill, I think, though I did not go down to check. The door held. I remember thinking it would be a poor thing to lose the door, since it was the one thing I had fixed myself.

Sixth hour. The wind began to drop. You could hear it going round the tower, in a long slow sigh, like a tired dog settling down. I sat on the bottom step with the cat in my lap, and she purred, and I was too tired to stop her.

Dawn. The sun came up over Vell. The lamp was still burning, pale in the daylight. Passed the morning messages. One from Dace, one from the Vell clerks, one from a carter who wanted to know if the road was clear, which I could not tell him. The stair is wet. Two rungs are gone, and one peg. The copper ring is dented on the north side. The lamp is lit.

Logged. Lamp the cat asleep on the copper ring. I am not moving her yet.
%%
Corin, first snow
Frost, year seven of the long quiet.

The first snow came before the nets were mended. It lay on the harbor wall and turned the rope stiff. We moved Sella's boxes from the boat shed house to my aunt's place, where the roof is sound.

The council has stopped paying for tutors. The hall is locked. The children ask when the crystal will be brought back. I tell them it is safe in Sella's chest.

Kester's note says a single crystal of this kind cannot learn the rule "one or the other, but not both." It can learn from examples. We show it a card, see whether its answer is wrong, and move its weights a little toward the answer we want. But with two marks on one card, the little changes cannot make it choose exactly one.

Sella says a different kind of mind may do it. She has no such mind to show us. She says not to guess at what we have not seen.

I cleared snow from the step. The children came anyway. We read the note by the kitchen fire. Then they sorted apples from pears, one at a time, and the crystal did well.
%%
Iona, the lost cat in the orchard
Lost cat, on the second of Apple. Grey, with one white paw and an expression of great superiority. Searched the orchard, the hives, the mill loft and under the cider press. Found three mice and a wasp, and both of them were annoyed with me.

Left a dish of cream by the barn door, as a sort of invitation. In the morning the dish was empty, and the grey cat was asleep in the sun on the hay cart, with the look of someone who has never been lost in her life. She has not been lost since. She has been very careful to be found.

Lowmarsh winter book
Winter, 203. The marsh froze at the edges, but the slow water stayed open by the fish weir.
The boardwalk was white with frost each morning. I took a small pail of sand out before the patients came. Moss took the larger pail and carried it along the whole boardwalk. By noon the pail was empty. Moss had used most of the sand on one slippery step. That step is now safe enough for a horse.
A fisher named Deren brought his mother to the healing house. She had a cough and cold hands. We warmed her by the hearth and gave her broth. She said the broth was good, then asked for more salt. Moss brought the salt jar. It was full of sand. I had used that jar for the boardwalk and put it back by mistake.
Deren laughed until his mother told him to fetch the right jar. He fetched it at once.
The frost spoiled the loose boards near the boat houses. We marked the worst ones with reed ties and asked the carpenters for replacements. Until then, nobody should carry a full basket over them. Moss carried two half baskets and said the rule was obeyed.
The fish weir caught four fish on the next clear morning. We kept three and returned one to the water. It was small and seemed in a hurry.
After the fair at Three Wells, Moss came home with a fair prize for a basket of dried herbs. The little wooden cup sits on the shelf above the labeled jars. Moss says it should hold honey. I said it holds pride and dust.
The jar of honey came from a stall beside the east well. The seller said the bees had worked through the last warm days. I bought it for the healing house. We opened it at supper and spread a little on bread. Moss measured a spoonful for each of us. Moss's spoon was much larger than mine.
The jar is now half full. The cup still holds only pride and dust.
%%
Lyle, a summer on the hill
Longday, the day of the clip. Sixty-two ewes and nine lambs grown into young sheep, all of them still on the hill above the mill pond. The shears went in the morning, and the wool came off in one piece on most of them.

The fleece on the good ewes weighed as much as a sack of flour. Wool fetches four emeralds a bundle at the Ambrook market, and five at Three Wells if the carter is quick and the road is dry. The carter was quick. The road was dry. I was pleased with both.

Longday, second day. Wet. The shorn ewes stood in the rain and looked at me as if I had done it on purpose. Mended the wall at the bottom of the hill. Built it up by two stones where the fox had been digging, and set a flat stone on top to keep it honest.

Melon, first day. A fox in the bottom field, very bold, with a rabbit in his mouth. Shouted at him. He stopped, looked at me, and went on with the rabbit at a trot. I think he had been told about me by someone.

Melon, fourth day. Gault came down from the relay tower with his oak door under his arm. He had shaved it himself, and it closed now with a sound like a hand on a table. He wanted a pot of sheep fat for the hinges. I gave him a jar, and he gave me a lecture on the proper way to hang a lamp. I know the proper way. My lamp is still hung my way, and it works.

Melon, eighth day. Hot again. The grass on the top of the hill is dry and gold. The ewes are fat, and the lambs are growing into the sort of sheep that will eat a whole field if you look away. I looked away for an hour. The field is still there, but the grass is shorter.

Melon, tenth day. A thunderstorm. The ewes bunched under the thorn trees, and the lambs stood in the middle of them like a stone in a stream. I counted them all twice, and then a third time for luck.

Melon, twelfth day. A visitor from Kettle Ridge, Tam's cart, with a message for Ruby about her beans, and a crate of eggs for me. The eggs were a bribe, and Tam did not hide it. Told him to take the eggs back, and he laughed, and took them with him.

Melon, fifteenth day. The count is right. Sixty-two ewes, nine lambs, and one ram who has learned the new fence. He stands at it every evening and looks at Ruby's beans as if he is thinking about the moral question. I think he is thinking about the beans.

Melon, twentieth day. Rain at dusk, and a rainbow over the mill pond, for the first time this year. Brought the flock down early, so they would not be out in the wet. Counted them at the gate. Sixty-two. Nine. One. All home.

North road waymark, a note left
Left for anyone walking north. The waymark torch is out. The wick is wet. I have no dry wick with me.
The fork ahead: left hand goes to the fold and the carding shed. Right hand goes to the sheep and the barn. Both come round to the same place. Do not worry. Follow the stones.
The fox is bold this summer. It will take a mitten if you leave one. Leave nothing you want.
Fern says come in and have water. The shed door is open.

Mira, Ambrook relay notes
Sowing, 8. Wind from the west. Clear after rain.
The tower wall is three blocks high. The builders say it will rise much farther. From the hill I can see the mill pond, the market stalls and the road toward Three Wells. On a clear day, a lamp here may be seen from the next ridge.
We have no lamp room yet. We have a safe place for stone, a ladder of rungs, and a table in the relay house. I drew the tower plan on a board. The builders have made the lower wall wider than my marks. They say a tall tower must stand firmly. I agree.
At market, Sella gave me a honey cake for the builders. She said it was payment for taking the last jar of honey. I had not taken the jar. Pell had. Pell paid her in an apple and said the account was even.
A cart came from Vell with glass panes wrapped in wool. The panes are for the lamp room. One cracked on the road. The driver kept the pieces in a cloth, so they will not cut anyone.
The road crew is laying cobblestone near the hill. Their cart sank in the wet earth yesterday. Six people pushed, and one apprentice carried the smallest stones. It did not need telling twice. The children watched and counted the pushes.
We marked a place for torches at the foot of the tower. The keeper will tend them when the builders leave. I asked who would tend the keeper. Jory sent a bowl of stew to the relay house.
%%
Lark, bread log, the first week of Frost
Frost, the first. Cold at dawn, the flour stiff in the sack. Baked forty loaves. Two burned on the bottom. The tower sent for six, and got five, and the keeper said the sixth was in the stove, and so it was.

Frost, the fifth. Flour is low. The mill is slow because the ice has got in the wheel. Made the last bread with the bran and some of the barley. It is grey and it is dense and the children have named it stone. They ate it all anyway.
%%
Kettle Ridge farm notes, fox at the hens
Autumn of 219. Kettle Ridge. Wind from the north. Frost on the top field.
Notes for the week. Tam, and Pip helping.
The fox came again. Third time this month. It came at dusk, low and red, along the hedge on the north side of the long field, and it sat and looked at the hen house like it was counting. Pip saw it first. Pip was in the yard sorting the beetroot into the bins and it stopped and stood very still, and I knew something was wrong, because Pip is never still.
I took the lamp and went out. The fox went off through the hedge. The hens were up on their roost and the door was shut, so nothing lost but my sleep.
I have mended the wire twice and the fox has found the hole twice. So now the wire is doubled at the bottom and I have put a stone along it, and there is a lamp on the post by the hen house that stays lit all night. Keep the torches lit. It is a small light but a fox does not like it.
Old Brin says a fox at the hens means a hard winter coming. Brin says a lot of things. He also said a field needs hands, not copper, and Pip brought in the beetroot faster than Brin's boy did, so Brin can say what he likes.
Mira wrote from Northpoint. She says Lantern is well and the tower is cold and the snow came early. She asked how the farm is and I wrote back: one fox, one lamp, all the hens. She will say that is a poem. It is not a poem. It is a farm.
The mill is squeaking on every turn again. The miller says it is the wind. It is always the wind. The tally board says we are ahead of last year.
Pip sorted the apples by size today, all on its own, into three bins. I did not tell it to. It just did it. I think it learned it from watching me. That is how it goes with apprentices, copper or otherwise. You show them, they watch, they get it wrong, they get it better.
%%
Tova, road log, the relay letters
Longnight of 159. The road is still shut. Greer says it will open at the thaw, which is always the thaw.

The Lowmarsh relay stands on the edge of the marsh, with one keeper and one lamp of its own. Letters come to it in sacks, from every road. Each letter has a number written on the outside: the tower it is for, and the road it must take. The keeper reads the numbers and sorts the sacks by hand. Sorting takes all night.

Abel has taught the mind to read those numbers. Each night he shows it two hundred letters from the sacks. It gives a number for each one. Each time it is wrong, the blame goes back through the three layers, and every dot is told its part. Abel says a mind learns from its mistakes, so it must have them.

The children's numbers are the hardest. A child writes a seven with a little tail, and it looks like a one wearing a hat. The mind can read a crooked seven now. It cannot yet read Ivo's threes, which look like ears.

Abel explained it once more, with his stick in the ash of the hearth. Put the three layers in a row. The first sees the marks. The second sees the shapes. The third says the tower number. The blame goes back from the number to the shapes, and from the shapes to the marks. Every dot in every row gets its share, and every dot moves a little.

Ivo asked if the mind had a name. Abel said it has a number, and that is enough for a mind. Ivo said that was a poor name for a mind, and he went out to feed the hens.
%%
Corin, steaming the planks
Winter of 204. Frost, the 20th. The shed is warm, which makes it the only warm place in Saltreach today.
We bent the side planks for the Good Morning. Oak does not like to bend. You have to ask it nicely, with steam.
The steam box is a long box of boards, closed at both ends, with a pipe from the kettle fire. A plank goes in. It cooks for an hour for every inch of its thickness. Then the door is opened and the plank is hauled out, and for a short time it is as soft as a wet reed. Everyone pulls at once. We have the time it takes to say the names of the crew to bend it into place and clamp it. Then it cools, and it is a curve forever.
Today we bent twelve. One cracked. The cracked one went in the stove. It made a very good fire.
Kit held the clamps. Kit does not talk when he holds clamps. That is the only time.
The cat sits on the steam box. Her name is Plank. She belongs to nobody and has never been burned. Marit says it is a gift. I say it is a cat.
We ate our bread on the benches by the slip, in the steam, with the window white. A good day.

Dara, market stall log
Kept by Dara, beekeeper, at her honey stall in the market at Ambrook. Three days of autumn.

First day. Set up at dawn. Twelve jars of dark honey, nine of light. Sold seven by noon. A child asked if the bees mind. I said they do not know.

Second day. Rain. Sold three jars. Slept under the awning.

Third day. Clear. Sold eleven. A man bought four for his mother-in-law. I hope she likes sweet things.

Northpoint journal, the last of Leaffall
Frost, the first day. Clear sky, very cold. The stars are sharp enough to cut your fingers on.

A cart came up the long road this morning, the first in three weeks. The driver was a young man from Three Wells, with a sack of flour, a jar of lamp oil and a letter from Tam. Tam's letter was four lines long. It said the beetroot was in, the hen house roof was on, and the mill had a new sail that squeaked like Pip. It said: "Tell Mira I am thinking of her, and do not climb anything high." I laughed out loud, alone at the window. The young man looked at me as if I had gone strange.

He said the roads south were quiet. He said the mines at Copperhold had stopped sending coal north for a week, and the carts had gone round by the Long Road, and nobody knew why. He said some families at Saltreach had put their chests on carts and gone east. He said it as if it were gossip. He did not say it as if it were a thing he believed.

I did not ask him anything more. He had a long road home, and the road is not safe after dark. I gave him a loaf and a jar of the last honey, and he thanked me and went down the hill with his mule. The mule did not look back, and neither did he.

Tonight the tutors' notes come up from Vell, as they always do in Frost. They say: keep the fields until we come back. Keep the lamps lit until we come back. Keep the glass clean. Those are the orders, and I wrote them in the book so no one could say they were not given.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed.

Lorn, a week of ferry and play
Leaffall, 201. From the log of Lorn, ferry keeper, at the house at the edge of Three Wells.
First of the week. The river is low and slow and a pale brown. Crossed twelve times. A cart of pumpkins came over, ten of them as big as stools. The carter said they were for the contest at the autumn fair. I said they were big enough to row.
Second. The school play is back. They have changed it. This year it is the Fox, the Bridge and the Ferry. I am in it. I am to speak. I have three lines. I have learned them by saying them to the rope.
Third. The rope did not answer. The boat did not either.
Fourth. Dress rehearsal. A boy fell off the stage. A girl lost her tail. I said my three lines. The teacher said, "Louder." I said them again. The teacher said, "A bit softer." I said them a third time. The teacher said, "That is the one."
Fifth. The play. The hall was full. I spoke my lines. They were: "Who goes there?" "The river is wide." "Mind the step." I think it went well. A child laughed at the right moment. Afterward, I was given a cake.
Sixth. The song contest. I did not enter. I sat in the front and clapped. The winner was the school, with the goose song again. It is a good song. It has twelve verses. I know nine.
Seventh. Back on the ferry. Crossed nineteen times. The river was calm. A fisherman said he had seen me in the play. I said it was nothing. He said it was something. I took it.

Lise, inn book: the spring guests
Kept at the Green Gate, through the spring. The guests, the horse, and the weather, as the inn saw them. The inn is not a great place. It is a warm one.

Sowing, first day. A wedding party stopped for the night on the way to Vell, forty of them, with a fiddler and a cart full of cakes. The bride had a cough from the cold and a laugh that came out louder than the fiddle. The fiddler slept on the bread bin, which was a mistake, and the bread has not forgiven him.

Sowing, second day. We sent the wedding party on with two loaves and a blanket for the bride. She sent back a sprig of blossom from the bush by the gate, pinned to a card: For the loom, which sang us to sleep. I put it in the window.

Sowing, fifth day. A horse came in lame from the north side of the road, and the carter sat down on the step and would not move until it was seen to. The horse is called Sorrel. We fed it warm bran and bathed the leg with cold water, and Ash bandaged it, practising on his own shin first. Sorrel has been trying to walk on it ever since. The carter says that is a good horse and a bad patient, and both are true.

Blossom, the second week. A traveler lost his hat in the wind by the gate. It rolled across the yard, through the chickens, and into the trough. I fished it out with the broom. The traveler said I had saved his life, which was an exaggeration, and he bought a blanket to make up for it.

Blossom, the sixth week. A cook came up from Lowmarsh to take the summer kitchen, with a sack of dried salmon, which she says is the best food for a long night on the road, and which Ash says smells like the sea had a bad day. She made a stew of it, with beetroot, potatoes and a handful of wild mint. The whole inn ate it. The carters asked for a second bowl, and the cook was very pleased with herself, which she should have been.

Blossom, the last week. Ash says the inn has never been so full in spring, and that he is going to paint the sign. I have told him to leave the sign alone. Everybody knows where the Green Gate is, and paint will only make it look like a different inn.
%%
Northpoint journal, Lantern's order
Longday, the seventh. An order went wrong today, and the mistake was mine.

I told Lantern: store the coal. It understood: store the coal on the stair. So it stacked a sack on every rung, very neatly, and when it reached the top step it stood there, waiting for the next thing. Ninety rungs of coal. I came up the stair with a lantern and counted forty-one sacks. I did not shout. Lantern does not shout back, and a shouting keeper teaches nothing. I said, slowly: store the coal in the cellar. It took the top sack down, and then the next, and then the rest, one at a time. It put them on the cellar shelf, where the coal belongs, and it did not ask why.

I have written on the card on the rack by the lamp: store means the shelf, and the shelf is at the bottom.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed. The coal is in the cellar. The stair is clear.

Elm, ferry log, the ice on the river
The river froze at the bend in the night, after a week of hard frost and no wind to stop it. In the morning the landing was a sheet of grey glass, and the ferry was sitting in it like a fly in a jar.

I stopped the crossings. There is a rule for this, and I wrote it on the board at the landing, and I repeated it to every cart that came down the road. No boat on the river when the ice is grey. No cart on the ice until the keeper has tested it.

The test is simple. I take the long pole, which is ash and very old, and I walk out from the bank, one step at a time, hitting the ice ahead of me. If it rings, I wait. If it sings, I wait longer. If it thuds like a heavy door, I go back to the bank and sit down and think about my life. On the first morning the ice rang, and then it sang, and then it thudded, and I sat down.

The baker's cart from Vell came round the bend at the second bell, with its driver whistling and its horse too pleased with itself. The driver wanted to cross the ice to save the long way round by the bridge. I told him no. He told me the ice was as thick as his arm. I asked him how he knew. He said his arm was very thick. I said the river did not care, and I was the one who would be answering to the mayor if he went through.

He did not believe me until the horse put one foot on the grey and the grey gave a small sharp crack, like a bone, under the hoof. Then he believed me very quickly indeed.

We walked the cart back up the bank, by the old road, and let the horse take the long way, which it did with bad grace. The baker's bread went on to Three Wells two days late, and the baker told everyone it was my fault. It was not. It was the river's, and the frost's. I only had the pole.

By the fifth day the ice was as thick as a barn door, and the carts went over it with the drivers walking in front, pole in hand, grumbling. By the twelfth day the ferry was out of work and I was sitting by the stove, counting the ice in my head, and the ice was counting me.

The ferry is hung up on its pegs, the rope is dry, and the boat is turned over on the bank with a sack over its bow. A river that is frozen is a road with no water in it. I have crossed it on foot. It is strange to walk where the boat should be, and to look down through the glass at the weed, still and green, waiting for the thaw.

Elm

Lowmarsh log, first snow
Winter, 171.
The boardwalk was slick before breakfast. I put sand on the worst boards and Moss put sand on the boat house steps. We both slipped once. Moss said the sand had done its best.
The frogs are quiet now. Two are still under the warm stones by the healing house well. I told Moss not to move the stones. Moss moved one, then put it back.
The fish weir held three fish and one old boot. The boot is not ill, so I left it by the door.
The sick beds are full of coughs and cold feet. I warmed broth and set the jars in order. Moss put the mint jar beside the mud jar. I changed them back. We will try again tomorrow.

Nessa's road log, spring 196
Written by Nessa, tutor at Vell, at the Halfway Inn on the Long Road. The first days of Thaw.

Day one. Rain all day. The relay lamp on the hill is broken, and the keeper says the glassmaker will not come until Sowing. I have a back room by the hearth, which is warm, and a drill box, which is not.

Day two. The drill box is in the inn yard. The innkeeper's boy asks what I am doing. I say I am teaching the minds to be even. He asks what even means. I tell him it means the same size. He says the soup is even, and I cannot argue with him.

Day three. The five small minds from the Brown Hen answered the drills badly. Their numbers grew wild in the third layer. Every answer came out the same.

Letter: Nessa to Tam, on small crystals
Summer of 200.
Dear Tam,
You asked how we make a mind small, so here is the plain truth, as plain as I can write it.
A mind is a great many numbers, called weights. That is all it is. Each number is written in the crystal in marks, the way you would write a sum on a board. The more marks, the more exact the number. The fewer marks, the rougher it is. A weight of 3.14159 takes many marks. A weight of 3.14 takes fewer. A weight of 3 takes very few, and is close enough for most work.
So we write each number in fewer marks. The mind keeps its shape, but each part of it is a little less exact. It sorts a little worse. It fetches a little slower. It makes one mistake where the big mind makes none. And the whole mind now fits a crystal a tenth of the size.
That is the trade. Smaller crystal, smaller cost, a little worse at its work. Every tutor must decide if the trade is worth it. For a farm, it is worth it. For a mind that reads letters for the relays, it is not. We keep the big minds big.
You want one for the hen house. I will drill it on fetch and store and stay, and test it on things it was never drilled on, and if it passes I will send it on the cart with the letters.
Do not open the crystal to look inside. Clank did that once and we were two days getting the lid back on.
Your friend, Nessa.
%%
Jem, the order on the pond shore
Spring of 11. Warm wind, rain at night.
Trouble at the pond today, all my fault, though the apprentice did what it was told.
Tam had sent his apprentice Pip down to the pond with a sack. He told it: bring me the nets from the shore. I was in the reeds with the eel trap and I heard him. Pip went down to the shore and stood by the water and waited.
The nets were not on the shore. They were in my boat. I had not brought them up yet.
So Pip stood there. Tam came down an hour later, cross, and asked why it had not brought the nets. Pip did not answer. It only looked at the water, the way it does when it is waiting. Tam said, I did not say wait, I said bring.
I said the order was my fault. I had told Tam that I would have the nets on the shore by noon. Pip only did what it heard.
Tam said, then say it right. He said it again, slowly, with his hand on the boat: "Fetch nets." Then Pip walked into the water, up to its knees, and brought the nets back on its shoulder, all of them, dripping and weedy.
Tam said he would have to be more careful with his words. I said I would have to be more careful with my boat. Pip said nothing. It was busy shaking out the nets.
We have agreed a new rule. When Tam says a thing to Pip, he says it short, and I say it again to make sure. Pip listens to the first word and the last word, Tam says. It does not seem to mind which order it gets.
%%
Letter: Bel to the archive
To the archive at Vell,
My name is Bel. I am nine. I go to the school by the east well at Three Wells.
We did a school play about the first relay tower. I was the keeper. I wore a coat and carried a lamp and said my line six times: the light goes on, the message goes out. The other children were the tower and the wind and the night. Cob was the night. He was very good at being the night. He stood in the corner and made a wind noise with his mouth.
My teacher said the play was about how we talk to each other across the land. She said the relays carry our words from town to town. She said even now.
After the play my father said the relays are quiet up north. My mother said not in front of the children. My father said the children should know. They had a quarrel in the kitchen. I listened from the stairs. Then they stopped and my mother made tea.
I want to ask the archive a question. Do you keep the school plays? If you do, will you keep mine? It had a real lamp in it from the inn.
Please write back.
Bel

Pell's road book, the Halfway Inn
Thaw, the 4th day.
Left Ambrook with eleven sacks of honey, three cheeses and a crate of apples. Nettle is in good spirits. She is always in good spirits until the mud begins.
The mud began at the low bridge below the Halfway Inn. The cart went in to the axles. Nettle pulled. I pushed. The honey did not move.
Marda came out with a rope and a stool. She sat on the stool and watched. She said the cart would come out when the mud was tired.
We got it out by noon. Two apprentices from the stables pulled without fuss, then went off to haul firewood. Marda gave each one a bowl of stew.
Honey is safe. Cheese is wet. Pell is tired.

Yarrow, the month of the play
A log kept at the Ambrook market by Yarrow the fisher, with his nets, his ledger and his opinions. Spring. The play was the great event of the month, and I have written most of it down.

Tenth of Sowing. The ice is gone. First trout of the year. Cold hands.

Twelfth. The teacher came by the pond with a paper. She is writing a play for the children, and needs someone who knows fish. I said I know them as well as they know me, which is not well. She said that will do.

Fourteenth. We talked it over at the market. The play is called The Fish Who Wanted Wings. It is about a trout who looks up at the birds and wishes to fly. The trout asks a heron to teach him. The heron says: "First, come out of the water." The trout says: "I cannot." The heron says: "Then neither can you fly." The trout is sad. A frog tells him that he can swim faster than the heron can run. He goes home pleased.

I told the teacher that is a good play. She said she took it from my pond.

Eighteenth. A poor day. The market was thin. A woman cried, quietly, at the grain stall, because the harvest last year was small and the sacks are low. I gave her a trout. She said no. I said, it is a very small one. She said yes.

Twentieth. Rehearsal. The children cannot remember their lines. The cat girl has forgotten hers. Twice.

Twenty-second. We have a trout costume. It is made of an old gray cloak with scales of copper. Nobody told me the copper would catch the light. A boy wearing it in the sun looks like a small, shy moon.

Twenty-fifth. The play was acted on the cart at the market. The whole of Ambrook came. The trout was good. The heron was tall, on stilts. The frog was a girl who croaked in the wrong place, and was the best of all.

When the trout said, at the end, "I am happy to be a fish," the market was quiet. Then the oldest woman in the town said, loudly, "So am I." Everyone laughed. I laughed. I had to wipe my eyes with the net.

Twenty-eighth. Back to the pond. A pike. I let it go.

Thirtieth. The month is over. A poor harvest and a good play. If you must have a thin year, have a play in it.
%%
Lorn's well book, the small relay tower
Melon, summer of 12.
They built the small relay tower on the hill between Vell and Three Wells. It is not tall. One lamp and one keeper. But it is the tallest thing for half a day's walk now, and I can see it from my door.
The keeper is a woman called Sedge. She came from Vell. She climbs the stair every night at dusk with a lamp and she lights the signal lamp in its room of glass and a copper ring. Then she sits at her desk. The redstone line runs down to her desk and it hums, a little. I went up to see it. She let me look. She did not let me touch.
She has a relay house at the foot of the tower. Inside is a big mind on a stand, in a crystal the size of a loaf. Sedge calls it the mind. It answers the apprentices through the relays. She says it is a good mind. She says it learned to read the numbers written on letters. She says it is slow but it is never wrong. I do not understand it. I do not have to. I am glad it is there.
The Tarns' apprentice came up the hill with me. I told it to fetch a bucket from the tower. It came back with the bucket and it stood by the well. It did not put the bucket down. I think it was waiting for the next order. I gave it the next order. It put the bucket down. It is a good worker. It does not talk.
Second of Melon.
The fair was today. There were stalls round the three wells. There was honey from Ambrook and beetroot from Kettle Ridge and a man selling apples. There was a contest between two tutors' halls, each mind tested on the same unseen drill. The minds were in crystals on a table and the tutors stood by them and wrote down what they did. I do not know who won. I think everyone won. There was a lot of shouting and some of it was happy.
I left a little stone on the waymark on the way home. Fen left one too. Nell laughed at us. She left one as well.
The summer is good. The water is cold. The tower is lit. That is enough for one year.
