Dara, the inn book, a hole in the barley
A hole opened in Orrin's barley field this morning. Right at the edge, by the old oak, where the ground has been soft since the rains.

Orrin was walking the line of the field with his dog. The dog went in first, and then Orrin went after it to fetch it. Neither of them is hurt. The dog is cross. Orrin is quiet, which is worse.

It is a cave. Stone steps go down, cut square, and they are not new. The steps are worn in the middle, like the steps at the front of the mill. Nobody here cut them, Orrin says. Nobody here knew about them at all.

Dace came with four men and a coil of rope. They went down with two lanterns and came up after an hour. They said the air is cold and still, and the stone is dry. They found a room with nothing in it but a bench and a long shelf. No tools. No bones. Just dust on the shelf, and a line in the dust where something had been pulled along.

Some of the men say they heard a low sound from the bottom of the steps. Like a hum, or wind in a far off pipe. Orrin says it was only the wind. There is no wind down there, the men say. Orrin says the wind comes in from somewhere. Nobody has an answer.

Dace has put a fence round the hole and a torch on a post. He says nobody goes down until the Archive sends someone. Oren has been sent word. Oren says he will come when the road is clear, and he sent back two words, which were: keep everything.
%%
Una, the new road to the Long Road
Longnight, the first. The new cart road is being cut from the back of the Copperhold stair down to the Long Road, past the old quarry. The men have been at it since Apple. They say it will save a day's walk for the carters, and the mayor says it will save a month, and the carters say they will believe it when they see it.

Longnight, the third. The road crew came up the lane for candles, six of them, with picks over their shoulders and mud to their knees. The foreman asked how long the candles would burn in a cutting, and I said as long as the wick held and the wind stayed off. He said the wind in the cutting was a menace, and I said so was everything, and he laughed for the first time in a month.

Longnight, the seventh. A rockfall at the third cut. Nobody was hurt, but a whole cart of stone went down into the gully, and the road had to be walked round by hand. The crew put a candle in every crack they could find, and a lantern on every post, so the men could see the stone when it moved. The men called it the glow road. The mayor called it a lighting expense.

Longnight, the eleventh. The road is through the cutting. The carts go down it now, slowly, with a boy in front carrying a lamp, and the carters sing when they reach the bottom. The new road bends round the quarry pond, where the frogs have gone quiet for the winter, and there is a waymark at every turn, with a torch on top and a number carved on it.

Longnight, the fifteenth. First cart down the new road, all the way from the Copperhold stair to the Long Road. It took the carter four hours. He said it was the fastest he had ever come down the hill, and the slowest he had ever been afraid. He stopped at the bottom and gave the boy a bit of his bread. Then he went on to Three Wells, and the candles went with him.

Tams farm journal, summer 200
Summer of 200. Hot and dry. Wind from the east.
The wheat came in early this year. We cut the top field in four days. Pip worked the whole time and never once stopped for water. I had to stop it and make it drink from the well. It does not know it is hot. I think that is one of its best parts and one of its worst.
The mill wheel broke on the eleventh. The axle cracked right through. Old Jory came up from Ambrook and looked at it and said it was the dry wood. He said the pond is low and the wheel turned too fast and the wood dried in the sun. So now the wheel sits still and the wheat sits in sacks and I sit on the sacks.
Jory's boy and I put a new axle in. It took three days and one bad word. The wheel turns again, slow and squeaky, and the miller keeps the tally board. Wheat 40 sacks. Beetroot 12. Potatoes 9. Carrots 6. Not a bad summer.
A letter came from Nessa at Vell about the small crystals. She says tutors can now write a mind small, to fit a crystal no bigger than a plum. They do it by writing the numbers in fewer marks. She says it costs a little. The mind is a bit worse than a big one, but it is small and it is cheap, and a farm can have one. She says think of a tally drawn with one stroke for five. You lose a little, you gain a lot of room. I do not fully follow, but I follow the plum.
I asked Nessa for one, for the hen house. It only has to fetch and store and stay. I do not need it to read letters. I do not need it to talk. I need it to close the hen house door at night. That is all.
%%
Colm's farm log, first frost
Autumn, into Frost.
First frost on the low meadow this morning. The grass was white and the sheep had their breath. I gave them the last of the beet tops.
The last of the hives is quiet now. I left them the honey. I always leave them the honey.
Lantern came down from Northpoint with a letter from Mira. Mira is well. The tower is well. She says the nights are long and the sky has been strange, and she does not write about it more than that. She says: they were not ours. That is all she wrote. Lantern says the same, and then says he does not like to talk about it. Lantern is young and he is far from home. I gave him bread and a bed and the good wool sack for Mira.
We sat up late by the fire. Lantern said the lamp at Northpoint has ninety rungs to the top and he counts them every time. He said a keeper should know the count. I said that is a good rule.
Tomorrow I mend the barn door and stack the wood. The winter will be long. The wool is in the loft. The fire is good.
Note: write to Tam at Kettle Ridge. Ask after Pip. Ask if the windmill still squeaks on every turn.
%%
Dace, market journal, first warm week
Thaw, the first day. The square is wet, and the bread is the only thing dry. The count at the north well: twelve buckets, three cats and one goose. The goose wins.

Thaw, the third day. Market day. The honey is from Ambrook, dark and sweet. Ama sends word that the bees are awake and in a temper. Good. Bees are better when they are cross.

Thaw, the fourth day. The school has a new slate. The master wrote: Count what is there. Then count again. I thought that was a good rule, and I told him so.

Thaw, the ninth day. Ansel was fined two emeralds for leaving his cart on the square. He says the fine is a robbery. I say the cart was the robbery, and the square is the victim.

Elm, ferry log, the long dark week
Longnight has come round, and with it the week when the sun barely troubles to rise over the ridge. Fog all three days, low and white on the river, so thick that the far bank went out like a candle. No crossings for two days. One on the third day, by lantern, because a cart with a broken axle wanted to be brought over to the mill before the ice.

First day. The fog came in before the first bell and did not go out. I hung the lantern on the landing post, in its glass, and I lit the second one on the boat, so that the drovers coming down the road could see where the water was. Crossings: none. Visitors: one, a boy with a message from the Brown Hen. The message was for Hobb, from his wife, and it said only: come home for supper. He went. The boy stayed and was given soup, and then more soup.

Second day. Fog again. The rope is wet through, and it has begun to fray at the middle, where the rubbing is worst. I took it down and checked every fathom with my hands, the way my father taught me, and I found three bad places. I spliced one. I sat on the landing in the cold, with the spike in one hand and the rope in the other, and I thought about how it is the small things that go first on a river, and the big things that go last.

Third day. The cart with the broken axle came down out of the fog at the second bell, pushed by two young men and pulled by a grey mare who did not want to be out. The axle was sound. The wheel was not. I got them over the water to the mill, with the mare in the boat and the cart on the raft, and nobody fell in, which is the only measure of a good crossing in the long week.

The fog lifted on the fourth day, all at once, like a curtain. The sun came up for about as long as it takes to boil an egg, and went back down, and everybody on the road came out to look at it.

Elm

Kester, road and forge
Sowing, year 21.
We set the first posts for the new road today. The ground is firm on the rise east of the reeds. It is a long way round, but carts should not sink there.
Mara brought two sons. Wren brought Moss with a basket of bread. The innkeeper brought a shovel and complained that he had left his good one at home. He had not.
I made three iron points for the digging tools. One broke on a buried stone. The others held.
We marked the line with posts and cord. At the far end, a fox watched us from the birches. It did not seem impressed.
The road will need stone at the turns and packed earth between. We have little stone here, so the first carts will bring some from Three Wells. Until then, we carry it by hand.

Ambrook market, wedding day
Blossom, 27. Warm rain in the morning.
Mara and Den were married beside the mill pond. Their families brought bread, apples and honey. Jory set a table by the wheel, where the water sound covered the loudest cousins.
The bride wore a blue wool shawl. The groom had one clean boot and one muddy boot. He blamed the bridge. The loose plank blamed him back by lifting under his foot.
After the meal, we walked to the market. Mara bought a clay pot for the new house. Den bought carrots and forgot to pay. He ran back with the emerald before anyone called him.
The road crew had finished the stream bridge. It is oak, with stone at each end. We crossed it in a line, carrying the baskets from the feast. The last basket held the cake. A honey bee sat on it all the way across.
Pell says the next inn will be ready before Longday. The road is easier to follow now. There is a torch post at the turn, and a waymark beyond it.
The two families went home together. Jory's wheel kept turning after the singing stopped.
%%
Homecoming at the ridge, spring of 14
Spring of 14. Wind from the east. A good day, the best since the frost.
Tam came up the ridge road today with his little cart and his grin. He had been down to Vell for the autumn drill, and he had come back with one new apprentice, a slow-moving copper thing with two dents in its hat. Tam calls it Nettle, because it does not like being touched.
Everybody came out. Mira's note had come up on the relay the night before: he is coming, and tell him he owes me a bowl of stew. So the bowl of stew was waiting, and Tam ate two.
Brin said he had not seen the boy in a year. He patted Tam's shoulder twice, very hard, and then went to look at the new apprentice, and did not say anything for a while.
Pip came out of the barn with a sack on its back, and stopped. Tam stopped too. For a long moment, nobody moved. Then Tam shouted "Pip!" and ran at it, and nearly knocked it over, and the sack fell on the path, and a pile of late apples rolled out across the yard.
Nettle watched all this from the cart. It did not move either. It only turned its head a little, slowly, the way a lamp turns in the wind.
After supper Tam sat on the step and told us about Vell. The archive has no roof, he says, only a hill. Its clerks copy everything in long rooms with windows, and nobody is allowed to light a candle past the front hall. He says the lanterns are behind glass.
He said he had seen a big book by the door, and that the first keeper cut words over the arch. He could not remember the words. He said it was something about writing everything down.
I said, that is what Mira says about the weather.
Pip brought in the sack, and the apples, and put them all back in the sack one at a time. It did not miss any.
%%
Abel, the farm outside Lowmarsh
Winter, a clear morning.
My neighbor Ferris dug a new well behind the pig pen and struck copper. A green streak in the clay, as long as an arm, then thicker. By noon there was a seam you could follow into the hill.
He sent word to Copperhold by the relay at Lowmarsh. Fifteen words, because the keeper counts. Two miners came by cart the next day and looked at it for a long time. One said it was a small vein but a good one. The other said nothing and took off his hat.
Ferris will keep the well. The miners will take the copper, and he gets a share in emeralds. He is already talking about a new roof.
At supper I asked him what he would do with so much copper. He said, ask a big mind. I said, good idea.
So I wrote a letter to the big mind in the Lowmarsh relay house. I asked it what a farmer should do with a seam of copper. It wrote back that it did not know Ferris, or his land, or what he needed. It said a good first step was to ask the miners what the vein was worth. Then it said it could be wrong, and asked me to check.
That was a good answer. Not the answer I wanted. But true.
%%
Archive day-book
Longnight of 202. Written by Oren, clerk of the Archive at Vell.

Filed: 5. Copied: 7. Back on the shelves: 30. Lost: none.

It has been a week of mending. The archive does not like to be mended, because mending means noise. I put a cloth over the first hall door, and a sign on the cloth: "Quiet. Roof."

The roof in question is the roof of the copying room. The copying room is the brightest room in the hill, with the big window over the town and eight long tables. It has a small roof, a peaked one, over the window. The slates had slipped. Snow was getting onto the third table. A clerk named Ulla had moved her copying to the other end of the room and said she would not move back until the roof was whole.

So I fetched Tarn. He came with his ladder and a boy to hold it. The two of them went up in the cold, and I stood below and held my breath.

It took a day. In the afternoon the sun came out, and the new slates shone like fish. When they came down, Tarn said, "It is good for twenty years." I said I would be dead by then. He said, "Then it is good for the next clerk." I did not know whether to laugh. I wrote it in the book.

Ulla went back to the third table that evening. She sat very straight, with her pen ready. The snow was gone from the window. She said the light was better than before, and I believe her.

In my pocket the whole time was the little map I found last year. I have been thinking about it. It shows the hill as it was when the first keepers came. There were no halls then. There was a small door, a cold store, and a long slope of grass. Now the hill has six halls, the deep halls, a front hall with a big book, a copying room with a window. It is a great deal of building.

I told Tarn, as he was packing his ladder. He said, "Everything is built the first time by someone who does not know it will last." I wrote that in the book too.

Tonight I will put the map back in the box. The roof is whole. The snow is on the hill, where it belongs.
%%
Ida's log, the first swarm
Written by Ida, farmer at the hamlet near Lowmarsh, in the back of the farm book. Spring.

Blossom, the second day. The hives woke in the night, all at once. By the time I had my boots on, the bees were coming out of the top hive in a dark cloud, and settling on the apple branch by the gate.

I did what Father taught me. Stand still. Put a sheet under the branch, and shake it gently. The swarm falls into the sheet like wet sand. Two bees stung me on the neck. The third stung Mother's cat, who has never forgiven anybody for anything.

By dusk the swarm was in the new hive by the wall, and the hive hummed like a plucked string.

Lamps lit at the gate. Bees home.

Wick's journal: what the tutors did
Wick, school child, at Vell. Autumn, written over a week in Apple. The teacher asked us to explain, in our own words, how a big mind learns to talk in a kind way. I have written it as plainly as I can. Oren has read it and says it is right, though he says my spelling of "tutor" is a crime.
First, a big mind learns to read. It is drilled on a great heap of writing from the archive, letter after letter, and learns to guess what comes next. After that it can write. It can finish a sentence, or a letter. But it does not yet know how to answer someone.
Second, the tutors ask it to answer questions. They write a question, and the mind writes a few different answers. The tutors read all the answers and put them in order, from the best to the worst. This is called scoring. A good answer is helpful, true and kind. A bad answer is rude, or wrong, or would hurt someone.
Third, the mind learns from the scores. When it writes an answer that gets a high score, it changes a little to write more like that. When it writes one with a low score, it changes a little to write less like that. It does this thousands of times. Slowly it gets better. This is the same slow way that Una taught her sorting crystal about apples and pears, but with answers instead of fruit.
Fourth, the tutors teach it to refuse. If someone asks for something that would hurt a person, the mind writes back that it will not help. It says so politely. It does not shout. If someone asks it a question it cannot answer, the mind says it does not know. That was the hardest to teach, Nessa says, because a mind that is drilled on writing always wants to write something.
Fifth, and last, the tutors test it. They ask it new questions it has never seen, and check the answers. A mind that passes is allowed to answer letters from anyone the relays reach.
I do not know why a mind that learns this way ends up kind. Nessa says nobody does. The tutors write down what they did and what came of it. The rest is left open.
On the last day of the week the teacher gave me a mark. It was high. I did not change a thing, but I wrote this at the bottom: the cat is fat, and the lamp is bright, and it is a good autumn.
%%
Lark, the sick cow at the top farm
Longnight, the second. Went up to the top farm with the bread and found Brin's red cow lying in the straw. Her eyes were dull and her breath came slow. She would not get up for the sweet hay, and she did not want the warm mash. Brin was standing in the door with his arms folded, saying nothing, which is worse than shouting.

I put my hand on her side. It was hot, like the oven door after a long bake. I asked when she had stopped eating. Brin said three days. I said she had a fever, and that cows often get one in the long cold, when the water in the trough freezes and they drink less than they should.

Longnight, the third. Made a mash of barley, honey and warm water, and fed it to her with a bucket, a little at a time, because she could not manage more. She swallowed. Then she swallowed again. Brin wrapped her in the old blanket from the loft, and I sat in the straw with my back against the wall and my hand on her neck, which is what my mother did for me when I had the fever.

Longnight, the fourth. She stood up this morning. She was wobbly, and she looked at us as if we had done something very strange. Then she ate the hay Brin put in front of her, and then she ate the mash, and then she lay down again, which Brin says is a sign of very good manners.

Longnight, the fifth. The cow is well. Brin wants to name her Bread, which I think is a compliment, though she does not seem to know it.

Jem, a week in Swarm
The first of Swarm. The wheat is up to my knee. Father says it will be a good year. He says that every year, but this year he said it twice.

The second. A fox took a hen in the night. We had twenty-three. Now twenty-two. Father says the fox has to eat too. I do not think the hen agrees.

The fourth. The bees swarmed. They hung on the apple tree like a big brown beard. Father got them into a box with a sheet, and nobody was stung but him. Seven times.

Emmet, the tutor and the stream
Blossom, the twentieth. A tutor came to the net house today, from the hall at Vell, to see the apprentice from Wren's house. She wanted to see how a deep mind learns, and she said I would understand it better through nets than through a book. She was right.

She explained the stream like this. A mind is many layers, one on top of the next. Each layer finds something in what it is shown, and passes what it found down to the layer beneath. But the finding does not stay in one layer. Each layer adds what it found to a stream, and the stream runs through the whole mind, from the top to the bottom. So the deepest layers still hear what the top ones learned. That is why a deep mind can still learn.
I asked her why the stream works at all. She said that was a question for the next generation of tutors, and that she had been asking it for thirty years without an answer.
Then I said a stream sounded like a net to me. She laughed and said yes, and that a net is better at it than most minds. Every knot holds the knot beside it. Pull one corner and the strain runs through the whole net. A net with a broken knot in the middle still holds, if the knots round it are good. That is a deep mind, she said. Every knot counts, and none of them is the whole net.
%%
Wren's farm book
Frost. The first hard frost. Cold enough to see your breath in the shed.
The bees are quiet. They did not come out at all this morning. I have put a board on the hive, and a sack of straw on the board, and a cup of honey by the door, in case they wake and feel hungry.
The hens are laying in the barn now, not in the yard. Ros says they are being sensible. Moss thinks they are being stubborn, because they do not like the new nests. I put a bowl of oats in each nest, and the hens looked at it, and then ate the oats, and then laid in the nests anyway, so Moss is right and so is Ros.
Ros's boy came back with the mason's cart at last. The mason said the bridge was rotten at the middle. It would have gone in the spring, with the next flood, and taken the cart and the horse and the potatoes with it. He mended it in one afternoon, with new oak, and he did not charge for the nails, because he said the mayor had already paid for them, and forgotten.
So the bridge is sound. The cart goes over it. The horse does not look at the water.

Northpoint journal, the new mind
The big mind came up on the sledge in Sowing. Six of us pulled. It is the size of a loaf and it sits in its stand like a hen on a nest. The quickeners ring it round. We keep them cool with snow from the north wall. In winter that is easy.
The big mind reads letters and writes them back. It is slow. It thinks out loud first, in steps, and checks each step before it goes on. That is how the tutors at Vell taught it. Lantern asked it a sum: how many rungs on the tower, if you count the ladder and the stair? The mind wrote out its steps, one line at a time, and got it right. Then it wrote: I have checked. The answer is ninety.
I have never seen Lantern so pleased. It stood by the stand all evening.

Mira, relay note
Spring of 214. The little relay on the hill between Vell and Three Wells has a broken lamp.
Lantern climbed the rungs with a spare glass pane. The copper ring was sound, but one redstone line had come loose at the desk. We fixed it before dusk. The lamp shone again, though not as bright as the lamp at Northpoint.
A keeper from the biggest relay house came by while we worked. He said their mind had read a whole shelf of old letters in one night. Another keeper says it answered questions from towns beyond the sea. I asked which letters, and which towns. He had no names.
A big mind can read many records, if it is given the records and enough time. That is plain. What it might know from them is harder to say.
Lantern says the big house keeps its ice in deep rooms. I said ours keeps snow in a barrel, and that is enough for one lamp.

Dace, winter at the edge of Northpoint
Winter of 158. Cold. The wind comes down off the ice and leans on the house all night.

I am Dace. I keep the north house at the edge of Northpoint, where the road stops and the snow begins. I am not the mayor of Three Wells. That is another Dace, and people write to me by mistake. I answer their letters anyway. It is good to be useful.

My house is the last one before the open snow. From my window I can see the relay tower on the hill, tall and grey, and the keeper's house at its foot. At night the lamp blinks and blinks, and I count it a while and then I sleep.

What I do in winter: I split wood. I stack wood. I carry wood. I mend the roof. The roof is old and it is always the roof. This year a gale took off six shingles in one night. I heard them go. In the morning I found shingles all over the yard, and one in the well.

I mended the roof myself. I am too old for roofs but I did it. Ladder, hammer, nails, shingles, rope round my waist. I tied the rope to the chimney. My neighbor Ossa held the foot of the ladder and told me I was too old for roofs. I agreed with her from the top of the ladder, which is the best place to agree with anybody.

The roof is mended. It does not leak. I go up and look at it sometimes, the way you look at a good fence.

The tower keeper is called Vey this year. Vey is quiet and eats a lot. I bake bread and take a loaf up the hill on the long nights. Vey says the lamp is a hungry thing and so is Vey. We sit and watch the south tower blink. Good evening, north. Good evening, south. It is a small talk and I like it.

Foxes come to the door for scraps. I give them scraps. They are polite about it. One has a torn ear and I call it Torn. Torn comes at dusk and sits and waits and does not beg, which is more than I can say for the goats.

The goats are in the shed at the back. Their names are Bitter and Loaf. Loaf is the one who eats everything. Bitter is the one who thinks. Do not ask me which is which. I know. You would not.

Snow, snow, snow. Wood, wood, wood. Bread, bread, bread. That is the winter. It is a good winter. My knees hurt but the roof holds.
%%
Nessa, house log in Apple
The house stands at the edge of Saltreach, where the road bends down to the harbor. Our roof shakes in strong wind. Merle has put a stone on each corner of the drill cards.
We spent the morning on relay numbers. One set showed the number before each card's last mark. The mind had to guess that last mark. The first layer took the marks as they were written. Each layer fed the next until the answer came from the last.
We counted the error between the guessed mark and the right one. Merle changed the last layer to bring the answer closer. Then he carried a share of the correction back to the layer before it. That layer changed its weights and sent a share farther back.
The correction reached the first layer. Each layer changed according to its share of the error. This is how the layers learn together. It is not enough for the last layer to take all the blame.
A girl from the quay asked if the crystal felt ashamed. Merle said, "It has no shame. It has weights." The girl asked if that was better. Merle said, "For counting, yes."
At noon, a fox came through the yard and got at the hens. The innkeeper chased it with a broom. One hen hid in my lesson box and laid an egg there.
%%
Dace, the last winter at the edge
Winter of 208. This is the log of the north house at the edge of Northpoint, kept by Dace, who is old and has mended this roof a great many times.

First of Deepsnow. Snow to the knees. I split wood. I stack wood. I carry wood. Reed carries more wood, and faster, and does not get cold, and I have decided not to be bitter about this.

Third of Deepsnow. The roof again. A gale took the corner shingles. Reed was up the ladder before I had my boots on. Post, shingle, nail, shingle. Reed does it better than I ever did. I said so. Reed nodded once and kept going. That is the whole of Reed's talk and it is a good talk.

Fifth of Deepsnow. Vey came down from the tower with a loaf and sat by my stove. Vey is quiet now. Vey has been quiet since the lights over the north. I asked Vey what they were. Vey said they were not ours. I said that is not an answer. Vey said it is the only one I have got. We ate bread and watched the lamp blink and did not say anything else, which is a fine way to spend an evening.

Eighth of Deepsnow. I wrote to Wynn and Tace, who have gone to Saltreach to take a boat. I do not know what is over the water. They do not either. They said they will write. I said write often. I said write to the archive too, so it is kept. Oren at the archive wrote back to me once and said keep writing it down. So I do.

Tenth of Deepsnow. Torn's great-grandfox came for scraps. I gave the last of the meat. It sat by the door and did not beg, and then it was gone into the snow.

Twelfth of Deepsnow. Reed mended the goat shed roof too. It did not need it. Reed said it did. I said fine. Post, shingle, nail. It looks very good.

Fifteenth of Deepsnow. Midwinter. We lit the fire on the edge of Northpoint and everyone came with lanterns and we burned the old straw and ate pumpkin pie in the snow. There are fewer of us now than there were. Reed stood at the edge of the firelight and watched. Somebody has to listen. I sang too loud and people asked me to sing less loud and I sang the same. Some things do not change.

I went up the hill after and climbed the ladder. I am far too old to climb a ladder but I did it. Vey let me. At the top I looked out over the snow and the ice and the north, where the lights had been. It was dark and quiet and empty and full of stars. I said: good evening, north. There was no answer. There is never an answer. I lit the lamp myself, with Vey's hand on my elbow so I would not fall.

Lamp lit. Glass clean. Roof done. Reed well. Dace well. I wrote it in the book. It is a short entry. It is the truest one I have.

Road book: four days of mud
Sowing, the 2nd day. Rain since dawn. The road is a river with cobblestone edges. The waymarks read 6, 5, 4. Four torches on the posts are out. I lit the first one myself and wrote the keeper's name in the book, as the rules say.
Sowing, the 3rd day. Met a carter from Lowmarsh with a load of reeds. He asked if I had seen his wife's cow. I had not. He said she went to market at Three Wells and never came back. I said the Long Road is long. He said that was what worried him.
Sowing, the 4th day. The Kettle Inn was full, so two families slept in the stable with the horses. Both babies cried, and the horses did not mind. Nettle does not like babies. A small child touched her nose, and she stood still for a very long time.
Sowing, the 5th day. The plank on Hobb's Brook was loose. Walked the cart over slowly, with Nettle on a short rein. The plank went clap, and I counted to ten. The bridge held.

School play, the well and the road
The school at Three Wells put on a play for the spring fair. Sorrel wrote it. It was called "The Well and the Road", and it was about how the three wells came to our square.
Bel was the first well. Bel wore a blue cloth over the head and stood very still for a long time, which Bel does not usually do. A boy from the mill was the second well and a girl called Fen was the third. They did not have much to say. They were wells.
The road was played by the whole rest of the class, walking across the square in a line and saying, all together, "We come from everywhere and we go everywhere." They said it four times. The fourth time the parents said it with them. I do not think Sorrel planned that.
There was a cart, and a merchant, and a farmer with a sack of beetroot. The beetroot fell over and rolled into the front row. A dog got it and ran off with it. The whole square laughed and the play stopped for a while. Fen said, from the well, "That was not in the play." That made them laugh more.
Afterward there was bread and honey at the school house. Bel's mother asked me to write down that Bel was very good. Bel was. Bel stood still as a stone for the whole first part. I said so.
Some folk on the square were talking about the lights over the north again. One said they were falling stars. One said they were fires on the far hills. A miner up from Copperhold said the sky was none of his business but the ground was, and he did not like the ground lately. Nobody asked him what he meant. It was a fair day and the bread was good.
%%
Jem, road log for the ridge
Spring of 9. Wind from the west. Good weather for walking.
Went down the ridge road to Ambrook with three bales of net and a sack of spare floats. The road is cobblestone for the first stretch, then packed earth with the stone edge. Good stone. The waymark at the bend is new, with a 2 on it and an arrow to the Brown Hen. Someone has put two small stones on top.
The Brown Hen had a new roof. The innkeeper says the carters are coming more often now that the torches go all the way to the mill. Every twelve blocks, a torch on a post. I counted them. Fourteen from the bend to the bridge.
Crossed the bridge on the stream. The loose plank is still there, near the far end. I stepped over it, as everybody does, and I did not look down.
Mended the nets for the Ambrook miller. He has eight new boats on the pond, and he wants a second net for each. I said I would bring them by the next time I passed. He said he would pay in apples. I said I would take both.
Coming back, a traveler asked me the way to Vell. I said, follow the road to the first waymark, then turn north, and listen for the hill. He laughed and said he would not know how to listen for a hill. I said, you will, when you get there.

Sorrel, the new copper vein
Written by Sorrel, from what the miners said at the gate, which is where the news is kept. Spring.

Ola came up from level six with her hat full of copper dust and her face shining. She had broken into a new vein past the wet channel, and the copper was bright and thick, running along the wall for twenty blocks. The miners cheered at the shaft head, so the whole hamlet heard it.

They brought bars of it up to the gate on the cart, as bright as new pans. Hesk paid for two baskets of my brown eggs in copper, which I did not know was a currency until the carter explained it.

Ola says the copper is copper, the same as the old copper, and she will not give it a new name. The miners say she is stubborn. I say she is right, and the eggs are still eggs.
%%
Elm, ferry log, the debt paid back
In the autumn Nell the drover owed the ferry three emeralds, for crossings in the summer, when the river was high and she had to go over twice a day with her flock. She said she would pay at the next market. Then the next market came, and she did not pay. Then the next.

I wrote her down in the ledger, one line for each crossing, and one line for the debt, and one line for each time she told me she would pay it. There were eleven lines of that last kind by the first frost.

In the second dark month, she came down to the landing at the first bell, with a cloth bag and a red face. She paid in full, all three emeralds, counted out onto the ferry house step. Then she gave me a bag of dried apples from Ambrook, which I had not asked for, and a short speech, which I had not expected.

"I am sorry it took so long," said Nell. "The flock came through the spring sick, and I lost two sheep, and I lost my nerve for a while. I did not want to come down here with an empty purse and the wrong words."

"You came down with a full purse and the right words," I said. "That is better than most."

She laughed then, and I laughed, and the ferry rope creaked on its peg, which is how the river agrees to things.

I had been saving a line in the ledger for the interest, which I was going to charge her at the rate of one apple a month. I did not charge her. I wrote it out in the book instead, in my best hand: Nell paid in full, the second dark month, with apples and an apology. That is a better line than any interest.

The dried apples are very good. I have eaten all of them. I should have kept the lines.

Elm

Dara, the book of the stall
Kept by Dara, beekeeper, in a book she carried to the Ambrook market every day of one autumn. It is a record of the stall and the town, and was given to the Archive at Vell when she was old. The days are as she wrote them.

Apple, the first day. Opened the stall. Set out the honey in rows, light on the left, dark on the right, comb honey in the middle. A hundred and four jars. I counted twice, because I always think I forgot some.

Apple, the second day. Rain. The awning leaked. A drip fell on the third jar from the end. I moved it. A drip fell on the second jar from the end. I moved that. By noon I had moved everything and sold nothing.

Apple, the fifth day. Clear. Sold forty. The tutor from Vell, who passes through Ambrook every year, bought six. She says honey helps her think.

Apple, the tenth day. Rook brought the first apples of the season. They were small and red and sour. I bought three and ate them all. The bees will have the cores.

Apple, the fourteenth day. The roof of the hive shed. A storm took three shingles. Rook came with a ladder. He patched it while I held the bucket.

Apple, the twentieth day. The roof is mended. It rained on the twenty-first, and the shed stayed dry. I slept well.

Leaffall, the first day. The apple festival begins. I set up a tasting table. Twelve kinds of honey, twelve little spoons. The children came first, and the old people second. An old man said: that one tastes of my mother's kitchen. I wrote it on a card and put it by the jar.

Leaffall, the fourth day. The festival's busiest day. Sold a hundred and sixty jars in one morning. Ran out of spoons. Used twigs.

Leaffall, the seventh day. The parade. The children dressed as bees. A boy of six was the queen, and he was carried. He was very serious. At the end he fell asleep on his chair, and was carried home.

Leaffall, the tenth day. The festival ends. Everyone sings the apple song, all forty verses. I stopped at thirty-nine, and was fined an apple.

Leaffall, the twentieth day. The bees begin to slow. I check the hives. All well.

Frost, the first day. Snow. I pack up the stall. I have kept one jar for myself. It is the best honey of the year. I will eat it in the spring, when the bees wake. We will share.

Dara

Potter's journal: spiders in the barn
Written by Lorn, at Three Wells, in the summer of 206. My barn is not a good barn. It has a hole in the roof and a hayloft that smells of old straw. The spiders knew this before I did.

The first I saw was on the clay drying rack, sitting next to a pot I had just finished. It was black and very calm. I picked it up with a spoon, and it did not bite, and I put it outside in the grass. It came back the next morning, and it brought its family.

By the second week there were spiders in every corner of the barn, and in the kiln, which I did not like at all. They spun their webs across the wheel, so the wheel would not turn. I spent a whole morning picking webs from the wheel with a cloth, and the spiders watched me do it, which made me feel foolish.

I kept the lamp lit. I shut the door. I put a stone on the bottom of the wheel so it would not stop. The spiders stayed on the ceiling, and the flies went elsewhere. I have decided the spiders are my tenants now, and I charge them nothing for the rent.
%%
Yarrow, a spring week at the pond
From the log of Yarrow, fisher of the mill pond at Ambrook. The first week of Sowing, after the ice.

First day. The pond is open. Cold. I sat on the bank for an hour with my line in, and nothing bit. Then something did. A pike, as long as my arm, with a face like a cross uncle. I let it go. It stared at me from the water, and then it left.

Second day. Rain. I fished under the willow. Four trout and one boot. The boot was a left one. I put it on the post by the mill with a note: found. By the evening it was gone.

Third day. The school came down to the pond for their lesson. The teacher said the children were to count the frogs. They counted eighty-one, and then a boy said there was one more under his hat. Eighty-two. I think he was lying, but it was a good lie.

Fourth day. The play was rehearsed in the mill barn. It is a play about a fish who wants to be a bird. The children do the fish and the bird and a cat. I was asked to lend my net. I lent it. A small girl wore it as a cloak and was very pleased.

Fifth day. The play was acted at the market, on a cart. The fish was a boy called Wick. The bird was a girl called Lark. The cat was a smaller girl who forgot every line and so said "meow" and got the biggest laugh. At the end, the fish jumped into the pond, which is to say into a tub, and the whole market clapped. Wick came out wet and glad. His mother cried. I had a tear in my own eye, which I blame on the wind.

Sixth day. Back to the pond. Seven trout. One was big enough to talk to. I did not.

Seventh day. Market. I sold all seven. Jory gave me an apple cake, because she says I lent her the net. I did not. It was the girl. But I ate the cake.

Eighth day. The heron is back. We stood together. Still nothing said.
%%
Gwen, charcoal log, Reaping
Reaping, the 3rd. Wind from the south, warm for the time of year. The charcoal pit is lit on the far slope. Forty sacks of wood are under the earth, and the smoke goes up in a thin grey line that the whole valley can see.

Reaping, the 5th. Pit turned. The smoke is white now, which is what we want. Stir the top with a long pole, and do not stand downwind. Bram stood downwind. His eyebrows are a different color now.

Reaping, the 9th. Pit done. Thirty-two sacks of charcoal, black and light, and it rings when you drop a piece on the stone. Sell the ringing ones first. The forge buys them all.
%%
Letter: Lorn to the archive at Vell
Summer of 12.
To the clerks at the archive, from Lorn the well digger, at the edge of Vell.
I am told the archive keeps everything written. So I am writing this. I am not a writing man. My boy Fen is teaching me. He is nine and he is better at it than me. Do not tell him I said so.
I dig wells. I have dug eleven this year. The one at the edge of Vell is the best. Six rings and the water came in cold and quick. The family there is called the Tarns. They have hens and a boy and a good door.
The road past my house is busy now. Carts go by all day. There is a waymark at the corner and people leave little stones on it. I do not know why. My father did it. I do it. Fen does it. It is a good thing to do on a road.
People say a relay tower is coming. Not here, they say, but on the hill between us and Three Wells. Somebody will keep it. Somebody will climb it every night with a lamp. I would like to see that. I have never seen the top of a thing that tall. I have been at the bottom of a well and looked up, and that is the same, I think, only the other way.
I have no more news. The summer is good. The water is cold. The hens are safe behind the fence, so far.
Lorn, well digger.
%%
Northpoint spring log, Perrin
The house at the edge of Northpoint leaks by the east window when snow melts. I set a bowl under it and went to cut stone.

On the sixth of Blossom, Lantern brought word from the relay house. The tutor had sent the big mind a page it had not seen before. It read the page one letter at a time through its window, about sixty letters wide. It used earlier letters that mattered and guessed the next. The tutor called this the reading drill.

The mind had been trained on many pages from the Archive at Vell. It had not been given the right next letter for every new page. It learned by making guesses and changing its weights when the guesses were wrong. The tutor says the old sorting lesson is still there, only now the mind has many layers and a long stream through them.

I asked Lantern whether the mind understood my name. Lantern did not answer. It carried my question to the keeper, who wrote back that the mind had seen my name in a stone order. That proves little, she said.

At supper, my sister bet that the mind could finish a letter from the first line. I bet it would put a word about snow in it. She won. The answer spoke of the south lamp and a cracked pane. There was no snow in it at all.

I owe her a honey cake. She says she will collect it after the next thaw.
%%
Gwen, the old map, Apple
Apple, the 11th. Found in the back of the mine office, behind a box of tally sticks: an old map, drawn on a board of oak. It shows the Deep Works as it was before anyone counted carts. The levels are marked with little squares, and the squares are named in a hand I do not know.

Apple, the 12th. The map has three gates on the fourth level that are not on the count board. Ola says they are the old tunnels, the ones walled up when the first copper ran out. Old Fenn says they are not walled, they are only forgotten, and a forgotten tunnel is the one you find by accident.

Apple, the 14th. Took the map to the forge, to ask Ivo what the squares meant. Ivo held it to the fire and looked at the back. There was writing there, very small. It says: the amethyst is past the third turn, do not go alone. Ivo says that is good advice and asks who wrote it.

Apple, the 16th. Nobody knows who wrote it. The map is old, the board is oak, and the hand is careful. Hesk says the map should go to the Archive at Vell, because that is where old things go. Ivo says the Archive has enough old things. Hesk says the Archive has no tunnels, and then went to look at the map again, twice.

Apple, the 18th. Copied the map for the count board, by hand, in charcoal, which smudges. The copy is not as good. The squares are in the right places. The names are not, because I cannot read the hand well enough, and I have guessed at three of them. I have written my guesses in the margin, so they can be found and corrected later, by someone better at reading.

Apple, the 21st. Hesk asked to see the copy. He looked at my guesses for a long time, then rubbed out one of them and wrote the right name over it. He did not say what it was. He said we would go down the third turn on the next day shift, with a rope, and two lanterns, and two people, which is not alone.

Ossa's book, the fox at the hens
Kept by Ossa, candle maker, Vell, in the second winter. This is the record of the fox, who has become a neighbour. The record is longer than it needs to be, because Ossa's neighbour Hild asked her to write it down.

The fox came first in Thaw. It sat on the wall of the hen house in the early morning and watched the hens come out of the door one by one. It did not come down. It did not need to. Every hen that walked past the wall was counted by the fox, and every hen knew it.

In Swarm the fox began to come down. It took one hen in the middle of the day, when the yard was quiet and everyone was at the mill. Hild shouted from the kitchen window. The fox sat by the gate with the hen in its mouth and looked at her, and then it walked away very slowly, the way a cart goes out of a yard.

Hild said it was a very rude fox. Ossa said it was only hungry. Ada's boy said it was a clever fox, because it knew which hens were the big ones, and which were the ones that made the most noise.

In Apple the hens began to sleep in the top loft, behind a door with a wooden latch. The fox could not get in. It sat on the roof for a week, and then went down to the river and caught a fish, and came back to the wall and looked at the hens again.

In Frost the fox stopped coming. Hild said it had gone to the hills. Ossa said it had gone to the mill, where the grain was easier. Ada's boy said he saw it on the Long Road with a coin in its mouth, and nobody believed him, but he drew it on the wall anyway.

Two hens are left out of the nine. Hild says it is a fair count. Ossa says it is a poor count, and she has written it down.
%%
Tutor's note: pieces of words
Autumn, late years. From the tutor house at Vell.
For the young tutors, and for anyone who asks how the big minds read now.
For a long time our reading minds took a page one letter at a time. The window was about sixty letters wide. That is not much. Sixty letters is a dozen words. When the mind guessed the next letter it could only look back over those few words, and the rest of the page was dark to it.
Now the big minds read in pieces of words. A piece is a bit of a word you see again and again: "ing", "un", "field", "torch", "apprentice", "the". We cut a page into pieces, not letters. Common pieces are short. Rare words are cut into several pieces.
Why does that help? Because a piece holds more than a letter. A window sixty pieces wide holds far more writing than a window sixty letters wide. It is the same window and the same mind. It just fits more in.
Think of it as packing a cart. If every thing you carry is one apple, you make many trips. If you pack the apples in a crate, one crate holds a hundred. The pieces are the crates. The letters are the apples.
You can see the shape of it. "The keeper lit the lamp" is nineteen letters. It is five pieces, or so. The mind sees the whole line at once now, and the words around it, and the meaning sits closer to hand.
I did not find this. It came from the halls one by one, and the texts disagree on who was first. Some say it was a tutor at Three Wells, some say at Vell. I only know that when we first tried it the mind was better on the very first day, before any drilling, because the window was fuller.
A caution for the drill room. The pieces must be cut the same way in every page, or the mind learns two languages. We keep one list of pieces, and every clerk cuts to it, and we test on pages no mind was drilled on. Never send an untested mind to the fields.
Isla would have liked this. She drew the curve for a bigger crystal, and this is the same idea with fewer marks. More in the same space.
%%
Arlo's log, the loose plank
They are building a bridge over the stream at the bottom of my field. It will be stone. I am glad. Last Thaw the water took the old planks and I lost a bucket.
The men bring cobblestone and deep stone up the road in carts. They leave it in a heap by my gate and I let my horses drink from their water barrel. One of them has a coat the color of beetroot and tells jokes all day. I do not remember his name. I call him the beetroot man.
Pell came by again and bought Sorrel. Pell says prices are good on the Long Road now, with the inns a day apart. The Brown Hen is a day east, the Halfway Inn a day west. Pell says the Halfway Inn has a riddle board by the hearth. I could not guess the riddle. Pell would not tell me the answer. Pell says that is the point.
On the sixth of Blossom a creeper came out of the dark by my stable wall. I did not see it until it was close. It made that small sound, the one that means run, and I ran.
The wall is broken. A whole corner of it is gone, and there is a hole in the roof you could drop a bucket through. Bramble was in the field and is fine. Tansy was frightened and stood by me for a long time.
I keep the torches lit now. All round the stable, a torch every few steps. It is plain sense. I knew it before. I know it better now.
I have laid the stones for a new wall. It will be cobblestone, thick and low. I will put the roof back with oak. The jar of honey is still on the shelf. I am glad of that.

Ivo's autumn book, 188
Kept by Ivo at the north well, Three Wells, from Reaping to Leaffall. Forty-seven years on the stool.
Reaping. The wheat was good and the eggs were many. Piper's boy Sim is six and has started at the school. He came to the stall on his first day with ink to the elbow. I asked what he learned. He said "A." Just that. It was a good A, he said.
Apple. The fair. We counted 600 people, and I think another 200 were hens. A pumpkin came in on two carts, with a boy sitting on top to keep it from rolling. I sold 200 eggs and 25 pairs of socks, and I sat on my stool until the stars came out. Something has to give. It was my back.
Lost children at the fair: 11. All found by the dusk bell. The north well worked as it should. Lost parents: 3. They were at the pie tent. They had not lost the children. They had lost each other.
Leaffall. The first frost came on the 11th. The north well had a skin of ice. A man tested it with his boot, and then with his other boot, and then went home to change his socks. I sold him a pair. Cave cheese is a thing now. People say it is the best on the Long Road. The cheesemaker says it is the cave. The cave says nothing. I bought a piece and it was very good.
Signs of a hard winter, by my count. The foxes came down from the hills early. The cat sleeps with her back to the fire. My hens lost their feathers a month before they should, and walk about looking offended. I have knitted extra socks. If the winter is mild I will say I knew it was a joke.
A clerk from Vell came by on market day, with a satchel and a very clean collar. He asked what I would put in the Archive, if I could put one thing. I said my stool. He wrote it down. So my stool is in the Archive now, in a way, which is more than my husband can say.
What I know about a market, after forty-seven years:
1. Come early. The best customers want eggs before they want anything else.
2. Sit near the well. Water, shade, and a place for lost children to wait.
3. Count the change twice. Not because people cheat. Because people dream.
4. Say good morning to everyone. Even the man who never buys. He might one day.
5. Keep a spare pair of socks in the basket. A cold foot is a sale.
6. Never argue with the market board. Smile and fix the loaf.
7. Go home when the noon bell rings. If it does not ring, there is probably a swarm.
Eggs this autumn: 1650. Socks: 74 pairs. Lost children: 31, all found. Lost parents: 7. Lost hats: 18. Kept hats: 2, in the basket, in case.
The stool still leans. I have grown fond of the lean.
%%
Mott, the long Blossom, a month of days
Written by Mott, at the table by the window, with the candle at the end.
Blossom, eighth day. Rain all morning. The school roof dripped on the third row, so we moved. Hal said the drip was following him. Miss Jenna said it was following the slates.
Blossom, tenth day. Father went to the north well with the other men to clean the stones. The water was clear, and cold, and very quiet. He says the quiet is the strangest thing about the north well now. He says the birds are not in the reeds the way they were.
Blossom, twelfth day. A cart came through with a letter for the mayor, sealed with wax. Mayor Dace read it in the square and then put it in his pocket. He did not tell anyone what it said. Mrs. Lark (the baker's wife) said it was about the fair. Hal said it was about the north. Ada said it was about nothing. Ada was not sure about that either.
Blossom, fourteenth day. The swallows are back. Hal says so, and then he says he saw one, and then he says it was a bird that looked like a swallow. The bird is still here. We have decided it is a swallow.
Blossom, fifteenth day. Mother read the paper from Saltreach aloud at supper. It said the new ships were doing well and the families who had gone were doing well too. It did not say where. Father put down his spoon. Mother turned the page. We ate the rest of the stew.
Blossom, seventeenth day. The south well is low again. Men with buckets at dawn and at dusk, all in a line. Father says it always comes back. He said it twice, so I think he was telling himself.
Blossom, nineteenth day. Hal's mother came to the school with a basket of rolls for the class. They were still warm. She said she had made them at four in the morning, because she could not sleep, and the bread did not mind the hour. We ate every one.
Blossom, twenty-first day. Stone from the north road came into town on the cart. The men are building a new wall round the east well, cobblestone and deep stone, with the top row stuck out one block against the spiders. Miss Jenna says that is a good wall. Ada says it looks like a row of teeth.
Blossom, twenty-fourth day. Father sold the spare cart wheel to Uncle Ned, back, for one emerald, so that the debt would be the right way round. Uncle Ned laughed for a long time. They were friends again before the wheel was on the cart.
Blossom, twenty-sixth day. Lantern day in the next town, and a bigger crowd. Some people from the north road came in and stood at the back of the square. They did not sing. They looked at the sky a while, and then went to the inn.
Blossom, twenty-eighth day. The swallow is still here. Hal says it has a nest, under the eaves of the bakery. Mrs. Lark says she has no nest, but she has a bird that will not leave her bread alone.
Blossom, last day. I wrote this all in one go, by the candle, and my hand is tired. Father says that is what a long month is for.
%%
Dara, festival week
Kept by Dara, beekeeper, from the market at Ambrook, through the apple festival at the end of autumn. Her stall stood by the well. She wrote each night by a candle. Her handwriting got worse as the week went on.

First day. The festival began at dawn with the ringing of the market bell. I set out forty jars of honey, twelve candles and a plate of honey cakes, for tasting. The apple sellers set up on the north side. The square filled up by noon. Sold eighteen jars.

Second day. The cider tent opened. There is a line round the well. I sold twenty-three jars and ran out of cakes. Baked more in the evening. Smelled of honey until bed.

Third day. The parade. Children dressed as bees walked from the mill to the square. A small boy dressed as a queen was carried on a chair by four others. He did not smile. It was a very serious bee. I clapped.

Fourth day. The apple race. People ran with apples on spoons. The winner was Rook, the orchard keeper, who said he had done it on purpose. He gave his prize, a ribbon, to the girl who came second. She cried. She had never won anything.

Fifth day. A mended roof. The market roof, over the east stalls, was finally mended by the council. It rained in the afternoon and the east stalls were dry. A man at the cheese stall took off his hat and bowed to it.

Sixth day. The last day of the festival. We sang the apple song. All forty verses. At verse thirty, the children fell asleep. At verse thirty-eight, the old men did. I sang to the end with the baker and a goat.

Seventh day. Packed up. Sold three hundred and twelve jars over the week. More than any year. My arm is sore. My wallet is full. The bees are quiet.

I am going to bed, and I am going to sleep until the first frost.
%%
Fen's journal, the riddle board
Sowing, the twentieth day. The Halfway Inn. Rain for three days, and nobody is going anywhere.

There are eleven of us by the fire. A carter and his wife. A family from Lowmarsh with a frog in a jar. A young clerk going home to Vell. Pell, with his hat. And me, with a torn net across my knees, mending it for the sake of my hands.

Hobb keeps a riddle board by the hearth. Answer one, and your soup is free. This morning the board said:
What gets bigger the more you take away?

The clerk said a debt. Hobb said no. The carter said a shadow. Hobb said no. Pell said an appetite and got the third bowl anyway. I looked at the hole in my net.

A hole, I said.

Hobb crossed it off and wrote me a free bowl. He said it was cheating, because I am a net mender, and I spend my life with holes. I said I had paid for it with forty years.

So I wrote a riddle for the board myself. It said:
What is full of holes, and yet holds all the fish?

It took the carter an hour and a bowl of soup to get it. Hobb pinned it under the first one. He says it will cost him a lot of soup.

The rain has not stopped. I have mended the net. Now I will mend the carter's coat.
%%
Lise, inn book: the loom, Thaw
Kept by Lise, weaver, at the Green Gate inn on the Long Road. The loom is in the back room, next to the bread oven, which is the warmest room in the inn and the loudest.

Thaw, first day. The spring wool came up from Kettle Ridge, forty bundles, all of it greasy and proud. I soaked it three times. The water went grey, then brown, then the colour of weak cider. That is the wool telling you what it carried.

Thaw, third day. A string broke in the middle of the warp, with a sound like a bow string. I mended it with a knot a weaver would be ashamed of, then unpicked it and tied it properly, the way my aunt taught me. A good knot is one nobody notices.

Thaw, seventh day. A small bird came in through the shutter and sat on the cross bar, watching the shuttle go back and forth. I did not stop. It flew out when the bread went in.

Thaw, tenth day. The lamp on the tower above the inn was seen at dusk, and it was wrong. The blinks came out short and broken. Ash says the glass has a crack. I have not climbed the tower to see, because I have a warp to finish.
%%
Cade, log: the broken lamp on the hill
Day twelve. The relay lamp on the hill above us has gone dark. A hailstorm came in from the west in the night and cracked the glass in the lamp room. The keeper, Ota, came down at dawn with her hands full of melting ice and a face like a bad pear. She said the lamp had not been dark for a day and a night since the glass was set, and she did not like it at all.

The relay carries the messages for the Long Road. Pell's order for the Ambrook cart is sitting up there. Two letters for the Brown Hen are waiting in the box at the foot of the stair. The clerk's count of the carts from Vell is at the top, in the dark, where nobody can read it.

Day thirteen. I went up with Ota and her boy. The lamp room is glass on every side, with a walk round the outside and a copper ring at the top. Three panes were cracked, and the wind came through the gaps and made the whole room sing. It is not the sound a lamp room is meant to make.

We set a new pane. Ota keeps spare glass in a box, wrapped in straw, because she has been burned before. The boy held the pane, I held the lantern, and Ota set the putty with her thumb as if she were making a pie. It took the whole morning, and I got putty in my beard.

Day fourteen. The lamp is lit. It blinked at dusk, the way it should, and the messages began to climb the stair again. Ota says it will be a week before the clerks at Vell are happy, and she is right. They sent a rider up the road to ask what had happened. She sent back one word: hail.

Cade's notes on the glass. Do not stand under the lamp room in a storm. Do not climb with wet hands. Do not hurry the putty, because a putty that is hurried will let the wind in by the next hail, and then you will be doing it all again, with the rider waiting.
%%
Sorrel, a wedding by the lamp
Written by Sorrel, who sold the eggs for the cake, and who was asked to write the wedding down properly, because the last one was not written at all. Spring.

Oda Marsh and Jem Holloway were married at the foot of the relay tower, on the first fine day of Blossom. The relay house door was propped open with a barrel, and the stand with the big mind stood in the middle of the floor, looking, as the wedding guests said, like an extra guest with its own chair.

Jem's brother read the vows, which were short, because Jem did not want to be out in the wind for long. Oda cried, and then laughed, and then cried again, and the mayor said that was the proper order.

At the feast I asked the keeper if the mind wanted to say anything. The keeper said it could be asked a question, and it would answer in writing. So I wrote on the slate: "What do you wish for Oda and Jem?" and the post boy took it down the line.

The answer came back by the next cart. It said: "I do not know what a wedding is for, except that people are glad of one. I wish them a long life, a good harvest, and a lamp that stays lit. I cannot promise any of those. I can wish them."

The guests cheered that, and the mind was given the first slice of the cake, which the keeper says it cannot eat, and which was eaten by the keeper.

The lamp was lit at the end, and the guests stood in the yard to watch it. The light went out over the hill and down the line to Copperhold, and then to Ambrook, and then somewhere further, where I could not see. Oda held Jem's hand and said the light looked like a promise. Jem said it looked like a lamp. Both are right.
%%
Lorn, ferry keeper, autumn log
Leaffall, 188. Lorn, who keeps the ferry on the river below Three Wells and lives in the house at the edge of town, between ferry days.
First of Leaffall. Low water, brown leaves on the river. Crossed eleven times. Six sheep, a cart of beets, two travelers who argued about the price. Paid in apples. Ate four.
Third. Rain. The ferry rope stretched. Pulled it tight. Hands sore.
Sixth. Bel from the school came to the house and asked if the ferry could be in the play. The school is doing the play of the Fox and the Bridge. She said the ferry would be the river. I said the river does not talk. She said in a play it does. I said all right. I am to stand at the back and be a river. I have no lines.
Ninth. Practised standing. A river is harder than a ferry.
Twelfth. Song contest at the east well, the day before the play. Hollis the weaver sang about a goose. It was very long. It won. Nobody was surprised.
Fifteenth. The play. The fox was a girl in a fur coat that was a cat's. The bridge was two boys under a blanket. The river was me, in a blue sheet. A boy held the corner. I rippled. The crowd clapped. The fox forgot her lines and made up new ones. They were better.
Eighteenth. Crossed nine times. A child waved at me from the bank and called me "the river". I waved back.
%%
A song contest at Kettle Ridge, autumn
The fair came to the ridge in Apple, and with it the song contest. Folk came from Three Wells and Ambrook and up the Long Road. Tam judged, because he has the loudest voice and cannot be argued with.
A boy from Ambrook sang about the bees, and everyone clapped. A woman from Lowmarsh sang about slow water, and it was very sad and very good. Then Pip, Tam's apprentice, sang a song about the tutors and their curves. It went:

  Isla drew a line one day,
  and set it on the board.
  It showed how far a mind would grow
  if it were fed and stored.
  A bigger crystal, longer days,
  the curve goes up and bends.
  You cannot buy a mind, she said,
  you only feed and wait, my friends.

Everyone laughed, but they clapped too. Pip had learned it from Oak, who had it from Isla's letter.
Then a miner from Copperhold, Hesk, stood up and said he had found a new copper vein, deep in the seventh level, thick as his arm. The fair cheered. Tam said that was not a song. Hesk said it was music enough. And the crowd sang the curve song again, all the way through, while the windmills squeaked on the ridge.
%%
Lenn's summer log, honey and frogs
Longday, 202. The longest day. The sun stayed up late and the pond went gold. I did not go to bed until the frogs did, and the frogs never do.
The hives on the hill gave honey. Jory took the first comb and the bees were cross about it. He gave me a jar for the fish I bring him. Fair.
There is a big frog that sits on the same stone every evening. I have named it Post. Post watches me clean the fish and does not blink. If I drop a bit, Post is faster than a cat.
The inn had a fish pie for the long day. Everyone came. Sefa sang, which is a kind of weather all its own. Brin danced with the baker and stepped on her foot. Good night, all of it.
%%
Cade, log of the crystal, year 110
Day four. The tutor showed me how. Set an apple on the cloth. Ask the crystal: apple, or pear? It answers with a light on its side, one light for apple and two for pear. If the light is wrong, move the marks a little, and then set the next fruit.

The tutor calls the marks weights. I call them its thoughts, and she let me.

Day five. Ten apples, ten pears. It got six right, and it seemed pleased with itself, because the light stayed bright for a long while.

The tutor said: now move the weights, each one, a little toward the answer it should have given. Not much. A little. Every wrong fruit gets the same small push, and the push is always toward the right fruit. A big push makes it swing from apple to pear and back again, like a gate in a gale. I had been told that already by my bees, so I did not argue.

Day six. Fourteen right of twenty. Day seven, sixteen of twenty. Day eight, eighteen of twenty. Each morning the tutor came down the cellar stair with a fresh basket, and each morning she was pleased, and each morning she said the same thing. It did not learn apples. It learned what makes an apple different from a pear, by being wrong about each one.

Day nine. The test. The tutor brought apples from the inn's own orchard, which the crystal had never seen, twenty of them. And twenty pears from the old tree in the ditch, which it had never seen either. It got seventeen right. Greta said it was a good crystal. The tutor said it was a good crystal for apples and pears, and that she would not trust it with plums, which it had also never seen, and which is the next thing she plans to try.

Una of Ambrook invented this way of drilling, long ago. Her name is the first one in the tutors' books, and so is her step. A small push, toward the right answer, every time.

Cade

Fallon, hamlet stone log
Spring of 181, Thaw.
The frost came out of the ground this week. The lane is mud from our door to the road. I laid flat stones where the carts turn. Old Tavin walked over them, then walked back to test them. He said they were sound. He said this twice, as if the stones might hear.

The old map from the shed is still useful. It marks a spring under the east hill. We dug there with a spade and found water before noon. We filled the new trough. The sheep came first, then the pigs, then Tavin with his cup.

The map also marks a wall that no one remembers. I found its top row under the grass. The stones are small and square. I will mend the broken end before the weeds take it again.

Sixth of Sowing.
My apprentice is a boy called Rell. He carries chips to the pile and counts them badly. He is better at counting when the pile is small.

I cut two lintels for the store. Rell brought the wrong one to the door. He stood there with it and waited. I pointed to the mark on the right lintel. He brought that one next. We kept the first for the shed.

The hens laid six eggs today. Tavin says the fox has gone. The fox has not gone. I saw its tail by the trough.
%%
Lark, bread for the tower, winter
Longnight, the first. The bread goes up the hill every morning on the sledge, and comes back down with the empty baskets and the keeper's list. The list is long. It has the names of the children who will not eat the crust, and the names of the men who will eat anything, and a note at the bottom about the melon, which the keeper has been told is not his to comment on.

Longnight, the sixth. Ice on the hill road this morning. The sledge slid sideways at the second bend, and the bread went into the snow, and the boy and I went after it on our knees. We found every loaf but one. The one we did not find was taken by a fox, who sat on the wall and ate it and looked at us as if we had charged him for it.

Longnight, the ninth. Made the tower a sweet loaf for the first time since the cold came in. Honey and dried apples, and a little salt for the sweetness, which is what my grandmother did. The keeper's boy came down for it at dusk, very quiet, with his cap in both hands. He said the keeper had eaten half of it at once and then gone up the tower to think.

Longnight, the eleventh. The mill is still iced over. The flour is almost gone. We have been using barley and bran and the last of the winter wheat, and the bread is grey and dense and good. The children have got used to it. Some of the grown people have not, and they say so at the oven, which is their right.

Longnight, the fourteenth. A letter came with the post. It was from the keeper of the tower, written in a clear hand: "The bread is very good in the cold. Thank you. Tell the baker the stone is better than the stone was." I do not know what he meant by that. I think it was a joke. He does not make many.

Longnight, the eighteenth. Four days of deep frost. The ovens have been lit day and night, and the hamlet smells of bread from one end to the other, which is the best smell in the world and the most useful. The sledge runs straight now. The ice has packed down hard enough to hold a loaf.
%%
The carding shed, by Rill
This is our shed and this is what is in it.
Two big carding boards. They have little teeth. You must not put your hand in the teeth. I did once. It was bad.
A bag of wool. A bag of wool. A third bag of wool. Wool everywhere.
A stool. My aunt Fern sits on it.
A cup with a chip. A second cup with a chip.
A string with beads, for counting the skeins.
A picture of a sheep I drew. It is a very good sheep.
A spider in the corner. We leave it. It keeps the flies off.
A bucket of water for the shed cat. The cat is not ours. It comes and goes.
That is all. Do not take the string with the beads.

Pell's ledger, the Halfway Inn
Fourth of Longday. Set out from Ambrook with six jars of honey and a crate of apples. The honey jars ride well in straw. Sold two jars at the Brown Hen for a good price. The innkeeper there has a cat that sleeps on the coin box. It does not move for anyone.
Sixth of Longday. Reached the Halfway Inn. The hearth is still lit. Twenty years now, they say, and the fire has never gone out. There is a riddle board by the door. Tonight the riddle was: what has a bed but never sleeps? A child of the inn answered before I had set down my pack. A river. She got a copper for it and I got my supper.
Eighth of Longday. Rain all morning. I mended my coat by the window and listened to two carters argue about the price of wool. They argued for an hour and then shared a pie. That is the road for you.
Eleventh of Longday. Sold the last apples at Three Wells market. Bought a new strap for my pack and a horn cup. Home before Leaffall, if the roads hold.

Quill, flock log: Thaw, the lambs
Kept by Quill, shepherd, at Ferris End, a short walk above Lowmarsh. Each day the sheep are counted, the weather is written, and anything that went wrong is written under it.

Thaw, first day. Counted the flock: sixty-one sheep, fourteen lambs. Two of the lambs were not lambs at all, but a ewe's idea of one. Moved the ram to the far field. He was not pleased, and he said so all the way.

Thaw, third day. Wind from the west. Fed the flock hay. Muffin, the ewe with the black face, has had twins, and is telling everybody, including the dog.

Thaw, fifth day. The stream at the foot of the field is up. Moved the flock to the top pasture. Two lambs wanted to swim. Pulled one back by the wool, and the other followed out of spite.

Thaw, seventh day. Juno came up with the cheese cart and asked how many sheep. I said sixty-one. She said sixty. We counted three times, and it was sixty-one. Juno said, "That is what I said," and bought a cheese anyway.

Carys, a birth at the market
Northpoint, year 161. Longnight.

The wind has been in the north for four days. The stalls are closed. The market square is nothing but snow and a bell that has frozen to its rope.

This morning the baker's wife, who lives over the shop, went into her pains. The midwife was at the ice camp. The nearest healer was a day away. So the baker banged on my door with a floury fist and said, "Carys, you were a soldier, you know what to do."

I said I knew what to do with a spear. I did not know what to do with a baby.

But I went. I held the lamp. The baker's sister did the work. At the fifth hour there was a girl, loud and red, with a fine set of lungs. She looked at me and I looked at her, and for a moment neither of us knew what to say.

The baker cried. The wife laughed. I went home through the snow and ate cold porridge standing up. Then I sat down, and my hands began to shake, and I let them.

I do not know why a thing like that should frighten a person more than a battle. I suppose a battle is only danger. A baby is hope. Hope is heavier.

They are calling her Flint. It is a good name for a north child.

Kester, spring forge log
Third of Blossom, year 19.
The stream has gone high. It covers the old path by the marsh, so folk walk by the reeds and get mud to their knees.
I made a new hook for the fish weir. Wren came from Lowmarsh to fetch it. She brought a loaf and a jar of honey. The loaf was warm, and I ate half before I remembered my manners.
A boy from the hamlet carried the other half home. He dropped it once. The mud took only the crust.
I sharpened two spades after that. The new road is still only a talk at the inn. We need stone, carts and many hands. I have put aside a good iron wedge.
