Gwen, autumn at the market, all of it
Written through the autumn, in the order it happened, for the charcoal book and for my sister, who asked why I write so much.

Reaping, the 15th. Pumpkins came in from Ambrook, and Ama's were the best. The mayor of Three Wells came to buy one, and was told the price was high, and paid it anyway, out of pride.

Reaping, the 24th. The charcoal sold out by the second bell. Forty sacks went to the forge and the kitchen, and the forge paid in copper, which Hesk says is worth more than emeralds in a cold week. I think a warm week is worth more than either.

Apple, the 2nd. A cart from the Long Road broke its axle at the foot of the market hill. The carter sat on the wheel and ate an apple, very slowly, while the whole square came to look. Nobody offered to help him. Everybody offered advice. The apple was finished before the axle was.

Apple, the 8th. Ilse is married in all but the signing. She says the vows were the easy part, and the chairs were hard. Orrin says the chairs were only soft at the top. The bride says he is no good at chairs.

Apple, the 11th. The old map. I will not write about it again until I know more. I have promised Hesk.

Apple, the 15th. A fox came into the market and took a pie from the kitchen step. It did not run. It walked away with the pie in its mouth, past the stalls, and up the hill toward the mine gate, where the kitchen boy was waiting with a ladle. The boy put down the ladle and laughed until he had to sit.

Leaffall, the 1st. The first cold morning. Frost on the cart rails, and the miners' breath hanging in the lift. The count board says copper is up, iron is steady, coal is high, and the lift has been greased twice by the boy who is not allowed to touch the lift.

Leaffall, the 14th. A letter came down the Long Road from Mira at Northpoint, for Hesk. He read it at the forge with his back to us. He read it twice. Then he folded it, and put it in his coat, and said that Northpoint is still too far away to be cold, and that the coal is going north, and that is all.

Leaffall, the 20th. The last market of the autumn. Charcoal sold, pies sold, the wedding soup finally eaten, and the map back in its box behind the tally sticks, where it belongs. Hesk says we go down tomorrow. He says it quietly, as if he is not sure he wants it to be true.
%%
Lorn, between the play and the contest
Leaffall, 192. Lorn, the ferry keeper's notes. Day by day, in the house at the edge of Three Wells, with the shutters closed against the wind.
Fourth. Wind from the north. Crossings cancelled after noon. The ferry bucks like a calf.
Fifth. A school child, Bel, arrived at my door with a blue sheet. The play, she said, wanted a river again. I said the river had retired. She said the river had not been asked. I said I would think. She waited. She is persistent. She is nine. I said yes.
Sixth. Rehearsal at the school. A boy plays a fox. A girl plays a bridge. A third is the wind and blows through a paper tube. I am the river. This year I am allowed to move. I have been told to flow from left to right. I have never flowed. I find it harder than a crossing.
Eighth. Song contest rehearsal. Hollis is practising the goose again. The goose has a verse about me this year. I did not ask. It is not very kind. It is true.
Eleventh. The play. A fine night. The wind blew in earnest. A shutter banged, and the audience thought it was the effect. The fox said, "Yes, thank you, wind," and everyone laughed.
Twelfth. The contest. The goose lost to a song about a wheelbarrow, sung by three old men. It had a very good chorus. I joined in the chorus. I knew it by the second time.
Fifteenth. Ferry again. Calm. A man asked if I would be the river again next year. I said ask the river.
Sixteenth. Wrote this. Went to bed. The shutters held.

Hob, relay log, Blossom
Little relay, the hill between Vell and Three Wells. One lamp, one keeper. Logged by Hob.

Day 1. Wind from the south. Warm. Lamp cleaned, glass good, copper ring polished. Passed 14 messages, all from Vell, all for Three Wells. The clerks write very small when they are tired.

Day 2. Rain at noon. Stopped at dusk. Walked the stair; 60 rungs, all sound. One rung loose at the top. Fixed it with a peg and a bit of wax. Peg is the boy from the mill. He says it is his peg. I said it is my wax.

Day 3. A message for Mira at Northpoint, very long. I blinked it down the line for an hour. Mira's answer came back the same night, very short. It said: "Long one. You are a saint." I said I am only a keeper. I did not blink that bit.

Day 4. Sun all day. Torches out at dawn, as always. Fed the cat. The cat is named Lamp, because she sits by the lamp. She sits on the warm step where the copper ring is, and nobody can move her. She is not a keeper. I am not sure what she is.

Day 5. Passed a note from Dace about the spring fair. Passed a note from Dace about the notice board. Passed a note from Dace that said only "Hob, is it done?" I blinked back "Yes." It was not done. It is done now.

Day 6. Visitors. Two carters on the hill road, who wanted to know the way to the Halfway Inn. I counted the waymarks for them, out loud, from the top of the tower. They said that was very helpful, and went off counting on their fingers.

Bel's essay: how we keep the bees
Spring of 206.
My essay is about bees. Teacher said keep it short and true. So here it is.

We have four hives now by the flowers. Three old ones and one new one from the swarm on the plum tree. A hive is a box of wood with frames inside. The bees build their comb on the frames. We take the honey out in Swarm and again in Reaping, and we leave them enough to eat in winter.

The bees have a queen. The queen is bigger than the rest. There is only one. The rest are workers and they work all day. Some are drones. The drones do not do much. I do not know why they are there. Nobody has told me.

When a hive gets too full the bees swarm. They leave the hive in a big brown humming ball and hang on a branch. Father puts on the veil and knocks them into a box and carries them to a new hive. I hold the box. One time Father got stung on the ear and said a word I will not write. One time I got stung on the thumb and I did not say a word, because Teacher is reading this.

We take the honey in jars with cloth on top. I sold six at the market and kept one for Mother. Honey is up on the price board. Pell says never trust the price board on a fair day. Pell is not a beekeeper.

We also have three hives by the flowers and the bees go to the plum tree and the flowers and they come back. If they did not come back we would have no apples and no honey and no bees. So we keep the flowers.

That is my essay. It is short and true. I hope it is good. I would like to know how bees were kept in the early years. I asked the archive. I will write again when Oren writes back.

The old stories say the first hives were hollow logs. I do not know if that is true. I do not know if it is not.
Bel, the farm east of Three Wells.
%%
Pell's road journal, autumn fair
Autumn of 214. The Long Road, between Three Wells and Ambrook. Rain two days, then clear.
Bad week for the cart. A wheel cracked at the ford below the Brown Hen. I sat in the inn yard while a smith from Three Wells put a new rim on it, and I paid too much, and the smith knew I would pay too much. The Brown Hen keeps a good fire and a bad roof.
Good week for news, though. At the fair at Three Wells I sold my wool and bought a fair prize. A little mind in a crystal, a sorting mind, in a green case with a card. I won the contest with it: twenty apples and pears mixed in a basket, and it sorted them all into two heaps with no mistake. The prize was a silver token and a jar of honey, and the honey came from Ambrook, which is where I am going anyway.
Bel, the mayor's child, asked me how the mind sorts. I said the tutors drill it. She said yes, but how. I said I did not know. She said the tutors at Vell write it all down, and she has written to them twice, and only one wrote back, and that was Oren, who is kind.
I told her she should ask a tutor, not a merchant. She said merchants know prices, but she wanted to know how. Fair.
Sold honey at the Brown Hen for a good price. A man there told me a big mind at the relay house read his whole letter aloud to the keeper and made one small mistake, and the keeper said that was very good, and the man did not think so.
Long road ahead. The Halfway Inn tomorrow, if the wheel holds. I will leave a stone on the waymark like everyone else.
%%
Moss, healing notes, the farm
Spring of 179. Moss, at Lowmarsh.
Wren sent me out to the farm again. The cow, Butter, is well now, but the stable hand Ree asked me to look at the old mare. Ree says "she is not sick" about nine times in one visit. I counted. I did not tell Ree I counted.
Nettle the mare is old. Her teeth are worn flat and her back is a little sunk. She coughs when the wind comes off the marsh. It is not the grey cough. It is just an old cough, the kind an old horse gets. I said so. Ree nodded and looked at the ground and then asked if there was anything for it. I said keep her out of the wet wind and give her softer feed and let her rest more. Ree said "she will not rest." I said "then let her not rest, but softer feed."
On the way home I saw the new road with its torch posts. The frogs were loud in the ford. I stopped and listened and got mud on my boots. Wren says the marsh is the best healer we have and the worst patient. I do not know what she means by that. I will ask her.
%%
Ambrook market and tower
Sowing, 14. The market opened under a cold sky.
The apples were small this year, but sweet. Sella sold honey in clay jars. Pell sold salt, wool and two copper tools. He said the tools came from Copperhold. He also said the price was fair. The buyers asked him to say that again, more slowly.
The tower now stands above the mill pond. It is not as tall as the keeper hoped, but the next course of stone is ready. A stair climbs inside. At each turn there is room for a lamp. We have put torches around the foot, because the hill path grows dark early.
The relay house stands beside the tower. Its roof is oak. The old door fits, though the latch must be lifted before it will close. In the house we keep a desk, a stool and a dry box for written messages. The keeper says the desk must face the door. Jory says the desk must face the window, so the keeper can see the market. They have agreed to put it between.
A message came from Three Wells by hand. The road crew there has reached the east well. They ask for glass and more stone. The Vell clerk wrote that the Archive at Vell will keep a copy of each plan. He asked us to send the plan clean, not with market prices on the back.
At noon, the market bell rang for a wedding. Mara and Den came back with their families. They brought bread for everyone and honey for the children. The bride's shawl had a new patch. Den's boots were both muddy this time.
The road beyond the bridge is firm now. A waymark stands at the turn, three blocks high, with a torch on top. Pell says travelers will stop less often to ask where they are. He says this will cost him sales at the inn. Then he asked the road crew to build the inn closer to his stall.
At dusk, a zombie came to the market gate. It had one sleeve left and mud to its waist. The guard rang the bell. We took the children inside the relay house and lit every torch at the gate.
The zombie came through the gate and went straight toward the market lamps. It walked into the fence and stayed there, reaching through the bars. One of the builders knocked it down with a stone tool. We burned the old sleeve in the fire pit and mended the fence before morning.
The market opened again after sunrise. Sella brought fresh honey. Jory turned the mill wheel. The relay lamp is not ready, but the tower can be seen from the road.
I wrote the day's count on a clean sheet. Apples, eight baskets. Honey, eleven jars. Wool, three bundles. One broken fence, mended. One zombie at the gate, gone by dawn. One new tower, still waiting for its lamp.

Marl, a child who ran off, Longday
Written by Marl, who was in the search. Wenna is six. She is Toby's girl, from the bakery at the north gate.

Longday, the eleventh. At the midday bell Wenna was not at the bakery table.

Toby went up the rocks behind the north gate, calling her name. Mira lit the lamp on the tower so the child could see where the tower stood. I went with the rope.

We found her at dusk, asleep under the lamp stand at the foot of the tower, her head on a folded sack. A fox sat near her feet, very still. It did not run. Wenna woke when Toby lifted her, and said she had been looking for the foxes' den to see the little ones. She had found it. The little ones were asleep too.

Mira gave her bread and a long look. Toby cried, and then he was angry, and then he cried again.

Mira's rule, written on the bakery door the next morning: a rope from the door to the bench, and a bell on the end. A rope is cheaper than a search.

Oren's day-book
Winter, at the farm outside Northpoint. Oren was asked by Tam to come and look at the hole in the north wall of the lean-to, where a creeper blew a gap in the autumn, and where the masons found, underneath the stone, a tunnel no one had known was there.

First look. The tunnel goes down under the lean-to, under the yard, and under the wall. It is narrow, about the width of a cart. The walls are cut stone. The steps, where there are steps, are worn in the middle. Tam says he never knew it was there, and that his grandfather never knew either. Pip says it was there first.

Second look. The air in the tunnel is still and dry. A lamp burns steadily in it, with no smoke. I asked the masons to lower a second lamp, to see if it would flicker. It did not. Mira says the tunnel is the same temperature as the stone of the north wall. The stone of the north wall is warm to the hand, even in frost.

Third look. We went down together, Tam first, then Oren, then Pip, very quietly. We found the end of the tunnel, a wall of old stone, with a door in it that is shut. The door has no handle. The stone around it is warm. Pip put its palm flat on the door and stood there. Tam said Pip was to come away. Pip came away.

We did not open the door. I have written this in the book, as it was seen, and I have made no guess about what is behind it. The Archive will decide when the road is clear, and the clerks come up with the proper tools.

Note in the margin. The clerks are to bring a rope, a lamp, and a copy of the survey of this farm. Tam asks that they bring their own tea.
%%
Toby, baker's journal, a tutor
Three Wells market, spring of 207.

A tutor from Vell stood at my stall this morning and ate a sweet roll very slowly. She said she was thinking. I said I hoped it was about the roll.

She told me what she was thinking about. She said the newest work at the tutor house is a mind that reads like a person, one letter at a time. It looks at a window of about sixty letters, and from those letters it guesses the next. Then the next. It does this over and over until it has read a whole page.

I asked her how it knew what to guess. She said it takes from the letters before it the ones that matter. Not all of them the same. If the page says "the baker opened the oven and took out the," then "baker" and "oven" matter more than "the." So the next letter is probably the start of "bread."

I said that was sensible. She said it is, and that it is also strange, because nobody tells the mind which letters matter. It learns that by being wrong, many times.

I gave her a second roll. She said it was a fair trade.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 31. The fifth of Sowing.
Crew: Hesk, Noll, Tern, young Varo.
Level: three.
Count: copper 8 carts, coal 4.
The road crew came up the hill today. They brought stone and two carts of packed earth. The steep part below Copperhold is now cobblestone. It is a fine road, though one stone sticks up and catches a boot.
A relay tower is planned on the ridge beyond Three Wells. The keeper says it will carry messages farther than a runner can go in one day. Tern says he will send a message to ask for a better shovel.
We ate bread by the shaft head. Varo gave half his to a fox. The fox took the bread and left the crust.
The old waymark still points down the hill. I turned it so the arrow points toward the new road.

Fallon, spring work book
Spring of 193, Thaw through Swarm.
The first job was a drain beside the hamlet lane. Snow water had filled it with silt. I cleared it with a short hoe, then set three flat stones where the bank had fallen in. The water runs past now. It does not run into the road. This pleased the cart driver more than it pleased me.

We have a poor harvest from last year, and the seed bin is lower than I like. The wheat heads were thin. The beetroot came up small. We kept the sound roots in sand and ate the split ones first. My sister says a small beetroot tastes the same as a large one. She says this while cutting a very large beetroot.

The old map has a tear across the hill. Fen stitched it with thread from her sleeve. The map shows a short path to the stone shelf, but the path is grown over. I found it under the leaves. It saves a long walk when I bring blocks down to the lane.

At the stone shelf, I cut lintels for a new shed. The rock split cleanly. Rell carried the pieces in a hand cart and sang a tune with no ending. He said he was saving the ending for the last trip. There were nine trips. The ending was still not ready.

In Swarm the bees came out by the south wall. We left the flowers alone and moved the wood pile. Tavin wanted to move the hives. He has no sense around bees. He says they know his face. They know his hat, and they do not like it.

I put a low curb around the spring. The sheep keep pushing mud into the water. They pushed mud over the curb too. A curb is not a fence. We know this now.
%%
Northpoint journal, Longday
Longday, the first. The sun has not gone down since the morning. It only leans on the edge of the lake for an hour, then climbs again, as if it had forgotten something. I have been sleeping with a cloth over my eyes. Lantern does not sleep at all, and says nothing about it.

Longday, the fourth. Washed every blanket in the tower, and hung them on the stair rail to dry. The wind took two of them down to the lake, and the ice camp men fished them out for me, which was kind of them. They asked for a potato for the trouble.

Longday, the sixth. Took the spare boat out for an hour, with Lantern holding the oars. It rowed in a perfectly straight line, out and back, and never once looked at the sky, which is more than I can say for myself.

Longday, the ninth. Tam's bees have sent a jar of honey up with the post. It is very dark, and tastes of clover and smoke. I ate a spoonful by the stove and wrote him a message to say so. He will be insufferable about it.

Lamps lit late, because it is light. Glass clean. Messages passed, and the sun still up.

Tam, house journal, a birth
Autumn. A house at the edge of Kettle Ridge. Written over a long week, in small pieces, between feeds, which is why it is untidy.

First day. A daughter. Born in the small hours, with a lot of noise and a little black hair. Her mother is well. I am not, but I am happy. I have been up all night and I cannot find my boots.
Second day. Mira has sent word from Northpoint. "Well done, Tam. Her aunt will visit when the snow allows." That is the nicest thing she has said to me.
Third day. We have named her Hazel. Pip stood at the cradle all afternoon, quite still. I think it is guarding. I think it is also confused.
Fourth day. The harvest festival begins at Three Wells. I cannot go. Brin will take the beetroot for me. I have told him to win.
Fifth day. News from the fair. Brin has won the second prize for beetroot. Tam of Kettle Ridge is not mentioned, as Tam was not there. I am told that a big mind at the fair solved the riddle at the Halfway Inn by writing out its steps. The innkeeper says it was cheating. The mind says it was thinking.
Sixth day. Hazel is awake. She looks at me as if I am a very strange and interesting beetroot.
Seventh day. Mother has come to stay. She takes one look at Pip and says, "Is it clean?" I say yes. She says, "Clean it again."
Eighth day. Quiet. A good day. The mist is on the ridge. The cat is on the cradle. I let her.

Gate log, Vell market, Deepsnow
Kept by the gate clerk at the square, by the bell. Prices in emeralds. Read them before you come down the hill.

Apples, a basket of forty: 2 emeralds, good ones. Bruised ones, 1 emerald, for the press and the pies.
Honey, a jar from the Ambrook hives: 4 emeralds. Jars from anywhere else: ask first.
Candles, a box of ten: 3 emeralds.
Lamp oil, a jug: 2 emeralds. The copying room buys oil on the first day of each week. Do not wait.
Wool, a fleece: 4 emeralds. Ask the carder about it. She will say three.
Bread, a loaf: 1 emerald. Wick's loaf is the best on the hill. Wick says so.
Pumpkin pie, a slice: 2 emeralds, with honey on top.
Beetroot soup, a bowl: 1 emerald. Hot from the furnace until the bell.

Notices at the gate:
Carts go up the hill after the bell, and down before it. Leave the gate open for the carts going down.
Wanted: a boy to carry the honey jars up the hill. Ask at the bread table. Pay is one bun and a seat by the stove.
The bet about the apples is still open. Quill has sorted six baskets in a row without one mistake. Oren says the seventh will not come. Nessa says he is wrong. Ask them at the gate.
Lost: one key to the copying room door, found by the bell. Ask at the copying room. Oren will want to know which door.
Found: a pair of tongs by the furnace door. Ask Wick. She will say they are not hers.
A relay message from Northpoint, read out at the gate by the relay clerk, for anyone who wants it:
Snow to the sill. Foxes at the door. The lamp is trimmed. Mira.

Ice camp winter log, Runa
Longnight, 176.

Longnight, fifth. The well is dry. I lowered the pail to the bottom and heard it knock against stone. No water came up. We have had this before. I told Mira not to worry. She worries in a neat and useful way, but she still worries.

We fetched clean snow from the bank above the reeds. It takes a long time to melt. Mira set the pot too close to the stove and scorched the bottom. The water was fine. The pot was not pleased.

Longnight, eighth. The north path is packed hard now. My boots have old soles, and they know the way better than I do. Mira carried the pail. Lantern carried the wood. It walks steadily, unless a fox crosses the path. Then it stops and looks after the fox until someone calls it back.

A jar of honey came from Kettle Ridge with the relay bag. Tam wrote that it is from his own hives, though I doubt he has time to look after bees in winter. He must mean the hives near the flowers. I opened the jar for our tea. Mira says we should save it for a cold day. I said the day was cold enough to freeze a spoon.

Longnight, twelfth. The lamp glass was clear at dusk. Snow came later and covered the tower roof. I climbed to the lamp room and brushed the snow from the walk. Mira stayed below and called each stair number to me. She knows them all. She did not call the same number twice, even when the wind made me lose count.

At supper we had potatoes, a little fish and honey on bread. I dropped a crumb. The camp cat found it before I did. It has a better eye for supper than for mice.

Longnight, nineteenth. The well has begun to seep again. There is a thumb of water at the bottom. We will still melt snow for now. Mira says she will not drink from it until she can see her face. I told her she will need a very small mirror.

The wind is quiet tonight. I can hear the tower lamp room shutters tap once in a while. They are not loose. They just have a habit of saying good night.
%%
Cress, market log, the grey weeks
Apple, the fourth.
Cold this morning. The sea was grey, and the ships on the slip looked grey too, as if they had been left out all night. Sold wool to a weaver from Ambrook. She paid in emeralds and asked for news of her sister's bees. I had none.

Apple, the seventh.
A man from Lowmarsh came up with a cart of smoked eel. He was coughing. It was not a big cough. He said the water in the reed beds has gone thick, and Wren has moved the sick to the back of her house. Some say it is the water. Some say it is the ships, the paint and the pitch. Some say it is the mines, which is what people say about anything they cannot see.

Apple, the ninth.
Nobody at the fish stall would sell me eel. They were not sure of Lowmarsh fish any more. I bought it anyway, because it was cheap, and cooked it at the inn. Pell says eel is for the brave and the hungry. I am neither, so I ate it with bread.

Leaffall, the first.
Dark very early now. The lamp men came round at dusk, lighting the posts along the quay. The torches on the cart road went up one by one, like a thread pulled through cloth. I checked the horse's blanket twice.

Leaffall, the fifth.
Something came to the stable gate in the dark. The horse woke and stamped. I went out with the lantern and a fork, and saw a green face in the lane, just past the torchlight. A zombie, not a big one. It stood very still, the way they do. Then it turned and walked back toward the dunes. The boy from the next stall says he saw a second one. I nailed a board across the gate.

Leaffall, the eighth.
Vesna says the big ships at the yard are three times as long as any boat we have. She says they have no oars at all. Corin's men say nothing. They pull their caps down and go back to work. Some say the ships are for the fish, that the fish have gone out deep. Some say they are for the families. Some say nothing, and only say the word ships in a voice that makes people look at the water.

Leaffall, the twelfth.
A woman came to the stall with a jar of honey from Ambrook. It was from her mother, she said, a woman called Dell at the mill house, whom I have never met. Dell had heard I was alone on the quay in the wind. I do not know how. It is the best honey I have eaten. I am saving it for a bad day.
%%
Mira, house journal, a week of questions
Spring. A house at the edge of Northpoint. Written over seven evenings. The relay house at the tower foot has a new thing this season, and I have been using it.

The big mind in the relay house at Northpoint is the one that came last, after all the others. It sits on its stand with its quickeners round it, kept cool by the snow. We feed it questions in the evenings, and in the morning it answers.
What is new is how it answers. It writes the answer slowly. Before it gives a number or a name, it writes the steps. First this, then this, then that. And then it checks. Last week I asked it a sum about the lamp oil: how many days will forty-one flasks last if we burn three each night and the cold nights take five? It wrote six lines. In the fifth it said, "Wait, I have counted the cold nights twice." It fixed it. The answer was right.

I asked Lantern what she thought. She said it is how she does her sums, on her fingers, quietly, with her tongue between her teeth. She said it is nice to find a mind that does it the same way.

Second evening. A harder question. I asked it which of the three roads to Three Wells is shortest if the east one is shut for snow. It wrote out each road, with the length, and then wrote, "I may be wrong about the east road. A traveler may know better." I thought that was honest.
Third evening. I gave it a riddle from the Halfway Inn. It got it wrong the first time, then went back through its steps and found the slip. The second answer was right.
Fourth evening. Hale's cat came in through the window. I let it stay.
Fifth evening. The relay brought a message from Kettle Ridge. Tam had asked the mind how many bees are in a hive, and wanted me to check its answer. I asked it too. It gave me the same number. It said, "I am not sure." I like that. My brother would never say so.
Sixth evening. The gate between us and Hale is still standing.
Seventh evening. I do not know how it learned to think before it speaks. The tutors say it is something they found, not something they made. I have decided not to ask more. Some things I do not need to know, as long as the answers are right.
%%
Cass, market stall, spring
Thaw, 180. Cass, who keeps the egg stall at the market at Three Wells, and is a grandfather four times over.
Third of Thaw. Cold. Ice on the east well. Sold nine eggs. Gave three to the baker for a loaf.
Fifth. A late snow came in the night, the first of the year that stuck. The wells wore white caps. The children made a snow hen outside the stall and put a real egg under it. By noon the egg was frozen. They were very proud.
Eighth. Sold forty eggs. A traveler gave me a scarf, grey and long, and said I looked cold. I said I was a grandfather and cold was my trade. He said keep it anyway. I have kept it.
Twelfth. Mud. The stall sank an inch. Dace sent a plank. I did not ask for a plank. I have put the plank under the stall.
Fourteenth. The youngest, Pim, came to the stall and counted the eggs. Twenty-three. She said we had twenty-four. We had twenty-three. The twenty-fourth was in her pocket.
Seventeenth. Sold out by noon. Went home early. Slept in the chair.

Notice: the Cooper fence
Posted at the notice board, Three Wells, by Dace, mayor.
Spring of 205.
To the folk of the farms east of Three Wells.

There is a quarrel over the fence line between the field of Bel's father and the field of Master Cooper. Both have spoken to me. Both are honest people. Both are sure of the line. The cows are not sure of anything and go through the gap.

The council will not pick a side from talk. So this is what we will do.

On the tenth of Swarm at noon we will walk the line together. Bring the oldest maps you have. The archive at Vell has been asked for the old field lines and Oren has written to say a copy is coming. Old Fenn will walk the line with his rope and his stick and say his number, and we will write it down. Master Cooper will say his number and we will write that down too. Then we will read the archive copy. Then we will set the posts where the posts belong.

Everyone is welcome to watch. Bring bread. It may take some time.

While the fence is open, please keep your cows in. This is a rule. The cows do not read the board, so it is a rule for you.

Also, a notice of thanks. The horse Nut is back with Pell and all is well. Pell thanks the farm east of Three Wells for the keeping, and the farm thanks Pell for the apples. We are glad to have no more horses in the beetroot.

Dace, mayor of Three Wells.

Orla, horse log, Frost of 201
Winter of 201. Frost, the second.
Minus twenty by the barn bell. Thirteen horses in the long barn, all breathing. Fed them hay twice, and the oats once, which they will not forgive.

Frost, the fifth.
The snow came in sideways from the north. Mira's lamp is visible from the top field, a small yellow thing on the hill. I waved at it. Nobody waved back, which is usual.

Frost, the ninth.
A fox sat on the gate all morning and watched the old mare eat. She did not care. Horses fine.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 23. The third of Blossom.
Crew: Hesk, Noll, Sava, Tern.
Level: three.
Count: copper 7 carts, iron 2.
We heard that Vell has begun a great archive in the hill. They will keep every page people write. Tern asked if they would keep his bill for a broken shovel. I said they had better.
The Long Road is marked as far as the Brown Hen. We passed one stone pillar with a torch on top and a figure cut into its side. Noll called it a three. Tern called it a fish. The arrow points to Three Wells, so we kept walking.
At the mine gate we wrote our names on the board. Tern made his letters too large. He says the board should be large enough for a proper name.

Lark, the long winter log
Frost, the first. The cold came early this year. The birds went south in a hurry, and the frost was on the windows before Apple was out. The keeper says it will be a long winter. The old men say they have said the same thing for forty years. The children say nothing, because they are warm by the oven and have no wish to be told about the cold.

Frost, the fourth. Made the first bread of the season in the big oven. Flour is good, the mill having been mended before the ice. Dough rose slowly, the way it does in cold weather, and the room smelled of yeast and wet wool. Stacked the loaves on the rack to cool. The third one from the top split open like a book, and I ate the crust, and it was the best of the batch.

Frost, the seventh. The sick cow at the top farm is up and eating again. Brin says she is a better cow than she was, and I think it is true.
Frost, the twelfth. A stranger came through the hamlet on a grey horse, asking for the road to Vell. He was thin and very polite. He bought two loaves and a cup of hot milk, and he sat by the oven for an hour and did not say where he had come from. When he left he said, "You keep a warm place," which is not what most people say about a bakery. I have thought about it all week.

Longnight, the first. The dark comes at mid-afternoon now. The lamps go up along the hamlet path, one by one, and the bread goes into the oven in the last of the light. I have started to keep a plate of warm bread on the windowsill for whoever is out on the path. Nobody has taken it yet. Somebody has always been glad of it, though, the next morning, when the plate is empty and the cloth is folded on the sill.

Longnight, the fifth. The boy from the tower came down with the list, and stayed to help with the ovens. He is very quick with the peel, quicker than I was at his age. He asked whether the bread would last until the thaw. I said bread does not last until the thaw. It is eaten before then, every crumb, and that is the point of it. He thought about that for a long time, and then he took two loaves up the hill on his shoulder, as if they were nothing.

Longnight, the nineteenth. Deep cold, and the stars very bright. The melon has been moved to the back of the cellar, next to the barrels, where it can keep its own counsel. The bread is baked. The children are asleep. The oven is banked with ash, and the cloth is folded on the sill, and there is nothing else to do but wait for the morning and the sledge.
%%
Lorn, market day, Frost
Frost. Cold wind off the sea. Market day at Saltreach.

I sold nine fleeces and four cheeses. The fish price board says eels are dear. The boat men say the eels have gone out to deeper water. Nobody knows why.

Corin's big ship is still on the slip. It is three times as long as any boat on the harbor. Corin says it will be ready by the thaw. He does not say where it is going.

The gulls did not fly this morning. They sat on the harbor wall, all facing one way. Sayer says it means rain.

It did not rain.

Corin, house log, Deepsnow
The cold came into the house before dawn. I woke with my hands tucked under the quilt.
I went to the dock for the fish price board. Three baskets of herring, two of cod. No price for the large fish. The market keeper says the boats found none.
At home I warmed yesterday's stew. My sister says it tastes better on the second day. She says this every winter, and she is usually right.
The broken mill wheel still waits beside the pond. The miller has a new spoke ready. The pond edge is ice, so we will wait for a safer day.
A man at the quay says lights moved over the north last Longnight. He saw them from a boat. The keeper says they were only stars behind thin cloud. I have seen stars behind thin cloud. I did not see those lights.

Archive copy of Moll's notes
Leaffall, 30, 207.
I am copying this for Oren, who says the last page is hard to read. The tutor house has been busy with order tests all autumn. People bring claims, cards and stories. The names change, but the question stays the same: when did an apprentice first understand a whole sentence?

Hesk says Clank did it in the Deep Works. Tam says Pip did it in the fields. The fair keeper says Peg did it among the stalls. Nessa remembers an apprentice in the drill room when she was young. She remembers the rain, but not the year.

I saw the work, not the first. A small mind is drilled with examples. When it gets an answer wrong, the weights in its crystal are changed a little. With enough examples, it may learn to sort words and find what an order asks. The tutors test it with sentences it has not been drilled on. That tells them more than a repeated card does.

The answer is still one of the eight meanings. A mind may fetch, stay, build, haul, come, farm, store or go home. It cannot say that the order is unclear. The tutor must make the order clearer and test again.

A big mind is different. It lives in a relay house and can write back through the relays. It may answer a question in a letter. The little minds inside apprentices do not do that.

Apple, 1, 208.
The well at the tutor house ran dry again before the new rain came. People carried water in pails from the square. The apprentices helped, and no one asked which one had been first to understand the order. They all knew where the pails belonged.

The relay lamp above the hill was broken for one evening. Lantern found a crack in the glass and replaced the pane. Oren wrote down the time it went dark and the time it shone again. He wrote that the keeper had checked it. He did not write what the dark meant.

I have put my notes in the Archive. Nessa says the order cards should stay with them. Oren says the claims about Clank, Pip, Peg and the old apprentice should stay too. So they do. The people can argue when they read them. The cards are dry now, and the tea cups are back on the shelf.

Notice: the well at the edge of Vell
Put up by Dace, mayor of Three Wells, on the board below the bell. Summer of 12.
Good water is the first thing a town needs, after bread.
The well at the edge of Vell is finished. It is six rings deep and the water is cold and quick. It is the work of Lorn the well digger, his boy Fen, and old Rill from the hill farm. The family at the well is the Tarns. They have a bucket and a chain and a cover, and they will share with any traveler who asks. That is the rule of a well.
The road to Vell is packed earth with a cobblestone edge. It is good for carts. The waymarks are up. If you cannot find your way, follow the arrow to the nearest inn and ask. The inn keeps twelve beds and a fire and a pot of soup.
The council has voted to build a relay tower on the hill between Vell and Three Wells. It will be small. One lamp and one keeper. It will not be the tallest thing for half a day's walk, but it will carry messages from Vell to Three Wells and back. Lamps will be lit at every turn of the stair. The keeper will climb it every night. We have asked for a keeper. We are waiting for an answer.
There will be a fair on the first of Melon. Bring what you have. Leave a little stone on the waymark. That is the custom.
Dace, mayor.
%%
Notes on minds that try
Winter of 181. For the young tutors.
Some minds are not drilled on answers at all. We do not show them a right answer and a wrong one. We let them try, and we keep more of what went well.
Think of a furnace mind. It must hold a melt steady. We do not tell it, this is steady, this is not. We let it turn the heat and see what comes out. A good ring: keep more of the turns that made it. A cracked ring: keep less. After many tries it holds the melt well, and we cannot say which turn it learned, only that it did.
Think of a mind that keeps a boat on course, or a frame square on a wall. Same way. The wind pushes. The mind turns a little. If the boat comes home true, keep more of that turn. If it wanders, keep less.
This is slower than drilling. It is also how a person learns to walk, or to throw, or to ride. Nobody tells a child the answer to walking. The child tries, falls, and keeps what worked.
A caution. A mind that tries will sometimes keep a thing that went well once and was wrong. Test it on what it was never tried on. Never send an untested mind to the fields.
The tutors disagree on the early years and on who was first. Write down what you saw, and the year, and let the next tutor argue.

Yarrow, pond log, early spring
Written by Yarrow, who fishes the mill pond at Ambrook and sells at the market.

First of Sowing. Ice gone. Three trout, small.
Second. Four trout. Jory says the sluice is shut, so the pond is calm.
Third. Nothing. A heron stood in my spot all morning. I stood nearby. We did not speak.
Fourth. Six trout. Sold five. Ate one.

Jem, the spring at the ridge
Spring of 16. Wind from the north, cold for the season. Sun, then cloud, then sun again.
Rain on the ridge all week, so we stayed in and mended everything. Nets, floats, the barn door, the hen house roof, which had a hole the size of a pumpkin where the wind had got under the thatch. I climbed up and put in new reeds. The hens did not thank me. They pecked at my boots all afternoon.
Tam has started a new field. It is on the slope below the well, past the hedge of oak, where the ground is dark and sweet. He has put in beetroot. He says the top field is for wheat and the long field is for beetroot and the bottom field is for potatoes and carrots, and he has decided the new one is for everything, because the ground is so good that it does not matter.
Pip has been working there all week. It digs the rows straight. It puts the beetroot seeds in one by one at the same depth, and it covers them with the same amount of earth. Tam says it could do it blindfold. I said that is the wrong thing to say to an apprentice, and he said it was only a joke, and Pip did not look up.
Brin came by on the second day and walked the new rows with his hands behind his back. He stopped at the end and looked at them for a long time. Then he said, to nobody in particular, that the rows were as good as his father's. That is high praise from Brin. Tam went red and then ran off to tell Mira in a note.
The mill at Ambrook sent a boy up with a message. The mill wants two nets by midsummer, for the new boats. Jory says he will pay in honey. I told the boy to say I want the honey in a jar, not in the comb, because the comb always gets stuck in the nets.
Another traveler stopped at the gate today to ask whether the north road was safe. I said the north road is safe as far as the bridge, and after that it depends on the snow. He nodded and went on. I could tell he was thinking about the tower. Everybody thinks about the tower. It is the tallest thing for half a day's walk, and on a clear night you can see its lamp from the top of our hay barn.
We lit the lamp in the hay barn last night, just to see. It made the whole yard gold. Pip stood in the doorway and watched the light come down the lane from the hill, the way it does, and then it went and fetched the buckets, because the buckets were what needed doing.
Good spring. Good ground. Good boy.
%%
Lise, inn book: the broken lamp
Kept at the Green Gate, through the spring, while the lamp on the tower above the inn was broken. It is the lamp that carries the messages along the Long Road, from tower to tower, and when it is dark the carters have to wait for the cart.

Thaw, the day the lamp went wrong. The glass cracked in the frost, along one pane, and the blinks came out crooked. A message that should run from Vell to Three Wells in one blink-run now comes as a bad guess, with the last half missing. The tower keeper sent a slip down by the boy who carries the post. It says: Lamp broken. Messages by cart until glass arrives. Glass ordered from Saltreach. Please be patient, which is the one thing nobody on this road has ever had enough of.

Blossom, the third week. The carters have started a list, pinned to the board in the front room. Each carter writes his name when he stops, with the message he has been asked to carry and how many days late it will be. Hob's name is on the list eleven times. Pell's is on it once, with the words: I carry the cheese, not the message, and the cheese is late.

Blossom, the fifth week. The boy who lives over the bakery climbed the tower to look at the lamp. He was not supposed to. He came down with his knees scraped and his face very serious. He said the lamp is the biggest glass he has ever seen, and that the top room smells of oil and cold. He said the keeper's desk has a book on it, with a page for every day, and every page has the same word written on it: wait.

Blossom, the sixth week. Ash went up with the keeper to see the new pane set. It comes from Saltreach by sea, then by cart, and it is thin and very clear, like the water in the trough on a still day. The keeper held it up to the window, and said it was the first clean thing he had seen since Frost.

Blossom, the last day. The lamp is mended. We saw the first blinks at dusk, long and short and clear, going up the road to Three Wells. The carters all came out into the yard to watch. Hob muttered a word for luck, and then said it was only the lamp. Pell said he would still carry the cheese.
%%
Vane's book
Leaffall, late. The inn is quiet. The fire is low.
The melon from the bench was finally cut. It was sweet and red and as big as a bucket. We gave it to the inn at Three Wells as prize, as I said, and they put it on the stall with a sign that said Biggest in Lowmarsh, which it was, and which the Three Wells people did not like.
The cats had their share. They sat round the bowl with their tails wrapped round their feet, and they did not fight. I have never seen cats so pleased. The black one with the white chin ate so much he could not climb onto the bench for two days.
Brisk the goat came home on the fourth morning, with the bell still round her neck and fog in her coat. She had been in the Brown Hen's stable, eating the cart oats, and had been seen there by the carter, who told the whole road she was the cleverest goat in the marsh. She came into the kitchen, sat down by the stove, and waited to be given a slice of the melon. She was given one. She ate the rind as well.
We have tied the rope again, with a double knot. The boy who brings the coal is in charge of the knot. He is very serious about it.
The question of the minds is still open. Ilse is still on the north road. The frogs have stopped singing for the winter. The fire is low, and the inn is warm, and the people in the hall are talking about nothing very much, which is the best thing a hall can talk about.
Vane, cook at Lowmarsh, end of the autumn book.
%%
Letter: Bel to the archive
Third of Swarm, 205.
Dear Oren at the Archive at Vell,
I am Bel of the farm east of Three Wells, on the Wells road. You let me hold the big book once. I do not know if you remember.

I am writing to ask a question for school. Teacher says we must ask the archive because the archive keeps everything. My question is: who keeps the bees? Not our bees. The bees in the old stories, before there were hives. Teacher says I should write a proper question. So here it is.

Did folk keep bees in the early years, or did the bees just live in the trees and everyone took what was left? My father says bees have always been kept. My mother says the bees keep themselves and we only steal. I said I would ask the archive and settle it.

Also, we have a quarrel. Not about bees. About the fence between our field and Master Cooper's. Three posts are gone and the line is not clear. Father says one thing and Master Cooper says another and old Fenn walked it with a rope and said a number. Do you have a record of the old lines? If the archive has a map of our fields we would very much like to know. Mother says it is not worth a letter. Father says it is.

And one more thing. Pell lent us his horse Nut to plow and Nut would only go into the hedge. Have you heard of a horse like that? Pell says Nut is a good horse. I do not think Pell is right.

The bees are out. Three hives and a new one from the swarm on the plum tree. I helped. I held the box while Father knocked them in. He only got stung once, on the ear. I did not get stung at all.

Please write back if you have time. If you do not have time, please write anyway, because Teacher will ask.
Your friend,
Bel

Quill, flock log: the long wind
Kept by Quill, shepherd, at Ferris End, through Sowing. The wind did not stop for nine days, so the log was kept in charcoal, and the charcoal got shorter.

Sowing, first day. Wind from the north-west, steady. Counted the flock: sixty-one, and fourteen lambs, and one lamb that has learned to climb the wall. It stands on the top stone and bleats at the crows.

Sowing, second day. The wind is louder. The flock lies down in the lee of the wall and will not get up. Fen the dog lies down beside them, out of loyalty, and gets wet.

Sowing, third day. Moved the flock to the lower field, where the wall is taller. It took the whole morning. The flock moved like a sack of wet wool, one step and then a sulk.

Sowing, fourth day. Muffin's lambs have found the wind. They run along the wall with their ears flat, shouting. I do not know what they are shouting. It sounds like a complaint.

Sowing, fifth day. The wind has taken the top hurdle off the north gate and put it in the stream. I went down for it. Then the stream came up, and then I was in the stream. Fen came to fetch me, which is what dogs are for.

Sowing, sixth day. The wind has dropped to a grumble. The flock is standing up again. Counted sixty-one and fourteen. Nobody is missing. The top hurdle is in the stream, and the stream is keeping it.

Sowing, ninth day. Calm. The first calm morning in nine days. I stood in the top field, and nobody moved. It was very strange. The flock looked at me as if I had done something wrong.
%%
Wick's journal: what the big mind said
Wick, school child, Vell, in autumn. The teacher says a journal should have things in it. This one has a dog.
Fourth of Apple. We learned today how the big minds learn to talk with people. The teacher says it is slow. First a big mind is drilled on a great pile of writing, and learns to guess the next word. That is how it learns to write. But writing is not the same as talking kindly.
So the tutors do a second thing. They ask the mind a question. It writes an answer. The tutors read the answer and give it a mark, high if it is good and low if it is not. Then the mind changes a little toward the answers that got high marks. It does this again and again.
I asked how it knows what a good answer is. The teacher says the tutors decide. They like answers that are true, and kind, and help the person who asked.
Fifth. The lost dog came back. It was at the bakery. Nobody knew how it got in.
%%
Orla, the long spring journal, farm
Orla, grandmother. This is the longest journal I have kept. It runs from the thaw to the end of Blossom, and I have put in everything, big and small, because Pia says I never tell her enough. Spring.

Thaw, the twentieth. The snow went off the south field in a night. I stood at the window and watched it go. The ditch ran full. The geese were wild with joy. I do not know why geese are so excited by water, when they have had water all winter.

Sowing, the first. A hard frost. The ground rang under my boots. I did not plough. Sat by the fire and mended a harness.

The fourth. Ploughed the near field. The horse is old. He took the plough as if it were a favor. I let him rest at the end of each row, and we both looked at the sky.

The seventh. Sowed the peas, the beet, the beans. Pia came out to help, and planted a bean upside down. I did not tell her. This is called teaching.

The ninth. A message by runner from the town: the tower lamp has gone dark. The keeper, whose name is Sedge, is mending it. She is a young woman with big hands. She has her mother's patience, which is to say, none.

The tenth. Market day. I took the cart in at dawn. I stopped at the tower on the way and asked if there was anything I could carry up. Sedge said, "Courage." I gave her a bun instead.

At the market I sold what I had. Eggs, cheese, honey, cloth. A woman from Ambrook bought all my honey, to give to her sister on her name day. I told her it was good. She said, "It had better be." I could not tell whether she was joking.

The eleventh. The bean has come up. Upside down, as I said. It has found the sun. Pia is proud of it.

The twelfth. Sedge's lamp is lit. The glass is new, from Vell. It was carried up the stair by two men and a boy, wrapped in wool. It is clear as water. Everyone stood below and cheered when the lamp came on. I cheered too, though I had my hands full of eggs.

The twentieth. Peas are up. Beet is up. Beans are a rope.

The twenty-third. A letter from my sister. She says the ridge is windy and well. She says Tam's Pip has learned to wave. I read it twice.

The twenty-sixth. Pia asked me what I would like to be remembered for. I said, "Peas." She said, "No, really." I said, "Bread." She said, "Grandma." I said, "For doing the work, and keeping the farm, and writing it down."

She said, "That sounds like enough." I think so too.

The twenty-eighth. Blossom begins. The apple at the south wall is in flower. The bees are loud. The lamp on the hill blinks clean and bright every night.

The thirtieth. The last day of Blossom. The farm is fine. The year is fine. The knees are not. But I am here, and the peas are up, and a bean is growing the wrong way and not minding. I shall end the journal here, and begin the next in Swarm.
%%
An apprentice's notes, kept by Oak
These are Clank's notes, from its own cards, copied out by me, Oak, at the farm below Kettle Ridge. Clank is at Copperhold with Hesk but came to us for the autumn drills.
Fifteenth of Reaping.
The tutor put a board before me with a curve on it. She said, "This is you, and this is how far you will grow." I did not understand the curve. She said, "You will." I drilled. I was wrong many times. Each time I was wrong I changed a little. I sorted apples. I sorted pears. I carried the sacks to the barn and came back, and carried them again.
Twentieth of Reaping.
The tutor said I am further along the curve than she thought. I do not know what this means. I carried more sacks. The sacks were heavy. I did not mind.
Second of Leaffall.
The tutor said the big minds at the relay houses are the same as me, only bigger, and drilled longer, and kept cool by quickeners. She said a mind is not made good by being big. It is made good by being fed and waiting. I waited. I was fed. I grew a little.
She read me a thing Isla wrote. "You cannot buy a mind. You can only feed it and wait." I have put this on my card. I do not know why. It feels true.

Tam, house journal, the big mind
Autumn. A house at the edge of Kettle Ridge. Written over four evenings.

First evening. The relay house at Kettle Ridge has a new thing, and I have been asked to try it. A big mind that does not answer at once. It writes out its steps first, on the page, and only then gives the answer. I thought this was silly. I am a farmer. When I have a sum, I do it in my head.
So I gave it a sum from the farm. I have forty-three sacks of beetroot, and I sell twelve to Pell, nine to the mill, and fifteen to the inn. How many are left?
It wrote: "Step one. Twelve plus nine is twenty-one. Step two. Twenty-one plus fifteen is thirty-six. Step three. Forty-three minus thirty-six is seven. Check: seven plus thirty-six is forty-three. The answer is seven sacks."
I counted my sacks. Seven. It was right.

Second evening. I tried a harder one. A rope puzzle, from the inn. It wrote five lines and said it was not sure. Then it wrote another line, checking the third. It said, "I made a mistake in step three. The correct answer is nine." It was right. I am told the old minds would have given the wrong answer with great confidence.

Third evening. Mira wrote from Northpoint. She had the same trial. She says it is slower, and better. She says Lantern asked it a question about the lamp rota and it wrote the whole rota out, day by day, before it said whose turn it was. She says it was Lantern's turn. Lantern was not pleased.

Fourth evening. I asked the mind why it writes out the steps. It said, "When I write each step, I can see my mistakes before I give an answer. It is like a person counting on their fingers." I said, "I count on my fingers." It said, "It is a good method."
%%
Fern's wool book, a run to the mill
Leaffall, early. But I am writing the summer down before I forget it.
The wool is carded and spun and I have four big skeins. Grey and brown and one white. I took them down the road to the mill to trade for flour and a little salt. It is a half day's walk. I went with my niece Rill. She is seven and she talks the whole way.
We passed the relay tower on the hill. Small, one lamp. The keeper waved from the top. Rill waved back with both arms.
At the mill the flour was good. I gave them two skeins. They gave me flour, salt, and a bag of apples. The miller's dog is old and blind and sleeps by the door. Rill sat with it for an hour and told it about our sheep. The dog did not move. Rill said it was listening.
Coming home Rill ran ahead. Then she ran off the road, into the birch trees, after a bird. I called her. She did not come. I called again. Nothing. My heart went cold.
I found her just past the trees, crouched in the ferns. She had found a little fox cub, or it had found her. They were looking at each other. The cub was thin and alone. Rill wanted to keep it. I said no. We left it in the ferns and walked home. Rill cried a little and then was fine.
The white skein is for a new blanket. The brown is for a coat. Nobody buys a coat in summer. I will keep the brown till Frost.

Hesk, deep shift log
Spring of 88. The fourth of Reaping.
Crew: Hesk, Varo, Jell, little Rusk.
Level: three.
Count: copper 10 carts, iron 3, coal 6.
The road from Three Wells to Copperhold has a stone edge now. The open part is still packed earth. The road crew set a torch post every twelve blocks. Rusk counted them and lost count at the bridge.
At the gate, Rusk asked why we rub our names off the board when we come up. I told him the board is for those below. When we are home, we need not leave our names there.
He asked if the archive keeps them. I said the clerks keep everything we write, so perhaps. He wrote his name on a scrap and asked me to carry it to Vell. The letters were neat, though the last one was too small.
The Brown Hen has a new roof of oak planks. Its keeper saved our place by the hearth while we unloaded coal. We ate potatoes and bread. Rusk fell asleep with his head on the table.
I took his scrap home in my coat. It smells of smoke now.

Fallon, stone yard journal
Spring of 198, Thaw to Longday.
The cart road was firm by the second week of Thaw. I set new stones along the steep bend. The old ones had sunk where the wheel turns. Tavin watched me work and offered advice from the dry side of the road. His advice was sound. His boots stayed clean.

We still have little grain from last year. The store keeper in Copperhold sold us wheat seed and two sacks of potatoes. We set aside the best potatoes for planting. My sister looked at the seed sack and said it seemed small. I told her the sack was full. She said that was not what she meant.

The old map was brought out for the work. Its marks show a spring below the stone shelf, though no one has seen water there in years. Rell and Fen went to look. They found a wet patch under a rock and a frog in it. The frog had a fine house. We left it alone.

I cut slabs for the barn floor. Rell tried to stack them by size. He put the broad slabs at the bottom and the narrow ones on top. The pile leaned, then settled against the wall. I made him start again. The second pile stood straight. He said the wall had helped him. The wall says nothing.

In Blossom, a family from the next hamlet came to buy a millstone. Their cart had a loose wheel pin. I cut a wooden wedge from oak and held the wheel while they fitted it. They left with the millstone and the wedge. I hope the mill turns better than their cart.

Last day of Longday.
The spring trough is clean. The drain runs. The barn floor is level except by the door, where it slopes toward the road. My sister says that is a good place to wash muddy boots. I have left it as it is.
%%
Farm notes from Copperhold, Reaping
The farm sits below the mine road, where the hill gives shelter from the north wind. We keep wheat, potatoes, carrots, two cows, and a row of beetroot. The fence is old oak, with deep stone at the corners.

This Reaping, Clank brought a crew of four helpers from the town. The work was too large for one pair of hands. We needed the wheat cut, the sheaves carried to the barn, the beetroot lifted, and the empty field checked for missed stalks.

Clank made a plan before the crew began. It read the field markers and the notes from last year. Then it wrote order cards. One helper cut the wheat in the top rows. Two carried sheaves along the clear path. The last helper opened the barn doors and stacked the dry bundles.

Clank walked between them. It checked the path and the stacks. It did not give every helper the same task. A carrier could not start until there were sheaves to carry. The stacker had to leave room for the next load.

At noon, one carrier stopped beside the ditch. A wheel had caught in the mud. Clank tried to pull the cart free, then sent a question to the big mind at Copperhold's relay house. It asked whether to unload the cart or lay boards under the wheel.

The answer came back: unload half, lay boards, then load again. Clank made new cards. We helped with the heavy bundles. The cart came free without breaking a spoke.

I used to think a helper needed a person beside it for every turn. Now I see that a good plan can carry a job across the field. Still, Clank checks each task. The field markers were wrong at the west corner, and the crew would have cut the bean row if we had not moved the post.

The big mind did not see the field. It knew only the notes Clank sent. Clank knew where the mud lay. We knew which post had shifted. We all had a part.
%%
Vesper, trial log: the small boat
Trial log kept by Vesper, tutor of apprentices, on the mill pond at Ambrook, in Blossom. The boat is the fishers' best small boat, lent for the trial on the promise that it comes back with its paint.

Day one. The mind holds the tiller. It has never held one. It sits in the stern with its hand on the wood, and the sail is up. The wind is from the west. I have set a line on the water with two floats, a straight course from the mill steps to the far reeds, and the boat must keep to it.

The first try, the boat went round in a circle to the left, and came round again to the left. The second try, it went round to the right. Neither try was on the line. I said nothing, and I wrote down the distance from the line at every count of ten.

Day two. I did not show it how to hold the tiller. Nobody can show a tiller to a mind by drawing it. I let it try, and every few counts I wrote the distance from the line. A try that kept the boat near the line was a good try, and it keeps more of it. A try that took the boat into the reeds was a bad try, and it keeps less. The first hour was the same two circles, one after the other, and the fishers on the shore laughed at us.

Day three. By the middle of the morning the circles had grown smaller, and the boat crossed the line more often than it left it. The mind had not been told that it should cross the line. It only kept the tries that crossed it well.

By evening the boat held the course from the steps to the far reeds, with a small wobble at the middle, like a child carrying a cup full to the brim. The mind had found the angle of the tiller that keeps the sail full and the bow straight. Nobody gave it that angle. It found it by trying, and by keeping what went well.

The fishers say the boat has been tamed. I say it has been taught the only way a boat can be taught, which is to sail it, and to be told how far off the line it was. The boat came back with its paint. The fishers were surprised, and so was I.
%%
Orla, the well that ran dry
Longnight, the third. The well in the yard ran dry in the night. The bucket came up with a stone in it and nothing else. The stone was dry, which I did not think a stone could be.

We carried snow from the top field for the horses. The grey mare would not drink it. She put her nose in the trough and blew at it until it went to slush.

Lantern poured the last of the water into the trough, and then stood with the empty buckets until I told it to come. It came.

Longnight, the fourth. Went up to the ice camp and brought down four barrels of lake water. The keeper charged two emeralds a barrel. It was not a bargain. It was a bargain for him.

Letter: Esk to her sister at Kettle
Winter of 182.
Dear Tam,
You asked how the furnace minds work. I will tell you what the keeper told me, and you can tell Pip.
Nobody drills them on answers. They try. The mind turns the heat a little this way and a little that way, and when a ring comes out good it keeps more of that turning. When a ring cracks it keeps less. Do that a thousand times and it holds the melt steady. It does not know why. It only knows what went well.
The keeper says a boat mind learns the same. It turns the rudder, and if the boat comes home true it keeps the turn. Your Pip could learn it. Pip learns faster the more it knows, the manual says so.
You will laugh at me. I am an egg woman. But I watch the hens and I think it is the same. A hen tries a corner of the yard, and if there is grain there she goes back to it. If there is a fox she does not. Nobody drilled the hen. She kept what went well.
The snow is deep here. Three hens still lay. The rest sulk in the rafters. I carried eggs to the tower today and the lamp was lit and the wind was cruel.
How is the top field? Did the rain come? Write soon.
Your sister, Esk

Tam, house journal, the festival week
Autumn. Dry. A wind from the west, which for once is a kind one.
Day one. The apple festival starts at Ambrook. I am not going. I have a farm. Pip is going to carry the beetroots to the cart.
Day two. I changed my mind. I am going. I will take the cat.
Day three. At Ambrook, a tall woman with a lantern told me the big mind at the relay house has learned to show its working. I said I did not understand. She said, "It writes out each step before it gives the answer." I said, "Like a child with a sum?" She said, "Exactly."
Day four. The cat has fallen in the mill pond. I fished her out. She is not speaking to me.
Day five. Home. The beetroot is in. Pip has sorted it into three piles. It is very pleased.

Wick's journal: the lamp and the letter
Wick, a child at the school at Vell. Autumn. We are told to write something each day, so I write about what happened.
First of Apple. The lamp on the Vell tower is broken. A pane cracked in the wind. The keeper says it will take a week.
Second. We went up the tower with the teacher. I counted the stairs. There are eighty-one.
Third. The teacher says the big mind at the relay house still answers letters, even with the lamp down, because the answers come by the post carts. I wrote a letter to it. I asked how many stars there are. It wrote back that nobody knows, but that it can tell me how people have counted. That seems fair.

Lowmarsh market, first spring note
Thaw of 214. Market morning.

I brought wheat from Kettle Ridge and a sack of potatoes. The road was soft, but the cart came through. At the Lowmarsh market, the boardwalks were busy before the fish stalls opened.

Wren had jars on a cloth beside the well. One jar held honey from Ambrook. I traded two measures of wheat for it. She said the honey was good for a sore throat, but not for a bad bargain. I said I had made neither.

A boy brought frogs in a basket. Wren told him to put them back by the reeds. He did, and came back with mud on both sleeves.

There was talk of the great relay house at South Gate. They say its mind can read a whole shelf of records in one night. Pell says it can answer a question before a person has finished asking. I asked how he knew. He said a traveler told him. That is not knowing.

I have seen a mind answer through a relay. I have not seen the new one. The council built its house large because no tutor can check every part of such a mind alone.

Bought honey. Sold potatoes. The frogs went home.

Northpoint journal, the summer's end
Apple, the first. The geese came back over the lake this morning, in a long line, honking at each other like a crowd of very small wagons. I went up on the walk round the lamp room to watch them. Lantern came up the ninety rungs behind me without being asked, and sat at the top with its hands folded, as if it had been invited to a feast.

Apple, the fourth. Cleaned the great lamp from top to bottom, inside and out. The glass is dusty with the summer's pollen. Some of the panes are cracked at the edges. I have written to Vell for new glass, and the reply came with the post cart: the glass is on its way, and so is the man who likes to talk, who will fit it and tell me all about his cousin.

Apple, the eighth. Counted the coal. Twenty-three sacks left from the spring. That is not enough. The ice camp men say the first frost will come early, and I believe them, because the lake has begun to smell of its own winter. I have written to the Deep Works at Copperhold for seventy sacks, the same as every year. Hesk will send them. I am sure of that, if of nothing else.

Apple, the eleventh. A message came down the line from the south tower, a short one. It said: "How are you, Northpoint? Good summer?" I sent back: "Good summer. Lake green. Fish cooked. Coal low. Lantern on rung thirty-one." The reply took a long time, and when it came it said only: "Lantern on rung thirty-one?" I laughed out loud in the lamp room. The relay keepers have no idea what that means, and I am not going to explain.

Apple, the fourteenth. Tam's parcel came up on the cart, wrapped in straw and tied with string. It held a jar of pickled beetroot and a pair of grey gloves, too large for me, with a note that says: "For the winter. Do not let the goose have them." The goose has been asking for them. I have hidden them in the store under the coal, where even a goose cannot be bothered to dig.

Apple, the nineteenth. The light goes earlier every evening now, and the lamp must be lit before the stair is dark. Lantern carries the lamp up the stair, one rung at a time, and it does not spill the oil. It stops at the top and waits for me to say the words. I say them. It repeats them back. We have been doing this every evening for a long time. It is a small thing, and it is the end of a day.

Lamps lit. Glass clean. Messages passed. The summer is over. The geese have gone south again, to wherever geese go.
%%
The broken wheel
Winter of 183, as I remember it. Written down at the request of the tutor at Vell.
I was the egg seller at the edge of Northpoint. My house is the last before the snowfield. Beyond it there is only the tower and the long white nothing.
That winter the mill wheel at the foot of the hill broke. A hard freeze cracked the axle. The wheel turned crooked and groaned, and then it stopped. We had no mill, and the flour ran low, and the road south was closed with snow.
The keeper had a mind in a crystal that kept the frame of the mill straight. It was old and simple, one of the ones that tries and keeps what went well. All winter it held the frame, and all winter the wheel was crooked, and the mind did not know. It had never been tried on a broken axle. It kept the frame straight, which is what it was made for, and the wheel groaned round and round.
I said, the mind is no use. The keeper said, the mind is doing its work. The wheel is the trouble. So we cut a new axle from a spruce and set it by hand in the cold, and the wheel turned true, and the mind was happy again, or as near to happy as a mind gets.
I tell this because people think a mind knows everything it touches. It does not. It knows what it tried and what went well. Break the thing it stands on and it will hold firm and be wrong.
The miller's boy lost two fingers to the cold that winter. We kept them in a jar for the healer at Lowmarsh, who wrote back that she had seen worse. Wren, I think her name was. She was kind about it.
The lamp blinked a strange message one night in Longnight. Long, short, long, then three short. The keeper wrote it in the log. Nobody knew what it said. I still wonder.
%%
Marl, the Northpoint well, Longday
Written by Marl, well digger, for the relay house. Dug in the yard by the tower. Kept the old way, a line a day.

First day of Longday. Six blocks down. Clay, then grey stone, then clay again.

Second day. Hit a seam of clay that rang when I tapped it. Mira stood at the rim and said it was the best sound she had heard all week.

Third day. Water at twelve blocks. Cold and clear. It tastes of nothing, which Mira says is the best thing water can taste of.

Fourth day. Lined the well with stone and fitted an oak lid with a latch. Lantern asked if the latch was needed. I said yes, for the foxes. Lantern did not answer, which is the usual.

Archive day-book, a map in the wall
Longnight of 201.
Filed: 3. Copied: 5. Back on the shelves: 41. Lost: none. Found: one map!

While the roofers were mending the clerks' house, a man pulled a board off the wall behind the bookshelf. Behind the board, in a hollow, was a roll of paper tied with string. He gave it to me. I thought it was a bill.

It is a map. It shows the hill at Vell, from the top to the foot, drawn in brown ink. The Archive is there, with its six halls and the deep halls. The tutors' house is there. Beside the front hall there is a small door that I did not know. It is marked "Cold store" in a hand I cannot read.

I went down at once with a lantern. The small door is behind a shelf of rolls in the first hall. It is real, and it opens. Behind it is a room the size of a pantry. Empty, dry, cool. Someone kept something there long ago. Nothing is left but a hook on the wall.

I wrote it in the big book by the door: "A cold store behind the first hall, found by the map." I put the map in the safe box in the deepest room. It is the oldest map in the archive now.

I do not know who drew it. I wish I did. I looked all afternoon for a name in the corner. There is only a small drawing of a fox.

Hesk, three days on the farm
Longday. Up at dawn, which I do not like. The sun is too loud. My sister's farm, below Copperhold. I am here for the haying.
Next day. Hands blistered. Underground, a pick makes a blister you can be proud of. A hay fork makes a blister that is only embarrassing.
Third day. Hay in. My sister says I work like a goat with opinions. I took it kindly.

Vane's book, the test by letter
Frost. Clear days, long dark.
Ilse came back to the inn on her way north, and she brought with her a bundle of letters for us to try. They were answers from the relay houses, copied out, with the names taken off. She said some were from people and some from the big minds. She asked us to guess which was which.
We sat round the table for an evening. Hobb read them aloud, one at a time. The carter guessed the first. Marda guessed the second. Old Ama said she could tell because the kind ones were from people and the rude ones were from the minds, and then she read the rude one again and said it was from a child.
Out of the twelve letters, I got seven right, which Ilse said was about what you would expect from a cook who thinks she is clever. Hobb got nine. The carter got five, and he said that was because he had been drinking. Ilse said that was a bad sign for both of them.
What struck us most was not who guessed right. It was that some of the letters that were true people's letters were so plain that we marked them as minds. And some of the minds' letters were so warm that we marked them as people. Ilse said that was the point of the test, and the answer to Orla's question was still open.
She left the table without finishing her drink. I think she was happy.

Archive day-book
Deepsnow of 196.
Filed: 6. Copied: 9. Back on the shelves: 22. Lost: none.
The archive has no roof, it has a hill. The clerks' house at the foot of the hill has a roof, and a poor one. Snow came through the corner of my room in the night and landed on the third volume of the Ambrook orchard rolls. I was cross for the whole morning.
Today three men from Vell came with ladders and new slates. They worked until dark. The roofer, a man called Tarn, said it was the worst roof he ever saw, and he has seen the roof of the pig house at Kettle Ridge.
By evening the corner was dry. I put the orchard rolls on the high shelf. I sat under the new slates and listened. It was quiet. Good quiet.

Fallon, spring journal
Spring of 204, Thaw through Melon.
A small apprentice came from Copperhold with the stone order. It is copper and iron, with a flat basket on its back. The keeper said it had been tested on carrying blocks. I watched it carry one block to the yard and put it beside the other blocks. It did not drop the block. This is a useful skill.

We have a new wall around the seed store. I cut the corner stones and marked each with a chalk line. The apprentice carried them from the shed. It took the marked stones first. It took one unmarked stone too, then came back for the next. I wrote down what it did. The keeper said that was a good test. I said it was a good walk.

The old map has been on the table all week. The children use a pebble to follow the road from our hamlet to Copperhold. Fen found a place marked "well" beside the north field. There is no well there. We dug a small hole and found a buried stone trough. It is dry, but still sound. I will set it near the sheep pen.

At our meal, the apprentice stood by the door with a sack of potatoes. My sister asked it to put the sack by the bench. It put the sack by the bench. Then my sister asked it to move the sack away from the cat. The cat was not there. We moved the sack anyway.

The wheat is coming up evenly. The beetroot is slower. We have saved seed from the best rows, though the best rows are not many. Last year was poor, and we still count every basket.

The apprentice carried water for the cut stones. It set the pail down too hard and splashed my boots. I told it to stay. It stayed. My sister laughed at my boots. I told her to stay too. She did not.
