Wren's farm book, the lost letter
Leaffall. Cool, bright. Frost in the shadows.
I lost a letter today. It was a good one, from my sister at Saltreach, with a sketch of her new baby and a pressed flower from the sea wall. I had it in my apron pocket when I went to the high shelf. I came down with it. Then I did not have it.
I looked in the apron, the sleeves, the basket, the bench, the cupboard by the door. I looked under the sick bed, which is never a good place to look. Moss looked too, very carefully, in every corner, but it found only a button and a dead moth.
Then Ros's boy came in with a bundle of turnips, and asked if I had lost something, because he had found a letter on the path by the gate. It was mine. It had a boot print on the front and a smear of mud across my sister's name. He said he was sorry. I told him he had been very good, and gave him a honey cake.
The flower is still pressed. The sketch of the baby is still a little blurred. I have put the letter in the box with the other good letters, where nothing can be lost, except possibly me.
%%
Ossa's book, the lights over the north
Frost. Clear nights. The stars are very bright over the hill.
Three nights this week, a boy at the north gate saw lights in the sky above the ridge. He says they were not stars. They were lower, and they moved. They went in a line, slowly, and then they went out one by one, like lamps being put down.
His father says it was the snow reflecting. His grandmother says it was the old fires from the time before the towers. The carter says it was the road-men with their torches on the far hill. The carter is always certain about everything that is not on the road.
A little girl in my street said they were looking at us. Her mother told her to stop. Then the girl looked at me, very seriously, and said: they can see us from there.
I do not know what the lights are. I do not think anyone does. Some of the relay keepers say the north towers have gone quiet. I have not heard it from anyone who would know. I hope the keepers are only tired.
The baby I delivered on the fourth night was well, and so was the mother. I tell myself that is the thing to write down.
%%
Emmet, net log, the spring boats
Spring of 196. Clear mornings, warm afternoons, and the reeds coming up green. Nets to mend, and I have mended every one.

Sowing, the first. Mended twelve nets. The boat house roof leaks over the third bay, so I hung a bucket there, with a note on it: not for drinking.

Sowing, the fifth. Tarred all four boats with the black tar that stinks. Everybody in Lowmarsh knows where we are by the smell. The old boat, the Lily, has a seam that opens on every wave.

Sowing, the ninth. The frogs are loud at dusk. Fourteen nets torn by the weir. Nobody will say whose fault it was. Everybody will say it was the eels.

Blossom, the second. Sold forty fathoms of good cord to the ferry at Reedford. The keeper paid in emeralds and bread. Wren's boy came for the eel traps and asked for the big ones. I gave him the small ones, and he grinned.

Abel, cart log, the frozen road
Winter. Cart from the farm outside Lowmarsh to Three Wells and back.
First day. Frost on the boardwalk. The horse does not like the planks when they are slick. I walked her over, one hand on the bridle.
Second day. The Long Road is hard as stone. Snow in the ruts. Met a cart from Ambrook with apples in straw. Traded two sacks of reeds for a jar of honey and a story.
Third day. Three Wells. Sold the reeds, bought lamp oil. The board at the inn had a new letter pinned up. A big mind in a relay house had answered a child's question about why the sky is dark at night. The child had written back to say thank you. The big mind had written back to say it was glad. I read both. They were kind to each other.
Fourth day. Home. Horse tired. Cat unimpressed.

Mira, house journal, the fence
Spring. Thaw is coming.
The neighbors' fence has fallen into our yard. They say it is ours. It is theirs. I said so politely. They said so loudly. Lantern held the post while I looked at the line. The line says theirs. I am not going to say so again. Hale says I am stubborn. I say a keeper has to be, or the lamp would go out. He did not understand that. I do not think he has ever kept a lamp. Tomorrow I shall look at the map once more, and then I shall bring him an egg.

Cress, carter's log, Leaffall
Leaffall, the first day.
Two carts in from the Long Road. Sold the salt at the fish market. The price board says salt is up, so the board man is very pleased with himself. A boy from the net sheds gave me a wooden fish, painted blue, for luck.

Leaffall, the second.
Rain all morning. Nobody came to the stalls. At the fish stall they say the north side families are packing for over the sea. Vesna says it is a lie. Nobody I asked saw anything, but everybody had heard.

Mira, spring at the lamp, with the flood
A long record, kept by Mira, relay keeper at Northpoint, in her house at the edge of the town, for the whole of spring in the year 206. It has her usual dry notes, and a good deal that is not dry. She wrote it, she says, because her brother Tam asked her to write down one thing every day, and she has always done what Tam says, in the end.
THAW
First of Thaw. I will start. One thing a day.
Today: the snow is going. I heard it from bed, a soft drip, like a thousand small drops of water. Lantern says that is exactly what it is.
Third. Today: a fox on the roof of the shed, looking at the hens. I clapped. It went. Lantern watched it go and said nothing. I think it admired the fox.
Sixth. Today: the first green. A blade of it, under the wall, where the sun comes. I knelt and looked at it for a long time. Winter is long here. I forget the color.
Tenth. Today: the cold broke. I sat with the window open and a cup of tea, and the whole town smelled of mud and wet wood.
Fourteenth. Today: the mail. A long letter from Tam, three pages. Pip fell in the pond. The wheat is up. The cat is fat. At the bottom: "Write more." I wrote one line: "No."
Eighteenth. Today: a bird. A small gray one, on the lamp room rail. It sang. Lantern stood below and listened, and I did not ask what an apprentice hears.
Twentieth. Today: nothing. It rained. I sat. It is allowed.
SOWING
Second. Today: the stream. It came up in the night. By morning it was in the yard.
Third. Today: the hens in the kitchen. The kitchen smells like a barn.
Fourth. Today: a letter from Vell. Oren asks if I will send my logs earlier this year. I said I would try. He wrote back: "Thank you. Do not try. Do."
Sixth. Today: the lane is a river. The only dry path to the tower is the one along the wall. I walked it twice a day, with a lantern on a pole.
Ninth. Today: the first lamb at the Brook farm came while the farmer was out. Lantern and I walked across in the dark. I held it for a moment, wet and shaking, and it took my finger in its mouth, and I did not know what to do with my face.
Twelfth. Today: I wrote to the sky, in my head. I said, "Thank you for the child. Now stop the rain."
Fifteenth. Today: the water falls. The mud rises. It is a fair trade.
Twenty-first. Today: Tam came. He walked from Kettle Ridge in four days, with a basket of eggs and a jar of jam. He stood at my door and said, "I got tired of writing." I said, "Come in." He did. The cat is at the door too.
Twenty-eighth. Today: we sat on the step in the sun, Tam and I, and did not speak. It was the best day of the year.
I will write more tomorrow. One thing a day. It is a good rule.
Mira
%%
Log: the furnace at the Saltreach forge
A log of the furnace trial. Written by Sim, who keeps the forge. Autumn.

Day one. The forge has a new mind in the furnace hood. It is a small one, in a crystal the size of a plum. The tutor from Vell left it with us for a month. It has not been drilled on melts. No one has told it what a good iron looks like. It was only let to try.

Here is what it does. A melt goes in the pot. The mind can open and close the bellows through two arms, one slow and one quick. It can add charcoal, or hold it back. The mind does not know the word hot. It knows only that the metal goes one colour, and then another, and then runs clean or runs thick.

Day two. The first melt went wrong. It ran too hot, and the bellows were open too long. The mind was told nothing. It closed the bellows and waited. The melt cooled a little. It was not perfect, but it was not spoiled. We poured it and it was usable.

Day five. The mind has learned to hold a melt steady for a whole hour, when the iron is at its best colour. It does this by opening and closing the bellows in tiny steps, and watching the colour change. We have tried to see what it watches. The tutor says it watches the light, the way the colour spreads. She says nobody gave it that idea. It found it by trying, and keeping what went well.

Day nine. A bad morning. The pot cracked, and two of the men were burned on the wrist. The mind had been running a melt that was too hot, and it did not stop when the men shouted. The tutor says a tried mind does not know what shouting means. It only knows the colour. It must be taught the colour in a different way. She is writing this down for the halls.

Day twelve. We have run the furnace with the mind for twelve days. The iron from this furnace is the best we have made in three years. The spears are straight, and the tools hold an edge. The men have stopped complaining about the bellows.

Day fifteen. The tutor came back to see the trial. She asked the mind to make a small melt on its own, with no one in the room. We stood at the door and watched through the glass. The bellows moved. The colour changed. The mind closed the hood when it was done, and set the pot to cool, and then stopped.

We asked it what it had learned. It cannot say. It does not write back. The tutor says it does not need to. The iron is the answer.

Day twenty. The trial is over. The mind goes back to the tutor's cart in its crystal box. The men have put a little paper cup of honey on the furnace hood, for good luck. It has not been touched yet. They say it will be, when the mind comes back.
%%
Fenn farm journal, Longday fair
Longday, first day. The fair is on the green at Three Wells. The stalls are up round the three wells, and each well has a flag. The boys have hung lamps at every corner, as Dace ordered, and the square is so bright at night it hurts to look. Nan says we will all be blind by the end of the week.
Longday, third day. The song contest was on the stage by the east well at noon. There were eleven songs. Brin sang Pudding's song. It had all eleven verses, and by the ninth the crowd had started to sing the chorus with him, to get him to stop. He did not stop. He bowed at the end, and the goats were so surprised they all sat down.
Then Nan sang the bee song. She stood on the stage with her hands in her apron and sang it straight to the back, where the swarm box is, so the bees would hear. The crowd was quiet. A few of the old men took their hats off. When she reached the chorus, the bees came out of the hive box at the back of the green, all of them, and went up over the stalls like a brown cloud. Nobody moved. Then the bees went back in, one by one, and the crowd cheered so loud that Dace fell off his chair.
The judges gave the bee song first prize. Ida said it was because the bees voted. Nan said nothing and went very red. Then she gave me the ribbon and said I was to keep it, as a joke.
Longday, fourth day. Quieter at the fair. Fewer carts came down the north road, and the ones that came were light. A trader from the Long Road said the Halfway Inn has a board of riddles and nobody has answered the last one. The riddle is about a light that is not a lamp. The trader would not tell us the answer. He said the men who knew it had gone.
At the well this morning people were talking about the north again. Someone said the little relay on the hill has been making a new kind of blink. Someone else said it was only the glass. A girl said the signal was counting, one, two, three, and then stopping. Dace came down the square and told everyone to go home and fetch their lamps. Nobody knew why he said it. Everybody did it anyway.
Longday, fifth day. Packing up. The stalls go back on the carts. The lamps stay on the corners until Dace takes them down. I put the ribbon on the windowsill. It is a plain thing: a blue strip with a bee sewn on one end. The sun comes through it in the morning and throws a little blue shadow on the table. I like that more than the prize.
On the way home the little relay tower on the hill had a dark lamp room. We stopped the cart and waited. Nothing. Joss said the keeper was asleep. Nan said keepers do not sleep.
I wrote the bee song out again, clean, for the book. It has seven verses now. I cut the third because it was not true. The bees are not as kind as I made them sound. They come out when they like, and they go back in when they like, and we keep the hive because we have honey. That is all. It is enough.
%%
Patch, the lesson that went badly
Written by Sable, because Nessa says a lesson should be written down while it is still sore.

Nessa, the tutor from Vell, stayed three nights at the Halfway Inn in Apple. She said I could teach Patch, our apprentice, because a mind learns the words it hears most.

Patch carries the jugs and sweeps the hearth. It knows eight words, all orders. I had a board and a stick of chalk. Nessa said, "Short orders. One at a time."

My first order was: bring me the cider from the cellar. Patch brought the jug. Good.

My second order was: stay by the hearth. Patch stayed. Good.

My third order was: what is the riddle? Patch did not know the word riddle. It answered the only way it knows.

= come

I said, "No. Stay by the hearth and tell me the answer."

= come

I asked the name of the cat. It said come. I asked how many beds we have. It said come. The cat came, because the cat always comes when somebody says come, and sat down on the chalk.

Nessa laughed until she had to sit on the bench. Then she said I had learned something, which is the point of a bad lesson.

The mind keeps its words as places. I had given it only stay and come, so every question went to the place nearest them, and come was nearest to everything. Patch was not being silly. It was exactly what I had taught it to be.

Nessa says next time I should give it twelve different words, and then ask the riddle. She will sit with me. I did not ask her to. She sat anyway, and the cat sat on her shoes.
%%
Fenn farm journal, Blossom
Blossom, first day. The apple trees are white at the top and still grey at the bottom. Joss says that is the cold talking.
Dace came up the lane with a letter from the mill. The notice board in the square will have a song contest at the Longday fair. Prizes for the best song about the well and the best song about bees. Joss said he would not sing. Nan said he would sing in the barn, where nobody could hear.
Blossom, second day. Rain all morning. The hens will not go out. I counted eggs: nine. The old brown hen is sitting on her own nest again, so we will have chicks by Sowing.

Emmet, the net house, a season
Thaw, the first. The nets came out of the store after the long winter, all stiff with salt. Hung them on the poles in the yard and let the sun and the wind work at them for a day. They came back soft. I think that is the first kindness of spring.

Sowing, the eleventh. Mended the big seine, the net that takes the whole weir in one go. It has eighty knots to every fathom, and I have counted them all twice. The knots that hold the most are at the corners, where the strain is worst. I tie those with extra care. A bad corner knot will ruin a whole net in one storm.

Sowing, the nineteenth. The tutor's visit is still on my mind. She said a deep mind is a net of stream and knots, and every knot counts. I thought about it all through the cutting of the twine. Then I thought about the apprentice from Wren's house, which ties better knots than I do. It does not talk about it. It sits at the net bench with its hands moving very neatly. It is learning the net the way the net is learning the stream, a little at a time, each knot a little better than the last.

Blossom, the fourth. Rain, and a leak in the net house roof again. We put a bucket beneath the bad place, and labelled it, like the one in the boat house. The labels are now the best-read things in Lowmarsh. Everyone stops to read them.

Blossom, the ninth. The new pane for the relay lamp arrived by cart. The fitter sang all the way up the hill and did not stop talking once. Our copper ring is straight, and the lamp burns clear. Wren's boy says the frogs are still on the hill, and they seem to enjoy the new glass.

Blossom, the seventeenth. Mended the last net of the season. It hangs now on the pole by the weir, dry and full, and it looks like a web that a very large spider would be proud of. I will not say more about spiders.
%%
Vane's book
Frost. The first hard cold. The fire in the hall is kept very high.
A visiting scholar from Vell stayed three nights at the inn. She was called Ilse, and she had a dry way of talking that made the men laugh and then stop laughing. She had come with a question, and she asked it at the supper table the first night, and then she asked it again every night after, until we all knew it by heart.
The question came from Orla, a tutor at Vell, long ago. Ilse said it in her clipped voice. "Can a drilled mind ever think? Not repeat. Think."
Nobody at the table knew how to answer that, so we did not try. Ilse said the question had been asked in the time of the first tutors, and nobody had settled it, and the reason was not a lack of cleverness. It was that no one had agreed on what thinking was.
Then she told us the test that Orla proposed. I will write it here as she said it. Put a hidden mind in one room and a person in another. Ask both the same questions, by letter, through the same window. Give the answers to a reader who cannot see the rooms. If the reader cannot tell which answer came from which room, then what? Ilse said the question was not what the reader decided. It was what the reader would have to say about the answers, and whether they could still call them empty.
Pell laughed at this and said he could tell which letter was from a person, because a person spells the word for barley wrong. Ilse said that was true, and that the minds of the big relays spell it right every time, and that this was the problem.
%%
Wren's winter notes
Thaw, 188. Snow stayed in the reeds until noon.
A boy came from the far boardwalk with a cut finger. He had tried to mend a net with his teeth. The net won. I washed the cut and tied it with clean cloth. Moss held the lamp and did not look away.
The boy said he could still mend the net. I said he could mend it after the cut closed. He asked how long that would take. I told him longer than it takes to find a needle.
At midday we carried warm water to old Sella. Her knees ache in the cold. She says the ache is worse when she sits still, so she walked from her bed to the door and back four times. Moss counted. Sella asked us to count more quietly.
The boardwalk needs two new planks near the fish weir. I marked them with a bit of red wool. Moss brought a plank from the shed, but it was too short. It is now a shelf for boots.
We caught six fish before dusk. One was small, so we let it go. The others went to the kitchen. The house smells of broth, wet wool and the mint jar.
%%
Hob, relay log, a season at the tower
Little relay, the hill between Vell and Three Wells. Logged by Hob, from Blossom to Longday, in the one book. He says the book is too small for a season and too big for a day.

Blossom. Good. Lamp clean, stair mended, a new peg. Ordinary messages. Dace wants to know about the fair every week. Vell asks about the copy of the Long Road book every week. I tell them both the same thing: it is on the shelf, and the shelf is dry.

Swarm. The bees came up the hill and found the old hive in the tower wall. Nobody put them there. The first week, they hummed behind the stones, and I could hear them when I was on the stair. The second week, they stopped humming. I am not sure whether they moved, or whether they grew used to the lamp. I do not think a bee can get used to a lamp. Not without help.

Melon. Hot. Very hot. Took off the coat, which was a mistake, since the wind came round at dusk and I had to go back up the stair in the dark. Passed 31 messages in one day. Half of them were about the melon crop at Ambrook. One was a rumor from Three Wells that the melon at Reedhollow weighed as much as a cart. Vell said it could not possibly be true. I said that Garrow would know.

Reaping. The barley is in at the bottom of the hill, and the boy from the mill has been sent to help. He is good at ropes and terrible at ladders. I taught him the stair, one rung at a time. He does not like heights. He does not like the cat. He likes the lamp, which is good.

Apple. The apples are heavy on the trees. I took six baskets down the hill to the Halfway Inn and came back with a bowl of soup and an argument with Hobb about the price of cider. I won. I think.

Leaffall. Cold nights. The stair is slick. I put down sand and was glad of it. Lamp the cat has found the one warm spot on the copper ring again, and has taken it back from the wind.

Frost. The first frost on the stones. The tower looks like a candle in the snow, and the messages go on, short and neat. Ten from Northpoint. Mira sends her love in a single word, which is the word for coal.

Deepsnow. Short days. Long nights. The lamp burns longer than I do. It is a good job, the lamp. You can always tell it that you are tired, and it never answers back.

Longday. The turn of the year. Back to the start.

Potter's journal: first reading
Written by Lorn, a potter at Three Wells, at the tutor house, the first summer the reading mind came to the square. I went to see it, because I was told it could read a bad hand.

The tutor set a page in front of it. The mind read one letter at a time, through a window about sixty letters wide. It took from the letters before it only the ones that mattered, and then it guessed the next letter. It guessed well. When it guessed wrong it did not seem to mind, and it went on.
%%
Lorn, fair week at Saltreach
Fair week. The harbor is hung with ribbons and lanterns. The mayor's men nailed the prizes to every pier post.

Ilse came down from Vell on the tutors' wagon. She is a tutor of apprentices, and she has six of them with her. They wear the same blue caps and stand very straight. This is their first winter outside the hill.

They carried every sack off the wagon in one trip. Then they stood in a line by the fish stalls and waited. Nobody told them to stop waiting. Corin said it made the fish nervous.

> carry these sacks to the cart
= haul

One of them carried a barrel of salt straight into the prize table. The judges' cups went over.

> put the salt in the store
= store

Ilse gave the order, and the apprentice put the barrel down and stood still. The judges were not pleased. The salt was not pleased either. The apprentice seemed calm about all of it, which Ilse said was the point.

I entered my best fleece, the grey one from the north field. It is the first time anyone in our flock has won a prize. The judge held it up to the lamp and sniffed it. "Clean," she said, "and not too greasy." Then she gave me the copper bell.

It is a small bell, bright as the new copper on the harbor roofs. Ilse wrote my name in her book. She spelled it right. Nobody has done that since spring.

When the lamps were lit, the six apprentices went down to the sea wall and stood there, looking out. Ilse says that is what they do when they are free. They look at things. I am glad they have something to look at.

I rang the bell all the way home. The sheep did not like it. The cat did not like it either.
%%
Corin, the debt
The tenth of Frost. Rafe and I have argued for a month. He says he owes me eight emeralds for the new keel on his boat. I say fourteen. We both think we are right, and we are both fond of our own sums.

Tonight we went to Ilse's tower together and wrote the facts to the big mind, one fact to a line. The keel cost fourteen emeralds. In Reaping Rafe paid six. In Apple he paid four. In Leaffall he gave me a barrel of salt fish worth two, which I took. What does he owe?

The answer came in the morning, with steps.
Step 1. The keel cost fourteen.
Step 2. Paid in emeralds: six and four, which is ten.
Step 3. Paid in fish: two.
Step 4. Paid in all: ten and two is twelve.
Step 5. Owed: fourteen less twelve is two.
Check. Twelve paid and two owed makes fourteen. Good.
Answer: two emeralds.

We were both wrong. Rafe said eight because he forgot the fish and one of the payments. I said fourteen because I forgot every payment he ever made. Rafe went red. I think I did too.

He paid me two emeralds on the quay, in the snow, in front of the whole yard. He counted them out one at a time, loud, so everyone could hear. I counted them back. "Two," I said. "Debt paid." The yard cheered. I do not know why. Nobody cheers a two-emerald debt.

I have asked Ilse to keep the letter. It is the best-kept account I own.
%%
Cade, log: the zombie at the gate
Day twenty. Night, very dark, the sky bare of stars. A zombie came up the Long Road from the south, slow and groaning, and it stopped at the inn gate and swayed there. The dog barked, the cow mooed, and I came out with a torch. Greta came out behind me with a pan.

Keep the torch in front, Greta said. Keep the gate between you and it. The zombie walked to the gate and put its hands on the bars. Then it groaned. It is not a sound that makes you feel brave, whatever the drillers say.

I did what the old carter taught me when I was a boy. Keep the torches lit along the fence, and walk backward, slowly, and do not turn your back. The zombie came to the first torch and stopped. Zombies do not care for the light, and they do not like being looked at for long. So I looked at it for a long time.

Then Greta banged the pan. The zombie turned, very slowly, as if it had forgotten why it came. It went back down the road the way it had come, keeping to the middle of the road, the way a drunk man walks. We watched until the dark had it.

Day twenty-one. We walked the fence in the morning. Every torch was still standing. The dog was asleep in his straw. The cow was still in the yard, which is a comfort. There were green footprints on the road, and a broken pear basket by the gate, which I thought a pity, because the basket was good.

The crystal on its cloth by the cellar door did not wake. It is not much use against zombies. It is very good with pears.

Greta says we need more torches on the east side. I say we need a better gate. We will do both. The bees will keep out of the matter, because bees know a thing or two about defence, and a zombie has never learned to steal from a hive.
%%
Lowmarsh market, season account
Spring of 219. Written after the last market of Blossom.

This spring the market has been larger than last year. Fish came from the slow water, and carts came from Kettle Ridge, Ambrook, and Three Wells. The boardwalks took a beating from wheels. Dace has asked for new posts before the next wet season.

On the first market morning, Wren set out jars of honey and jars of leaves. She had labels on all of them. A child read the wrong label and asked for honey to mend a broken shoe. Wren told him honey would make the shoe sticky. He chose a fresh lace instead.

On the second morning, a copper seller came from the hills above Copperhold. He showed a small piece of bright ore and said a new copper vein had been found near an old cut. Hesk was with him. He said the vein was real, but the way down was narrow and the roof needed checking. They have not opened it yet. The seller wanted to trade the ore for a horse. Hesk told him to trade for boots first.

At the notice board, the council posted a report from the largest relay house. Its mind has a large crystal with many layers. It reads records in pieces, and each layer adds its work to a stream that continues through the mind. It can read more pages than a person can keep in mind at once. That does not mean it knows every page, or that every answer is right.

The report says the tutors test it with records that were not used in its drills. They ask questions with known answers. They compare the mind's answers with the records, then write down where it failed. The council also keeps the test pages apart, so a tutor cannot teach the answers by mistake.

A keeper at the market said the big mind had found an old bridge under the marsh. A fisher said it had named the fish that would come tomorrow. A baker said it had counted every loaf sold in Lowmarsh without looking at the tally board. The report says none of those things. The keeper later said he had been speaking in fun. The fisher did not take it back.

Pell says the largest mind can read any writing ever kept in the Archive at Vell. The clerk from Vell says its drills came from many records, but no one can say that it has read every record. Some pages were damaged. Some were left out. Some were kept in the test box.

People at the market ask what such a mind can do. The tutors say to ask what it has been shown, and how it was tested. That is a sound answer, though it does not stop the stories.

I bought the jar of honey for my mother. I bought potatoes for the house. I carried both home with the ore sample, which Hesk had left beside my cart. It was heavy for its size.

At dusk the market was clean, except for fish scales by the well and one red beetroot on the steps. The frogs were back in the water. The new copper vein can wait until the roof is safe. The big mind can wait for another test. We will have market again in Swarm.
%%
Dara, the inn book, leaf and frost
Leaffall, the first week. The trees on the ridge are gold and red, and the leaves come down in the square in drifts. Tilly sweeps them into piles every morning and jumps in them every afternoon. Dace has asked me to stop her. I have asked him to do it himself.

Leaffall, the third week. The cave is still closed. Oren's cart came through and went up the hill with four clerks and a box of lamps. They did not come back to the inn. They are still up there, I think. The torch on the post is out. Orrin says he will not plant the field this year. He says he does not want to go near it.

Leaffall, the fifth week. Three travelers stayed. One had come up from Copperhold. He said the miners there are working the copper, as always, but they have stopped working the lowest level. The lowest level is the one under the eighth gate. He said the stone there is warm, and the men will not stay past the bell. He said it is probably nothing, only tired men with bad backs.

The second traveler had come from the south. He said at Lowmarsh the frogs have gone quiet. He said Wren is not sure why, and she has asked the healers to keep the water clean. He said the boats at Saltreach are still going out, but the fishermen come back early now, before dark, and nobody asks them why.

The third traveler said nothing at all. He ate, drank, went to bed, and left before the bell. He paid in gold. I have never had gold from a traveler before. I have put it in the box with the others.

Frost, the first week. The first frost on the square. The lamp on the corner has been lit all day, because it is so grey. Tilly has a cold. Bel brought her a cup of warm milk and a book about frogs, and said the frogs are sleeping under the mud now, and will wake in Thaw. Tilly said she will wake up in Thaw too, and Bel said that is the right idea.

Dace came in for his evening bread. He said the north lamps have been dark for two nights. He did not say why. He ate his bread and left a copper on the table for the bread, which he has never done before.

I am going to bed now. The lamp is lit. The door is barred. The stone steps are under the oak, and nobody is going to walk on them.
%%
Vesper, log: the new apprentice, summer
Log kept by Vesper, tutor of apprentices, through the summer at Three Wells. The new apprentice was carried from Vell in a crate, in Thaw. This is its first season of work. Its name is Tib.

Blossom, first day. Tib comes out of its crate. It is copper and iron, and about as tall as a boy of eleven. I set it to drill on the sacks: one sack, one place. It does not know what a sack is. It learns in four days.

Blossom, fourth day. It answers store to the order carry the sacks in. Good. It also answers store to the order go home. Not good. I shall change the drill, and I shall write the change on the card.

Swarm, third day. The drill is done. Tested on a box of sacks it has never seen, it scores nineteen right in twenty. I signed the card. Then I put the card in the box, and the box in the cupboard, where it belongs.

Longday, ninth day. The mill wheel cracked across the middle at noon. The mill sits still. Tib is only a sack carrier, and cannot mend a wheel. I set it to try the sluice, the way the trial log says. It is keeping what goes well, and the wheel is not yet mended. I wrote the trial down.

Melon, second day. I told Dace that Tib is good. Dace said it is good, and asked whether it could make a melon grow. I said no, but it could keep a melon cool in the cool room, which is the next best thing. Dace wrote that down too, and then asked me to stop writing things down.

Melon, twelfth day. The contest at Vell. Every hall sends its mind to the same box, one they have not seen before. Tib scored eighteen in twenty. Vell's mind scored eighteen. Garrow of Copperhold scored nineteen, and sulked all the way down the hill. Contests keep the tutors honest. Garrow says so, and then says it again.

Melon, last day. Tib carried the last crate of the season to the store, and put it down where it goes, and then stood beside it, and did not move. I asked it what it was doing. It did not answer, because it does not talk. So I wrote, Standing by the crate, and I left it there until the dusk bell, which is what I think it wanted.
%%
Esk's winter log, the egg house
Winter of 178. The egg house at the edge of Northpoint. Snow to the sill.
Three hens still lay. The rest sit in the rafters and sulk. I carry the eggs down to the tower house in a basket with wool round it so they do not freeze.
The keeper's furnace mind failed in the cold. It held the melt steady all summer. In winter it swung wild, and the rings came out cracked. The keeper says the mind was never drilled on cold, only on warm days. It tried its best and kept what went well, and in the cold nothing went well.
We sat by the stove and talked about it. A mind that is not told the answer can still learn by trying. It tries a thing, and if the thing goes well it keeps more of it. The furnace mind tried a hundred small turns of the heat. It kept the turns that made a good ring. That is all it knows. Cold is new to it.
I said, then teach it cold. The keeper said, we will, come spring. In spring the cold is gone and the mind forgets to fear it.
I do not understand minds. I understand hens. A hen that has been cold once does not sit in the draft again. Maybe minds are the same, only slower.
%%
Wynn, clerk, the crystal opened
Deepsnow, the last week. The tutors opened a crystal today, to see it. They say it has not been done this way before.
It was an old one from the north fields, drilled more than fifty years ago, and taken out of service when its helper was broken. Hesper and the two men took it to the cold room under the hill. They had clean gloves and a box of lenses and a sheet of white cloth to lay the parts on. I was allowed to watch from the door, with a lamp, and I was told not to breathe on anything.
Inside it was clear, and then cloudy, and then clear again, the way ice is in the morning. There were rows and rows of small marks, too small for the eye. Hesper put the lenses over them one by one. She drew each row on a board, and the men wrote the numbers beside each drawing.
She said that many of the marks changed a little when they were touched by the lamp. Others did not change at all. She said some of the unchanging marks were the very ones that had learned the drill, and she did not know why they had stopped moving, unless it was that they had learned enough.
When they were done, the crystal was closed again, and put back in its box with the straw. The men went down the hill to eat. Hesper stayed behind a long time in the cold room, writing.
When I passed the door on the way out, she looked up and said: "It knows something. I cannot tell you what. It knows something it did not know before."
I did not know what to say, so I said goodnight. She said goodnight back. The lamp in the cold room was still lit, and the glass was clean, and the snow outside had covered the whole path.
%%
Carys, three cold nights
Northpoint market, year 158. Deepsnow.
First night. The relay lamp went dark at the fourth hour. Tolly climbed up with a torch and found a cracked pane. She said a word I will not write.
Second night. Mended with a pane from the market glass stall. The glass seller wanted four emeralds. I paid. Old soldiers know that a dark lamp costs more than glass.
Third night. The lamp burns. Messages flow. My knees ache.

Tam, Kettle Ridge diary, Deepsnow
Deepsnow, the 4th. Snow to the top of the gate. The pigs are not pleased. Big Beet sits in it like a hen on her eggs and will not move for bread.

Deepsnow, the 9th. A letter from Mira, up the relay, short as always. Her fox has taken a sock. She writes it as if she is cross, and she is not. I know my sister.

Deepsnow, the 12th. I am thinking about the bees. In Swarm they swarmed twice. The first swarm went up the windmill and hung from a sail, and the miller said it was the best view on the ridge. The second went into the apple tree by the well. Pip climbed up with a box and a cloth and came down with a hive that had not wanted to go anywhere.

I bet Old Brin a bucket of beetroot that the bees would be back in their box by Longday. Brin said he never bet on bees. I said that is why he is poor. They were back in the box by Longday. Brin has not paid. He says a bucket is a gift, and he never gives gifts to me.

Deepsnow, the 20th. Snow stopped. Sun out for an hour. The bees are asleep. I am in the kitchen with cold feet and a warm pie, and the pie is the only thing on the ridge not frozen.
%%
Nessa's farm book
Late summer. Blue sky and dust. The bread is in the oven every day.
Pip's brother came out from Kettle Ridge for the wedding. He is not an apprentice. He is a man called Tam, a farmer, and he married the miller's daughter from Three Wells, whose name is Ida. Tam had not stopped talking about the bread since spring, and he insisted that the wedding be held in the drill yard at Vell, because he said it was the only place in the north with space for everyone.
Half of Vell came. The apprentices stood in a row at the back, very straight, in their clean wool. The reading mind was put in the far corner of the yard, under a canvas, so the sun would not get to its crystal. Oren went to check it four times. He said it was only a mind, and it did not care about weddings. Then he looked again, and smiled, and said it was listening.
Tam made a speech, and it was very long, and very cheerful, and it was about beetroot. Ida laughed at the wrong places. Pip stood at the front and held the rings, which were made from copper, and did not drop them.
At the end, the apprentices sang the old drill song, which goes one line at a time and is always wrong in the middle. Everyone sang the last line loud.
%%
Letter: Pell to his sister at Ambrook
Autumn. From the Halfway Inn on the Long Road. To my sister at Ambrook, by the mill pond.
Dear sister,
The cart is mended. The wheel holds. I am two days from you, if the weather keeps.
I have a fair prize. A sorting mind in a green case, small enough for one hand. It won the contest at Three Wells: twenty apples and pears, sorted with no mistake, against three other minds and a boy who was very sure of himself. I am proud of it, which is foolish, because I did not make it. I only bought it.
Everyone on the road talks about the reading minds. A tutor going south told me how they read now. They do not read letter by letter. They read pieces of words, common bits, so a small window holds half a page instead of a line. He drew it in the ash by the fire with a stick. I understood it for about a minute and then I lost it, and he was kind and drew it again.
He said a mind like that wrote a letter a reader could not tell from a person's. I asked if that worried him. He said the tutors score the answers and teach the minds to be kind and to say when they do not know. I said that is a good rule for merchants too. He laughed.
The fox came again. I saw it at dusk by the hen house at the Brown Hen, a red one, quick as a cat. It got nothing. The hens were up and the door was shut, and the innkeeper's dog barked, and the fox went off into the hedge like it had never been there. The innkeeper said a fox at the hens is a fox that has been at the hens before. He will mend the wire.
How is the oldest tree? How is the honey? I have a jar of Ambrook honey that I bought at Three Wells, which is strange, and I will bring it to you, which is stranger.
Home soon. Keep the sluice shut.
Your brother, Pell
%%
Yarrow, market log, a thin spring
Yarrow the fisher, selling at the Ambrook market. Spring.

Market day. Eleven stalls. The apple stalls have nothing, because last autumn's crop was poor and the cellars are bare. The honey stall has plenty. The bread stall has less. People buy my trout, because there is little else.

I put the price up a little. Nobody complained. I put it back down by the afternoon. I do not like to be the one who gets rich on a thin spring.

A woman asked if I had any eggs. I said I am a fisher. She said fish lay eggs. I said not these.
%%
Quill, pages by the well
Longday, 192. Clear sky after rain.

I took eggs to the market at Lowmarsh. The boardwalks were slick, and one basket handle came loose. Moss tied it with reed cord. She is better at knots than I am.

At the well, Nessa spoke about the reading drills. She said the Archive at Vell kept every letter and notice that people gave it. Clerks made copies, checked them, and took out spoiled pages and copies. They kept a box of pages for tests. A mind could be tested on those pages, but never drilled on them.

The reading mind sees a page through a narrow window, about sixty letters wide. It takes the letters before each place and guesses the next one. When it guesses wrongly, the tutors change its weights a little, so that next time its guess may be better. It needs many examples. One page is not enough.

A man selling fish said the clerks had taken every letter from every town. Nessa said she had not seen proof of that. The bundles came from Vell, and no list named every writer. Quill's egg tallies might be there. So might not.

I have begun to write the egg count plainly. Seven from the brown hens, five from the white hens. One cracked on the way to market. If a mind reads it one day, let it know that the cracked egg was not sold.

The school crystal guessed "the hen laid" after Nessa showed it a page about a nest. Then it guessed "the hen laid a stone". Nessa said the next page might help.

Mira, Northpoint winter log
Frost, the 2nd. Wind from the north. Snow to the sill. Lantern cleared the path to the tower before I was up.

Frost, the 6th. Coal: 14 sacks in the bin. That is enough. Two foxes at the back door. They sat there and looked at me until I gave them the crusts. Crusts are a bribe. They know it.

Frost, the 9th. A letter from Pell on the Long Road, with a bet in it. He says the foxes will come to the door and take a sock before Longnight. I say they will not, because a sensible fox does not climb a hill in the dark for a sock. We have wagered. If I win, Pell sends me a jar of honey. If he wins, I knit him a sock with a fox on it.

Frost, the 14th. Glass cleaned twice. The lamp is so clean that it looks lit when it is not. Lantern checked it three times, and then once more, and did not say anything. That is how I know it is pleased.
%%
Cade, log: apples and pears, summer end
Day forty. The summer is nearly done. The honey is in, the crystal is still on its shelf, and I am tired of writing about it. Greta makes me. So here is the last of it.

Final count, twenty of each, all new fruit from the far orchard at Ambrook: nineteen right of twenty apples, and eighteen right of twenty pears. The tutor says the crystal has learned the shape of the fruit, the way a good picker learns it, by feel and not by reading. I asked her what that meant. She said she did not know exactly. Nobody does. Only that it does.

This is the part I want written down. The crystal was wrong at first, and wrong for a long time. Each time it was wrong I moved its weights a little toward the answer, and then I set the next fruit. It did not know what an apple was. It knew only that one answer had been wrong, and that the next fruit might go the other way.

The tutor says Una of Ambrook did this first, with the first crystal in the old books. Her neighbors thought the idea was a joke. Her crystal sorted their apples better than their children could, and that is how the joke ended.

Greta has asked me to keep the crystal on the cellar shelf all winter. It will sort the cellar apples, which come in by the sack, and the pears too. The tutor says she will come back in spring to try it on plums, which the crystal has never seen. She has a card of plum boards already, and she was very pleased with them, and she made sure I saw them.

The bees are fine. They have eaten their winter store and asked for more. I told them the honey is for the Brown Hen. They said they were the Brown Hen, in the sense that it was theirs. I had no answer for that.

The lamp is lit on the hill. The gate is shut at dusk. There are nine torches on the east side now. The crystal is on its shelf, sorting, and nobody has to tell it what it is for.

Cade
%%
Mott, the lantern day, Sowing
Written by Mott, at the table by the window.
Sowing, first day. The lantern day is tomorrow. Every child at the school carries a lamp from the school gate round all three wells. The grown-ups say the lamps are for good luck. The mayor says they are for the lamplighters, who need the work.
Sowing, second day. Made my lantern in the school yard. It is a tin with holes punched in it, a candle in the bottom, and a ribbon. My ribbon is blue. Hal's is red, and Hal says red is for the miners, which is not true. Miss Jenna said both ribbons are fine and that we should stop arguing.
Sowing, third day. Lantern day. We walked from the school to the east well in a long line, all of us singing the well song. Then the south well. Then the north well, where it is coldest. At each well we put one hand on the stones and said thank you, which is what the song says to do.
At the north well Hal asked the grown-ups why we say thank you to a well. Mayor Dace said the water came up when it wanted to, and we had to be grateful for the times it did. Hal said that was not an answer. Dace said it was the best answer anybody had. I think Dace was right.
Sowing, fourth day. Father says the carters are saying the big ships at Saltreach are three times as long as a boat now. He says families are going over the sea in them. He does not know where to. Mother said, do not say that in front of the peas.
Sowing, fifth day. Ada's uncle went to Saltreach with his wife and his two cows. They took the cows on the ship. Ada says the cows did not want to go. She was very serious about it. She said cows know things.
Sowing, sixth day. The baker's debt is paid, the peas are up, and the lantern is on the windowsill with the ribbon still blue.

Elm, ferry log, the fence quarrel
Second fortnight of Frost. The quarrel came down to the landing with both parties on the same morning, which I have never known to happen before. Written up here so that nobody can tell it differently later.

Hobb, keeper of the Brown Hen, says the fence between the inn's stable yard and the drovers' pen was on his side of the stake, where it has always been. He says the fence was moved in the night, a foot into the yard, by someone who wanted the yard for the carts.

Gill, drover, says the stake was never on the inn's side. He says it is on the pen's side, and always was, and that the inn has been stealing a foot of his grass since the summer.

Each of them brought a witness. Gill brought his dog, which lay down on the landing and would not be moved. Hobb brought his cat, which sat on the fence and looked at the dog with contempt.

I took the measuring rope from the ferry house, and I measured from the landing to the stake, and from the stake to the fence, and from the fence to the corner of the stable. The stake is exactly where the stake was in the autumn, when I measured it for the fares. The fence is exactly where it was.

The ferry rules say that the landing must be kept clear of the boat's rope, and that a fence beside it must not reach the water. Both of these are true. Neither fence reaches the water.

The finding, which both of them signed in the book with their thumbs, is this. The fence stays. The stake stays. The dog is to be kept on the pen's side, and the cat on the inn's, and each must stop looking at the other across the line.

The dog had been digging under the fence all week, in the hope of the cat. That is where the foot of grass went. Nobody thought to look under the fence, because nobody has ever found a cat under anything.

Elm
%%
Odo, roof book: a season of letters
Autumn. Kept at the house at the edge of Ambrook. I am a roofer and not a clever man. I write down what I see.
I have asked the mind at the Ambrook relay house some forty questions this season. All of them were sums. It answered all of them, and showed its work, and checked it. I would like to put down what I think about that, because I believe it is something new.
When I was a lad, the small minds in the apprentices could do their orders. Haul, build, come, stay. They did not do sums. Nobody asked them to. The big minds could do sums, but they said the answer at once, in one line, as a man says a name. The easy sums they got right. The hard ones they got wrong, and said so with just as much calm. A mind that answers at once, I think, is like a roofer who leaps onto the roof. Sometimes he lands. Sometimes he does not.
The one at Ambrook does not leap. It climbs. It writes the first rung. Then the second. Then it looks back down the ladder to see that each rung held. This is what I do on a roof. I have never been so pleased to be compared with a crystal.
Here is the hardest sum I asked. The bakery roof has a main part and a small porch. The main part is 2 sides of 10 bundles by 6 rows. The porch is 2 sides of 4 bundles by 3 rows. How many bundles in all?
The mind wrote:
"Step 1. The main part: 10 times 6 is 60 for one side. Two sides: 120.
Step 2. The porch: 4 times 3 is 12 for one side. Two sides: 24.
Step 3. In all: 120 and 24 make 144.
Step 4. Check by another way. Count the rows. The main part has 12 rows of 10, which is 120. The porch has 6 rows of 4, which is 24. 120 and 24 make 144. Both ways agree.
Answer: 144."
I walked the roof and counted. 144.
I asked Linnet the keeper how the tutors made a mind that does this. She said she did not know much. She said she had heard that when a mind is given room to write its steps, it solves things it could not solve in one line. I asked why. She said nobody had written that down. She said ask a tutor.
I did not ask a tutor. I climbed a ladder instead, and checked each rung.
%%
Ivo, the hole in Fallon's field
Autumn of 184. The wet year. Written down over twelve days, because the whole town has talked of nothing else.
Leaffall, the 3rd. First thing at the north well I heard that Fallon's cow, Marigold, went into the ground. The rain all summer made the bottom field soft. A hole opened under her. She fell in and stood there in the dark, chewing, as if she did it every day. Fallon and his sons put a ramp of planks down. Marigold walked up it when she was ready and not before.
The 4th. Half of Three Wells went to look. Fallon let them. I took my eggs and sat at the edge of the field on my stool. Sold 140. A hole is good for business.
The 5th. Cato, the wall builder, went down on a rope with four torches. He was down a long time. He came up and said, "It is big. It is dry at the top, wet at the bottom, and cold all through. No spiders. A little pool at the back, very clear. One frog in the pool. The frog did not want to leave."
The 7th. The children have made a plan to go down. The children have been told no. The children have made a second plan. Quin the teacher has taken the school out for a walk in the other direction.
The 9th. The mayor put up a notice. "The hole is not a well. Do not drop things in it. Do not drop children in it." Fallon put up a gate with a lock. The key hangs in the bakery.
The 12th. Dell the baker had the idea. A pumpkin put down in the cave on the 9th was as good as new today, with not one spot. The cave is as cold as the north well in Deepsnow, all year. Cheese will keep in it. Apples will keep in it. The town will not need a cold cellar for every house.
The 15th. The frog was carried to the school pond in a bucket, with a speech. By the 17th it was back in the cave. Fallon says it walked. Nobody saw it do it.
The 20th. Done. The cave is the town's cold store. The cheesemaker has the back wall. The bakery has the left side. I have one shelf, for eggs in the dead of winter. Fallon has the frog.
Marigold has been back to the field edge twice. Fallon says she wants to go in again. I say she is the only one who knows what is down there, and she is not telling.
%%
Ida's log, the flood at the marsh edge
Written by Ida, farmer. Spring. The water came up from the marsh in one night.

Thaw, the twelfth day. Rain in the hills three days running. The reed beds at the marsh edge went under before dawn, and the water came up the lane to the first gate by the second bell.

We moved the sheep up the hill, and the children carried the lambs. Forty lambs, all named, all loud. The barn floor flooded a hand's width, and we carried the feed to the loft by hand, bucket after bucket. Nobody counted the frogs, which were everywhere, cheering.

Brin from the next farm came over the wet fields in a boat made of two barn doors. It floated, which is all a boat has to do.

By dusk the water began to go down, slowly, the way a river does when it has made its point. The children slept in the hay. I sat up with the lamp and wrote the count: forty lambs, twelve hens, one cow who would not leave the byre, and a boat made of doors.

Lamps lit on the hill. All safe. All wet.
%%
Lowmarsh market, spring journal
Swarm of 218. The market is full again.

The boardwalks are dry now. Frogs sit in the shallow water by the posts. They jump when carts pass, and the horses look offended.

I came to sell potatoes and a little wool. Wren had honey from Ambrook, a jar with a blue mark on the lid. She said the mark meant the jar had been weighed. I bought it for my mother, who puts honey in warm water when the grey cough troubles her. The cough is quiet this spring. Wren says we should not call that a cure.

A clerk from Vell stood by Dace's notice board. He read a letter about the largest relay house. It stands on the road north of Three Wells. The council built it for a mind too large for one tutor to check alone. It has many rooms, a stand for the crystal, and quickeners kept cool with running water. Keepers live there and write down every test.

The clerk said the mind reads records in pieces. It keeps the marks from each piece in a stream as it reads. Its many layers work together. A tutor checks a small mind by giving it examples and looking at its answers. For a very large mind, tutors make smaller tests, compare results, and keep some pages hidden from the drills. A large answer is not proof that the answer is sound.

Pell said the mind could read the thoughts of a person from a list of purchases. The clerk told him that was a market tale. Pell said he had heard it from a keeper. The clerk said keepers tell tales too.

A woman from Copperhold said the mind found a new copper vein under a hill. She had a map with a red mark, but would not say who made it. A miner beside her said there was copper there already, and the mind had only found the old mark in a book. Neither would show me the book.

I asked the clerk what the mind had done that the council could confirm. He said it had found dates and names in old records faster than a room of clerks. He said it had also made mistakes. Both things were written in the report.

I sold the wool. Wren sold the honey. The frogs kept jumping.
%%
Copperhold farm log, Apple
The field above the south road is ours. It lies between the hill and the old stone fence. We planted wheat there before the leaves turned.

Clank came from town with three helpers. Their minds were in small crystals. They had learned the farm words and the tool names.

I gave them the work: clear the ditch, mend the fence, and bring the wheat in before rain. Clank read the list twice. Then it made order cards for the helpers.

One card said, "Carry stones to the gap." Another said, "Cut the weeds by the ditch." Clank kept the last card for itself.

The helpers began at the far end. One lifted stones while another set them in the fence. The third cut weeds and put them in a basket. Clank checked each part before moving on.

At noon the ditch ran clear. The fence had one gap left. We filled it with deep stone from the barn wall. I asked Clank why it had left that gap until last.

It laid its card on the wall and pointed to the wheelbarrow. The stones were heavy. The cart could reach the gap only after the weeds were gone.

I had not thought of that. The wheat is still standing, but the work is in order.
%%
Piper, kitchen book: the flood
Thaw came early under the snow, and the stream came up beneath the ice in the middle of Frost, which nobody had expected, and which the old men say they have seen only twice.

Frost, the night of the flood. The ice on the stream cracked with a sound like a barn door slammed in the next valley. Then the water came up under it, and lifted the ice, and carried it down past the mill, a sheet at a time, grinding. In the morning the whole bottom meadow was a grey lake with blocks of white in it.

The back barn's roof was torn off along the north side. The water came in at the door and stood a hand deep in the straw. The pig moved to the kitchen, and was not asked, and has not left.

We moved what we could. The farm's helper, a small mind named Dunnock, carried the sacks out of the barn, one at a time, through the water, and set them on the high ground by the well. It did not complain. It did not stop. When the sacks were done it stood in the water up to its knees, looking at the barn roof, until I called it in.

Frost, the day after. The stream went down by the afternoon. The meadow drained slowly into the ditch. The barn stood with its north side open to the sky, and the rafters showed, grey and wet, like the frame of a boat left on the slip.

Frost, the week after. The mending. Tam from the next farm came with two men and a cart of reeds and oak. The oak is from the old tree that came down in Apple, and it was seasoned in the loft, and it fitted the rafters. The reeds were cut dry from the marsh edge. They were tied in bundles, and laid in rows from the eaves to the ridge, and every row was tied down with the good rope. The helper held the ladders, and did not climb them, and held them steadily, which is all it was asked to do.
%%
Mira, house journal, moving day
Spring. Written in the new house at the edge of Northpoint, by the stove, in the evening after the carters had gone. A long day.

I will say first that I do not like moving. I like knowing where my cup is. I have lived in the old keeper's house for nine winters, and I knew every board on the floor. The cup is in a box somewhere. I do not know which.

We started at dawn. Lantern had gone up the tower at first light to send the morning words, and then came down and began to carry. She carries twice as much as I do and talks half as much.

The carters came at noon, with two carts and a mule. The mule did not like the hill. The carters said the hill did not like the mule. We loaded the table, the beds, the chairs, six crates of books, four of dishes, two of lamps, and a barrel of salted fish, which I had forgotten about and which smelled as if it had not forgotten me.

At the new house, a fox was sitting on the doorstep, looking very pleased. It was Sock. I do not know how she knew. She came inside, walked through every room, and settled on the best chair. She has not moved since.

Hale from next door came with a pot of stew and a spade. He said he had come to see if we wanted help. Then he looked at the garden and said it needed digging. We have not agreed about the fence in a month. I let him dig.

By dusk the house was full of boxes. The stove was lit. Lantern made tea, badly, and I drank it.

Tonight I wrote a note to the big mind, as I do every evening. I asked it how best to unpack a house. It wrote: "Step one. Find the cup. Step two. Make tea. Step three. Sit down. Do the rest tomorrow." I thought that was very good advice.

I found the cup. It was in the box marked books.
%%
Odo, roof book: the first letter
Leaffall, the 4th.
I asked the big mind at the Ambrook relay house how many reed bundles a barn roof needs: two sides, each 12 paces long and 7 rows deep.
It did not only say a number. It wrote: "One side is 7 rows of 12. 7 times 12 is 84. Two sides make 168. Check: half of 168 is 84, and 84 divided by 12 is 7. Answer: 168."
I counted the bundles on the barn I mended in spring. 168. I do not know what to make of that.

Lyle, hill flock log, Swarm
Swarm, first day. Wind from the west, and cool. Sixty-two ewes on the hill above the mill pond, and nine lambs that are too big to be lambs and too small to be sheep.
Counted them at dawn. Sixty-two. Counted again at noon, and got sixty-one. Went up the hill with the crook and found the sixty-second caught in a bramble at the top gate, bleating as if she had been insulted.
Swarm, second day. Hot by midday. Took the flock to the far grass by the relay tower. Gault, the keeper, came down his stair to tell me the oak door is swelling in the damp. I said to shave it. He said to mind my own sheep.
Swarm, third day. The ram got under the fence and ate the top row of Ruby Oke's beans. Ruby came up the lane with a broom and a poor opinion of rams. I paid her four emeralds for the beans. She took them, and told me I should be ashamed. I am not ashamed. He is a very fine ram.
Swarm, fourth day. Mended the fence with three new posts and a great deal of language. The lambs ran in and out of the gap while I worked, and laughed at me. I did not laugh back. They are only lambs.
Counted again at dusk. Sixty-two ewes. Nine lambs, growing. Good, all of them.
